Quaestiones in Epistolas Pauli
by Hugh of Saint Victor
Source text: Public Domain. English translation/apparatus/encoding: CC BY-NC 4.0, Wroot Press.
https://migne.app/pl/175/quaestiones-in-epistolas-pauli/
----------------------------------------

## I. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE ROMANS.

[0431C] *Paul* is a common noun among the Hebrews, the Greeks, and the Latins, though not with the same ending [n: (Rom. I)]. Among the Hebrews it means *wonderful* or *chosen*; among the Greeks *quiet*; among the Latins *small*. This name was aptly imposed on Paul as a mark of a twofold, or a threefold, virtue, which the aforesaid interpretations suggest; or Paul was called an Apostle from Paulus Sergius the proconsul, whom he converted in Cyprus; or he bore two names.

QUESTION I. It is asked how Paul calls himself a servant, since elsewhere he says: *For you have not received the spirit of bondage again in fear [n: (Rom. VIII)]*. And elsewhere: *Now he is no longer a servant, but a son [n: (Galat. IV)]*. And the Lord in the Gospel: *I will now no longer call you servants, but friends [n: (Joan. XV)]*. Was Paul then not a friend? Solution. [0431D] Two kinds of fear make two kinds of servants: namely servile and filial. Paul, then, was a servant by filial fear, and not by servile: which servitude does not take away liberty or friendship, but rather establishes it.

QUESTION II. Why does Paul say that Christ was made from [0432C] the seed of David, when we confess him born of the Virgin? Solution. That he might note that Christ was conceived not after the manner of other men, but by the sole working of the Holy Spirit, from the Virgin, without male seed: whence other men can beget other men, but not make them.

QUESTION III. How was the incarnation itself brought about? Solution. I say that the very Word of God was made flesh, that is, man: yet not changed or converted into man or flesh, but clothed with flesh (that he might appear to mortals); for that union was so brought about that neither was the divine nature changed into the human, nor the human into the divine: nor was a new nature or a new person made out of the two natures, but ineffably the two natures are united in Christ: so that the one assuming should confer upon the assumed by grace all that it had by nature, and all [0432D] that was the assumed's by nature should become the assuming one's by condescension; whence the whole is called God, the whole man, and in turn man is God, and God man, which does not happen in the substances of man.

[0433A] QUESTION IV. How is it here said that the whole is God and the whole man, since those two natures are not parts of that person? Solution. This is said on account of a likeness, because the two natures are so united in Christ as parts in a whole, but differently. For not as a whole has its being from the conjunction of its parts, does the person of Christ have its being from the union of the humanity and the divinity, for neither nature has any new being. But that those natures are not parts of the person is clear from this, that either of them is predicated of the person, as when it is said: Christ is the divine nature. Likewise the names of the natures are predicated of one another, as when it is said: God is man and man is God: which does not happen in an integral whole and its parts.

QUESTION V. How is the image referred to the essence [0433B] only, when it is said of the Son only in a relative sense? Solution. Image sometimes signifies the divine essence, and then it is predicated in common of the three persons: it also signifies a relation, and then it is said of the Son alone.

QUESTION VI. What is not in the substance of man, that is in the substances of Christ? Solution. Because neither is the flesh the soul, nor the soul the flesh; nor is man the flesh or the soul, as God is man, and man is God, and Christ is both. And this comes about because the union between God and man is greater than that between flesh and soul. For the soul united to the flesh does not confer upon it all that it has by nature, since the flesh is not capable of the many things that are the soul's, as God united to man wholly infuses himself [0433C] into him.

QUESTION VII. What is it that is said, when it is said: Man is God and God is man, or Christ is God, Christ is man? Solution. In answering this question the modern doctors disagree among themselves. For some say that the same is predicated of itself, namely: this person, Christ; which is astonishing, since God means one thing, man another; and the son of man one thing, the Son of God another, as authority says. Which also seems to me to be false, because this name God means the same, when it is said: man is God, and: Christ is God, and: the Father is God. If it be said, it does not follow that the same is God, man, and Christ, and God the Father. Likewise man and God are common [0433D] nouns; how then do they properly signify this person, or when, or by whom was such an imposition made? Likewise Christ is man, and the Virgin [the Word] is man, is not the same thing predicated of both? If it be said no: then they are not of the same nature, since the name of the nature is not common to both according to the same signification. Others say that when it is said: man is God, that which is signified by this name God is not predicated, but rather to be personally united to God; and when it is said: God is man, to have man united in person is predicated. But according to this neither is man truly God, nor is God truly man. Others say that when it is said: man is God, the person of the divine nature is predicated of the person of the human nature; and when it is said: [0434A] God is man, the person of the human nature is predicated of the person of the divine nature: yet the person of the divine and of the human nature is the same, namely Christ; yet another is understood when one says: man, and when one says: God. These plainly concede that Christ is two things, of which one is simple and the other composite; one eternal, the other temporal; and that Christ is something composite according to his humanity, from flesh and soul, and something simple according to his divinity, and that the divinity is not a part of this person, but is the very person, while the flesh and soul are only parts.

QUESTION VIII. Whether Christ was twice begotten. Response. He was twice born and twice begotten: once from eternity out of the substance of the Father, again in time of the Virgin. [0434B] He has therefore two nativities, two generations, two filiations, an eternal and a temporal, yet he is not two sons or two born, but one and the same Son of God and son of man, though not by the same filiation.

QUESTION IX. Whether a person took up a person, or a nature a person, or a nature a nature. Solution. A person is not assumed, therefore neither did a person assume a person, nor a nature a person. But that a person assumed a nature, all concede; whether a nature assumed a nature is doubted by some: but, beyond doubt, the divine nature assumed the human, not into a unity of nature, but of person, that is, the union was so brought about that the one assuming and the assumed should be one person, not one nature, which, although authority manifestly [0434C] often says this, many deny, saying: if that which is assumed is a person, how is a person not assumed? But we say: although a person is assumed, yet God did not assume the person of man, but the nature, because he was not made of another person than he had been before, as he was made of another nature than he was, retaining his own; for in assuming the humanity, he did not lose the divinity. Therefore it is conceded that a nature, not a person, is assumed, not only by a person, but by a nature. Those authorities which say that the Word alone was incarnate exclude only the other persons, not the divine nature.

QUESTION X. What is predestination? Response. [0434D] The preparation of grace. Sometimes also the very applying of grace is called predestination.

QUESTION XI. Likewise it is asked, of what predestination was made: whether of the person, or of the nature? Solution. Of the person, not according to the divine nature, but according to the human. It can also be said that the nature was predestined, or the man assumed, so as to be exalted such that there was nothing higher to which he might be lifted.

QUESTION XII. Whether it ought simply to be conceded that Christ is a creature, or made. Solution. It is not to be conceded without such a qualification, according to the flesh: lest we seem to consent to the heretics who say that Christ was made according to either nature, and also on account of the implicit negation [0435A] contained in this name, creature: because that is called a creature which began to be and did not always exist. For it does not follow, if according to his humanity Christ did not always exist: therefore he did not always exist; as it rightly follows, if according to his Divinity he always existed, therefore he always existed.

QUESTION XIII. Whether Christ according to his humanity is the Son of God, or God? Which they wish to prove thus: according as he is man, he is predestined to be the Son of God: but that is, that he might be, he was predestined according as he is man, therefore according as he is man, he is the Son of God. Solution. It is not true that if according to man he is predestined to be the Son of God: therefore according to man he is the Son of God, unless "according to" be expressive of the person. But if [0435B] it notes a cause or a condition, it is not true, as appears in this like case: This man, according to the magnitude of his sin, repents that he may be saved, therefore according to the magnitude of his sin he is made saved.

QUESTION XIV. Whether according as he is man he is a person. Solution. Some say: If "according to" signifies a cause or a condition, according as he is man, he is not a person; saying: If according as he is man, he is a person, therefore a third in the Trinity, or another; but there is no other, therefore a third in the Trinity, and thus according as he is man he is God. Again, according as he is man, he is not distinguished by a personal property from the Father or the Holy Spirit, but by filiation alone, which he had from eternity. Others say that, inasmuch as he is man, he is a person. Whence it rightly follows, [0435C] if he is man, he is a person, and that consequence: if he is a person, he is a third person in the Trinity or another, they call false. For the whole posited does not posit the part, as the removed removes it, but the part posited posits the whole. But the part removed neither posits nor removes the whole. But that that which is assumed is a person is clear, since Augustine says that that which he took up and that which is assumed are one person.

QUESTION XV. Whether the soul of Christ is God? Solution. It is not to be conceded simply, lest we seem to consent to those who said that the Word assumed flesh only, and that the Word itself quickened the flesh in place of a soul. For this reason also the soul is not called God, because the name of soul redounds more upon the nature [0435D] than upon the person.

QUESTION XVI. Whether it ought to be conceded, That man existed from eternity? Solution. If by the pronoun "that" a person is indicated, it is true that that man existed from eternity; but if the human nature, it is not true, That man existed from eternity.

QUESTION XVII. Whether the soul of Christ is a person? Some say that it is not, while it is joined to the flesh: but separated it is a person. Others say that Christ's soul is the same person with the Word.



## I. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE ROMANS. (cont.)

QUESTION XVIII. Whether that which is signified by this name Christ is a universal? Which seems to be so: because it is predicated of several, as when it is said, man is Christ, and God is Christ. Solution. It need not [0436A] be conceded on that account, since in each case the predication is not conformable, although too there are two things which are predicated of several according to some, yet it is not that which is predicated of several. Likewise they wish to prove that it is that which is predicated of several, because the person, which is said of the one assuming, is also said of the assumed. Solution. Person is as it were a comprehensive name, for two, or several, are one person. Yet there are some who concede that what is signified by this name, Christ, is common to several: and signifies as much as this term, the person of the divine and human nature, yet Christ is not a universal, but two singulars according to these men.

QUESTION XIX. Whether the man assumed is God. Solution. [0436B] Many say that he is not, whom authority plainly contradicts. For the Apostle says that in him dwells the fullness of the divinity [n: (Col. II)]. And Ambrose: Whatever the Son of God has by nature, the son of man has by grace. Likewise the Lord speaking of himself: *All power is given to me in heaven,* etc. [n: (Matth. XXVIII)]. If he has omnipotence, he is omnipotent; if he is omnipotent, he is God. Likewise John the apostle says of him, that *he received the Spirit not by measure [n: (Joan. III)]*; but according to those who deny that the man assumed is God, the Spirit was given to him by measure, since he does not have all that the Word has, to which he is personally united.

QUESTION XX. Whether that man can forgive sins, since he is omnipotent? Solution. That man [0436C] can forgive sins, not because he is man, but because he is God, just as he can reduce the world to nothing, if he willed.

QUESTION XXI. Whether the creature is made equal to the Creator, since the soul of Christ, or the man assumed, has wholly by grace what God has by nature? Solution. The creature is not equal to God, because it is one thing to be wisdom, another to be wise by wisdom, another to have something by nature, another by grace.

Likewise the objection is made thus: It has been said that whatever the Word has by nature, the man has by grace: but the Word has eternity by nature, therefore the man has the same by grace, and if this is so, the man himself is eternal. Solution. This term, eternal, notes [0436D] a negation. For that is properly called eternal which always was, and did not begin to be, whence, because "eternal" does not simply predicate the person of the divine nature, but also designates such a negation, the man is not to be called eternal. Or it can be put thus: that man is the eternal God, he is not an eternal man.

QUESTION XXII. Whether the man assumed is an adoptive son, or a natural son. Some wish to prove that he is an adoptive son thus: by grace alone he was predestined, so as to be the Son of God, therefore he is a son of grace, and thus of adoption, therefore adoptive. Solution. To be the Son by grace alone he has not from grace but from nature, that is, the man assumed is not an adoptive son, [0437A] but a natural son, not by nature but by grace, through which he has whatever he possesses.

QUESTION XXIII. How does the Apostle show his affection toward the Romans, as the expositor says, by giving thanks to God, since for all the good things that God gives to good and bad alike, thanks are to be given to God? Solution. He gives thanks not only in respect of the gifts, but rather in respect of the Romans, namely because the gifts were conferred on them. But when someone gives thanks for evil gifts conferred, he does this only in respect of the gifts, not of those on whom they are conferred.

QUESTION XXIV. The expositor says that the Apostle in the Romans does not praise the faith, but the readiness of faith, which seems not to be praiseworthy, because he who [0437B] easily believes, easily disbelieves. Solution. There is a twofold readiness; the one, which comes from lightness of mind, and this is unworthy of praise; the other, which comes from vigor of mind, and much consideration of reason, and this is worthy of praise.

QUESTION XXV. The Lord forbids in the Gospel to swear by heaven or by earth [n: (Matth. V)]; the Apostle swears not by a creature but by the Creator, which is more, when he says: *God is my witness [n: (Rom. I)]*. Whence he seems to be a transgressor of the precept, and thus to sin unto death. Solution. The Lord forbids what is evil, namely to swear falsely, or truly without necessity; he counseled to speak the truth; he permitted an oath when it is necessary, in which three things ought to be present: judgment, as to the usefulness of discretion; [0437C] justice, as to whether it is to be done; truth, as to knowledge. If one of these be lacking, the guilt of perjury is incurred.

QUESTION XXVI. What it is to swear by God. Solution. The sense is: So may God profit me, or not, or as the truth is in God, so in this man; and if he empties it out as far as in him lies, he annihilates the truth of God, setting God up as a guarantor to those men: but if it is not true, he in a manner imprisons God.

QUESTION XXVII. Likewise what it is to swear by a creature? Solution. By God, who made it. Whence Augustine says that he who swears falsely by a stone is a perjurer.

QUESTION XXVIII. Whether an oath is good, [0437D] or evil, or indifferent: for if it is good, it is not to be forbidden; if evil, it is by no means to be done; if it is indifferent, how is it always from evil? Solution. It is not itself evil, but nevertheless an occasion of evil; whence the Lord himself counsels to avoid it, lest by its constant use we incur perjury.

QUESTION XXIX. How does the Apostle show his affection toward the Romans by saying: *I desire to see you that I may have some fruit among you [n: (ibid.)]*, since he seems to attend to his own advantage rather than to theirs? Solution. He does not seek his own advantage so much as theirs, while he desires them to bear fruit in good works, so that he too may obtain some advantage from their progress.

[0438A] QUESTION XXX. If it is true, as many hold, that the will with the deed is not worth more than the will without the deed, how does the Apostle say: I desire to see you that I may have some fruit among you; since he would not have more merit from the deed than he had from the will alone? Solution. To us, however, it seems that the deed with the will is a greater good than the will alone without the deed. But those men say: He desired to come for this reason, that with the deed the will might grow, and the merit become greater. What will they say about the passion of Christ? Did he not merit more by suffering than before by desire alone? For they cannot say that his will in the passion was augmented, so that thus the merit might grow.

[0438B] QUESTION XXXI. How does the Apostle say that he is a debtor to the Greeks and to the barbarians: did he not preach to them freely? therefore not out of debt. Solution. He does not say that he owes them anything from their merit, but from the office enjoined he owes to evangelize to them; and out of grace alone, as regards them, he preached, so this debt therefore does not take away grace, as he says elsewhere: *Necessity lies upon me to evangelize [n: (I Cor. IX)]*. Which necessity does not take away the will, as this debt does not remove grace.

Likewise it seems that there was in them that whence he owed this to them; from the law of nature, by which he owed them that which he would wish to be done to himself. Solution. We concede that by natural law he was bound to illumine them with the light of truth, yet it is not simply to be conceded that he [0438C] owed this to them.

QUESTION XXXII. Likewise when it is said: *The just man lives by faith [n: (Hebr. X)]*, it is asked why the efficacy of justice is attributed to faith, and not to charity; since faith is somewhere where there is no justice; charity nowhere without justice. To this some respond, saying: Charity and justice are the same, and therefore neither is the cause of the other. Or otherwise: although charity is the cause of justice, yet fittingly faith, which is the cause of charity, is said to be the cause of justice; because whatever is the cause of a cause, is the cause also of the effect. And it is to be known that it is one thing to believe that God exists, which is the faith of knowledge; another to believe God, which is called the faith of consent; another to believe in God, which is called the faith of trust; another to believe unto God, which is by faith [0438D] and love to tend toward God. Likewise it is one thing which is believed; another that by which it is believed; each is often designated by the name of faith. Likewise that by which it is believed is sometimes informed by charity, and then only, according to some, is it called a virtue; but when it is without charity, it is an unformed quality; it is neither a virtue nor does it justify. To others it seems that wherever there is faith, even of knowledge, as far as in itself, it always justifies: yet its effect is sometimes hindered by the abundance of evil. Faith is a virtue by which are believed the things that are not seen: or a certitude of invisible things pertaining to religion, above opinion and within knowledge. Charity justifies, and faith, and grace, and God: therefore four justify. [0439A] Solution. It is not therefore true, that four, because this would be separately: for in faith and through faith charity justifies, and in charity, grace, and through grace, God.

QUESTION XXXIII. How does he say in the plural, the invisible things of God, since God is simple and one, nor is there anything in God that is not God, nor is anything here called the invisible of God which is not God? Solution. That which is one and simple in nature, comes into our knowledge not as one and simple, but as many: and this is rarely grasped, why, namely, it comes thus, because the inner eye is not yet able to attain to that simplicity and ineffable unity which is God, so as to understand him as he is. Whence, when it understands God as good, wise, omnipotent, [0439B] and the like, as it were several things come into the mind, which is one in nature. From which a higher question arises: whether those things which are understood by man's reason as several, are in themselves, or in God, distinguished by any difference. They do not differ among themselves substantially or personally: as the eternal reasons of things, which were in the mind of God, it is clear that they differ in some way, yet in what way is not certain.



## I. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE ROMANS. (cont.)

QUESTION XXXIV. Likewise, since it is said: His everlasting power also, and divinity, it may be asked: These two, the power of God and the divinity, since they are invisible, how are they distinguished from the invisible things? For it cannot fittingly be said, animals live, and men, and horses, since men and horses are animals. Solution. [0439C] The names that are said of God, some signify what God is not, as immortal and immeasurable, eternal and infinite. Some note what is in God, as wise, good: some by their predication suggest what God is, as goodness and wisdom. Lest anyone then should suppose that only those are beheld by the creature through the understanding of God which indicate what God is not, as immeasurable, he added, his everlasting power also, etc. Otherwise according to others: by the invisible things is understood the Father; by the power, the Son; by the divinity, the Holy Spirit. According to this it seems that the philosophers had knowledge of the supreme Trinity through the things that are made. But Augustine on Exodus says that the philosophers did not attain to the knowledge of the third person, [0439D] but only περὶ τοῦ αγάθου, that is, concerning the Father, and περὶ νόον, that is, concerning the Son, did they philosophize. To this some say that that distinction of the supreme Trinity which the catholic faith confesses they did not have, nor could have, except by revelation. For God is known in four ways: in two ways inwardly, namely by natural reason, which the Apostle notes according to some, saying: *What is known of God is manifest in them [n: (Rom. I)]*, and by divine inspiration, which the Apostle there notes: *For God has manifested it to them [n: (ibid.)]*. In two ways outwardly, by the created work, as the Apostle suggests, saying: *The invisible things of God [n: (ibid.)]*, and by Scripture, which way is sufficiently plain. God willed therefore to lie hidden in some things, that faith might have [0440A] its merit, and to appear in some, that unbelief might have no excuse. Note, in the magnitude of the universe the divine power is noted, in its beauty the wisdom, in its usefulness the goodness, whence it is clear that not only in all things together, but in each single thing, there shines a certain image and vestige of the Trinity. Nevertheless it is to be observed carefully, when likenesses are brought in, whether it is to demonstrate the identity of essence, or to suggest the personal distinction, or to show that the incarnation pertains to the Son alone, although it is itself the work of the Trinity.

QUESTION XXXV. Why is the Father understood through the invisible things rather than the Son, or the Holy Spirit, since they too are invisible? Solution. Because the Father is nowhere read to have appeared in a visible form, as the Son in the man [0440B] assumed, and the Holy Spirit in the form of a dove, and in tongues of fire.

QUESTION XXXVI. Why the Son through the power? Solution. Because he himself is the operative power of the Father, through whom all things were made.

QUESTION XXXVII. Why is the Holy Spirit signified through the divinity? Solution. That it may be shown to proceed in common from the Father and the Son, he obtains a common name as though proper. Or by the invisible things the Holy Spirit is understood, whence "invisible things" is said in the plural on account of the diversity of gifts. By the power the Father, to whose property power is wont to be referred. By the divinity, or Deity, is understood the Son, for thus different men hold different views.

QUESTION XXXVIII. It is asked concerning that which the Apostle says [0440C] of the philosophers, that they were inexcusable: *Because when they had known God, they did not glorify him as God [n: (ibid.)]*. For they seem to have had an excuse: for not so great a grace was conferred on them, out of which they could glorify God. For could they glorify God without charity? Could anyone have charity without faith? Could they, from that degree of knowledge which they had, love? Solution. They were therefore inexcusable, because they did not do as much as they could.

QUESTION XXXIX. Likewise it is asked, if they had done as much as they could, whether they would be worthy of salvation, if they had departed from this life in such a state. For who is worthy of salvation without faith, or who can perish with charity? [0440D] Master Peter Abelard says that they were worthy of salvation, that is, that there should be given to them the means whereby they might be saved; because if they had done as much as they could, God would never permit them to pass on without faith. Others say that they were therefore inexcusable, because from that degree of knowledge they could love: and at once from this state faith would be given to them. But according to this, faith is from charity, not charity from faith. But a third group hold that by no degree of knowledge could they love; yet if they had done what they could, at once faith would be given to them, out of which they would love God, and thus glorify him. To whom it is objected: Could they merit faith? Perhaps they will say: That, although they could not merit faith, yet from that which they had, they could be made fit and apt to receive faith. But how do they know that [0441A] from this they would be made fit; or if fit, how do they know that faith would be given to them? Were not the Tyrians and Sidonians fit? yet the preaching was not made to them. Solution. But we believe that they could glorify God in part, even if not yet perfectly, for if they attributed to God what they had received, and sought his glory and not their own, they would glorify God according to something: in which they would love God, even if they could not perfectly.

It is objected. From that knowledge which they had, they could at least in part love God: therefore from charity they could do this, which they had, or which they did not have; but not from that which they did not have: therefore from that which they had, and thus they had the love of God, and thus were worthy of salvation. Solution. It is to be conceded [0441B] that they could love God from the charity which they had; yet it is not simply to be said that they had the love of God: just as this sick and weak man carries a stone from the strength which he has; yet it is not simply to be said that this man is strong, or has strength. For we do not concede that those who are in mortal sin can do no good work, as some hold, but they do many good things, although insufficient for salvation on account of the greater evil which they have.

QUESTION XL. Whether natural reason can do anything of itself without the aid of grace? Solution. Some say that natural reason can do much of itself, as appears in the philosophers, who, relying on reason alone, knew much not only in the comprehension of truth concerning creatures, [0441C] but even concerning the Creator, namely that God is, and is one, and that he is triune. But to this thought they seem not to have attained without the aid of grace. Whence the Apostle: What is known of God is manifest in them; and at once he adds: For God has manifested it to them.

QUESTION XLI. How then without grace, if God manifested it to them, is not this grace? To this some respond thus: God is said to have manifested, because he made such creatures that from them the Creator himself could be known. Whence he adds: For the invisible things of God, etc.

QUESTION XLII. Likewise if natural reason avails so much, as to suffice for this degree of knowledge, [0441D] it is asked in what it was more efficacious before sin than now; or how it is now weaker than then? For as then it knew that God is, and is one, and triune; so also now. And as now it cannot comprehend the mystery of the incarnation without the aid of grace, so neither then that mystery which is hidden in God, who made all things, as the Apostle says, as though nothing served the creatures whereby this could be known. Solution. Reason before sin comprehended more easily and more perfectly what now with great difficulty, and less perfectly, and from afar it descries: it would also then have known many things which now it does not know. It is objected against those who say that natural reason can do something of itself: Can not the outer eye see nothing without the illumination of the light coming upon [0442A] it, or of the ray of the sun, or of another? So the inner eye can do nothing of itself without the illumination of the light which illumines every man coming into this world. What then can natural reason do without grace, since such light is from grace? To which some respond thus: In the first creation there was set out and set before the inner eye the illumination of the supreme light: as far as and to what end, from such a setting out and setting before, without any grace supervening, it might of itself avail to attain: which illumination was not of the substance of reason, nor of its nature, but of its gratuitous gift. Yet it can be said that it sees naturally; because, with the nature was given the aptitude and fitness for seeing, that light being set out, of which the discourse before proceeded. And so without every kind of grace the eye of the mind can see [0442B] nothing: yet it can well see without a supervening grace other than that which was conferred with the nature, which supervening grace is most especially wont to be called grace.



## I. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE ROMANS. (cont.)

QUESTION XLIII. *God gave them up to the desires of their heart, and to the passions of shame, and to a reprobate sense.* From these authorities, and many others, such as this: *He gave them the spirit of compunction, that seeing they might not see, and hearing they might not hear [n: (Rom. XI)]*. And this: *The heart of Pharaoh was hardened [n: (Exod. VII)]*. And this: *Whom he will he hardens, on whom he will he has mercy [n: (Rom. IX)]*. From these and many others certain evils seem to be brought about by God's operation. Augustine too heaps up many things into one for the proof of a matter of this kind. Afterward he infers. From which it is manifest [0442C] that God works in the hearts of men, in the good by inclining them to good things out of mercy, or in the evil by inclining them to evil things by his judgment, sometimes hidden, sometimes manifest, but always just. Likewise: *Is it not just, that he who is in filth should be filthy still? [n: (Apoc. XXII.)]* By whom is this just? Is it not by God, from whom is all that is just? Therefore God seems to work this. Likewise that sin which is the penalty of a preceding sin, from whom is it? All that is just is from God, and that sin is a just penalty. Therefore it seems to have its origin from God. Solution. To this indeed some respond, saying that all sin is from God, not only that which is the penalty of another, but also that which is only fault, conceding that theft, robbery, [0442D] adultery are from God, according to that saying of the prophet: *There is no evil in the city, which God does not make [n: (Amos. III)]*. Because also by such a reasoning they attempt to prove the same. Every essence is from God, but an evil will and an evil action are sin, and have essence: whence it is gathered that sin is from God according to the opinion of these men. Against whom the objection is made thus. What is it to make sin, but to sin? What is it to make adultery, but to make to be committed in adultery? to make theft, but to steal? Whence if it be conceded that he makes sin, it follows that God sins, steals, commits adultery, kills: which it is not only wicked to say, but even to think. That which is objected concerning the evil will and the action not good, that they are something, and thus from God. Thus it is resolved. Of sins one is according to [0443A] itself, another according to another. Sin according to itself is a certain disorder, as the privation of justice; and it itself is nothing but the absence of justice, whence it is not from God, since it is nothing. For God is not the author of that which is nothing. An evil will, and an action not good, is sin according to another, namely according to the disorder, and these are something, and are called sins not from that which they have, but from that which they do not have: for they are sins for this reason, that they do not have order or measure. That saying of the prophet, namely that he says: There is no evil in the city, which God does not make, is understood of the evil of adversity, not of the evil of perversity. Or perhaps we are not even compelled to understand this of the evil of adversity, if we look more diligently at the context of this Scripture. For thus [0443B] it stands in the prophet: There is no evil in the city, which the Lord does not make known to his servants the prophets. Likewise they object concerning the power of sinning, that it is from God: and it itself is sin, and thus sin is from God, as it seems. Solution. This is nothing, because the power of sinning is neither sin, nor a sufficient cause of sinning without the will.

QUESTION XLIII. Again it is asked thus. Whether every will is from God, since nothing is, which does not have its being from God? Solution. It is not to be conceded that every will is from God, since nothing is, which does not have its being from God: for this would be both good and evil alike. The same is understood of action. For when an evil action or will is spoken of, the expression redounds more upon the quality than upon the essence.

[0443C] Wherefore, although it has that whence it is said to be from God, yet since its quality is not from him, an evil action or will is not to be said to be from God. Note that disorder is said to be the quality of an evil action, on account of the similar manner of response; or "quality" is taken broadly for that by which it is assigned of what sort something is, or is not. Likewise this proposition: The essence of this action is from God, is understood in two ways: either that it itself is from God, or that from which it has being is from God. But others say that not every sin is from God; but only according to that which is the penalty of another, is done by God's operation; saying: If what is said, God gave them up to a reprobate sense, be referred to permission alone, this could be said generally of every sin, that God works [0443D] it; but God is never said to give anyone up to any sin, except that which is the penalty of sin. Likewise, if it be referred to the withdrawal of grace, from this it is plain that God works it, since he withdraws grace. Likewise since that follows necessarily from the withdrawal of grace, who is the cause of it but the withdrawer? as if a house had some prop without which it could not stand, if anyone took away the prop, who would become the cause of the ruin, but he who removed the prop? Or if some were in a little boat on the sea, if anyone sank it, since without it men could not live, who is the cause of death but the sinker? By these likenesses, then, they attempt to assert that God works certain sins, if not [0444A] all. But there does not seem to be a likeness between a house, which can merit nothing, and a man, by whose fault is offered that without which he cannot stand. But a more fitting likeness would be where, if a house were about to fall, and someone put his hand under and held it up lest it fall, if afterward, reason requiring it, he withdrew his hand, he would indeed not be the cause why the house fell, but he was the cause why it did not fall sooner. As if someone were naked, and another gave him clothes, and that clothed man then offended the one with whose clothes he is clad, so that he reasonably took away the clothes, and thus he died naked, who is the cause of death? Is it not he himself who dies naked? And if anyone, cause requiring it, withdraws teaching from someone to whom he before displayed it, he does this not by doing something, but rather by not doing what he was doing. [0444B] So too we say that God, by withdrawing grace, our fault requiring it, is not himself the cause why grace is withdrawn according to us: nor did he do this by doing something, but by not doing what he before did. Thus therefore it is by no reasoning to be conceded that sin is done by God's operation, whether it be the penalty of sin, or not; this therefore, which Augustine says, that God works in the hearts of men, either by inclining to good or to evil, is said to work either by the likeness of the diction of this verb *to withdraw,* which is construed with the accusative, as if it signified to do something; or, what is better, *to work* is taken for to work, or not to work, as here: *For what I work I do not understand [n: (Infra, c. 7)]*. For otherwise, if it were not so taken, to do evil, and not to do good, [0444C] would not be parts of working. And that: *He will render to each according to his works,* namely for those which he did, and for those which he did not do, when he ought to have done them. But that which is said: *He who is in filth, let him be filthy still [n: (Apoc. 22, infra, c. 2)]*, is understood thus: the justice is not from the quality of the defilement, but from the judgment of God, by which it is done. Whence, if it be called just, not the justice which is in it, but in the rule of God, not that its being defiled is from God, or that God does it. Likewise concerning any sin whatever which is the penalty of a preceding sin. For that such a penalty is called just, this is not from the quality of itself, but from the judgment of God.



## I. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE ROMANS. (cont.)

QUESTION XLV. Whether the penalty itself, which is also [0444D] fault, is from God. Which seems to be so, because all that is just is from God, and every such penalty is just. Therefore it seems that such a penalty is from God. Solution. It is not necessary to say this: since from the quality of itself it does not have that it is just, but from the law of God, as has already been said: for it is written: *God did not make death, nor does he rejoice in the destruction of the living [n: (Sap. I)]*. Or, even if it be conceded that the penalty is from God, yet not the fault, although penalty and fault are the same. For it does not have from God that it is fault, even if in some way it has from God that it is penalty. For since a penalty is something in itself, on three accounts it is said to be from God, namely on account of its matter, which God makes, as the matter of fire; and on account of the nature, namely that such a thing cannot be in such a thing without suffering: which nature is from God, and on account of the judgment [0445A] of God, by which such a penalty is inflicted on such a fault. Yet we do not say that God is the cause of that penalty; for this would be to say that God had made the fault of that penalty, because he is not the cause of the corruption; which if it were not, he who is punished would not suffer anything. Therefore a penalty of this kind, which is something, is said to be from God on three accounts. On account of the matter, and the nature, and the judgment. But according to two, namely the fault and the corruption, it is not from God. But that penalty which is sin, on two accounts can it be said to be from God, namely on account of the judgment of God, and on account of the nature, which is that no one can so sin, without being punished by that very thing. But the fault and the corruption are in no way from God. It is not therefore to be said that by God's operation or impulse anyone is cast headlong into sin; [0445B] nor even into that which is penalty; but the Scriptures which seem to hold this are to be referred either to permission, or to the withdrawal of grace. Some also refer them to the opening of the way, because wickedness conceived within cannot go out unless a way is opened for it, as Nebuchadnezzar had first conceived malice within, and God, by exposing to him the Jewish nation, opened a way for him, and thus he exercised the malice which he before had shut within. But others refer it to the closing of the way, because God closed all the other ways, that it might not be permitted to go out, so that it has not another by which to go out. Hence one is said to be cast headlong into sin, because he does not close this way by which it goes out, as the others by which it does not go forth. Whence it is clear that God is not the cause why it comes forth by this, but he himself who [0445C] goes out; but why it does not go out by another, God is the cause, and why also rather by this than by another, is from both; why he does not sin worse, God is the author; but why so much, and not less, is from him. Just as, if someone were in a tower, wishing to cast himself down through a window, and some other closed all the windows except one, and that man cast himself down through it. See that not through another: he who closes the rest is the cause that it is through this one, not he who closes; but he himself, who cast himself down. But that it is rather through this than through another, is from both. There are also those who refer them to occasion, as when the Lord entered Jerusalem, the whole city was stirred, and the malice before conceived, having taken occasion, was excited to envy; and thus to persecution. [0445D] Therefore it is referred either to permission, or to the withdrawal of grace, or to the opening of the way, or to the not-closing of the way, according to some, that which is said: *He gave them up to the desires of the heart, and to the passions of shame,* etc. [n: (Rom. I.)] But we rather say all of them, than any one of the several, even if all do not everywhere concur; yet always three, namely permission, and the withdrawal of grace, and the not-closing of the way, do concur; but the opening of the way, and occasion, are not found in all.

QUESTION XLVI. How is God said to give them up to the passions of shame, since they not only suffer nothing there, but even take delight? Solution. On account of the effect; for because eternal penalty follows, [0446A] the passions of sin of this kind are called penalties. Or even in the present life they suffer. For they cannot so sin, without suffering, without nature being harmed, corrupted, and deprived of some good, and defiled; and thus it is true that they already suffer in the present.

QUESTION XLVII. It is asked again what evil it brings upon the reprobate, to be given up to a reprobate sense, since they are not going to repent. For those who are blinded, so that they put light for darkness, and darkness for light, seem to sin less through such blinding, since they do nothing against conscience; whence what is a greater sin in itself, is less to him by whom it is done, since it is less done against conscience. Therefore since these sin less against conscience on account of the reprobate sense, it seems to profit them to be thus blinded. Solution. Such a necessity [0446B] of ignorance does not have excuse, because it comes from a perverse will.

QUESTION XLVIII. How does God not sin by working in their hearts, so that they are inclined to evil? Solution. If the aforesaid things be called to mind, the response is plain: for by doing this he does not send in malice.

QUESTION XLIX. How can it be defended, that God does not act cruelly in condemning this man, who, all grace being withdrawn from him and left to himself, cannot but sin: for if he does not avoid what he cannot avoid, what fault is it to him? or if for this he is condemned, it seems that he is dealt with unjustly. Solution. That impossibility is inexcusable, which proceeded from the fault and vice of his own will; because he first cast himself headlong, although [0446C] now he sins unwillingly; yet God justly condemns him for his sins. *For it is just that he who is in filth should be filthy still [n: (Apoc. XXII)]*.

QUESTION L. How does God not consent to those who sin, since he knows the evil, and can forbid it, and the expositor says: To consent is not to correct when you can. Solution. God corrects and reproves those who sin in many ways, both by natural reason, and by written law, and through his ministers, and through his own or others' scourges: whence he is by no means to be said to consent to those who sin.

QUESTION LI. Whether those who do and those who consent sin equally. Solution. In matters of this kind, which can vary according to different causes, a general judgment [0446D] cannot be given: for according to the intention such things are to be judged.

[n: (Rom. 3)] QUESTION LII. *According to his hardness, he is impenitent,* etc. It is asked what it is to repent. Solution. Penitence is a compunction of mind concerning things past, and a resolution concerning the future, or a grief, because he made a resolution which he will no more carry out. For what else is it truly to repent, than to bewail things committed, and, by resolution, not to commit further things to be bewailed? Penitence is also called satisfaction for sins. Whence the priest is said to enjoin penance. Likewise penitence is of one kind late, of another kind fruitless. Fruitless, which does not profit: as that of Judas, and of the senseless who say: *We senseless ones,* etc. [n: (Sap. V.)]. Late, which is wont to be done tardily when there is no time. Thus it is said: Truly [0447A] penitent is he who is contrite in heart, and confesses with the mouth, and renders worthy satisfaction.

QUESTION LIII. *When it is said in the day of wrath,* etc. It is asked concerning the day of judgment, why it is called the day of wrath rather than of mercy; for as the evil are to hear: *Go into eternal fire,* so the good are to hear: *Come, blessed of my Father,* etc. [n: (Matth. XXV.)] Solution. In that it is called the day of wrath, provision is made for us, that we may always fear that day, and by fearing may beware of the eternal burning. Note that it is called day not for time, but for manifestation. Likewise it is to be noted that all will come to judgment, both good and evil. Of the good, some to judge, namely the very good; others to be judged, as the less good. Likewise of the evil, some are already judged, whose damnation [0447B] is already certain; others yet to be judged, whose damnation is uncertain.

QUESTION LIV. How is it to be understood concerning the saints, that they will judge the evil. To which some respond that this is nothing other than that the excellence of their glory will show how great a penalty they are worthy of who persecuted them, or did not imitate them. Or they are for this reason called judges, because in the judgment itself the eternal reasons will be made manifest to them, according to which the judgment will be made, and the merits of each, concerning which the judgment will be made. According to that of Daniel: *The judgment sat, and the books were opened [n: (Dan. VII)]*, namely of the reasons and merits: those therefore whose penalty or glory is not yet so plain, are to be judged in the day of judgment.

[0447C] QUESTION LV. It is asked again concerning that *according to the impenitent heart,* concerning the sin against the Holy Spirit, what it is, and why it is called irremissible. Whether because it cannot be forgiven; or for this reason because it will never be forgiven, although it could be; or because it is scarcely, and rarely, and with difficulty forgiven. Solution. Some say that the sin against the Holy Spirit is out of envy to disparage the divine goodness: of which sin so great is the stain, that those who so sin can never submit to the humility of penitence. Others say that despair or impenitence is called the sin against the Holy Spirit. Others that it is to sow discords among brethren with the torches of envy. But to those who say that this sin can be forgiven, the objection is made thus: If this is possible, that such a fault be forgiven, from this supposition no [0447D] impossibility follows; but the truth says that *this sin is not forgiven, neither in this world nor in the world to come [n: (Matth. XIV)]*; but if it were forgiven, it would be false that it is not forgiven: which is impossible, because the Truth cannot lie. Of this sin no one is certain: yet this is known, that if anyone has sinned it, he will never obtain pardon.

QUESTION LVI. How does he *render to each according to his works,* since this man's works are good, and his intention evil, and that man's works evil, and his intention good, since the name of the work is imposed from the disposition: will he be condemned for good works, because the intention is evil, or saved for evil ones, because the intention is good? Solution. Intention does not suffice in all cases for works to be called good, but it is always required that they be [0448A] good to him by whom they are done: for if the works be good in themselves, and the intention evil, he is punished for the evil intention, not for the good works; nor will he be rewarded for them, because they are made useless to him through the evil intention. But for this, that the works be evil to him, an evil intention is not required; for the intention can be good or evil, while the works are evil: whatever the intention may have been, from the fact that the works are evil, it harms him who did them: but in those things which are indifferent, that is, as far as in themselves neither good nor evil, judgment ought to be referred according to the intention.



## I. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE ROMANS. (cont.)

QUESTION LVII. How is it rendered to each according to his own works, since this man works good things through his whole life and falls at the end, and another works evil things through his whole life and rises at the end, through penance; [0448B] will not he who worked good things be damned, and he who worked evil things be saved? How then according to works? Solution. A man's works are said to be those with which he departs from this life, by which alone God will requite him; but those good things which he did who fell at the end are mortified, and become not his own through the supervening evil. So too, in the case of him who lived badly and rose at the end through penance, the evil things he did become not his own through the supervening good things; or else judgment is made with respect to works, so that he is punished less, because he did good things, or will have less glory, because he sinned.

QUESTION LVIII. It is likewise asked how [it is] according to works, since this man has the will to do evil but not the power to carry it out, and another has the will to do good but not the power to fulfill it. Solution. [0448C] Their works are said to be theirs from the fact that they are in their wills, and it does not rest with them that they are not done. Whence the Lord in the Gospel: *Whoever shall look upon a woman to lust after her has already committed adultery with her in his heart [n: (Matth. V)]*; nor yet do we say that he is now punished as much as he would be punished for the works themselves fulfilled, but as for works, though he would not be punished as much as for the will alone.

QUESTION LIX. It is asked concerning an unbaptized child how requital is made to him according to his works, since he dies at once before he does anything. Solution. The works of his parents come to be in his conception and in his birth, and for them he will be damned as though he had actually done them. Likewise concerning a baptized child we say that [0448D] he is saved by the grace of baptism without his own merits.

QUESTION LX. It is asked concerning him who suffers for Christ and does not work, how it is rendered to him according to work? Solution. "Works" are taken both for sufferings and for works, as "deed" is taken for what is done and for what is not done: when that is done which ought not to be done, it is called a sin; but when that is not done which ought to be done, it is a fault.

QUESTION LXI. Since guilt is temporal and the punishment eternal, how will he render according to works? Solution. The guilt of the wicked, so far as lies in themselves, is eternal, for it was in their will to remain always in evil: whence by the just judgment of God they will be punished for eternity. Or else the evil will, and the guilt of fault, and the impenitence of heart will be eternal in him. Whence the Lord [0449A] is to say to them: *Depart from me, all you who work iniquity [n: (Luc. I)]*. He will not say, who have worked, but who work. For not to repent is a sin, and they cannot repent. And so they will sin for eternity, and thus for eternal guilt the punishment will be everlasting.

QUESTION LXII. Likewise, since the good are to receive more than they have merited, and the evil are punished less than they might be according to justice, how [is it] according to works? Solution. There is no comparison between merit and reward, but the sense is: he who has merited more will receive more, and he who less will receive less, according to quality and quantity: for the merit of one and the reward is at one time equal and unlike, at another time like and unequal, with the merit and reward of another; as two martyrs, or two confessors, who are of the same [0449B] and equal merit and reward, are equal and alike in justice and glory; but if one martyr and one confessor are equal, they are nevertheless unlike according to quality, because the one is a martyr and the other a confessor, etc.

QUESTION LXIII. It is asked concerning that which is said: *Tribulation and anguish upon every soul,* etc. [n: (Rom. II.)] Shall a man be punished in the soul alone, and not also in the body? Why then does he say, upon every soul, and not upon the body, since he sinned in both? Solution. There are certain sins of the soul, for which it alone will be punished, such as anger, envy, unbelief, of which the Apostle treats especially in this place: and therefore he makes mention of the punishment of the soul, not the punishment of the body. There are also certain sins of the body, for which [0449C] it will be punished, such as gluttony, lust, murders, contentions, which are performed by the ministry of the body; whence it is clear that they will be punished in both, according to that word: *With a double destruction destroy them [n: (Jerem. XVII)]*.

QUESTION LXIV. It is asked concerning the glorification of the body, what it confers upon the man or upon the soul, since the sole beatitude of man is the vision of God, of which the soul alone is capable, whence it seems to confer nothing upon the glory which will be in the body. Solution. The glorification of the body will be unto the increase of glory and joy for the soul itself. For it will greatly rejoice, when it sees the body, formerly so weak and feeble, thus made solid, that it can suffer no further injury.

QUESTION LXV. It is asked, since the glory of the soul is to contemplate God, and to enjoy the vision of him, and in him [0449D] to know all things — for *God will be all in all [n: (I Cor. XV)]*, being the mirror of every creature, just as every creature now is a mirror of God — since this, I say, is so, how will the glorification of the body be said to befit it, so that its beatitude is increased? Solution. It will know this glorification already in God himself, and the contemplation will be a part of its beatitude.

QUESTION LXVI. Since the Apostle says: *God is no respecter of persons [n: (Act. X)]*, it is asked what it is to accept persons. Does not the Apostle say: *To whom honor, honor; to whom fear, fear [n: (Rom. XIII)]*? Ought we not to show greater reverence to one than to another? Solution. He accepts persons who venerates a man on account of something that is the man's, and [0450A] prefers him to others — which God does not do. For when we show fear or honor to those who are established in dignity, we do not do this except on account of God, whose person they bear, or whose ministers they are.

QUESTION LXVII. *The doers of the law shall be justified.* It is asked whether the fulfilling of the law justifies, which seems so from these words, when a lawyer asked of the Lord: *What shall I do to possess eternal life?* The Lord answered: *What is written? How do you read? And he: Thou shalt love the Lord thy God with thy whole heart, and with thy whole soul, and with thy whole mind, and thy neighbor as thyself; and the Lord said to him: Do this, and thou shalt live [n: (Luc. X)]*. Behold, the commandment of the law, when observed, confers eternal life. Whence it is read that the law fulfilled justifies [n: (Rom. II)]. The same in another place, when the young man [0450B] says to him: *What shall I do to possess eternal life?* And the Lord to him: *Honor father and mother; thou shalt not kill, thou shalt not steal, thou shalt not commit adultery, thou shalt not bear false witness, thou shalt not covet thy neighbor's wife,* nor his property. And the *young man says: All these things* from *my* youth *have I kept.* And the Lord *looked upon him, and loved him [n: (Matth. XIX; Exod. XX; Marc. X; Luc. XVIII)]*: which he would not have done, unless he were just from the observance of the commandments of God. But that which is added: *If thou wilt be perfect [n: (Matth. XIX)]*, etc., pertains to the perfection of justice. Likewise Paul: *The commandment of the law, which was unto life, was found by me to be unto death [n: (Rom. VII)]*. Likewise Bede: The law, kept in its own time, conferred not only temporal goods, but also eternal; whence it is manifest that the law fulfilled [0450C] justifies. But the Apostle asserts that the law brought no one to perfection, saying: *by the works of the law shall no flesh be justified, and if justice is from the law, then Christ died in vain [n: (Galat. II)]*: and many other things in this manner. And therefore some say that those precepts whose fulfillment confers justice are the precepts of the Gospel, even though they are written in the law. For he who does them is at once made an evangelical man; nor is he a man of the law. These also say that those precepts, according as they are understood to be fulfilled in the law, do not justify immediately, but only make one fit to receive the faith of Christ, through which alone salvation is had: for they say that he who shows outwardly that he loves God and neighbor, even if inwardly he does not love in his heart, keeps the law so far as the law goes [0450D]; it restrains the hand indeed, and not the mind. To which solution it is objected thus: If by the observance of those precepts, made only outwardly, they become fit and worthy to receive the faith of Christ, faith is given not from grace, but from merits. Likewise, if they are fit, how do they know that they will receive it? Whence they do not seem suitably to resolve the foregoing question. For what is a greater perfection than to love God with the whole heart, and one's neighbor as oneself? And therefore we say that the fulfilling of the aforesaid precepts justifies immediately, yet not the law, which does not suffice but is weak without grace for its own fulfilling; nor does it give grace, nor does it openly show the faith of Christ, without which the precepts of the law [0451A] are not fulfilled: it shows indeed the homeland, but does not point out the way by which one must go, nor whither. Whence the Apostle: By the works of the law shall no flesh be justified.

But someone will say: This authority manifestly contradicts your solution. Are they not contrary — that there is justice by the fulfilling of the legal precepts, as you asserted above, and that no one is justified by works? Solution. The Apostle calls "works of the law" those which are done from fear alone, which the law instills, from which there is no justice. It is to be noted that "law" is said in several ways: for sometimes "law" is called the book of Moses, sometimes the ceremonies and legal observances, sometimes the ten precepts written on the two tablets, sometimes the book of psalms, sometimes also "law" is said [0451B] of any observance whatever, as when it is said: This is the law of this or that matter. Natural reason too is called law. In the New Testament also "law" is taken in several ways; whence the law of faith, the law of the spirit, the law of grace, the law of the flesh, the law of the members, the law of sin, the law of death.



## I. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE ROMANS. (cont.)

QUESTION LXVIII. It is asked concerning circumcision — to whom it was commanded, and why, and why in the genital parts and not in others, and why to males and not to females, and what efficacy it had, and why baptism succeeded it. Now circumcision is the cutting off of that little skin which is over the male parts; whence that skin too is called the foreskin (*praeputium*); from which the gentiles are called foreskinned (*praeputiati*), because they are without pruning, that is, cutting. It was first commanded to Abraham; whence [0451C] he and his whole household was circumcised. Nor was this command made to all in common or generally, but only to the Jews. Whence it is said: *The soul that shall not have been circumcised shall perish from his people [n: (Gen. XVII)]*. The people of God is the people of the Jews. This was commanded with reason, for God willed his people to be set apart from others, and their hearts to be circumcised both from vices and from concupiscences, and that he might show this in outward things, he commanded them to be circumcised in the flesh: and in the genital parts rather than in others. For since there are two things by which fellowship is chiefly contracted among any people, namely marriage and food, in both God willed his people to be set apart; whence he forbade to the gentiles a common food, namely [0451D] swine's flesh. He also commanded them to be circumcised in the genitals, lest they should pollute those sanctified parts by some intercourse with unclean women, and that they might keep the flesh, as it were the gate of our birth, uncorrupted from vices. And the gentile women, seeing all the males cut, would avoid marriages with them. It was commanded to males alone for this reason, that no one who was without sin was to be born of male seed; but from a woman he was.

QUESTION LXIX. It is likewise asked: of what efficacy was it? And some say that it was of the same [efficacy] as baptism is now, except that it did not send to the kingdom. But if this is so, then in circumcision there was remission of sins; therefore justice, and thus from the law, which the Apostle denies. Solution. We say that in circumcision [0452A] there was not remission of all sins, but only of original sin; but in baptism there is made remission of all, and moreover a conferring of virtues. But whether baptism had this full efficacy before the Passion is wont to be asked. And some indeed say that it did not. Yet it can be said that after Christ's death, upon those who were purified before the cross, there was conferred by the sacrament of baptism itself as much as upon those also who were baptized afterward.

QUESTION LXX. Next it is asked why baptism succeeded circumcision. Solution. For three reasons: for its gentleness, for its comeliness, and for the imperfection of circumcision itself. For it was hard to be circumcised, and more seemly to be washed with waters than to be cut with a knife: baptism is also more general, being suited not only to hands, [0452B] but also to women, and to Jews as well as to gentiles.

QUESTION LXXI. It is asked how the Apostle here says that *to be circumcised is profitable,* when in what follows he says: *If you be circumcised, Christ will profit you nothing [n: (Galat. V)]*. Solution. This is said according to the state of the different times. For in the time of the law it was profitable to observe it; in the time of grace it does not profit according to the letter, but rather harms.

QUESTION LXXII. It is asked of what state it is said: *What then is more,* etc. Solution. Of the former, in which the Jews were before the incarnation of the Lord.

QUESTION LXXIII. Likewise it is asked what it pertains to the matter that he says [this], since those to whom he was writing were not in that state, but in the state of faith. Response. That we, [0452C] who have believed from among the gentiles, might not be proud against the Jews.

QUESTION LXXIV. It is asked: is the assumed man a liar? It seems that he is, because he is a man: and every man is a liar. For one is called a liar, not because he lies or sins, but because he is changeable, *and never remains in the same state [n: (Job XIV)]*, and through sins can dissolve away. All of which some presume to assert even concerning the assumed man, which they attempt to affirm thus. If the assumed man could have not been assumed by the Word, he could have sinned; but he could have not been assumed, therefore he could have sinned: which seems [to follow], if the antecedent be conceded; but all concede that he could have not been assumed. Solution. It is not to be conceded [0452D] that that man could have sinned: for when one says "that man," by "that" the personal property is noted, in which it is impossible for him to sin; but in the consequent, when it is said, if that man could have not been assumed, he could have sinned, that personal property is not understood, but, without regard to it, the human nature is given to be understood. Likewise by another way they wish to prove the same. Christ, when he came into Jerusalem, could have gone into Galilee, and if he had then gone into Galilee, he would not now suffer; and if he did not suffer, he would not fulfill the command of the Father, by whose command he suffered: but he could then have not suffered in Jerusalem, and been disobedient, and so have sinned. Solution. He could indeed thus have not gone to Jerusalem. He could therefore have not fulfilled the precept of the Father: this does not follow — for if he did not then suffer, which [0453A] was possible; whence he says: *I have power to lay down my life, and to take it up again [n: (Joan. X)]*: if this, I say, were the case, that he did not then suffer, it would not be the command of the Father that he should then suffer there; but that these two should be at once is impossible: that the Father should have commanded him to suffer then, and that he himself should not then suffer: this is the common solution.

QUESTION LXXV. It is asked concerning this hypothetical: *If our iniquity commends the justice of God,* etc. Whether it is true, and whether the consequent necessarily follows from the antecedent, and how it is to be understood. Solution. Our iniquity commends the justice of God: this is understood in two ways: either that, by its own quality, it makes the justice of God more commendable, which is false; and according to this understanding the consequence would be true: [0453B] if this were so, God would be unjust, who punishes sins. It can also be understood that from the comparison of our injustice the justice of God appears more commendable, and this is true; but according to this understanding the consequence is not true, nor does the consequent follow from the antecedent: for the false never follows from the true.

QUESTION LXXVI. It is asked how this is to be understood: *why am I still judged as a sinner?* — whose voice is it, of one converted or of one not converted? If a converted man speaks, he is not judged as a sinner; if one not yet converted speaks, God is not yet glorified in his lie. Solution. It is the voice of one converted, and again fallen.

QUESTION LXXVII. It is asked whether God wills evil [0453C] to be done, since he himself uses our evils for good and for his glory, just as we abuse his good things unto our own reproach. Solution. He does not will evil to be done. For it is not to be conceded that he wills evil to be done, or wills it not to be done: for if he willed evil to be done, he would be the author of evil, since, when he wills something to be done, it comes to be by no other than himself as author. Likewise, if he willed it not to be done, and yet it were done, something would resist his will. There are, however, those who say that God wills evil to be done, but according to these "will" is said in several ways, namely for permission, for good pleasure, for command, and for prohibition. He wills evils to be done, that is, he permits them, not that he is their author.

QUESTION LXXVIII. Whether it is good that evil should be? [0453D] Solution. "Good" is said in several ways: the expedient good, the good by its own quality, etc. Likewise "evil" is said in many ways, namely that which harms, that which is corrupted: a depraved act and a depraved will, which is sin — the privation of every good; any disorder whatever is called sin. When therefore it is said, it is good that evil should be, it must be diligently seen what is understood by the name of good or of evil.

QUESTION LXXIX. Since he says: *We have charged that all are under sin,* etc. It does not seem to be a necessary argument for demonstrating that the Jews do not excel the gentiles; for both were under sin, even mortal sin: for it could have happened that these were under a lesser, and those under a greater sin: whence they seem more worthy and fit to receive grace than the rest. [0454A] Solution. If both were under sin, namely mortal, which the Apostle notes, who says "under sin" as though pressed down and enslaved: it is clear that neither did they merit grace, but punishment. Wherefore if grace is conferred on them, namely faith and the other gifts, this is not from their merits, but from the sole goodness of God: therefore, so far as lies in themselves, neither do they excel. We do not deny, however, that those who were under a greater sin were less fit to receive grace: with whom it was dealt more mercifully than with the others, who were under a lesser sin. *But where the offense abounded, grace did more abound [n: (Rom. V)]*: even though the grace of these was not greater than that of those in itself, yet grace superabounded, according to the number of the men. It is not [0454B] to be discussed in this place whether the Jews were subject to a greater sin than the gentiles.

QUESTION LXXX. It does not seem to be true which is said: *There is none that understands, or that seeks after God.* For many in that people had understanding of God, namely that he is one, that he is Creator, and almighty: for if one is called "not understanding" because he did not understand perfectly, then the men of grace also, because they do not yet understand perfectly except through faith, can be called "not understanding." Solution. One can be called "not understanding" who, though he know God in majesty, does not yet know him in humility and mercy. Or one is said "not understanding God perfectly" who, though he have some knowledge, does not have it through the experience of charity [0454C]; or "not understanding God perfectly," namely because he alone is the Creator of all, and moreover is the author of all justice, which the Jews do not understand: establishing their own justice, they are not subject to the justice of God; nay, in a certain manner they make themselves God, when they say that they are justified by their own power without the grace of God, asserting themselves to be authors of justice, which God [alone] can do. Whence the Apostle says that *in the Gospel the justice of God is revealed, not of man (Rom III)*. And elsewhere: *But now without the law the justice of God is made manifest [n: (ibid.)]*. And if at times the justice of man is read of, as David often says *my justice:* it must be so understood that it is the man's who receives, and the same is God's who gives.



## I. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE ROMANS. (cont.)

QUESTION LXXXI. It is asked concerning that which is said: [0454D] *Whatever the law says, it says to those who are under the law,* etc. Even if it speaks to the Jews, do all things therefore pertain to the Jews, because they were said to the Jews? Solution. It is to be understood thus: It so speaks to them that the things which it says pertain to them. Which again seems to be false: for many things are said about the gentiles in the law. Whence one must understand "whatever it says" without determination and distinction, from which it is certain whether the discourse is made to the gentiles: so that it speaks about those gentiles, I say, who are of the law, in such a way that what is said pertains to them. To which opinion it is objected thus: Were not David and many other just men, and yet were under the law? and if this is so, how does that which is said pertain to them, "there is none that understands"? Solution. To be "under the law" notes two things, namely to seek justice [0455A] from the law, and to lean wholly upon it, as those who out of fear observe it outwardly; and according to this David and the other just men were not under the law. For they did not seek to be justified from the law itself, but from the faith of Christ yet to come; and thus they were men of the Gospel, not of the law. Those also are said to be "under the law" to whom the law was given, and who do the works of the law: and according to this David and those like him were under the law, because to them the law was given, and they kept it for the sake of those to whom the law was necessary, lest by their example they should despise the law. It is to be noted that there are three kinds of men, namely men of the natural law, of the written law, and of grace. Men of the natural law are said to be those who have the natural law alone, and nothing is superadded. Men of the written law are those to whom the written law was given, [0455B] and they have nothing superadded. Men of grace are those to whom grace itself was given. Or otherwise: Men of the natural law are those who seek to be justified by their own powers; men of the written law are those who lean upon the law, supposing that the law justifies. Men of grace are those who seek justice and salvation from nowhere else but grace alone.

QUESTION LXXXII. Since it is said: For by the works of the law shall no flesh be justified, it is asked concerning Moses and David and the other just men who lived in the time of the law, whether they are just from the works of the law: which seems so, because they did those [works] out of charity. Solution. By faith alone in [the one] to come, so that those ancients were just not from the works of the law. Note that "works of the law," according to some, are said [to be] those which were instituted with the law and terminated with the law, [0455C] namely the ceremonial, which were not instituted for justification, but for the signifying of things to come; but according to others "works of the law" are said [to be] those which are done from fear alone, and not from love: concerning which it is clear that they do not justify: whence the law is said to restrain the hand, and not the mind.

QUESTION LXXXIII. Why do the works of the law done with charity not justify as the works of the Gospel do — do not the moral precepts, when fulfilled, justify? From these it seems that the works of the law justify. Solution. Not whatever we are bound by a precept of the law to do is called a work of the law; but that which was instituted with the law and terminated with the law. Or, what is better: works of the law are those for whose fulfilling the law suffices, which are done [0455D] from fear alone of temporal punishment, which justify no one.

QUESTION LXXXIV. It is asked what is the difference between the works of the law and [those] of the Gospel; for if the works of the Gospel are done from the fear of temporal punishment alone, they confer justice on no one, just as neither do the works of the law. Solution. To the works of the law belong only outward things: whence the law forbids the hand. To the works of the Gospel belong also inward things, such as the inner affections and motions, in which justice consists.

QUESTION LXXXV. It is asked concerning that which is said: *Through the law the knowledge of sin,* etc. — of what sin has knowledge been made through the law? For "sin" is said in several ways, sometimes [as] guilt, sometimes [as] the punishment of sin. Whence God is said to bear our sins, [0456A] that is, the punishment for sin: sometimes [as] a victim for sin, whence *Christ was made sin for us [n: (II Cor. V)]*, that is, a victim for sin. Sometimes also it is said [to be] satisfaction for sin, or a penance enjoined for sin: any disorder whatever of anything whatever is also called sin. Whence the lyre-player is said to have sinned, if he always strays upon the same string. Likewise "sin" is said [to be] the work of sin, and the guilt of sin: which, according to some, is other than the fault, according to others the same as the fault. It is asked, therefore, of what sin knowledge has been made through the law? Solution. Some say that knowledge of that sin which is in the will has been made through the law, because before the law the sin which is in the deed was alone believed to be sin, and concupiscence was not known to be [0456B] sin.
