QUESTION LXXXVI. It is again asked why the law does not justify, since it thus makes sin manifest. For what else does the Gospel do than make sin manifest, teaching what is to be followed and what avoided — which the law also does? Likewise, some wish to prove that the law leads to perfection by the words of the Lord, who concerning a precept of the law answered the young man: *Do this, and thou shalt live [n: (Luc. X)]*. Likewise: *Thou shalt love the Lord thy God,* etc. [n: (Matth. XIX.)] This is a precept of the law, but this, when observed, justifies; therefore the law leads to perfection. Likewise Bede says: The law, observed in its own time, conferred not only temporal things, but also eternal. Solution. That a response may be made to all questions of this kind, we say that the law is called a commandment without grace, [0456C] which never confers salvation; but the Gospel is called a commandment with grace, which justifies and leads through to eternal life.

QUESTION LXXXVII. *But now without the law there is justice,* etc. It is asked how both are true, that the justice of God is made manifest without the law, and is testified by the law and the prophets? Solution. The Apostle says that the justice of God is without the law, not that it is made manifest without the law.

QUESTION LXXXVIII. Likewise, when it is said: *The justice of God through faith in Jesus Christ,* it is asked concerning this virtue of faith, whether it can be had equally by the good and by the evil? Solution. In the answer to this question the moderns seem to disagree. For some say that this virtue of faith is had only by the good, and in no [0456D] way by the evil: for it is that [faith] which works through love. And therefore, they say, where there is no love, neither is there faith. But to others it seems that it is had equally by the good and by the evil. For what else is it to have faith, than to believe those things which are to be believed? But all that this good man believes, this evil man also believes; how then does he not have the same faith, especially since Augustine says that faith can be had without charity, and the Apostle: *If,* he says, *I have all faith, so as to remove mountains, but have not charity, I am nothing [n: (I Cor. XXXI)]*: how would he say this, if faith could not be had without charity?

QUESTION LXXXIX. *Justified freely through the grace of Christ.* How does he say *freely through grace?* Would not either "freely" or "through grace" suffice? It seems that one of them [0457A] is superfluous. Solution. He says "freely," that is, without any merit of ours; "through grace," that is, through his gratuitous gifts. For often he confers many things on us through grace indeed, yet not without any merit of ours: which nevertheless does not come about without his grace.

QUESTION XC. *Through the redemption which is in Christ Jesus,* etc. It is asked why God redeemed man through his own death, whom he could have freed by a word alone? Solution. Although another mode was possible to God, yet none was more suited to our misery: because in the same [death] he both conferred on us a remedy, and furnished an example of humility and of love. The remedy is considered in this, that the devil laid his hand upon him who was free from sin: in whom he found nothing that was his own. Therefore he deservedly [0457B] lost those whom he seemed to hold by a certain right, [namely] those believing in him who, through his own death, was made the cause of salvation to all who obey him: for he suffered not for himself, but for us, granting us his merits, so that for them there might come to be for us what would come to be for himself, if he were in need. To humility he provoked us in this, that, descending from the secret bosom of the Father's majesty, he so emptied himself as to take the form of a servant [n: (Philipp. III)]. To charity he invited us in this: that, though he is the Lord of glory, such and so great, he died in such a manner for the impious and for sinners. Note that if he had redeemed man by a word alone, no injury would have been done to the devil. Or if God had reformed the human race through an angel, salvation would not on that account be ascribed to the angel. For God works many things through the angels [0457C] which nevertheless are to be attributed not to the angels, but to God: and therefore he redeemed us through himself, not through another, because the merits of no other could be so great as to suffice for the redemption of the whole world.

QUESTION XCI. It is asked whether God could have made a more fitting mode of redemption? If it be said that he could not, it seems that the power of God has a limit, and is not immeasurable: if it be said that he could, how is this [mode] the most fitting? Solution. Although in this he has a limit, it is nevertheless not to be conceded without qualification that he has a limit. Or, although this mode is the most fitting to our misery, it is nevertheless not necessary that it be the most fitting absolutely.

[0457D]QUESTION XCII. It is asked to whom our price was given, whether to the devil or to God? Solution. It was given to God, not to the devil: because no injury was done to the devil, since he was nothing but as it were a jailer, nor would he even wish to receive it, that he might lose the man: but it was not to be given to one unwilling, lest injury should be done to him.

QUESTION XCIII. It is asked from whom man was redeemed? Solution. From the devil, from sin, from torment. Moreover he is reconciled to God: and this is the twofold efficacy of the blood of Christ.

QUESTION XCIV. It is asked in what the power of the devil has been diminished through the death of Christ. For as before he had the power of tempting the good and the evil, so also now. Solution. It has been diminished not according to essence, but according to [0458A] efficacy: because he cannot prevail as much as before, especially because powers of resisting have been given to man, and the stronger man is for resisting, the weaker is the enemy for assailing.



## I. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE ROMANS. (cont.)

QUESTION XCV. It is asked, when it is said *unto the showing of the just one,* etc., concerning the ancient just, who were held in hell, whether their sins had been remitted to them through faith and penance: and if they had been remitted, why were they held in hell: for without the remission of sins they were not just. Solution. All sins had been remitted to them through faith and love, but they obtained the full effect of remission. Twofold is the effect of remission, namely, to be without punishment, and to enjoy glory; only the one of these they had before the death of Christ: [0458B] because they did not feel actual punishment, the other they had not yet received, because they did not see God. Again it is objected: Were they not just, and so worthy of glory? Why then was not that given to them of which they were worthy? Solution. Their justice was not so great as to suffice for obtaining life without the death of Christ, nor even were their sins remitted simply, except under a certain expectation and pledge of one to come, who should make satisfaction for them. Whence the Apostle says that Christ died not only for the remission of present sins, but also of preceding ones; *because he is the lamb who was slain from the origin of the world [n: (Hebr. IX)]*.

QUESTION XCVI. Whether God could damn them with eternal punishment, since they were just, and he could not justly punish the just — for what is unjust, God [0458C] cannot do: and if he could not punish them, in what did he sustain them? Solution. It was necessary that satisfaction be made for original sin, and since they themselves could not make satisfaction unless another made satisfaction for them, God could justly punish them. Yet it is not necessary to concede that he would punish the just. For this would be after the death of Christ: for they are not to be called just simply, but only in comparison with the death of Christ: for they did not have so great an actual justice from which they could justly demand eternal life, just as neither do we without the death of Christ: yet Scripture calls them just, because they had as much as God demanded, since what was lacking to them Christ was to supply.

[0458D]QUESTION XCVII. When it is said: *By what law of deeds?* etc., it is asked why the Mosaic law, the written law, is called the law of deeds, and not the Gospel: but the law of the Gospel [is called] the law of grace and the law of justice: for as that one has works, so does this. Solution. The Mosaic law is called [the law] of deeds, because it only commands the things which are to be done, and does not confer the grace through which the things commanded may be fulfilled: and therefore the killing letter is called a commandment without grace. But the Gospel indeed commands the things which are to be done: yet moreover it confers the grace through which the things commanded may be fulfilled. Or for this reason: the written law is called the law of deeds, because the men of the law placed their whole justice in the works of the law: but the law of faith or of grace is so called, because the men of grace place the whole sum and efficacy [0459A] of their salvation in grace alone: knowing that, as no one is saved from the justice of works, so no one is justified from the works of justice. For justice is not from good works, but good works from justice.

QUESTION XCVIII. *Is he not also of the gentiles,* etc. The Apostle proves that God is [God] of the gentiles, because he is their creator. But thus it seems that he is the God of stones. Solution. He is indeed the creator of stones, but he is the creator of the gentiles in another way, whom he created to his own image and likeness: whence he is deservedly called [God] of the gentiles, as one truly to be worshiped by them.

QUESTION XCIX. Whether the works which precede faith are entirely useless, or whether they profit for anything; for it seems that they profit nothing, because the whole life of unbelievers is sin. Solution. Good works which are done [0459B] before faith, even if they do not profit for meriting life, are nevertheless of avail for receiving it, as it seems to some, as appears in the case of Cornelius.

QUESTION C. *Do we then destroy the law through faith? God forbid: but we establish the law.* It is asked how this is true, when elsewhere he says: *If the things which I have destroyed I build again, I make myself a transgressor: and through the law I am dead to the law [n: (Galat. II)]*. There he says that he has destroyed the law: here he says that he does not destroy it, but establishes it. Solution. To destroy the law is taken in two ways. Whence here the Apostle says that he does not destroy the law, that is, he does not show the law to be useless in its own time, and to be of no avail, nor that it is not to be fulfilled spiritually. Elsewhere he said that he destroyed it, that is, he preached that after the fulfillment of the truth it ought to cease as regards [0459C] the carnal things.

QUESTION CI. *Abraham believed God, and it was reputed to him unto justice [n: (Rom. IV)]*. It is asked why he says *it was reputed,* as though it were not the true justice which he had through faith, but something which was reputed unto justice: for if you owed me a horse, I would not fittingly say: Give me a horse, and I will repute it for a horse; but I could suitably say: Give me an ass, and I will repute it for a horse. Solution. If man had not sinned, he would have justice in every respect, which consists in the fulfilling in every respect of the precepts of God, so that he would desire nothing at all against reason, and would love God with his whole heart; but after sin, and on account of sin, man could not have this perfect justice [0459D] to which eternal beatitude is deservedly owed: but God through his grace gives man faith, which likewise, through the same grace, he reputes for that perfection: as if he had the perfection of justice.

QUESTION CII. *But to him who works, the reward is not imputed,* etc. It is asked of what works he here treats: whether of the inward, or of the outward. Outward [works] are such as to clothe the poor, and the rest of this kind, which many do not perform, although they have the time for performing them, such as contemplative men, whence they seem unworthy of salvation, if the discourse here be about works of this kind. Inward works are such as to believe, to love, to pray, which are common to all the faithful, because without them there is no salvation, concerning which, if it is here treated, how is faith without works reputed unto justice for him [0460A] who does not have the time for working? Solution. He treats of outward works, which are required of those who have the means of doing them, and for those who do not have the means of doing them, the will is reputed for the deed.

QUESTION CIII. *According to the purpose of the grace of God,* etc. Here it is wont to be asked concerning grace and merit. For it seems either that the whole is from grace, so that merit confers nothing; or, if anything is from merit, that not the whole is from grace. But that the whole is from grace, the Scriptures testify. Whence the Apostle: *What hast thou that thou hast not received? [n: (I Cor. IV.)] By the grace of God I am what I am [n: (I Cor. XV)]*. And that: *Grace for grace [n: (Joan. I)]*. What then is to be said? To say that merit is nothing is the error of the Manicheans, just as to assert that the whole is from free will is the error of the Pelagians. Solution. [0460B] When it is said that the whole is from grace, merit is not excluded, since merit is from grace. And therefore it must be seen what the grace of God works in us without us, and what it works in us not without us. And so prevenient grace, which is also called operating, heals the free will, freeing it from the yoke of sin: and this it does in us without us: then the healed will is not idle, nor does it receive the grace of God in vain, which it works not by itself, but with grace; nay, the grace of God cooperates with the free will: whence it is called cooperating; and the same work or merit is said to be from grace and from will: for it does not work separately, but together. Whence, although the whole is from grace, it does not follow that nothing is from merit, or from free will; as if someone should find a little child lying in the mud [0460C] and unable to rise, and should lift him up, then hold his hand so that he might walk, the walking itself would be from both, just as the lifting of him is from the finder alone and not from the child: so, from prevenient grace alone it is that we will the good, just as from subsequent grace — I do not say alone, but also from free will, healed and deliberating through grace — it is that we work the good.

QUESTION CIV. *Blessed are they whose iniquities are forgiven.* It is asked concerning original sin what it is, of which the doctors discourse somewhat obscurely. For some say that original sin is the guilt of eternal punishment, that is, the debt and liability by which we are bound over to punishment: but according to this original sin is not [0460D] a fault, but a punishment. But that it is a fault, the authorities testify, which one must concede. But others say that original sin is the tinder of sin: concupiscence, or concupiscibility: the law of the members, the law of the flesh, the languor of nature, the tyrant who dwells in our members: the innate vice which makes the child apt to lust, the adult lusting: by these and other names original sin is called.

QUESTION CV. Why it is called "original" is wont to be asked. Solution. Because it is drawn from the vicious condition of our origin.

QUESTION CVI. Likewise it is asked why it is imputed to posterity? Response. Because the intercourse of the parents does not [0461A] come about without lust, nor the conception of the children without sin.

QUESTION CVII. How is it destroyed in baptism? Solution. Wholly, as to the guilt, it is mitigated, and it is weakened as to the act or the affection. But others say that original sin is the privation of a certain original justice which man would have, if he had not sinned: and therefore, because he is deprived of justice, he is deprived also of glory, nor will those who are punished for original sin alone suffer any other punishment, except that they will forever lack the vision of God. Likewise, since the soul is not from transmission (*ex traduce*), but the flesh alone [is], it is asked how this sin is drawn through the flesh: for the flesh itself cannot without the soul have fault, which is not, nor can be, except in a rational creature: for that which is not [0461B] capable of justice, [is] neither [capable] of sin.

QUESTION CVIII. How then, in the propagation of offspring from the parents, does that pass through the flesh which cannot be in the flesh alone without the soul? Solution. Sin is said to pass, because its cause passes, which is a certain pollution and uncleanness which the soul finds in the flesh when it is infused into it, and by which it is polluted: whence, although the soul alone lusts, yet the soul is not said to lust, because the soul lusts from the flesh.

QUESTION CIX. Whether the soul is ever such as it was created by God: for if it was created clean by God, and from the moment it was joined and coupled to the flesh it was unclean, it follows that it is never such as it was created by God. Solution. It can be conceded that it never [0461C] was altogether such as it was created by God, as if I had given you a clean apple, and you received it with unclean hands: it would be true that you never have such an apple as I gave you.

QUESTION CX. It is wont to be asked why the guilt of original sin is imputed to the soul, created clean by God. The doctors say that this question is insoluble; yet it can be said that it is not unjust that glory be not given to the soul not having justice: for this is to be subject to the guilt of original sin, which is to be deprived of original justice. What wonder, then, if such a fault be punished with such a punishment?



## I. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE ROMANS. (cont.)

QUESTION CXI. *And he received the sign of circumcision, and the seal of justice,* etc. Concerning circumcision [0461D] it was said above. But here it can be asked whether Abraham obtained any benefit from circumcision. Solution. The doctors say that through it he was only shown to be just, not made [just]. Did it then have greater efficacy in his posterity than in Abraham himself? Which seems so, since to them it was given as a remedy at least for original sin; but to Abraham himself it was given only for the showing of justice.

QUESTION CXII. Likewise it is asked whether from circumcision remission of original sin was given to those children who were held by no other [sin], how they were not justified through it, and thus from the law. The solution of this question is clear from what has been said.

QUESTION CXIII. It is asked concerning the children who died before [0462A] the eighth day, on which circumcision was performed, whether they were damned or saved. Solution. The same judgment is concerning those uncircumcised as concerning the unbaptized, namely that they are damned by the guilt of original sin alone. But if it be asked concerning those children who died as soon as they were born, before circumcision, perhaps they were succored by the faith of their parents.

QUESTION CXIV. *For not through the law was the promise,* etc. It is asked how the Apostle says: the promise was not *made* to *Christ* through the law, *but through the justice of faith;* was not the promise made in the law and through the law, namely in David, to Christ himself? Likewise: What is it for the promise to be made to Christ through the justice of faith? Solution. The Apostle does not say thus, that the promise was not [0462B] made through the law, but through the justice of faith; but thus, that the promise was not made — that he should be the heir of the world — through the law, but through the justice of faith.

QUESTION CXV. Likewise it is asked how the promise was made to Abraham through justice; was it by the merit of his own faith? but if by the merit of Abraham's own faith the promise was made, then by the same merit also [was] the fulfillment of the promise itself, how then [is it] by grace alone? Solution. The letter must not be construed thus: The promise was made through the justice of faith; but thus: The promise was made to Abraham, that he should be the heir of the world, through the justice of faith: through which he himself was made the father of believers, and believers are made the sons of Abraham, that is, just men and heirs. The expositor here says otherwise than we.

QUESTION CXVI. Because it is said: *The law works the wrath of God.* [0462C] It is asked how this is to be understood. Is not the law good, and wrath evil? How does that which is good work that which is evil? Is not that whose effect is evil itself also evil? How then does such a cause have such an effect? Solution. The law does not work wrath immediately, and by its own quality, and as an efficient cause, but as it were by occasion; because if the law had not been given, the wrath would not be so great, which was increased through the transgression of the law. For many things are said to effect certain things, not because they effect them, but because without them they would not come to be; whence Christ too is said to be *set,* not only *unto resurrection* by cause, but also *unto ruin [n: (Luc. II)]* by occasion. And the Apostle is said to be, not only *the odor of life,* but [0462D] also *the odor of death [n: (II Cor. II)]*. According to the same reasoning one must be vigilant concerning like cases.

QUESTION CXVII. How does the Apostle prove that the law works wrath, saying: *For where there is no law, neither [is there] transgression?* For this too does not seem to be true. Where there is no law, cannot there be transgression of the natural law, or of the Gospel? How then [is it] true, where there is no law, neither [is there] transgression? But if it be so expounded, where there is no law, neither [is there] transgression of the law, by the same argument it is proved that where there is no Gospel, neither is there transgression of the Gospel. Solution. By the name of "law" the Apostle often designates a commandment without grace; but by the name of "Gospel" is always understood a commandment with grace: whence it stands true, where there is no law, that is, a commandment without grace, neither [is there] transgression. [0463A] Likewise, where there is a commandment with grace, there is not transgression, but fulfillment of the commandment.

QUESTION CXVIII. Likewise, when it is said: *Who against hope believed in hope.* It is asked concerning the faith of Abraham, which the Apostle here praises: which is reputed to him unto justice: what or of what thing it was? For if the words of the Apostle and of Genesis are diligently considered, it seems that that faith, by which he believed that offspring was promised to him by God, was reputed to him unto justice; but if this is so, then some faith besides the faith of Christ is a justifying faith. But if it be said that he then had the faith of Christ, it is asked whether two faiths justify, or why justice is rather said to be from that one than from this? Solution. The Apostle commends the faith of Abraham, by which he believed all things which were to be believed: by which faith, among other things, [0463B] he believed God to be truthful in the promise of offspring.

QUESTION CXIX. How is the generation of Isaac said to be against nature? What is nature? Is it not a certain power of creatures, implanted by the Creator, through which like things are procreated and propagated from like? Did not nature there work? Was there not a certain tempering in the works of the parents in the act and from the mingling of flesh? how [then is it] against nature? Solution. When the Author of nature works in nature beyond the accustomed course of nature, that is, not according to the lower causes, but according to the higher, then something is said to come about against nature, but it would more properly be called above nature.

QUESTION CXX. *Most fully knowing that whatever [0463C] God has promised, he is able also to do.* The expositor says that by much beholding of the divine power he knew God to be almighty, and the Apostle [says he was] "most fully knowing," etc., which seems to be harmful to faith; because faith has no merit, to which human reason furnishes proof. Likewise, if he knew him to be God, what great thing [is it], if he believed him to be almighty; or if he did not know him to be God, how did he believe him? Solution. That beholding of the divine power which Abraham had was not from human reason, but from the constancy of faith: by which he was as certain as if he most fully knew, or saw.

QUESTION CXXI. But as to what is asked, what great thing it was that he believed God to be almighty, since not only the good, but also the evil believe that? [0463D] We say that to believe that was then a great thing, when almost the whole world was straying in the worship of demons, nor had the faith of one God yet been preached, or manifestly declared by the Scriptures, as [it is] now.

QUESTION CXXII. *And not only was it written,* etc. It is asked how the Apostle says that this was written on account of Abraham, that faith was reputed unto justice? does he obtain any benefit from the fact that he is praised in the Scriptures, or imitated by us? Solution. Perhaps the Apostle does not say this because it profits him, but it is to be understood thus: And not only, etc., that is, this was written not only that it might be shown whence Abraham himself was [0464A] just, but that it might be demonstrated to us also in what alone we can be justified, namely in the grace of faith, and nowhere else.

QUESTION CXXIII. *He was delivered up for our offenses,* etc. So the authority says. It is asked by whom he was delivered up? And it is certain that [he was delivered] by God the Father, by Judas and by the Jews, and by Pilate. But it is asked whether that which was done by God and by the Jews was good or evil? If it be said good, then the Jews did good. But if it be said evil, then God did evil. Likewise: Did not the Jews do only evil, and God only good? how then did God and the Jews do the same? Solution. The work or deed of the Jews is taken equivocally for the act and for the passion; the act of the Jews [was] only evil; the passion [0464B] of Christ, which followed from their act, was good. Therefore the doctors sometimes unite God and the Jews, even in the same deed or work, on account of the passion of Christ, which came to pass both from the action of the Jews and from [that] of God; sometimes they distinguish [them] on account of the diversity of the acts. But if it be asked whether the work of the Jews was good, distinguish thus: Their act [was] evil, the passion which they inflicted was good.

QUESTION CXXIV. Likewise it is asked why the Apostle distinguishes thus, saying: *That Christ was delivered up for our offenses, and rose again for our justification,* since both — both the passion and the resurrection — both free from sins and justify? Solution. Although each of them is a cause, yet [not each is] a figure.

QUESTION CXXV. *And not only so, but also [sic: sedet] we glory [0464C] in tribulations [n: (Rom. V)]*, etc. How does the Apostle glory in tribulations, which are bitter, and not to be sought for their own sake? Solution. Not on account of the tribulations themselves, but on account of their effect, is one to glory in them. But it is objected that by the same reasoning one ought to glory in sins, not for their own sake, but for the good, which they often [produce], as when they bring about humility, whence the Prophet: *Before I was humbled, I offended [n: (Psal. CXVIII)]*. Solution. Tribulations by their own quality cooperate with the inner grace unto the attaining of patience, but sins do not; rather God by his mercy makes some good come forth from our evils.

QUESTION CXXVI. When the Apostle says: *But hope does not confound.* It is asked concerning hope, what it is, and whether it is had without charity? Solution. Hope is the certain expectation [0464D] of future goods, which differs from faith in this, that faith is about things past, present, and future, both good and evil; but hope [is] only about things future and good. But it seems that it is not had without charity: for without good works and a good life, which is not without charity, to hope for future goods is not hope, but rather presumption, as Augustine says. Likewise Augustine seems to assert the same, that hope precedes charity. Solution. One is the hope of pardon, another is the hope of glory; the first is had even by the evil, the second perhaps only by the good.



## I. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE ROMANS. (cont.)

QUESTION CXXVII. *The charity of God is poured forth,* etc. It is asked whether the charity by which God loves us, and that by which we love God, is the same? Solution. There are those who [0465A] say that it is the same; whom Augustine meets, expounding this passage and saying that here the discussion is of the charity by which we love God, just as there, *God is charity [n: (I Joan. IV)]*, the discussion is of the charity by which God loves us: which he would not say, if it were the same. Likewise elsewhere he says: I call charity a movement of the mind toward loving God for his own sake, and the neighbor for God's sake. God is not a movement, therefore there is a charity which is not God. Likewise charity can be increased and diminished, but God cannot, therefore there is a charity which is not God. And so it must be firmly held that the name of charity is said equivocally of God, when it is said God is charity, and of a certain virtue, when it is said, a certain virtue is charity, or the charity of God is poured forth in our hearts. The Apostle [0465B] Paul almost everywhere designates by this name the virtue which is not God, but from God. John by the same name signifies God himself. But as to that which Augustine says, that fraternal charity, by which we love one another, is God: we concede it, saying that God, [who is] charity, makes us also love one another, but by the mediation of charity, just as he makes us believe by the mediation of faith; for from whom faith is, from the same is charity, as Augustine says. But it is objected: God is charity in us, and also charity which is a virtue; therefore there are two charities in us, or we love God by two charities. Solution. They are not on that account two. Just as the sun illumines us and the ray of the sun, therefore two illumine us — it does not follow, because this would be apart and separately: for the sun through the ray, and God through charity [0465C] the virtue, illumines or enlightens us.

QUESTION CXXVIII. It is likewise asked whether charity, once had, can be lost? And some say that it cannot, because it is written: *Charity never falls away [n: (I Cor. XIII)]*. Solution. This was said of perfect charity, or it was said that charity never falls away for this reason, that it is had in the present and in the future.

QUESTION CXXIX. Charity is that fountain of which it is said: Let the fountain of thy water be thine own, and let no stranger share it with thee. It is asked who the strangers are, if not the reprobate, and those who are to hear: *Go, ye cursed, into eternal fire [n: (Matth. XXV)]*, etc. But many of the number of such love for a time, and thus as strangers share [0465D] this fountain. Solution. Strangers are so called according to foreknowledge — of whom he is not now speaking — but according to present injustice, namely all who do not love now, whether they be elect or not; and these, as such, do not share the fountain of love.

QUESTION CXXX. How is this true, which is said: *Christ died for the ungodly.* Did not Christ die only for those to whom his death is profitable? but it is profitable only to the godly, therefore he died only for the godly. It seems, therefore, that he did not die for the ungodly. Solution. He died for the ungodly, that is, for the love of those who were formerly ungodly, and through faith were made godly; and so it is true that the death of Christ was made for the ungodly, and that it profits none but the godly.

[0466A]QUESTION CXXXI. *We are reconciled to God through the death of his Son.* It is asked how this word is to be understood. Was he perhaps angry at us before the death, as at sinners, according to that: *Thou hast hated all who work iniquity [n: (Psal. V)]*; and through the death of the Son was he appeased toward us, and did he then first begin to love? But if this is so, how did he choose us before the foundation of the world? Did he not choose while not loving; or how, being not appeased, did he deliver up the Son for us? Solution. The wrath of God is called not a passion of the mind, but a just vengeance; and when such wrath is ended — which was done through the death of Christ — God is said to be appeased toward us, and we to be reconciled to him; nor yet, because he reconciled, did he love; but because he loved, he reconciled. Of that justice by which Christ conquered it has been said above [0466B], and of the manner of the redemption. Nor is it needful to do again what is done: let only the words of Augustine be briefly set down, by which he plainly expresses it. He says therefore: This is the justice by which Christ conquered the devil. The devil, a lover of power and a deserter of justice, slew Christ, in whom he found nothing worthy of death. Whence it is just that those whom he held should be let go free, believing in him who was slain without any evil desert. He willed not, therefore, to use power against the lover of power, but against the deserter of justice he willed to use justice, that he might instruct us how we must fight against the same enemy; namely, not by power, but rather by justice, and so we shall be victors.

QUESTION CXXXII. *Therefore as through one,* etc. It is asked why he says rather that sin entered into the world through one man than [0466C] through one woman, since the beginning of sin was rather in the woman than in the man? Solution. He kept the order of custom, as Augustine says, because posterity is wont to be named not from the woman but from the man. Or for this reason, because man and woman are one flesh; and therefore whatever was done by him or by her is said to pertain to the first man. Or otherwise: if the man had not sinned, God would perhaps have provided him another companion, of whom he would have begotten offspring innocent and conformed to himself, or by his merit would have forgiven the sins of the wife; but because he sinned, through him not undeservedly is sin said to have entered into the world.

QUESTION CXXXIII. It is likewise asked why sin is said to have entered through man and not through the devil, [0466D] since it was first in him, and through him entered also into the first man, whence it is written: *By the envy of the devil death* entered into the world [n: (Sap. II)]. Solution. In one way through the devil, in another way through man did sin enter. For through the devil by imitation alone, and not by propagation. Through man it entered not by imitation alone, but also by propagation. It is to be known that the Pelagians said that original sin entered through Adam by imitation alone and not by propagation: which if it were true, he would have called not man, but the devil, the author of sin: whence also they said that in baptism original sin is not forgiven to little ones, because according to them nothing is contracted in the newborn: but the catholic faith does not hold this, but preaches this, and the Scriptures testify to this, that, just as [0467A] Christ, beyond the example of imitation, works illumination and justification inwardly and secretly, by which grace alone he inserts into his body the regenerated little ones who cannot imitate him; so Adam, beyond the example of imitation, by the taint of his concupiscence corrupted all who were to be born of him through concupiscence.

QUESTION CXXXIV. Julian asks thus: He does not sin who creates, he does not sin who generates, he does not sin who is generated: through what chinks, then, amid so many safeguards of innocence, do you feign that original sin has entered? To whom Augustine answers thus: The Apostle says: *Through one man sin entered [sic: inravit] into this world [n: (Rom. V)]*; what does he seek more openly? what does he seek [sic: *nculcatius*]? what does he seek more plainly? why does he seek a chink, [0467B] where he has a most open door?

QUESTION CXXXV. It is likewise asked whether original sin is from the will or from nature: if from the will, the will is evil; if from nature, nature is evil. To whom Augustine responds: Every evil work proceeded from an evil will as from a root, but no evil preceded the evil will itself, but it arose first in the angel, then in man, both of whom were the good work of God; and thus in the good, evil arose, which had no efficient cause, but a deficient cause.

QUESTION CXXXVI. It is likewise asked whether original sin is from the will. Solution. It is indeed from the will of the first parents, whence it too can be called voluntary.

[0467C]QUESTION CXXXVII. *In whom all have sinned,* etc. We were not yet; how then could we, who were not yet, sin? Solution. In him we all sinned, that is, in him was done that from which we all should sin. Or thus: From him we drew the cause of sin; or thus: We are all guilty of that sin which he committed.

QUESTION CXXXVIII. It is also asked how we all were one man in Adam? Solution. That is, from him, who was one man, we descend by propagation.

QUESTION CXXXIX. It is asked again how from that small mass, which was in Adam, so many and so great things could descend, or how so small a particle could be divided into so many parts, that the body of each may thence materially consist. This question is wont to be raised concerning those *seven loaves,* with which the Lord satisfied so many thousands of men without the addition of anything [n: (Matth. XV)]. Solution. This was done by the law of propagation. For that particle which was in the loins of Adam, from the time it was separated in the generation of his son, was increased and multiplied in itself into the perfect stature of a man: from which again a small particle was separated in the second generation, which again was increased and multiplied in itself: from which again, thus multiplied, another was separated, and so on thereafter by the law of propagation it grew into so great a multitude without any extrinsic addition, or change of foods into it. It is agreed [0468A] that God can make from one atom whatever weight he will.

QUESTION CXL. If therefore one atom were thus multiplied in itself into something great, in which there were infinite atoms, it is asked whether that one would be all these, or one of the number of the others, or whether these are that one. Solution. It is not necessary to concede that that one is all these, but that it is multiplied and increased into these; nor that all these are that one, but from that one, or that they were in that one — just as that grain grew into a tree, nor is that grain the tree, but the tree is from it, or materially in it. Expressions of this kind are to be noted carefully. It is to be noted that there are several kinds of changes: sometimes essence is changed into essence without change of accidents, as in the sacrament of the altar; [0468B] sometimes a change of properties takes place without change of essence, as when water was made wine; sometimes a change takes place according to quantity alone, into something greater, as in the seven loaves. But in what manner the rod of Moses was changed into a serpent — whether according to properties alone, both substantial and accidental, or according to essence — is not certain to me.

QUESTION CXLI. It is written: *God did not make death.* Likewise: *Death and life are from God [n: (Sap. I)]*, which seems to be contrary: for nothing temporal is from God which he himself has not made; how then did he make death, and not make it? Solution. God did not make death, that is, the cause of death, namely sin; but that penalty which is called death, according to some, God did make: and it is numbered among the good works, [0468C] because it is just; and everything just is from God.



## I. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE ROMANS. (cont.)

QUESTION CXLII. *But not as the offense, so also the gift.* It is asked how the Apostle says that the gift of Christ abounds unto more than the offense of Adam: for not more are saved through the grace of Christ than are damned by the offense of Adam. Solution. This superabundance of grace is not to be considered in the number of persons; but to those very ones to whom the gift of Christ is profitable, it confers more in good than the offense of Adam harms those whom it harms in evil; because from Adam not all damnation [comes], but that alone by which those are punished who lie subject to original sin alone, apart from that by which those are punished who have added their own offenses; but the grace of Christ not only frees from original sin, but also from the things superadded, advancing unto justification, and at length unto eternal life.

QUESTION CXLIII. Whether actual sins are from the sin of Adam? Which seems so to some, because from the tinder of sin we are prone to sinning. Solution. Such proneness is not an efficient cause, even if it be a cause without which it would not come to be, as it pleases some; but neither is it required, since it was neither in the devil, nor in the first man before the fall, and yet there was sin in them without it: for it was not the cause of sin, but sin was the cause of it.

QUESTION CXLIV. It is asked whether the other actual sins of Adam are imputed to his posterity? Solution. The Apostle says by *the offense of one,* he does not say by offenses, *many are made sinners:* in which he intimates that not many, but one alone is imputed.

[0469A]QUESTION CXLV. It is asked whether the sin of Eve too is imputed to us? Solution. Note the words of the Apostle saying by the offense of one, he does not say of two; or if it is imputed, for the aforesaid reasons, he says that through one man sin entered into the world, or by the offense of one.

QUESTION CXLVI. It is asked whether the actual sins of nearer parents are imputed to their posterity? Which seems so, because the Lord says: *I am the Lord thy God, a jealous [God]: visiting the iniquity of the fathers upon the children unto the third and fourth generation [n: (Exod. XX)]*, etc. Solution. The sins of parents, even of nearer ones, are not imputed to the children, unless they themselves make the sins of the fathers their own by imitation; nor then are they punished because [they are] parents, but because they themselves sinned. According to another Scripture: [0469B] *The son shall not bear the iniquity of the father [n: (Ezech. XVIII)]*, etc.; but the contradiction which seems [to lie] between these two authorities, the doctors sufficiently elucidate.

QUESTION CXLVII. But if it be asked for what reason the sin of the first parents, and not of the nearer ones, is imputed. Solution. Because that one despoiled us; the other sins of others, finding us as it were naked and despoiled, could not take anything away from us: for original sin, as has already been said, deprives only of a certain original justice.

QUESTION CXLVIII. Man, of himself and through himself, before sin was able to sin; but to advance of himself and through himself, without the help of grace, he was not able; therefore he was more prone to evil than to good; but he was not yet [0469C] other than such as God had made him: therefore God made him more prone to evil than to good. Solution. Before the fall man was not prone to sin; nor did God make man such that he was prone to sinning, but such proneness afterward came in from sin; nor even did he have the power of sinning from God, but from his own nothingness; not from the good which he had received, but from its limit; not because he had received so much, but because he had not received more, as has already been said above concerning the same.

QUESTION CXLIX. *As through the disobedience of one,* etc. It is asked whether man, first, from the good which he had before the fall, without the addition of others, could obey grace and fulfill the commandment given to him? If it be said that he could: therefore, from that which he then had he could [0469D] advance: which is denied by almost all. Likewise, if it be conceded that he could not obey without the help of grace, what then was his fault, if he did not do what he could not do without grace, and grace was not conferred; nor was it his fault that it was not conferred. Solution. He did not sin because he did not do what he could not do; but because he did not do it when he could.

QUESTION CL. It is likewise asked whether Christ, by obeying God the Father, merited anything? Some wish to prove that he merited nothing, because neither according to his humanity nor according to his divinity. God, they say, cannot receive anything from anyone; or through anything that he does acquire something else not previously due [0470A]: therefore he cannot merit anything. Likewise, according as he is man, he is good and loves; but he cannot not be good, or not love: therefore of necessity he is good or loves; how then can he merit? Solution. To merit has a twofold signification: for one is said to merit when through a good work he is made worthy of something of which he was not previously worthy — according to which signification it seems to us that neither according to his divinity nor according to his humanity did Christ merit anything, even in his very death. One is also said to merit when he does some good which is worthy of remuneration — according to which God too is said to merit, when he bestows benefits on us for which we are bound to praise him for eternity; and Christ, according to [0470B] his humanity, in his passion merited for us the entrance of eternal life. Beforehand indeed he merited many things for us; but the passion alone was not only precious, but the price of the world.

QUESTION CLI. *The law entered in, that [sin] might abound,* etc. It is asked whether the law is the cause of evil, which seems so, because it is the cause of the abounding of the offense. Solution. 'That' [ut] is sometimes causative, as: I [go] to the market, that I may buy a toga; sometimes operative, as: he placed man in paradise, that he might keep it, etc.; sometimes it is consecutive, as: he went outside, so that he died; sometimes also it denotes occasion, as: the law entered in, that sin might abound. For the Jews received a more abundant occasion of sin from the law, which was good, not the cause of evil; but their vice was the cause of this evil, [0470C] just as if I take occasion of envying from another's knowledge; which [knowledge] is not the cause of my grief, but my own vice.

QUESTION CLII. *Where the offense abounded, grace also superabounded.* Did not the offense superabound in the Jews, because [of] the transgression of the law; and yet grace did not also superabound, because they were blinded. How then is it true, where the offense abounded, grace also superabounded? Solution. He does not say: Wherever the offense abounded, grace also superabounded; but 'where' indefinitely, which is to be understood as regards those who believed, in whom, before they believed, the offense abounded; because besides the other sins the transgression of the law was in them: but grace forgave all; moreover it conferred faith and charity — not [0470D] as regards those who died in their sins, in whom the offense abounded, and not grace.

QUESTION CLIII. *What then shall we say: Shall we remain in sin, that grace may abound? [n: (Rom. VI.)]* Concerning these things it is asked what it is to remain in sin. Solution. Not to repent of sin, or, by a certain torpor of mind, persevering in sin, to await grace.

QUESTION CLIV. *Whoever of you have been baptized in Christ,* etc. It is asked what sins are forgiven in baptism? Solution. Augustine says that not only past or present [sins], but even future ones.

QUESTION CLV. But it is asked how future [0471A] sins, which are not yet, of which no one repents, nor is yet held [liable] for them, because no one is yet guilty for them — how, I say, are they forgiven? Solution. Future sins are said to be forgiven in baptism for this reason, that through the grace given there they are guarded against, or for this reason, that afterward he who receives this sacrament more easily obtains pardon.

QUESTION CLVI. Whether sins are forgiven to one who approaches feignedly? Solution. Augustine says that yesterday's day is loosed, and whatever there was over and above fraternal hatred, in the very hour of being baptized; but they return at once because he does not repent.

QUESTION CLVII. It is asked how what Augustine says is true? Is he not, from the fact that he does not repent, a member of the devil? how then is he a member [0471B] of Christ? And if this is so, how are his sins forgiven him? he has not faith, he has not the spirit of Christ; therefore he is not a member of Christ, nor are his sins forgiven him. Solution. Some say that the aforesaid words of Augustine are to be understood thus. Yesterday is loosed, etc., that is, baptism has such efficacy even in him who approaches with a heart not contrite, that, unless the feigning remained in him, he would obtain pardon of all offenses, and afterward he will be able to obtain it, if he be willing to repent of his feigning.

QUESTION CLVIII. *Our old man is crucified,* etc. It is asked whether the old man, and the outer man, and the new man, and the inner man are the same? Solution. Not the same, because the outer man is called that which we have in common with the animals, the inner man that which [0471C] we possess in common with the angels. But the old man pertains to both; for not the outer man alone, but also the inner is old through [sic: *por*] fault, concerning which old [man] we must treat in the present chapter.

QUESTION CLIX. It is asked, therefore, what the old man is? They say that the tinder of sin is so called; but it is truer that the tinder itself is called a part of the oldness. But it seems to us that oldness is understood according to two [things], namely fault and penalty. Now of penalty, one kind is eternal, another temporal; but of fault, one kind is original, another actual. Likewise original fault consists in the tinder itself, and in its act, and in the guilt of both. Again, actual fault is of one kind venial, of another mortal. Venial consists in three [things], namely [0471D] consent, act, and the guilt of both. Similarly mortal is understood by the same three modes. Behold the elevenfold number of transgression, in which the old man is regarded; for there is one pair in penalty, one triad in original fault, another in venial fault, a third in mortal fault, and thus three triads of faults with the pair of penalties constitute the number eleven. It is to be seen, therefore, according to what the old man is crucified. According to eternal penalty he is wholly destroyed in those who are Christ's; according to temporal penalty he is weakened and mitigated. Similarly according to mortal fault he is nonetheless utterly destroyed; but according to venial, mitigated, not yet fully taken away; further, according to original guilt [0472A] wholly not imputed; but according to act and the original tinder so crucified, that it does not have dominion.

QUESTION CLX. It is asked whether in Abraham the tinder of sin was crucified through the death of Christ? Some say that through faith in the death of Christ, then to come, it was in him also weakened.

QUESTION CLXI. It is likewise asked whether in the Apostle himself it was so weakened and crucified, that he never consented to its movements. For how did he not consent to the movements, when he committed venial [sins]? Likewise the venial [sins] which he committed — were they not voluntary, and so he willed them? how then did the tinder of sin not draw the Apostle to consent to sin? Solution. The Apostle, although he committed [them] voluntarily, nevertheless did not will the sin. [0472B] For a sin is not called voluntary from the fact that someone willed it; but because it proceeded from some will. Nor did he consent to the tinder. For to consent is to do, from deliberation and diligence, what the movements suggested: and so to consent seems even to be mortal.



## I. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE ROMANS. (cont.)

QUESTION CLXII. *That you obey the concupiscences.* It is asked what difference there is between obeying the concupiscences and presenting the members to sin. Solution. To obey the concupiscences is to consent in mind to carnal delights. To present the members to sin, so that they may be weapons of iniquity, this is to fulfill iniquity itself in deed.

QUESTION CLXIII. Whence it is asked why, after the first, he forbids the second; for in forbidding to obey [0472C] the concupiscences, he also forbids presenting the members to sin; for he who does not consent, does not act. Solution. He forbids the work also after the consent, for this reason: that if it should sometimes happen to consent in mind, yet such consent may not proceed into act, but rather be cut off. But some understand by 'to obey the concupiscences' the operation; by 'to present the members to sin,' the consent; and thus fittingly, after the prohibition of the operation, follows the prohibition of consent.

QUESTION CLXIV. *Sin shall not have dominion over you, for you are not under the law, but under grace.* Does not sin have dominion over many who are under grace? How then is it true: Sin shall not have dominion, etc. Solution. To you, who are obedient to grace, [0472D] there is already given a power by which you can resist sin, that it may not reign. Whence it is clear that if at any time it resumes its strength so as to have dominion, this comes about by our own fault.

QUESTION CLXV. *I speak a human thing on account of the infirmity.* It is asked what the Apostle calls 'human,' namely light, and easy to do, which belongs to a grown justice and not a perfect one. Solution. He himself shows it, subjoining: As you presented your members to serve uncleanness and iniquity unto iniquity, so now present your members to serve justice unto sanctification. Which is, briefly and openly, to say: Serve justice with that love with which formerly, without compulsion, by delight alone, you served uncleanness. Which is therefore called 'human,' because more is owed to justice than [0473A] to sin: for no one ever so loved sin, that he would not fear to die for it. But since we are prone to evil, which is done without labor and without any difficulty, and moreover with great sweetness and [sic: *immmoderata*] delight, but to good [we are] slow and sluggish, which is not done except with great labor, and huge difficulty, and often little or no delight; because the way of the virtues is arduous and hard, strait and narrow.

QUESTION CLXVI. But it is asked how the Apostle says it is a human thing to serve justice with as great a love, and as great a delight, as we formerly served iniquity, since this seems to be perfect and consummate? Solution. It is indeed true that when we see someone serve justice with love, we say that he is perfect, especially in this time [0473B] when the holy [man] has failed: yet as regards the truth itself, he is imperfect, unless for justice's sake he despise not only other things, but even death itself. It belongs, therefore, to a grown [adulterinae] justice, and not a perfect one, to do the commandment with love, with the resolve of dying for Christ, even if he does not yet have so great a charity or constancy from which he could endure death itself; and this degree suffices for salvation, and is required; because without it no one is worthy of glory. But it belongs to a perfect and consummate justice to have only virtue [enough], which may suffice for enduring death for the truth and the love of justice. Peter is seen to have had the first degree when he said: *Even if I must die with thee, I will not deny thee [n: (Matth. XXVI)]*. But he did not yet have the second, [0473C] which he attained through the coming of the Holy Spirit.

QUESTION CLXVII. *While the husband lives, the woman is bound by the law of the husband [n: (Rom. VII)]*, etc. It is asked concerning David and the other just, whether, while the law was living, that is, holding its standing, they were bound to keep the law, and whether they are to be called adulterers, because they were with another, that is, with Christ, in whom they believed, and by whom they sought to be justified? Solution. The law indeed is not laid down for the just [man], being as it were the schoolmaster of little ones; yet David with the other just were bound to keep the law on account of those to whom the law was necessary, lest by their example they should transgress it. Nor because they were with Christ through faith and love are they to be called adulterers: but [0473D] if, the legal sacraments being despised, they should already presume to introduce the sacraments of the New Testament, then they would seem adulterers.

QUESTION CLXVIII. *For when we were in the flesh, the passions of sins,* etc. It is asked what he calls 'passions,' whether the first movements, or something else. Solution. It can be said [that he means] the inborn concupiscences, which are the cause of sin, and, in those who are not in Christ, even mortal sin. Or the first movements, which are not imputed to the reborn; which he therefore calls 'passions,' because they harm nature, and deprive it of some good.

QUESTION CLXIX. *What shall we say? Is the law sin? God forbid!* It is asked whether the law itself makes [one] sin, or teaches [it], and so is evil. Solution. It is not [0474A] evil, but good; nor is it the cause of sin, even if it may seem the occasion.

QUESTION CLXX. *For I knew not concupiscence,* etc. It is asked of what concupiscence the Apostle says this. And the doctors say, of that which is forbidden in the Decalogue. Concerning which again it is asked what that is? For by the name of concupiscence there is sometimes signified the vice of concupiscence, namely the tinder of sin; sometimes the first movement, which is called propassion; sometimes the second movement, which is called passion, or delight; sometimes the consent; sometimes the outward endeavor; which, then, does the law forbid, saying: *Thou shalt not covet? [n: (Ibid.)]* Solution. In the response to this question the modern doctors disagree. For some say that the law forbids the first movements; but others [0474B] say the consent; others expound thus: Thou shalt not covet, that is, know concupiscence to be evil, and avoid it as much as you can, and so, according to this, nothing is forbidden. But whatever is contained in the Decalogue is either a precept or a prohibition: whence it must be that something is forbidden when it is said: Thou shalt not covet. To those who say that consent is forbidden, the objection is made thus: the Apostle says: *I do what I will not; but if this is so: I consent to the law by not doing, but by not willing (ibid)*.

QUESTION CLXXI. It is asked, therefore, what that is which the law forbids, and the Apostle, unwilling, was doing? but these say that the law forbids consent; yet it is clear that the Apostle was not consenting, because, if he consented to concupiscence, he would in no way consent to the law, [0474C] but would act against it. But Augustine shows openly what it is that the Apostle was unwilling [to do], and yet was doing. Namely, he felt the movement of concupiscence, but nevertheless did not consent to it — nay, he willed not to feel it. Whence it is clear that the law forbids the first movements of concupiscence.

QUESTION CLXXII. But it is asked again why God forbade what no one ever could avoid? For the law seems not only useless, but even irrational, since it interdicts what is impossible to avoid. For this reason the aforesaid doctors are unwilling that the law forbid the first movements. To whom the objection is made thus: Did not the law command to love God with the whole heart? But this precept, as Augustine says, [0474D] no one in the present can fulfill: if therefore something is commanded which cannot here be done by anyone, what wonder if something is forbidden which cannot be avoided?

QUESTION CLXXIII. It is asked, therefore, why God either forbade what cannot be avoided, or commanded what cannot be fulfilled. For he seems cruel, or not fair. Solution. That he might humble the proud, and illumine the blind, and thus prepare [him] worthy to receive grace. In this, that he forbade what no one can avoid, and commanded what no one can do, he shows what the perfection of divine justice is, and what God can justly exact from man. Moreover [he shows] what kind of [being] man was before sin, namely such a one as, with grace helping, would covet nothing without any difficulty, [0475A] and would love God with his whole heart: and of what kind he was made through fault, namely weak, carnal, unable not to covet, or to love God perfectly. What then remains, except that man, no longer presuming upon himself, take refuge in grace, and say: Lord, answer for me, for I am weak. And so not cruelly, but by a great dispensation, God commanded what cannot be done except by the sole mediator.

QUESTION CLXXIV. It is asked where the law thus forbids concupiscence in general; for it is not found where the law makes a prohibition except concerning the property or the wife of a neighbor. Solution. In the particular the general is understood.

QUESTION CLXXV. It is asked whence it comes about that we strive toward the forbidden, and that which is forbidden pleases the more [0475B], and becomes sweeter from the prohibition itself? Solution. Human nature is so created that it naturally desires liberty: which by prohibition seems to be diminished, and reduced to servitude; whence, as much as it can, immediately when the prohibition is made, it resists, striving against [it].

QUESTION CLXXVI. It is asked in what person the Apostle speaks when he says: *But I am carnal [n: (Rom. VII)]*, in his own, or in a general one? For if he speaks in his own person, how does he say that he is carnal, since he was spiritual? but if he speaks in a general [person], so that what he says befits every just and unjust [man] of any time whatever, how does it befit the unjust to hate evil, and to will good, and to consent to the law of God? Solution. Augustine says that he speaks in his own person [0475C], saying that he is carnal on account of the movements which he felt, and the same [man] was spiritual, not consenting to those disordered movements. But it cannot be denied that he says certain things which do not befit his own person, such as that: *I lived once without law [n: (ibid.)]*. There are those who say that he speaks in the general person of the just [man]; but how does it befit the just [man]: *But I am dead? [n: (Ibid.)]* But we say that the Apostle speaks in such wise that certain things befit the man of the natural law, certain things the man of the written law, certain things the man of the law of grace; but what befits whom is to be carefully noted; but it is of our purpose to pursue the questions, not to expound the letter.

QUESTION CLXXVII. *For what I work I understand not,* [0475D] etc. It is asked how he says that he does not understand what he works, since through the law he had known sin? Solution. 'I understand not' is put for 'I do not approve.'

QUESTION CLXXVIII. But when he says: *For not what I will, this I do;* it is asked how he says that he does not will what he does; is not that which he does a sin, and every sin is voluntary, and thus he seems to will what he says he does not will? Solution. There is a threefold willing, namely of nature, of fault, and of grace; whence the same [man] can will according to the flesh, and not will according to the mind.

QUESTION CLXXIX. *But if what I will not, this I do,* etc. It is asked what that is which the Apostle says he wills not, and yet does, the law forbidding [it]? [0476A] Solution. To covet: for the law forbids to covet, and the Apostle wills according to the mind not to covet, and yet according to the flesh he was coveting.

QUESTION CLXXX. But it is asked how he says that he does [it], and does not work [it]: for the same [holds]: if he does something, he works it, and if he does not work it, neither does he do it. Are affirmation and negation at once true of the same thing? Solution. He does [it] according to the flesh, and does not work [it] according to the mind.



## I. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE ROMANS. (cont.)

QUESTION CLXXXI. It is asked whether it is consequent that he does not work [it] simply, if he does not work [it] according to the mind; just as it is consequent that, if he does [it] according to the flesh, he also does [it] simply. Solution. It is indeed true that upon a determinate affirmation [0476B] there follows a simple affirmation almost always, but a simple negation does not usually follow a determinate negation; whence one must lay stress on the pronoun when it is said: *I do not work [it] [n: (ibid.)]*, as it were the inner I, in which the truth of the man consists, because [it is] the image of God; and thus the negation, which seems to be simple, is not simple, but rather determinate.

QUESTION CLXXXII. *But the sin that dwells in me.* It is asked how sin, which is nothing, but only a privation of good, is said to dwell in someone. Solution. By this name 'sin' is signified the tinder of sin, which is called the law of the flesh, or the law of the members, just as consent or operation is called the law of death.

QUESTION CLXXXIII. *I know that there dwells not in [0476C] me,* that is, *in my flesh, good.* It is asked whether he says universally of all good, or indefinitely of a certain good, that it does not dwell in the flesh? But of all good how can he say this, since the flesh, even corrupted and subject to languor, is the work of God, nor deprived of all good, especially since evil is not a thing existing by itself, nor can it be except in a good thing. Whence it is consequent that where there is no good, neither is there any evil; and where there is any evil, there is necessarily also some good. Solution. He says of health, or of the integrity of nature, that it does not dwell in the flesh: whence he fittingly proves that sin is the tinder of sin, which is the privation of health, or of the integrity of nature; it dwells in the flesh: for where there is not health [0476D] and there ought to be, there is the privation of the same, which the Apostle signifies, saying: Good does not dwell in the flesh.

QUESTION CLXXXIV. *To will lies near to me,* etc. It is asked concerning that will which the Apostle says lies near to him, what it is to be called? Solution. Nothing other than the natural affection of the soul, which from creation is in the soul, and by which the soul naturally wills good; but this affection always lacks effect, unless it be helped by the grace of God.

QUESTION CLXXXV. *To will lies near to me, but to accomplish I find not; since evil lies near to me.* By these words the Apostle seems to intimate that, if evil did not lie near, not only would 'to will' lie near to him, but he would also find 'to accomplish,' since nothing would impede toward [0477A] good, or impel toward evil. But in this very thing he intimates the first state of the first man before sin, to whom 'to will' good lay near, but evil did not lie near, whence he could not say, 'to will lies near to me, but to accomplish I find not, because evil lies near to me.' But whether that good, which he naturally willed, he could accomplish without the addition of greater grace, might be asked. Was not that willing, which Adam had before sin, much more intense than that natural willing which the pagans now have? but the pagans do great and many good things (even if they are not worthy of eternal life, because done without faith) from the natural affection which they have: how then could Adam, before sin, from a greater will, not advance even a little, as almost all assert? [0477B] Likewise, if God did not confer greater grace, but left [him] in that state in which he created him, could not God justly exact from man that, for the good already conferred, he should love him perfectly? but God could not justly exact what man could not render: and thus it seems that man before sin could advance from the good already received, which many deny.

QUESTION CLXXXVI. But someone will say: Can not God now justly exact from man what man cannot render, as has already been said above? For unless God could justly exact that he covet nothing against reason, and that he love God with his whole heart, he would not have commanded man both. In a similar way it seems that man before sin could not render the whole which God could justly exact. [0477C] Solution. Man before sin was not made weak or impotent by any fault; therefore he could easily render what God could exact: after sin, and through sin, he was made such that he cannot pay every debt, which nevertheless God can exact, and does exact from those who presume upon themselves, and do not take refuge in grace. It is to be known that this text, from that place: I am carnal, up to that: *therefore there is now no condemnation [n: (Rom. VIII)]*, etc., is wont to be read both of the man of the law and of the man of grace; and how it ought, or can, be expounded of the man of grace has been said according to Augustine. But how it is to be read of the man of the law, they will be loved, is to be considered. Now he is called the man of the law who, instructed by the law, knows through sin; to which, unwilling to resist, [0477D] he is conquered and succumbs, and consents to the tinder — who is called carnal, not only feeling the tinder, but even consenting to it. Since, therefore, consent is of reason.

QUESTION CLXXXVII. It is asked how it befits the man of the law: *I will the good, I hate the evil, now* I myself do not work *it, and I consent to the law of God.* Solution. Although consent is of reason, yet because carnality drew it to consent, it is ascribed not to reason but to the flesh.

QUESTION CLXXXVIII. *That the justification of the law might be fulfilled in us [n: (Rom. VIII)]*. It is asked what he calls the justification of the law, since from the law there is no justice. Solution. He calls the justification of the law not that which the law conferred, [0478A] but that which the law commanded and promised, which is fulfilled in us through the grace of Christ, who fulfilled the law fully and perfectly, and gave us the grace of fulfilling [it], and what we do too little: he himself supplies, and answers for us.

QUESTION CLXXXIX. Whether it is the same thing to walk according to the flesh, and to be according to the flesh, and to savor the things that are of the flesh. Solution. It is not the same. For to walk according to the flesh is to fulfill in deed the things which the flesh covets. To be according to the flesh is to consent to the concupiscences, or to be disposed toward carnal things. To savor the things that are of the flesh is to take delight in such things as the flesh judges highest, or not to perceive the things that are of God. For in two ways is one called carnal, namely by life and by doctrine: either who [0478B] indulges the flesh, or who binds the divine power to the natures of things, that is, who believes that God can do nothing except what he sees in the nature of things. Similarly, to walk according to the spirit is to fulfill in deed the things that are of the spirit. To be according to the spirit is to consent to the spirit, or to be disposed according to spiritual things. To perceive the things that are of the spirit is to take delight in spiritual things, according to which three [things] one is called truly spiritual. One is also called spiritual through understanding; and there is one spiritual by life, and not by understanding; another by understanding, and not by life; another in both ways; another in neither, etc.

QUESTION CXC. *But you are not in the flesh,* etc. It is asked how the Apostle says that they are not in the flesh, since he wrote to them to repress their proud contention. [0478C] Solution. He calls them in the spirit and not in the flesh, because they did not walk according to the flesh, or, what is better, among the Romans there were certain spiritual ones, and of perfect faith: on account of which he says: You are not in the flesh. And there were others contending among themselves, and of imperfect faith, looking to whom he subjoins: *If yet the Spirit of God dwells in you [n: (ibid.)]*, whom he exhorts to perfection.

QUESTION CXCI. *But if Christ is in you.* It is asked what it is for Christ to be in someone, or the spirit of Christ? Solution. Augustine says: Christ in a man is faith in the heart. But according to this, in whomever [there is] faith [there is] also Christ; but in the evil [man] there is faith, therefore also Christ, or the Spirit of Christ is in the evil [man]. For it is the same [thing], for Christ, or the spirit of Christ, to be [0478D] in someone; but the spirit of Christ is not in anyone except in whom there is love. Solution. Not everyone in whom there is faith has faith in the heart; for faith is not said to be in the heart, except when it sits in the heart and pleases [it], that is, except where it works through love; and according to this it is the same [thing], for Christ to be in someone, and for the faith of Christ to be in his heart.

QUESTION CXCII. *The body indeed is dead on account of sin.* It is asked on account of what sin the body is dead, that is, subject to the necessity of dying. Solution. On account of the guilt of original fault: but since such — nay, every — fault is forgiven in baptism, it is asked why such a penalty, inflicted for such a fault, is not taken away. Solution. Although the fault for which such a penalty [0479A] is inflicted is forgiven, yet such an infirmity remains, lest men should hasten to the reception of that sacrament more for the advantage of temporal benefit than for the faith and love of the future life, and thus it would not profit the soul to be reborn in Christ. Or *the body is dead on account of sin [n: (Rom. VIII)]* to be avoided. For the human spirit is such that, unless it were liable to infirmities, it would be exalted beyond measure, and thus would not be saved — which cannot come about without humility. Not cruelly, therefore, but mercifully; nor from impotence, but from a great dispensation, is our mortality, with the other penalties, not taken away in baptism.

QUESTION CXCIII. *For you have not received the spirit of servitude,* etc. It is asked whether the spirit of fear [0479B] and the spirit of adoption are the same? Solution. The same Spirit, on account of various effects, is called by different names. Note that the gift and the author of the gift are called by the same name.

QUESTION CXCIV. It is asked how, speaking to the Romans, he says: You have not again received the spirit of servitude, again in fear, etc. For the spirit of servitude was not before given to them as to the Jews. Solution. 'Again' does not denote an iteration made in the same persons, but in different ones, as if he said: To the Jews indeed, in the giving of the law, was given the spirit of servitude in fear, and again *to you is given a spirit not of fear, but of adoption.* Fear is of one kind worldly, of another servile, of another initial, of another filial. Worldly fear is, according to some, when we forsake good or do evil, yet keeping the will [0479C] good, on account of the shame of others, lest we be held cheap — which, according to the same [authors], is also called human. But according to Cassiodorus, worldly fear is when we fear the perils of the flesh, or to lose the goods of the world, on account of which we transgress; and this fear is evil, and in the first degree is forsaken along with the world: which the Lord forbids, saying: *Fear not those who kill the body [n: (Matth. X)]*. Servile [fear], according to the earlier [authors], is that which restrains the hand from an evil work, the evil will being kept.



## I. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE ROMANS. (cont.)

QUESTION CXCV. Concerning which it can be asked, whether it is a gift of the Holy Spirit; which if it is, it is good; but it seems evil, since on account of penalty it makes [one] serve. Solution. This [fear] is good, having a good effect, namely, to restrain from an evil work; but this, that the evil [0479D] will remains, is not from it, but from the vice of man. But according to others, servile fear is that by which hell is feared: by which good is done, but not well: which is called initial according to the earlier [authors], and, according to the later ones too, is the beginning of wisdom: which prepares a place for wisdom, and leads to charity. Initial fear is also spoken of in another way, when that which was hard begins to be loved, and this is as it were midway between servile and filial, having something of both. According to both opinions, filial fear is that by which not penalty is feared, but lest the bridegroom be offended, or depart — lest we offend, lest we be without God: which is the companion of perfection.

QUESTION CXCVI. But which of these was in [0480A] Christ is wont to be asked? That the worldly was not [in him], since it is evil, is agreed: likewise neither the servile nor the initial, since neither could be in perfect charity: therefore either the filial, or none, seems to have been in him; but filial is that by which we fear to offend; but did Christ fear to offend, or to be separated? Solution. Filial fear was in Christ not according to the effect which it has in the present in us, but according to that which it has in the angels, or will have in the future in the saints; but according to reverence, which is love mixed with subjection, whence the Apostle: *And he was heard for his reverence [n: (Hebr. V)]*. Likewise, did not Christ fear penalty, namely death, *whence Jesus began to be afraid [n: (Marc. XIV)]*: by what fear, then? was it perhaps filial, since no other was [0480B] in him? Solution. By natural fear he began to be afraid, which is not contained in the aforesaid division; because it is equally in the good and the evil: which is called natural, not because it is concreted with nature, but because from corruption it grew somehow into nature: which, with the other penalties, Christ took upon himself.

QUESTION CXCVII. Servile fear was given in the law; but servile fear, according to Augustine, is that by which hell is feared: therefore fear of hell was given in the law: but does not fear of hell restrain not only the hand, but also the mind; whereas the law [restrains] the hand only, and not the mind?
