Likewise, Augustine says that servile fear is never with charity; but does not everyone who fears eternal penalty have fear of hell? but someone who has charity begun, [0480C] still fears penalty, and thus it seems that servile fear is in charity. These questions I raise, not that I may solve them, but that I may rouse the reader to inquire with me.

QUESTION CXCVIII. It is asked still concerning servile fear, whether it makes [one] a servant of Christ, or of the devil. Solution. Neither of God nor of the devil, but it makes [one] a servant of penalty; because in some way it takes away liberty, and does not allow [one] to do the work which he wills, on account of penalty.

QUESTION CXCIX. *And coheirs of Christ,* etc. It is asked whether Christ is an heir, and if he is, according to which nature; for it seems that according to divinity he is not to be called an heir, since an inheritance is the firm possession of a successor upon someone's dying: but neither does the Father die, nor does the Son succeed him, because both [0480D] are from eternity: how then [is he] an heir? Solution. Thus indeed among us an inheritance has [its] being through succession; but not so in God: for *the Son, because he has [his] being from the Father, has also all things which he has from the Father [n: (Joan. I)]*; but not conversely: therefore the Son is the heir of the Father: but according to his humanity he received the inheritance, according to the fullness of which we all have received.

QUESTION CC. It is asked what it is to be coheirs of Christ. Solution. Partakers of the same inheritance. But according to this it seems false that we are coheirs; because the possession of the nations is the inheritance of Christ. Whence: *Ask of me, and I will give thee the nations for thy inheritance [n: (Psal. II)]*: of which inheritance we are not partakers, [0481A] and thus we are not coheirs. Solution. The inheritance of Christ, according to which we are coheirs, is eternal life, not the possession of the nations.

QUESTION CCI. *If yet we suffer with [him].* It is asked how one is to suffer with Christ, who now does not suffer. Solution. In two ways we suffer with Christ: either by calling to memory his sufferings, which he endured for us, and thus by compassion grieving with him, or by enduring tribulations in his likeness, with him, and on account of him. Compassion is sometimes the name of a natural affection, sometimes of the consent to it, which is a virtue.

QUESTION CCII. *The sufferings of this time are not worthy [to be compared],* etc. It is asked whether the merits of the saints suffice for attaining the future life: for if they have true [0481B] justice they are worthy of the crown; but that the fathers of the New Testament are truly just, the Apostle himself intimates, saying: *For the rest, there is laid up for me a crown of justice, which the just judge will render to me: and not only to me, but also to all who love his coming [n: (II Tim. IV)]*. But here he seems to say that the tribulations which the saints endure do not suffice to merit the future glory, which is to be revealed: for this is [what it means], that the sufferings are not worthy [to be compared] to the future glory. Solution. The Apostle does not deny that the merits of the saints suffice for attaining glory, but for meriting so excellent a glory they are not worthy [to be compared]; because God from his grace will superadd more than our merits have merited, which is, briefly, to say: our merits are least [0481C] in respect of the rewards. For the creature is subjected to vanity. Note that vanity is threefold: the first is of mutability, the second of mortality, the third of iniquity; and man is all vanity, that is, subjected to every vanity.

QUESTION CCIII. *Not willing, but for the sake of him who subjected it in hope.* It is asked what the creature subjected to vanity does not will: if it does not will evil, the will is good: but then that which follows is nothing, 'But for the sake of him,' etc.; but if it does not will good, it sins, since it ought to will good. Solution. 'Will' is spoken of in several ways; for sometimes it is called the natural affection, as there, 'not what I will, I do'; sometimes the consent to it, according to which the will is said [to be] damnable or to be rewarded; sometimes the horror of the flesh, as there: [0481D] *Not as I will, but as thou [wilt] [n: (Matth. XXVI)]*, and in this way it is taken here, and the sense is: Although the penalty which I endure is bitter, yet I take delight to endure it for the sake of Christ.

QUESTION CCIV. *Every creature groans,* etc. It is asked how this is true, since a stone is a creature, and yet does not groan? Solution. 'Every,' here, does not gather the individuals of the kinds, but the kinds of the individuals, and the sense is: Every creature, that is, man, in whom is every kind of creatures. For there are three species of creature, namely corporeal, and animal, and spiritual: which all are in man, and thus every creature groans in man.

QUESTION CCV. *And not only it, but we also,* etc. [0482A] Are not the good understood by the name of creature? how then does the Apostle distinguish between the apostles, who have the firstfruits of the Spirit, and the creature, as though they themselves were not good? Solution. By the same reasoning by which, between the creature and the sons of God — although the sons of God are creature — he distinguishes between the creature and the apostles, although they themselves are creatures, making a distinction which is regarded not in a diversity of another quality, as the glosses, diligently inspected, declare.

QUESTION CCVI. *Hope that is seen is not hope,* etc. It is asked what it is that is signified by the name of hope, when it is said: Hope that is seen; and from what it is removed, when it is said: It is not hope. To that which is not hope, how does the name of hope befit, or is it fitted? Solution. Just as equivocally faith is called that by which one believes and that which is believed; [0482B] so equivocally hope is called that by which one hopes, and that which is hoped for. And thus hope that is seen, that is, the thing hoped for, is not the hope by which we hope, namely that virtue. Or otherwise, hope that is seen, that is, that which is of a visible thing, is not our hope, namely, by the merit of which we are already made saved.

QUESTION CCVII. *The Spirit himself asks for us with unspeakable groanings.* It is asked, since the Spirit needs nothing, nor suffers any straits, how he is said to ask or to groan. Solution. What the saints do, with him as author, is attributed to the Spirit: he asks, therefore, or groans, because he makes us ask, or groan.

QUESTION CCVIII. Whether the Spirit asks for something, and does not obtain [it]? Whenever we ask for anything out of charity, [0482C] does not the Spirit himself ask in us; but often we ask for him who lies in crime, and we are not heard, and thus it seems that the Spirit, by whose teaching we pray, is not heard. Solution. There are two species of just prayer: either when we ask what is to be asked, or when we ask where it is to be asked; but sometimes we ask where it is to be asked, yet not what is to be asked, and the Spirit then teaches how it is to be asked, but does not teach what is to be asked, and therefore we do not obtain [it]. But as often as we ask what is to be asked and where it is to be asked, we obtain [it].

QUESTION CCIX. *We know that to those who love God all things work together unto good.* Do not some love for a time, and afterward fall and thus are damned? But to these [0482D] not all things work together unto good, and thus it seems that to those who love God not all things work together unto good. Solution. He does not say simply that to those who love God all things work together unto good, but *to those who love and are called saints according to purpose:* for some love, and yet are not called saints according to purpose, just as some are preordained to life, yet do not yet love; and to neither do all things work together unto good, but to those who love and are called saints according to purpose.

QUESTION CCX. It is likewise asked how the very sins of such [persons] work together unto good for them. Solution. They rise up more humble and more learned.

[0483A]QUESTION CCXI. But would they not, if they had not fallen, but had then done good, be better than they now are; as this corrupted [woman], if she had, along with those good things which she now has, also virginity, would she not be of a higher degree and of greater merit? Solution. It is indeed true that if Mary Magdalene, with so great devotion, had also virginity, she would be of greater merit; but her fall cooperated toward a certain perfection, and perhaps if she had not fallen so basely, she would never have loved the heavenly physician so much: yet [one is] not to sin, that grace may abound.

QUESTION CCXII. It is likewise asked how the sins of the evil work together unto good for the good. Solution. Nothing is done in the world that does not confer something of usefulness [0483B] to the good of the universe; and thus all things, whether goods, or evils, whether one's own, or another's, work together unto good for the good.



## I. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE ROMANS. (cont.)

QUESTION CCXIII. Whether all things work together unto evil for those to be damned? That their own evils harm them is certain; but whether the good things which they sometimes do work together unto evil for them can be asked, which seems [so]: because it is written: *It is better not to know the way of truth, than after knowing it to turn back [n: (II Petr. II)]*. Solution. Not all things work together unto evil for those to be damned; because for the good things which they sometimes do, they will be punished less. But that which is said: It is better, etc., was not said of those to be damned who were once just; it was said of heretics.

QUESTION CCXIV. *Whom he foreknew, he also predestinated.* It is asked whether foreknowledge is the cause of predestination, [0483C] just as predestination is the cause of calling, calling the cause of justification, and justification of glorification — which seems [so] according to the supposed order: but since each is eternal, how can one be the cause of the other? Solution. Although each is eternal, yet one can be the cause of the other; just as the Son and the Holy Spirit, though each is eternal, yet the Son is the cause of the Holy Spirit; so, according to some, foreknowledge pertains to knowledge, and thus to the Son; predestination to election; election to will, which pertains to the property of the Holy Spirit: and therefore, just as the Son is the cause of the Holy Spirit, so also foreknowledge is the cause of predestination, which it precedes naturally, not in time.

[0483D]QUESTION CCXV. Whether foreknowledge and predestination are the same in God? For the solution of this question it must be known that the divine essence, though it is one and simple, yet on account of various effects obtains diverse names; for it is called wisdom, knowledge, foresight, providence, disposition, predestination. But wisdom and knowledge are concerning all things past and present and future, good and evil, and foresight concerning the same; providence concerning things to be governed, which is sometimes taken for foresight; disposition concerning things to be done; predestination concerning those to be saved; and thus in God, as to essence, foreknowledge and predestination are the same.

QUESTION CCXVI. Whether foreknowledge would be in God, if [0484A] there were no future things? That [it would] not, they seek to prove thus. If there were no future things, God would not foreknow any future things, and if this were so, foreknowledge would not be in God; but foreknowledge is the divine knowledge, and this is the essence of God, and thus it seems, if there were no future things, that God would not be; which God forbid! Solution. Foreknowledge does not simply signify the divine essence, but designates about it a relation with respect to future things: whence that expression, if there were no future things, foreknowledge would not be in God, is understood in two ways. For if it be said: If there were no future things, foreknowledge would not be in God, that is, if there were no future things, there would be no objects of the divine knowledge whence it would be called foreknowledge, the understanding is true. But if it be understood thus: If there were no future things, foreknowledge would not [0484B] be in God, that is, the knowledge by which he foreknows future things, the understanding is false.

QUESTION CCXVII. Whether God's foreknowledge is the cause of future things, or future things of foreknowledge? There would be no future things, unless God had foreseen [them]; and thus it seems that foreknowledge is the cause of future things. Whence Augustine: God knows all creatures not because they are; but they now are, because he knows [them]. But since he knows equally good and evil things, it seems that foreknowledge is the cause both of evils and of goods. But Origen says: A thing will not be for the reason that God knows it to be future, but because that thing is future, God foreknows [it]: this seems to be contrary to that earlier opinion of Augustine. Solution. Sometimes "knowledge" is taken for good pleasure, and then it is the cause of future things; but only of good things: [0484C] thus [it is] that Augustine took [it] above; but sometimes it signifies knowledge or cognition alone, and then it is not the cause of future things, but is related equally to goods and to evils; and thus Origen took [it]; and thus foreknowledge is not the cause of future things, but neither are future things the cause of foreknowledge, unless it be called a cause without which [there is] not [the thing].

QUESTION CCXVIII. Whether foreknowledge imposes on future things a necessity of coming to pass? Which seems [so], because if God foreknew something to be future, it cannot not come to pass; and if this is so, whatever he foreknew will necessarily come to pass. Likewise it seems that God's foresight could be deceived; because if that which is foreseen can come to pass otherwise than it comes to pass, it can come to pass otherwise than it is foreseen, as if God foresaw that someone would read today: [0484D] it is conceded by all that such a one, who is to read today, can not read, and thus it can happen otherwise than is foreknown. Solution. This is the common one: these and similar [statements] can be expounded by conjunction and by disjunction; the sense of the conjunction is this: If God foresaw, it is necessary [that it] come to pass, that is, both cannot be at once, that God foresees and that it does not come to pass, and thus it is true. The sense of the disjunction is this: This future thing cannot come to pass otherwise than in the manner in which it comes to pass, and than it is foreseen, and this is false. Likewise, if it came to pass otherwise than is foreknown, the divine providence would not be deceived; because then this would not have been foreseen, but the other thing which would then come to pass. To the aforesaid solution it is objected thus: If God foresaw something, it will come to pass; [0485A] this hypothetical [proposition] is necessary: whence if the antecedent is necessary, so is the consequent; but the antecedent is necessary, because what is foreknown cannot be not foreknown, and thus it seems that whatever is future is future by a certain necessity. Which also seems able to be proved otherwise: God from eternity by his eternal Word said of every future thing, that it will be; but it is impossible for God to lie: but if that which he said to be future did not come to pass, it would follow that God were a liar, and so what is future will necessarily come to pass; whence Augustine: As it is necessary that what was should have been, so it is necessary that what is future should come to be. And elsewhere, speaking of God: Whose will, he says, is necessity, because, if he wills, it will necessarily be. Solution. There are some who [0485B] concede that God can not-have-foreseen what he foresaw from eternity: and therefore they say that the antecedent of the preceding hypothetical is not necessary, and therefore neither is the consequent. But how what they say is true, I do not see. Likewise let them answer the second objection: how God is not a liar, if that which he foretold to be future does not come to pass; or how the will of God is necessity; or how what Augustine says is true, [that] it is necessary that what is future should come to be. But we, following Augustine, say that there is a twofold necessity: one which is regarded according to lower causes, which is sometimes impeded by the higher ones; and another which is understood according to higher causes, which it is impossible not to be fulfilled. But of those things which are future, some come to pass equally both according to higher causes and according to lower causes, some [0485C] only according to higher causes: but whatever is future, as regards the necessity which is understood according to the totality of causes, cannot be hindered from coming to pass. But the usage of speaking is formed according to the lower causes, because they are more known to us. Whence we often say that something can be, and the same can not be, because those causes permit both; but according to the higher causes only one can be, namely what God wills, what he disposes, what he foreknew from eternity and foretold by his Word to be future, and it can in no way be hindered from coming to pass: regarding these causes, Augustine said that what is future will necessarily come to be.

[0485D]QUESTION CCXIX. Likewise it is asked whether God's knowledge can be increased or diminished. That it can, they seek to prove thus: God knows that this man will read, but he can not read: therefore God can not-know that this man will read. Or thus: This man is not going to read, but he can read: therefore God can know that this man will read, who is not going to read: but it can come to pass that he read, therefore something can be known by God which is not. Solution. God's knowledge is immutable, nor can it be increased or diminished: yet some concede that God can know what he does not know, and not-know what he knows, and know more things than he knows, not knowing how to respond to the aforesaid objections. But we say that God cannot know what he does not know, nor not-know what he knows, nor know more things than he knows; because if [0486A] some things which will never come to be were to begin to come to be, they would not thereby begin to be known by God, who from eternity knows all things perfectly, not only those which sometimes come to be, but whatever can come to be: for he knows both by what causes they can come to be, and what they would be like, if they came to be. For he does not now have a fuller knowledge of the world than he had from eternity: nor now a lesser knowledge of those things which will never come to be, though they could come to be, than he would have if [it] came to be. Yet it is a great question whether any such thing is in mere possibility, which is never in act: for that which does not have a cause in God, how can it come forth into act? But of this, which we have now touched upon incidentally, it must be spoken elsewhere. But responding to the aforesaid objections, we say that this [statement], God knows that this man will read, posits that this man [0486B] is to read and that God knows this: and therefore, since he is not going to read, it is not to be conceded that God knows that this man will read — not because God does not know something: for whether he read or not read, God does not therefore know or not-know more or less. The same judgment [holds] of similar cases. It is also to be noted that when it is said that he who is to read can not read, or that he who is not to read can read, this is said according to the lower causes; but if we look to the higher causes, what is future will determinately be, and not the other, nor will it be able not to come to pass: yet because usage is formed according to the lower causes, which compel neither but permit both, we say that both can be and not be, although in truth it is true that the one is future, and not [0486C] the other.



## I. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE ROMANS. (cont.)

QUESTION CCXX. Whether the number of the predestined can be increased or diminished? The same is asked concerning the number of the reprobate. That it can, they seek to prove thus. God can not apply grace to him to whom he applies it, and if he did this, the predestined would be damned; and he can apply it to him to whom he does not apply it, and if he did this, the reprobate would be saved. And thus he who is predestined can be damned, and he who is reprobate can be saved: and someone can pass from the number of the predestined to the number of the reprobate, and conversely. Solution. This they resolve after the likeness of the aforesaid, by conjunction and disjunction. But we say that it is possible according to lower causes, [and] perhaps impossible [0486D] according to higher causes. Likewise it does not seem to follow, God can save or damn this man, therefore this man can be saved or damned. For our power does not follow from God's power, as [it would] if God could redeem the human race otherwise, that therefore the human race could be redeemed otherwise than through the death of the Son of God: and if God had this in his power, that man therefore had in his power to be saved otherwise. Cannot God, if he willed, save Judas? Can Judas therefore be saved? It is to be noted that "lower causes" are called those which God implanted in creatures at the first constitution of things, according to which like are born of like, as from such a grain such a tree or fruit proceeds. But "higher causes" are called the divine power, will, disposition, and predestination: [0487A] which sometimes work with the lower, sometimes without them: which always have their effect, but the lower not always; rather they are sometimes impeded by the higher.

QUESTION CCXXI. *To be made conformable to the image of his Son,* etc. It is asked why the Son is called the image of the Father. Solution. Because he is most like him, and because he is of the same essence.

QUESTION CCXXII. But since the Holy Spirit is equally like the Father, being of the same substance, why is the Son and not the Holy Spirit called the image of the Father? Solution. Because image pertains more to the property of generation than of procession: for those things which are generated are wont to be more alike than those which proceed.

[0487B]QUESTION CCXXIII. Whether there are two images in the Son, because he is the uncreated image insofar as he is God, and the created image insofar as he is man. Solution. Not for that reason two images, because in Christ the uncreated image and the created image are not two images, but one: just as he is the Son of God and the son of man; yet not two sons, but one Son of God and son of man.

QUESTION CCXXIV. *Whom he called, them he also justified,* etc. It is asked how the Apostle distinguishes between calling and justifying, since in calling he justifies. Solution. By calling he understands the infusion of charity; by justification the grace that follows and preserves.

QUESTION CCXXV. *Whom he predestinated, [them] he also called,* etc. It is asked whether all who are called are predestined, [0487C] which seems [so], because calling is the effect of predestination. But some are called who are not chosen, because it is written: *Many are called, few chosen [n: (Matth. XXII)]*; but only the chosen are predestined; therefore not all who are called are predestined. Solution. Calling is of one kind common, which is made outwardly by preaching, and also sometimes inwardly by inspiration: of another kind special, which is made according to purpose, according to which none is called except the chosen and predestined. But according to the common calling it was said: Many are called, but few chosen.

QUESTION CCXXVI. *Who shall accuse against the elect?* None, because what God does not will, another cannot [do]. But it is asked: what is it for God to accuse someone? Solution. For God to accuse is to permit [0487D] a man to fall into sin. But for the devil to accuse is to cast [him] into sin through temptation, and by casting [him down] to make him liable to accusation.

QUESTION CCXXVII. It is asked how Christ intercedes for us. Whether by voice, or only by mind and desire? or in some other way? Solution. For Christ to intercede for us is, by the merits of his obedience shown to God the Father in his humanity, to reconcile us to him, [we] adhering [to him] by faith and love.

QUESTION CCXXVIII. It is also asked whether the saints, whose patronage we ask, intercede for us, and how? Solution. For the saints to intercede for us is nothing other than that God, for their merits, [0488A] rewards the good affections which we have toward them for God's sake; and therefore it makes no difference whether they hear us or do not hear us.

QUESTION CCXXIX. *Who shall separate us from the charity of God,* etc. It is asked whether the Apostle was of such great perfection that he could not sin mortally; for if he could not be separated from God, neither could he sin mortally: for this is to be separated. But since he says elsewhere: *Lest the greatness of the revelations should exalt me, there was given me a sting of my flesh [n: (II Cor. 12)]*, he intimates that if he could be exalted, he could also sin mortally, and thus be separated. Solution. No other than himself could separate the Apostle; yet he could separate himself, because in this he merited, that he could transgress, and did not transgress. For when he confessed Christ before Nero, then he could deny him, if [0488B] he willed: what therefore neither life nor death could [do], he himself could.

QUESTION CCXXX. It is asked how the cause makes the martyr, and not the punishment, since none is a martyr without punishment, just as neither without a cause. For as punishment without a cause does not suffice for the crown of martyrdom, so neither the cause without punishment. Solution. That which is said, the cause makes the martyr and not the punishment, is namely to be understood as "alone." But someone will say that by the same reasoning it can be said, punishment makes the martyr, not the cause, so that it be understood as "alone." Response. The cause without punishment profits; punishment without a cause not only does not profit, but harms; and that punishment profits, when it profits, this it has from the cause; therefore this is said rather than that.

[0488C]QUESTION CCXXXI. *Great is my sadness, [and] continual sorrow to my heart [n: (Rom. IX)]*. It is asked why the Apostle says that he grieves, that formerly, erring with those who erred, he persecuted the Church: for since great advantage came from that persecution, he ought not to grieve over it, but to rejoice? Solution. The Apostle did not grieve over the good which followed from his evil, but over the sin which he himself committed, over which it is always good to grieve.

QUESTION CCXXXII. *I myself wished to be anathema from Christ for my brethren.* It is asked how the Apostle wished to be separated from Christ, whether according to glory, or according to justice. If according to glory, he seems to have loved his brethren more than God, which is in no way to be done; if he wished to be separated according to [0488D] justice, this he could not [do] without sin and without offense against God, which cannot reasonably be wished by anyone. Solution. He did not wish to be separated from Christ in this way or that, but by these words he shows his wonderful affection which he had toward the Jews, which kind of expression Moses used, saying: *Either blot me out of the book of life, or forgive them this offense [n: (Exod. XXXII)]*. Or it can be said that each, both Moses and the Apostle, preferred in his desire and wish the salvation of so great a multitude to his own salvation, nor in this did they set the love of God after; rather, preferring the glory and honor of God to their own salvation, [they were] willing rather that the glory of God be magnified in so many saved, than [0489A] diminished in one saved; and this perfection exceeds every perfection, because a greater cannot be conceived.

QUESTION CCXXXIII. *What then shall we say? Is there injustice with God?* It is asked whether God is unjust in reprobating, and in hardening, and finally in damning this man who cannot act well without the grace of predestination, especially since he cannot merit that predestination itself? Solution. There is assuredly no injustice with God. But that it may be plain that there is no injustice with God, it must be seen what predestination is and what its effect, and what reprobation and its effect. Predestination is the preparation of grace: by the name of grace are here signified the gratuitous goods by which in the present we are justified, or in the future [0489B] are crowned; but what "preparation" signifies must be seen. It was said above that the divine essence, though it is one and simple, obtains diverse names on account of the various effects of things: of which one is predestination. Preparation therefore is nothing other than God himself preparing, and discerning, and appointing, and purposing, or choosing in himself, that he confer these gifts on these, or those in time; and this purpose or this preparation is the cause of the future goods by which we are adopted as sons of God. Behold, it has been said what predestination is, and what its effect, namely calling, justification, and glorification. But is reprobation something that was from eternity in God, or God himself, that it be the cause of future evils just as predestination [is the cause] of goods? Which [0489C] if it be conceded, it follows that God is thus the cause and author of evils. Whence it must be known that reprobation does not posit anything; because for God to reprobate someone is nothing other than not to choose, and not to predestine, [and] not to prepare gratuitous goods. Whose effect is to harden: which is nothing other than not to apply grace; such as the cause is, such is the effect. But there is no injustice or unrighteousness, if God does not give something to him to whom he owes nothing. God is therefore not unjust in that he reprobates or hardens someone.

QUESTION CCXXXIV. But it is asked why God did not reprobate all, or why he did not predestine all; but predestined some and reprobated some. Solution. If he predestined all, the divine justice would lie hidden, [0489D] because it would not be known what was justly owed to fault; if he reprobated all, the goodness of God would not appear. But the wisdom of God judged it better that there be good and evil, than only good, although it itself made nothing but good.



## I. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE ROMANS. (cont.)

QUESTION CCXXXV. But it is asked why he chose Jacob rather than Esau; likewise the same can be asked concerning any elect and reprobate whatever. For it cannot be said that one was chosen and the other reprobated on account of future good or evil merits: for thus that which is temporal would be the cause of that which is eternal. Likewise, if it be said that Jacob was chosen by grace alone, [and] Esau reprobated on account of original sin, it is asked why on account of the same sin Jacob was not reprobated, or why Esau was not chosen out of the same [0490A] grace? Solution — if indeed it ought to be called a solution to show that something is insoluble. They say it was so done because God willed it to be done; but if it be asked why he so willed, the question is foolish, because the cause of the divine will is sought, of which there is none; rather it itself is the first and principal cause of all things. But blessed Jerome says that God did nothing because he wills, but because there is a reason for it to be so done. And therefore it can not incongruously thus he asks why he willed this, and be answered: Because the judgments of God are a great deep. [n: (Psal. XXXV)]. Yet we can say, saving the reverence due to secret things and without the arrogance of assertion, that he chose Jacob rather than Esau for this reason: because he foreknew that greater benefit would come to the good of the universe from the election of Jacob than of Esau. But someone will say: Therefore [0490B] that which is temporal is the cause of that which is eternal, namely the good which he foresaw would then be future is the cause of predestination. To which we say that this is not true; yet the cause looks to that, namely the eternal reason: for that he should choose that from which greater benefit would come to the good of the universe — this reason too was eternal, which looks to that temporal good that would be future from the election of Jacob.

QUESTION CCXXXVI. But again it is asked why greater benefit came from the election of Jacob than of Esau? Solution. Because grace is more commended by the election of Jacob than of Esau. Likewise it is asked why it is more commended by the election of Jacob than of Esau. Solution. Because he was younger in birth. For if the elder-born were chosen, it would seem that this [0490C] was done by the privilege of birth.

QUESTION CCXXXVII. It is asked why Jacob was not the firstborn and Esau the later-born, so that thus at least Esau might be chosen? Solution. This is to ask why Jacob is not Esau, and conversely: and therefore it is a question without reason.

QUESTION CCXXXVIII. From those things which come to pass in time, the same difficulty arises: for it is wont to be asked why God confers grace on this man rather than on that, since they are indifferent, and neither can merit grace, without which he cannot be saved. For it seems that it is not to be imputed to me if I do not do what I cannot do without grace, since grace has not been conferred on me, but rather to him who does not confer on me the necessary [0490D] grace, since he cannot [do so] without harm to himself. Solution. Concerning grace different men think differently. Some said that God cannot do [anything] except what he does: which is certainly not true. Others say: There is a certain seed-bed of virtue (which they call the root of charity) in this man, from which he is apt, so that by grace conferred on him he may merit eternal life: which because it is not in that man, he can merit neither charity nor eternal life. But because this is against the Church, let it be wholly passed over. Others say that God offers his grace to all in common, which he who lays hold of will be saved; he who does not lay hold of will be damned, as if someone had prepared good food for you in a tower, and said: Come up to take the food: but because you cannot go up without a ladder or some help of this [0491A] kind, therefore they say: There is need of another grace, that you may ascend to that higher one. Likewise for laying hold of that, another, and for that another, and so on to infinity. But these attribute the whole to grace, and leave nothing to merit. There are others who say that grace is offered, even by being held out to the man, that he himself may lay hold of it, without which, once laid hold of, he cannot be raised up, just as if a rope be let down to one who is in a pit, without which he cannot get out of the pit; but if he stretch out his hand to the rope and lay hold, he is drawn out, and something is from the man, although merit cannot exist without grace. But because this opinion divides between merit and grace, [holding] that something good is there from the man, at least that he raises his hand and lays hold of the rope, which because it [0491B] cannot be done without grace, this opinion too, together with the aforesaid, is to be guarded against. But it can be said that the grace of God is offered equally to the good and to the evil, that is, to the predestined and to the reprobate, which yet not every one lays hold of or follows as it draws; rather he, into whom the ray of grace is poured, closes his eyes; and thus repels the ray by which he is touched, whence grace is deservedly withdrawn from him, because he withdraws himself. For it is in grace as in the ray of the sun, which presents itself to the eye, by which the eye, being touched, exercises sight. For the eye is of such a nature that through it sight is exercised, if it be struck by the ray of the sun, without which there is no vision in the eye: yet unless it were of such a nature, even when touched by the ray it would not see, as a wall or a stone does not see, even if it be flooded with the ray of the sun; so the soul [0491C] has naturally the power of meriting, which yet it cannot exercise unless it be flooded with the splendor of grace. But when it is touched, it is moved and merits, whence the whole is from grace, yet in such a way that merit is not excluded, just as if a boy who cannot yet walk be led by someone, and does indeed walk, which yet he could not [do] by himself, nor even with another leading, unless he had the natural power of walking: yet the whole is said to be from the one leading, that he walks; so for this, that the soul may merit, two things are required, both grace and natural power: yet the whole authorship of meriting belongs to grace alone, because natural power posits nothing without grace.

QUESTION CCXXXIX. *For to this same purpose have I raised [0491D] thee, that I may show in thee my power,* etc. It is asked whether God inclines the mind of a man to this, that the man be made worse? Solution. Pharaoh, in that blinding, was not made worse than he was before; but through the signs set forth outwardly, the malice which had before been conceived in the mind was stirred up, and broke forth into deed, just as the envy of the Jews broke forth when the Lord entered Jerusalem with such great glory, and brought to ripeness the crime already conceived. Or for God to stir up is nothing other than to permit [one] to be cast headlong by a just judgment: concerning this question it has been said more carefully in the foregoing.

QUESTION CCXL. *On whom he will he has mercy, and whom he will he hardens.* It is asked whether the will of God, as it is the cause of mercy, is likewise the cause of hardening: which seems [so], [0492A] since the Apostle says: On whom he will, etc. Solution. The will of God is not the cause except of that which is something; hardening does not posit something, but rather removes [it]; yet [the Apostle says this] so that he may show that God can justly give to whom he will, and not give to whom he wills not to give: for God does not act by necessity, but by will alone. But that it is the cause of good is certain, whence the Apostle above: *It is not of him that wills, nor of him that runs, but of God that shows mercy [n: (ibid.)]*.

QUESTION CCXLI. But it can be asked how willing is not of him that wills, since no one can [will] this without will. Solution. The good will is both man's, but as of one receiving; and God's, but as of one giving and [as] author.

QUESTION CCXLII. *Has not the potter power [0492B] over the clay,* etc. It is asked: to what end this likeness is brought in; does God form *one vessel unto honor, another unto dishonor?* Or does the dishonorable have its being from God? Solution. That one vessel is unto honor is from God; but that it is dishonorable is from the vessel itself. Yet the likeness is good, because just as the potter makes one vessel unto honor, another unto dishonor: so God is said to form one vessel unto honor, another unto dishonor; or, what is better, God is said to form one unto honor and another unto dishonor for this reason: because, God being author, even those things which are vessels of wrath or of dishonor have their being, yet the vessels of dishonor do not have their being from God, but from their own vice.

QUESTION CCXLIII. It is asked what it is to be written in the book [0492C] of life according to foreknowledge. Solution. To be foreknown and foreordained to life, just as to be written according to justice is to be in a state in which, if he departed from this life, he would be saved. There are therefore some written according to foreknowledge and not according to justice, as those who do not yet have virtue, yet are foreordained to life. But some are written according to justice and not according to foreknowledge, as those who have charity, yet are foreseen unto death. But some are written according to foreknowledge and according to justice, as those who have charity and are predestined to life. But some neither according to foreknowledge nor according to justice, as those who were never good nor [0492D] are predestined. But those things which are there written according to justice and not according to foreknowledge are said to be blotted out thence; but those which are written there according to foreknowledge are never blotted out thence.

QUESTION CCXLIV. It is asked whether those who are written in the book of life according to merit, and not according to foreknowledge, are written by disposition. Solution. It can be said that they are written by disposition on account of merit, yet not simply according to disposition; because if this were so, they could not be blotted out thence; with such a qualification it can be said [that they are written] by disposition on account of merit, [in the sense] that God disposed [it] as [he does] every good.

QUESTION CCXLV. It is asked how that is to be understood which is said concerning this authority: *The elder shall serve [0493A] the younger [n: (Gen. XXV)]*, this is [said] concerning foreknowledge. Solution. The sense is: by this Scripture it is shown that God was foreknowing of future things.

QUESTION CCXLVI. *But the justice which is of faith,* etc. It is asked what it is for justice to be of faith. Solution. This is for justice to be through grace, because not only does one come to faith from grace, but also after faith grace is necessary, that faith may be fulfilled by good works, whose fulfillment is called justice.

QUESTION CCXLVII. Likewise it is asked how that is to be understood which is said concerning this authority, *Jacob I have loved, Esau I have hated [n: (Malach. I)]*, this is [said] concerning judgment. Solution. By this prophecy it is shown to have been fulfilled, what was in God's foreknowledge. Note, [0493B] that this authority, Jacob I have loved, Esau I have hated, can be expounded concerning the eternal predestination of the one, and the reprobation of the other, or concerning the temporal application of grace, or the withdrawal of the same: whence that gloss, In Jacob he found nothing to be loved except the gift of his mercy, is thus understood, that is, out of grace alone God disposed to confer on Jacob grace in time whereby he would be saved. And that gloss: In Esau nothing to be hated [sic: *odiendium*] except original sin, that is, he foreknew that on account of original sin grace was not to be conferred on Esau, by which he would be saved in time.



## I. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE ROMANS. (cont.)

QUESTION CCXLVIII. Someone says: Why on account of original sin was grace not conferred on Esau, since it was conferred on Jacob, although he had original sin? [0493C] Solution. Because the same grace was assuredly offered, but Esau withdrew himself from grace and closed his eye, while in Jacob grace blotted out sin, because he yielded to grace: he followed it as it drew, God working and disposing all things according to the judgment of his most just will.

QUESTION CCXLIX. *That he might show the riches of [his] glory,* etc. It is asked whether the torments of the evil profit the good in the future life? Solution. They say that they profit, because God would never allow evils to be, unless they conferred some benefit on the good of the universe.

QUESTION CCL. It is asked what benefit comes to the good from this, that they see the evil punished. Do they therefore love or praise God the more; or do they take delight in the punishment of the evil? Solution. They take delight not in the punishment of the evil, but in the justice of God; and their own [0493D] grace shines forth the more by comparison with the evil, especially when they see themselves freed from the same punishments by mercy alone. But someone will say: Although the evil were not punished, would not the good know that they are freed from the same punishments as now? Solution. Perhaps not so effectively, and in this very thing they would love less. Likewise, if none were to be damned, would not God requite the good according to their merits? And thus it seems, although all were saved, it would be no less good than now — rather it would be more good; because the good would rejoice more over their salvation then, than they now do over their damnation. Likewise the good in the future either will have compassion on the evil, or will not have compassion; if they have compassion, how [are they] blessed: if they do not have compassion, [they are] cruel. Solution. [0494A] Augustine resolves this question thus: It was more good that there be goods and evils, than only goods, so that God might be praised more wonderfully from that very diversity.

QUESTION CCLI. *Following after the law of justice,* etc. Since the law does not justify, it is asked how it is called the law of justice. Solution. Because it is a certain preparation for justice, therefore it is called the law of justice.

QUESTION CCLII. *They stumbled at the stone of stumbling* — concerning which, because the Lord [says] in the Gospel: *Whoever shall fall upon this stone shall be broken; but upon whomever it shall fall, it will grind him to powder [n: (Matth. XXI)]*, it is asked what it is to fall upon the stone. Solution. To offend God imprudently, just as to be ground by the stone [is] to be eternally punished by Christ: whence Christ is called the rock of scandal and the stone of stumbling, [0494B] because [being] humble, [he was] therefore apt for the proud to stumble against.

QUESTION CCLIII. *I bear witness to them, that they have a zeal, or emulation, of God,* etc. [n: (Rom. X.)] It is asked whether that zeal is good, and what it is, and what its effect? That it is good is clear from this, that the Apostle says this to the commendation of the Jews, and the expositors also say that it is the love of God: but if this were so, they would seem to have something that were worthy of eternal life; but were they not worthy of death, who killed Christ? How then did they have the zeal of God, that is, love? Likewise, if it was good, it had a good effect, but was not its effect that which they did out of that zeal? But this was mortal [0494C] sin, namely the persecution of the martyrs and the killing of Christ: how then did the good produce evil? Solution. That good zeal was an affection toward God, by which they were ready to do what their conscience dictated must be done for God's sake: yet it was not so great as to make them worthy of eternal life, nor ought it to be called charity, nor simply the love of God: yet if it is sometimes called the love of God, this is for the reason that they had it toward God: but its effect was not the persecution of the martyrs, or the death of Christ, but the avoidance of contempt of God. For they were placed in such a strait that whether they killed Christ or not, they would sin mortally: yet not [that], if they did not kill Christ, they would sin in this very thing; but because by not killing [him] they would despise God: for often by one [0494D] sin another is avoided.

QUESTION CCXLIV. Likewise, by killing Christ they were not acting against conscience; rather [they did] that which they believed must be done for God's sake: how then did they sin? Solution. Although they were not acting against conscience, yet they sinned mortally, because they were blinded: for they did what their conscience ought to have dictated to them must not be done.

QUESTION CCLV. *Wishing to establish their own justice,* etc. It is asked how the justice of the law is called the Jews'. Solution. Because in this, that they believed they fulfilled it by their own powers, they made [it] their own justice, but not according to knowledge, that is, they did not exercise that good affection as it [0495A] was to be exercised by the judgment of reason, and in this they erred.

QUESTION CCLVI. *Christ is the end of the law.* It is asked how Christ is the end and consummation of the law, since the justice of the law is without assisting grace, and they had no merit with God. Solution. Christ is not called the end or consummation of the law according to that by which it was kept by the Jews, but because he spiritually fulfills it in himself and in his own.

QUESTION CCLVII. *But the justice which is of faith speaks thus: Say not,* etc. *In thy heart, Who shall ascend into heaven?* etc. It is asked how the Apostle brought in this authority: for it is taken from Deuteronomy, where Moses uses it in another sense: for he forbade the Jews to say, Who has ascended into heaven, [0495B] to bring us the law, or who has crossed the sea to carry the law over, or who has descended into hell to bring it forth to us, because the word is near, in thy heart, that is, to have the law in the present. Solution. It is fittingly brought in, although the Apostle does not here express through it the literal sense of Moses: for it can come to pass that, just as to the Jews that was literally a sin, so in it there is figuratively a precept to us, that we not ask, Who has ascended, etc.; whence the Apostle brings in the words of Moses under such a sense, [and] fittingly also brings in Moses as speaking about it.

QUESTION CCLVIII. But someone will say: If Moses speaks of the justice of faith in the law, it seems that the justice of faith was in the law, and thus the law truly justified. Solution. It does not follow, if in the law there is speech about the justice [0495C] of faith, that it was therefore in the law, nor is it even to be admitted that Moses speaks of the justice of faith, unless "to speak" be taken for "to signify." For by saying this he figures the justice of faith [as] near, that is, not far from the nature of minds; because, as the gloss says, it is agreeable to reason to believe.

QUESTION CCLIX. *The word is near, in thy mouth and in thy heart.* It is asked how this is true. Do we not believe many things which reason did not provide for, whence it is written: *Faith has no merit, for which human reason furnishes proof.* Solution. If anyone consider how all things concerning Christ were foretold beforehand in the law and the prophets, and how, [being] fulfilled, they were approved by signs and prodigies; how true, how holy and pious, and how powerful in deed and word [0495D] he himself was in his own person, what remains, except that he proclaim: *Thy testimonies are become exceedingly credible? [n: (Psal. XCII.)]* And reason is in no way permitted to doubt even a little: or [it is] for this reason called agreeable to reason; because to speak and to believe concerning that which pertains to the salvation of the soul pleases reason itself, rather it desires this above all things.

QUESTION CCLX. *With the heart one believes unto justice.* The gloss says that a man can [do] the rest unwilling; but he cannot believe except willing. But how is this true? Can no one hope and love except willing? How then does it say, a man can [do] the rest even unwilling, since these are other than to believe? Solution. By "the rest" it understands external things. But again it seems [0496A] false that anyone does external things unwilling: for if he in no way willed, how would he do [them]? Solution. "To will" is put for "to approve": for we often do many things which we do not approve.

QUESTION CCLXI. *For everyone, whosoever shall call upon.* It is asked how this is true, since many call upon, and yet are not saved, whence the Lord says: *Not everyone who says, Lord, Lord, shall enter into the kingdom of heaven [n: (Matth. VII)]*. Is not to say this, to call upon the name of the Lord? Solution. To call upon is to call inwardly, that is, unto the honor of God and for God's sake, or to desire knowledge [of him], which is not done without faith, hope, and charity.

QUESTION CCLXII. *They cannot believe, because Isaiah had foretold,* etc. It is asked concerning this prophecy, whether [0496B] the prophet willed that what he said be fulfilled? For if he willed [it], he seems to have willed that the blinding of the Jews come to pass: for in the blinding of the Jews the prophecy was fulfilled. Solution. It is not to be conceded that he willed the Jews to be blinded, although he willed his prophecy to be fulfilled, just as Christ willed to be true what he said to Peter: *Before the cock crow, thou shalt deny me thrice [n: (Matth. XXVI)]*: yet he did not will Peter to deny [him]: for many things are so joined that one cannot come to pass without the other, yet we can will one without the other.

QUESTION CCLXIII. *Lord, who has believed our report,* etc. It is read in the Gospel concerning the Jews, who *believed not, that the word of Isaiah might be fulfilled [n: (Joan. XII)]*: whence they object thus: What fault of the Jews [is it], that they [0496C] believed not, since it was necessary that the prophecy be fulfilled, and thus it was necessary that they not believe? Solution. God foretold through the prophet the sins of the Jews, but did not do [them]: for the foreknowledge of them does not impose necessity on them, according to that [saying]: so that when it is said, they believed not, that the word, etc., it only notes that that prophecy was fulfilled in the blindness of the Jews, and that it was foretold before [it was] fulfilled.

QUESTION CCLXIV. It is asked how the Jews sinned by not believing in Christ, since it is written: *Therefore they could believe* not because *Isaiah said: God has blinded their eyes, and hardened [their] heart [n: (ibid.)]*, or if they sinned by not believing in Christ, therefore they could believe, and did not believe: and if this is so, [0496D] how is this true: Therefore they could not believe because God blinded [them], etc. How [is it] at once true [that] they could believe and could not believe? Likewise he seems to place the cause of their unbelief with God, saying: Therefore they could not, because Isaiah said: God blinded [their] eyes, etc. Solution. It is true that they could not believe, as the Gospel says, and yet by not believing they sinned; because they came to that impossibility from the vice of their own will, and therefore such impossibility has no excuse. Both therefore are true, that they could not believe and that they could believe, but not in the same respect: they could [believe] by nature, and could not by fault: for they were so made, that they had the possibility not only of believing, but even of seeing God [0497A] face to face: and therefore it is said that they could believe by nature; but because through fault they were made such, that it was just with God not to have mercy on them, therefore it is said that by fault they could not believe. A like expression is had concerning the good angels: [namely] that they are mutable by nature, immutable by grace; but this which is said, God blinded [them], etc., is to be understood thus, that is, he permitted [them] to be blinded, not by imparting malice by which they would be made worse, but by not conferring grace by which they would be made better.



## I. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE ROMANS. (cont.)

It is to be noted [regarding] that which is said, They could not believe, because they were unwilling: this exposition seems to be too general; for the same can be said of all who are in sins unto death, whence according to this exposition nothing special is said of the blinded. [0497B] Solution. It can be said that the blinded merit that they be unwilling to believe, and that God not have mercy on them: which not all who sin mortally do. Likewise that which is added: *While [they are] proud,* etc., seems to be said too generally and simply: for this too can be said generally of all. Solution. Because "while" too is to be taken causally: for through pride and the other vices they merit that it be unjust for God to have mercy on them.

QUESTION CCLXV. It is asked how this prophecy is brought in: was he not speaking to those who were of the present state, and yet the prophecy was not yet fulfilled, and if this is so, how by this authority did he reprove those who then were, concerning that which was still future, how did it pertain to those then present? [0497C] For this seems to be the force of the proof, that all who were in the world heard the word of Christ, and therefore are to be reproved, as hearing and not believing. Likewise, if the prophecy was not yet fulfilled, how can he use a verb of past time in the sense of present time? for if it be taken in the sense of future time, the Apostle's proof seems to be ridiculous. Solution. He was speaking to those present, but not on account of those present only; rather he brought in this prophecy also on account of all of the future state, and he well reproves those present of that time, because although in their time the prophecy was not fulfilled, yet there was begun in them what the prophecy had foretold would be future. Note that certain Scriptures seem to mean that Israel [0497D] did not know [him]; others seem to mean that it knew Christ, which seem to be contrary: but they are not, because they are understood of different [persons]. For in that people there were some knowing, others not knowing.

QUESTION CCLXVI. *But if of grace, then not now of works,* etc. [n: (Rom XI)]. It is asked, if the whole is of grace, what [is] of merits, or if [there is] something of merits, how [is] the whole of grace? Solution. The whole is of grace, although something is of merits, because the merits themselves are of grace. Yet it seems that justice is from preceding merits, as in Cornelius, whose prayer obtained this, that he be converted by the preaching of Peter, [and] that he receive faith: which faith is of grace, wherefore grace was obtained from works. Solution. It is not to be said [0498A] that grace or justice is from works; because this would be, that someone be justified from works without grace: which cannot be. But that Cornelius prayed, that he might receive faith, this was from preceding grace: for which also a greater grace, namely justifying grace, was conferred. For he was not without all grace, when he believed in one God and, as the doctors say, had the faith of the incarnation, but did not yet know the Word of God [to be] incarnate, which afterward he learned by the preaching of Peter. Note that compunction is of one kind, of envy, by which one is pricked and grieves, and pines away at another's goods; of another kind, of fault, by which one is tormented in his own conscience, as Judas, who hanged himself with a noose for his crime. Of another kind is the compunction of grace, which is either of beginners, who repent of evils, [0498B] that they may abstain, or of the perfect, who fear God out of love.

QUESTION CCLXVII. This which it sought, Israel did not obtain. It is asked how this is true: did it not seek external justice, but obtained that: therefore what it sought, this it obtained. Solution. This is said for the reason that they lacked the end for which they were acting: for they wished to be reputed just with God, which did not befall them.

QUESTION CCLXVIII. *That they hear not, unto this present day.* That the Jews of this time are blinded, this we plainly hold, and the saints in many places say the same, establishing the persecution of Christ as the cause of the blinding, and still more that they killed James the Just. But it does not seem agreeable to reason, [0498C] that the present Jews should be punished for this, since the crimes which their fathers committed displease many [of them]. Solution. Not undeservedly are the modern Jews too blinded, because if the power were given them, what their fathers did to Christ, the same they would do also to the members of Christ, even if it could be done to the Head himself.

QUESTION CCLXIX. *I will honor my ministry,* etc. It is asked what it is to honor one's office. Solution. He honors his ministry who expends something beyond what he owes from his office, as Paul, who from his office ought only to preach to the Gentiles, did more, by preaching also to the Jews. But since Paul believed that some of the Jews could be converted to the faith by his preaching, and knew that this must be done [0498D] for God's sake, if he did not do [it], he would sin: wherefore he ought to do this — how then does he say that he honors his office, since he owed also that which is superadded? Solution. The debt of office is one thing, that of occasion another: beyond the debt of office Paul preached to the Jews, and thus honored his ministry; yet he owed to preach to them as regards the debt of occasion, since he knew it must be done for God's sake.

QUESTION CCLXX. *For if the loss of them be the reconciliation of the world,* etc. It is asked how the unbelief of the Jews and [their] blinding was the cause of the salvation of the Gentiles, would the Gentiles not be saved, unless the Jews were blinded? Could not God save the Gentiles otherwise? Solution. Their unbelief was not the efficient cause why the Gentiles should be saved, yet from the fact that [0499A] those blinded ones repelled the word of preaching, the Apostles took occasion of preaching to the Gentiles; and thus the salvation of the Gentiles followed, God fulfilling his disposition through evils, from which he draws out a good effect.

QUESTION CCLXXI. *If the firstfruit be holy, so is the lump; and if the root be holy, so are the branches,* etc. It is asked concerning this consequence, whether it is true? Cannot the antecedent be true without the consequent, how then is the consequence true? Solution. The consequent of each hypothetical, although it be uttered simply, is nevertheless not to be understood simply, but with such a qualification, "naturally": for although the branches be broken, yet they are naturally good: and those few who were converted to the faith, if they were so constant, it is plain that those who will be converted at the end will be constant.

[0499B]QUESTION CCLXXII. But since the nation of the Jews is often called in the Scriptures most hard, how is it here called naturally holy, as though having a fitness for holiness? Solution. The Apostle treats of the holiness by which is understood the inconstancy of faith; not of the fitness which they would have for holiness.

QUESTION CCLXXIII. It can be asked what the firstfruit is? It is answered. The taste of a small part of some thing, for the testing of the whole lump. But the lump is the multitude of those to be converted after the death of Antichrist. The Jews are called natural branches; because [they were] born of the patriarchs, in whom was the origin of faith, and of whom Christ was born according to the flesh. Note that "nature" is called the accustomed course of nature, against which God often works. Sometimes also the divine [0499C] disposition is called "nature," against which God does nothing; but does all things according to it.

QUESTION CCLXXIV. *For without repentance, that is, without change, are the gift and calling of God.* God sometimes changes his sentence, but not his counsel. What is it to change a sentence? To do something which he did not seem about to do; or not to do something which he seemed about to do.

QUESTION CCLXXV. The expositor says that the evil, while they act against the will of God, [in that very act] the will of God is fulfilled by them: but if the will of God is fulfilled by them, do they not themselves fulfill the will of God? and if this is so, how do they act against the will of God? Solution. "Will" is said in two ways: the precept [0499D] of God, and his disposition. While therefore they act against the precept of God, they do what God disposed to be done: for often good things are done through the evil; but always from the evils which the evil do, God draws out some good.

QUESTION CCLXXVI. *He who hears the Father comes to the Son.* For no one comes to the Son, unless the Father draw him [n: (Joan. VI)]. It is asked, then, what it is for the Father to draw to the Son? Solution. From the disposition which is from the Father, to come to the knowledge of the Son, that one may be saved by him: the Father draws to the Son, when the Father reveals the Son to be equal to himself.

QUESTION CCLXXVII. *God has shut up all things under sin, that he may have mercy on all.* It is asked how this is to be understood: is not to shut up under sin an evil? how does God do this, or how [0500A] is this the cause of good? Solution. God shut up all things in sin, that is, permitted [them] to be shut up, and this is the occasion of good, not the efficient cause.

QUESTION CCLXXVIII. It is asked whether God worked our salvation in this way rather than in another, namely by shutting up all things under sin? Solution. It so pleased God, that seeing themselves unable to be justified, they might be humbled, and seek grace, and thus ascribe the whole to God. *O the depth of the riches of the wisdom and knowledge of God,* etc. Here a threefold kind of causes is wont to be distinguished. Some are formal, some judicial, some final; some according to which, others through which, others on account of which things come to pass. Through judgments, judicial causes; through counsels, final causes; through which [they are] formal, or through wisdom and knowledge, formal.

[0500B]QUESTION CCLXXIX. Concerning the foregoing exclamation it is wont to be asked. For the Apostle seems to exclaim less advisedly concerning the blinding of the Jews and the entry of the Gentiles, saying: O the depth of the riches of the wisdom and knowledge of God! since he himself knew how to render a cause for this, whence he says: *I would not have you ignorant of the mystery [n: (ibid.)]*, etc., and afterward adds: *God has shut up all things under sin, that he may have mercy on all [n: (ibid.)]*. But if he exclaims for this reason, that under this [matter] human understanding does not suffice to render a reason perfectly, by like reasoning would he have had to exclaim concerning many other things? Solution. He exclaims concerning that rather than concerning another, because there one more quickly errs in rendering reasons, and sins unto death.

QUESTION CCLXXX. *For from him, and through him, [0500C] and in him are all things.* It is asked whether sins, insofar as they are, are from God: for if all things [are], then sins [are], or sins are not of the number of all things. Solution. Some say that sins, insofar as they are, have their being from God. But Augustine, in *On the Nature of the Good*, says that only those things which are naturally ought to be understood, when it is said that all things are from God, not sins, which corrupt nature; therefore sins are not from God in any way.

QUESTION CCLXXXI. It is asked whether all things which have their being from God ought to be said to be of God. Solution. Only those things which have their being of the substance of God ought to be said [to be] of God, as the Son and the Holy Spirit are from God the Father in such a way that [they are] of him, because they are of his essence, [0500D] consubstantial with him. But creatures are from God, not of the substance of God, but out of nothing. Note that by the threefold preposition, namely from, through, in, the trinity of persons is here intimated; by the same pronoun the identity of nature is signified, which is whole in each person: for saying, from whom, he understands the Father; through whom, the Son; in whom, the Holy Spirit.



## I. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE ROMANS. (cont.)

QUESTION CCLXXXII. But it is asked how, by this that he says, from whom are all things, he understands the Father, since just as all things are from the Father, so also from the Son and the Holy Spirit? Solution. On account of the authority of the [sic: *principit*], because all things so have their being from the Father that he himself does not have it from another; but the Son and the Holy Spirit, although they are one principle of all creatures, yet have a principle, or author, namely the Father himself, [0501A] from whom they have being: and this very thing, that they are the principle of all, they have from the Father, as all else that they possess. The expositor says that in the Trinity there is the highest origin of all, the most perfect beauty, the most blessed delight, referring origin to the Father, beauty to the Son, delight to the Holy Spirit: and thus these three — origin, beauty, and delight — are determinate, because they are referred, each determinately to each person, as has already been said. But because again the whole Trinity is the highest origin of all things, the most perfect beauty, the most blessed delight, these same three are called infinite. And this is, as I judge, what is said: that the aforesaid three are both determinate in relation to one another, and infinite in themselves.

[0501B]QUESTION CCLXXXIII. Likewise it is said: in the Father unity, in the Son equality, in the Holy Spirit the concord of unity and equality: but since the same unity, and equality, and concord belongs to the three, it is asked why unity is said to be in the Father, and equality in the Son, and concord in the Holy Spirit? Solution. Saving the reverence due to hidden things, we say that unity is said to be in the Father for this reason, that as unity is the principle of numbers, but itself has no being from any other number, so also the Father, since he is the cause of all, has no cause. But equality is said to be in the Son, because in him is the first distinction, and discretion, and the first plurality, and the second personal unity, which, that it may be shown undifferentiated and unequal in nothing from the Father himself, is not unfittingly marked by the name [0501C] of equality, wherein it is declared that the Son so has his being from the Father alone that he is neither the Father, nor diverse from him in nature, but in all things equal and consubstantial with him. But the Holy Spirit is for this reason called the concord of unity and equality, that it may be intimated that the Holy Spirit is so from both that he is equal to both — this I say saving the catholic faith, whose hidden meaning I would rather hear from another than say anything about these matters out of my own slenderness.

QUESTION CCLXXXIV. Likewise it is read: All things are one on account of the Father, all things equal on account of the Son, all things connected on account of the Holy Spirit. It is asked, then, how these words are to be understood? Are not the three persons one by the same unity, or [0501D] equal by equality, or concordant by concord? Solution. All things are one on account of the Father, that is, the three persons are one, having the same natural unity, which is wont to be referred to the Father, though it is common to the three persons: in like manner it is to be understood of equality and concord: for the three persons are equal on account of the ineffable and natural equality which they have, being thus connected. In creatures a vestige of the Trinity shines forth, because they show that they hold in themselves unity, and species, and order, since each single thing is both some one thing, and is formed by some species, and holds some order, whence it is said: *He made all things in number, and weight, and measure [n: (Sap. XI)]*. For number pertains to unity, weight to order, measure to species. For of anything whatever [0502A] it is true that, from the moment it begins to be, it at once falls under number, because it is either one or many. Weight is said to pertain to order for this reason, that single things hold their order according to the nature of weight. For there are two kinds of weights: one which tends downward, as the nature of lead; the other which tends upward, as oil. Likewise order is considered according to place and time: whence also the angels, according to their greater or lesser affections and delights [dilectiones.], obtain their orders; but the species of things, which is a certain mode of them, and measure.

QUESTION CCLXXXV. It is likewise asked whether the Holy Spirit proceeds from himself, or is sent as from the Father and the Son. Solution. The procession, or mission, of the Holy Spirit is twofold, [0502B] eternal and temporal: according to the eternal procession he proceeds only from the Father and the Son, not from himself; temporally he proceeds also from himself, because his temporal procession is the operation of the Trinity. In the same way it is to be understood of the temporal procession or mission of the Son. For just as he sanctified himself for us and did not deliver himself up, so also he sent himself, that is, his own mission or incarnation (which is the same) he wrought. But the eternal procession of the Son from the Father ought to be called, not mission, but generation.

QUESTION CCLXXXVI. It is asked further how the Holy Spirit is the love by which the Father loves the Son, and the Son the Father. Is it not the same for the Father to love, and to be, and by that whereby he has being, also to love? If [0502C] then the Father loves by the Holy Spirit, how does he not have being from the Holy Spirit? Solution. It is not said that the Father loves by the Holy Spirit, but that the Holy Spirit is the love by which the Father loves the Son; because the Holy Spirit is the divine nature, and the Father loves by the divine nature; but if by this term, the love by which the Father loves the Son, a personal property is signified, it is not true that the Holy Spirit is the love by which the Father loves, just as the Holy Spirit is not the Father.

QUESTION CCLXXXVII. Hilary says: As it is impious to preach two gods, the Father and the Son, so to preach a singular God is sacrilegious. Likewise the expositor says, speaking of the Trinity, that it is singularly [0502D] one according to substance, which seems to be contrary. Solution. Singularity sometimes excludes a plurality of persons, and according to this it is sacrilegious to preach the Father and the Son a singular God. Sometimes singularity is put for unity, and thus it is taken when it is said to be singularly one according to substance.

QUESTION CCLXXXVIII. *Not to be more wise than it behooves to be wise,* etc. [n: (Rom. XII.)] It is asked who is wise more than he ought. For no one in this life has so great a knowledge that he could not have a greater, and that he ought not even to desire more: how then does the Apostle command not to be more wise than it behooves to be wise, as though anyone could, by investigating concerning God, comprehend more than is necessary? Solution. The Apostle does not forbid the investigation [0503A] of truth, but lest anyone through excessive investigation fall into doubt of those things which ought to be firmly and undoubtingly believed. Or lest anyone ascribe to himself, by proud presumption, that which he knows, which is to be proud of one's knowledge: for this is not to be wise, but to be foolish.

QUESTION CCLXXXIX. *He who shows mercy, in cheerfulness,* etc. It is asked, then, whether God punishes anyone without mercy, that is, whether he punishes those whom he punishes as much as they deserved to be punished. Which seems so, because *judgment without mercy to him who has not done mercy [n: (Jac. II)]*. But again it is written: *All the ways of the Lord are mercy and truth [n: (Psal. XLII)]*. Whence it seems that he does not even punish the wicked without mercy, according to that: *Neither did he kindle all his wrath* [0503B] [n: (Psal. LXXVII)], that is, he did not punish them as much as they deserved; because he might justly have punished more if he willed. Solution. God punishes no one without mercy, because he punishes none as much as he deserved, and yet judgment without mercy to him who does not show mercy, because he is not further corrected to this end, that he attain eternal life. And so it is true that, just as to the good God will confer of grace more good in the future than they merited, so to the wicked less evil than they merited, and in both God is just and merciful.

QUESTION CCXC. It is asked why rather, judgment without mercy, etc., he says this of this than of another, since it could be said of any criminal whatever. Solution. Understand it to be said of this one for this reason, that there are many fallen into this error: that they believe it enough for them, in order to [0503C] attain eternal life, to harm none, and to turn away from evil, although they do not bestow their goods on the poor, or show forth works of mercy: which error the Apostle here openly represses.

QUESTION CCXCI. *Rejoice with them that rejoice,* etc. It is asked whether one should rejoice over temporal prosperity, as over the abundance of someone's riches. Solution. Since they are given to the good and to the wicked, and more often to the wicked than to the good, it does not seem that one should rejoice if they are given to anyone; nor grieve, if they are taken away: but God is always to be praised in his gifts.

QUESTION CCXCII. *Weep with them that weep,* etc. It is asked concerning the weeping which the Apostle commanded, whether it is reasonable, since over the adversities which befall the good or the wicked [0503D] one should rather rejoice than grieve? For adversities are either for a greater crown to the good, or for the correction of the wicked. It is read also that they wept imprudently over the death of the martyrs, when they should rather have rejoiced than wept, if the reason of grief admitted it. Solution. One weeping is of piety, another of compassion: that which is of piety is, as it were, of nature, and according to some does not merit; but that which is of compassion, merits. For we ought to have compassion on the infirmities of the brethren, that we may gain those whose salvation we ought to desire, and give our labor that they be saved.

QUESTION CCXCIII. *Rendering to no one evil for evil, and love your enemies.* It is asked how the saints do not render evil for evil, since it is written: [0504A] *The saints cry out: Avenge, O Lord, our blood [n: (Apoc. VI)]*. And that: *The just shall rejoice when he shall see the vengeance [n: (Psal. XXXVII)]*; therefore since equally the good and the wicked wish to be avenged by the Lord, wherein are they distinguished? Solution. The good do not rejoice over the punishment as the wicked do, but over the judgment of God.

QUESTION CCXCIV. It is asked whether Christ observes what the Apostle above commanded to all, that he render evil for evil to none. They wish to prove thus, that Christ does not observe it. The wicked man deserved that evil be rendered to him for evil: unless therefore God render him evil, he will not render him what he deserves. Likewise God renders him something for evil, therefore either good or evil; if not evil, therefore good, and thus the wicked deserved good. Likewise, does not God render punishment for fault, and [0504B] thus evil for evil? Solution.
