Neither a good judge nor God renders evil for evil; because this would be to exercise vengeance with an evil intention: whence it must be diligently guarded against, lest this be inferred in all the aforesaid matters.

QUESTION CCXCV. It is asked whether, from what has been said, the justice by which someone is hanged for theft, and the like, is of the Gospel. Which seems so, since the Church hands him over to the prince, and all justice of the Church ought to be that which is there taught. *Behold, here are two swords [n: (Luc. XXII)]*. But if it is of the Church, as it seems, how can it be denied that the justice of the Gospel is heavier than the justice of the law, since by the law it was commanded, *a tooth for a tooth, an eye for an eye [n: (Matth. V)]*; but here the head of a man is given for a she-goat [0504C]? Solution. This is in no way the justice of the Gospel, that a man be slain for a horse or an ox, nor is this precept found in the whole Gospel, nor does the Church do it, but only permits it.



## I. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE ROMANS. (cont.)

QUESTION CCXCVI. *Providing good things not only in the sight of God, but also in the sight of men,* etc. It is asked what it is to provide good things in the sight of God. Solution. In the secret of the heart, that is, to do those things which conscience dictates ought to be done for God.

QUESTION CCXCVII. But since God requires of us that we provide good things also in the sight of men, how does he divide between this and that? Solution. The sense is: The Apostle commands that we do good things also so circumspectly that the weak be not scandalized, but [0504D] receive a good example of profiting; and in this very thing he represses the proud presumption of certain men, who care nothing about the scandals of the weak, considering only what is lawful, not what is expedient.

QUESTION CCXCVIII. *If thy enemy be hungry, feed him,* etc. In the law it is written: *Thou shalt love thy friend, and shalt hold thy enemy in hatred [n: (ibid.)]*. Which seems to be contrary to that precept: *Love your enemies [n: (ibid.)]*, and to this: If thy enemy be hungry, feed him. Solution. It is not a precept in the law to hold enemies in hatred, but a permission; and it is, according to some, a counsel, not a precept, to love enemies in the Gospel, and thus there is no contrariety. Understanding which, David says: *If I have rendered evil to them that repaid me evils, let me deservedly fall away empty before my enemies [0505A] [n: (Psal. VII)]*.

QUESTION CCXCIX. *There is no power but from God,* etc. [n: (Rom. XIII.)] Concerning the power of the good it is agreed that it is from God; concerning the power of the wicked it is asked whether it is from God. If it is from God, it is good, because God is the author only of good things; but how is the power of the wicked good, since through it evils are done, which power also is often called in Scripture iniquitous and unjust? On the contrary, that the power of the wicked is from God, Scripture testifies: how then is it evil? Solution. The power of the wicked is good, and from God; but nevertheless it is called evil because of the malice of those who abuse it, just as the law indeed is good, but nevertheless an occasion of evil, and the light of the sun, and many like things.

QUESTION CCC. It is asked what he calls power. [0505B] Solution. Some say that it is the ordinance of God, from which some indeed have to be set over others. Others say that it is the very persons constituted in eminence, as kings and princes, who must be obeyed in all things that pertain to their power. But if they enjoin anything which is against God, they are not to be heard.

QUESTION CCCI. It is asked whether the power of sinning is from God. Which is proved thus: The power of Pilate, by which he could crucify the Savior, was from God, as is had in the Gospel [n: (Joan. XIX)]: therefore the power of sinning is from God. Likewise, on the contrary, God, from the fact that he is omnipotent, cannot sin: therefore to be able to sin is not to be able, nor is the power of sinning a power, but an impotence. Solution. The power of sinning is called [0505C] a certain force of doing something which cannot be done without sin, or a certain dignity from which someone can lawfully do that which is not done without sin: as the power of Pilate was indeed good, namely from God, from which he had the power to crucify Christ, just as from royal power someone can exercise tyranny, and under the appearance of ordained power rage against his subjects: yet to crucify Christ, or to exercise tyranny, is not a power, but rather an impotence, which is often called the power of sinning, which is present from a defect, or limit, of good: which also, if it was in the first man before sin.

QUESTION CCCII. *He that loveth his neighbor hath fulfilled the law,* etc. It is asked whether love of neighbor and love [0505D] of God are the same. If they are not the same, how is love of neighbor the fullness of the law? how is the whole law restored in love of neighbor? how are the three precepts of the first table, pertaining to God, fulfilled by love of neighbor? Likewise, if this and that are the same, why was one precept given separately concerning love of God, and another concerning love of neighbor? Solution. Love is the name of a virtue, and thus it can be said that the love by which we love God and neighbor is the same; and it is the name of a motion of the mind, and thus love of God is one thing, love of neighbor another, and love of God is greater, and love of neighbor less, since love of God is contained in love of neighbor: for he who loves God, it follows that he loves his neighbor, and conversely; and therefore the one is put for both [0506A] commandments. For love of neighbor is a motion of the mind toward the neighbor for God's sake: how then can there be love of neighbor without love of God? For love of neighbor is a certain matter in which love of God is exercised, which lies more hidden, whose effect appears in love of neighbor. Love of God is a motion of the mind toward God for his own sake.

QUESTION CCCIII. But since love of God is worthier than love of neighbor, why did the Apostle rather mention love of neighbor than love of God, saying that it is the fulfilling of the law? Solution. Because love of neighbor appears more in daily life and conduct.

QUESTION CCCIV. It is asked whether love of neighbor can be without love of God. Which seems so, because [0506B] someone can love his neighbor not for God's sake, but for something else. Solution. It is not to be conceded that anyone loves his neighbor, unless he love him for God's sake: for otherwise to love is not to love, but rather to hate; because *he that loveth iniquity hateth his own soul [n: (Psal. X)]*; and thus neither does he love another, who hates himself.

QUESTION CCCV. It is likewise asked whether we ought to love one another for the sake of eternal life: which if it be granted, it seems that we do it for our own advantage, and thus we are hirelings. For Ambrose says: he who serves in hope and desire of the heavenly fatherland is a hireling. Solution. The neighbor is to be loved for the sake of eternal life, nor is anything on that account preferred to God, because he himself is eternal life: and in him there is not [0506C] anything outside him besides himself, and therefore it is not fitting to extend the aforesaid words of Ambrose from their place; because they seem to sound against the usage of the Church: yet it seems to intimate this, that if anyone, thinking eternal life to be something apart, besides God, should do good, attending only to his own advantage, not because God willed this to be done, he would have to be called a hireling.

QUESTION CCCVI. It is asked whether all are to be loved equally. Solution. Some say that according to affection all are to be loved equally, but not according to effect, nay rather some more, some less, according to that which is written: *He set in order charity in me [n: (Cant. II)]*. To others it seems that even according to [0506D] affection some are to be preferred to others, as the better to the less good, and parents to strangers. And so charity is ordered according to affection and effect: which can be done in many ways; sometimes the affection ought to be unequal, sometimes the affection equal, and the effect unequal, and conversely.

QUESTION CCCVII. It is asked where the precept, or doctrine, is given, how a man ought to love himself. Solution. When a man is taught how God is to be loved, in this very thing he is taught how a man ought to love himself. For what is it to love oneself, but to love one's own good? but what is the good of man, but God? He therefore who loves God, in this very thing loves himself; and in as much as he loves God, in so much does he love himself.

[0507A]QUESTION CCCVIII. It is asked whether a man ought to love his neighbor as much as himself. Because it is written: *Thou shalt love thy neighbor as thyself [n: (Matth. XXII)]*. Solution. "As" notes a likeness, not a quantity, according to some, when it is said: Love thy neighbor as thyself, that is, unto that unto which thou lovest thyself, that is, unto this, that he may have God, and as much as thou canst, give thy labor that he may do those things whereby he may be saved. But what has already been said above concerning charity need not be repeated. For not all things can, or ought to, be said everywhere.

QUESTION CCCIX. *Now him that is weak in faith,* etc. It is asked how he takes "weak." Solution. He here understands "weak" not according to constancy, but according to the knowledge of faith.

[0507B]QUESTION CCCX. *He that is weak, let him eat herbs. And let every one abound in his own sense.* It is asked how the Apostle persuades that the man who distinguishes foods ought to be left to himself, since it is evil to distinguish foods, and to think this one clean and this one unclean, when he ought rather to be drawn back from such an error, for this seems to concede it to him, since this is to foster him in error. Solution. He permits the lesser evil to be done, that the greater evil may be avoided: for it would be a greater evil to eat against conscience than to abstain from certain foods, when one thinks he must abstain from them: for whoever acts against conscience, sins.

QUESTION CCCXI. It is asked whether he who thus distinguishes foods sins. Does he not live according to the doctrine of the Apostle? if this too is so, how does he sin? Solution. [0507C] The Apostle does not here teach that it is to be done as though it were good, but he shows that it is a lesser evil: for it is a lesser evil to err out of good zeal than manifestly to sin against conscience: wherefore, lest he incur a greater sin, he is rather to be borne with than provoked. But there are certain things which even in the New Testament are forbidden, as that no one eat the blood of animals, or use things strangled. From certain things also we abstain, because it is not necessary to use them, as from horse-flesh, not because it would be as good to eat as beef; but because there is no need. From certain things we abstain, because they are harmful, as the serpent, the toad, and the like: which indeed are venomous, and therefore deadly to those eating them.

[0507D]QUESTION CCCXII. It is asked how they distinguished foods. Did they establish some uncleanness in these more than in those, on account of which they refused to eat them? If this is so, they were casting the fault back upon God through the medium of the creature, and thus sinning unto death. But if they sinned unto death, how does the Apostle bid them be tolerated in their death? Solution. They erred and sinned, but venially, not unto death; for they abstained from certain foods, not because they believed some uncleanness to be in them, but because, being placed under the law, they had been accustomed to abstain from them, and it was grievous for them to lay aside the custom.

QUESTION CCCXIII. It is asked to whom what the Apostle here says pertains. Solution. What he says seems to pertain to prelates, *Now him that is weak receive [take up* ]* [0508A] [n: (Rom. XIV.)] Likewise he seems to say certain things in this chapter which seem to pertain to the weak, as that: *He that eateth not, let him not despise him that eateth; and he that eateth, let him not judge him that eateth [n: (ibid.)]*: for by these words he seems to instruct the weak.



## I. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE ROMANS. (cont.)

QUESTION CCCXIV. *To his own Lord he standeth or falleth.* The Apostle seems, when he says this, to take away from us all judgment concerning the good: for, since a man is good from intention and charity alone, which do not lie under human judgment, how can we judge anyone to be good? Solution. The Church judges only of manifest things, and not of hidden things: for it does not judge that charity is in someone, because then it would judge that to be which it did not know to be, but that the signs of charity, which are in him, show charity to be in him. Likewise, [0508B] when it judges or condemns anyone, it does not judge that he is guilty of the crime of which he is accused, but that the signs concur in his accusation, on account of which it gives a just sentence against him. If it be objected that the witnesses are false by whom he is convicted, and the accusation false, because he is not held guilty of the crime imputed to him, we say that the accusation is true and the witnesses true, even if he is not guilty of that crime; because the accusation is called true, that is, irreproachable, when it is made in judicial order. Likewise we say that the Church condemns the guilty, although the crime be not in him of which he is accused, because we take him as guilty there when convicted in judicial order.

QUESTION CCCXV. Doubtful things are to be turned to the better part, as the expositor says. It is asked, then, how [0508C] we ought to turn ambiguous things to the better part: for since I am equally in doubt about a good thing, whether it be good, as about an evil thing, whether it be evil, I seem to act no less indiscreetly in judging it to be good than in judging it to be evil; but since neither is defined and certain to me, I ought to define neither: how then ought we to understand what is said, to turn ambiguous things to the better part? Solution. To believe, to judge, or to turn, is here taken for to wish: for this the Apostle counsels, that when we see ambiguous things being done, we wish them to be done with good intention, and not with evil: for with what intention they are done, since this cannot be known by us, we ought not to judge what we cannot know.

[0508D]QUESTION CCCXVI. Likewise the expositor says: He who thinks evil to be evil is deceived; but he who thinks evil to be good is not deceived: is not evil's being evil true? and evil's being good false? How then is he who thinks that which is true deceived, and he who thinks that which is false not deceived? Solution. Without prejudice to a better opinion, we say that this is to be so understood: He who thinks evil to be evil is deceived, is slowed in his course, because he despises and spurns it, or at least loves it less. But he who thinks evil to be good is not deceived, that is, in the way of conduct he is not offended, nor slowed, because in that he believes it to be good, he loves it the more, and thus in a certain manner profits by erring. It is to be noted that there are certain things manifestly good, certain things manifestly evil, [0509A] concerning which it is lawful to judge, even if we do not know with what mind they are done. But there are also middle things, which can be good and evil, concerning which to judge is perilous, and forbidden by the Lord: and these we ought to turn to the better part, as the blessed Jerome says: If you see a priest raising his hand over a woman, say that he does this to bless her. Which authority is to be understood according to the exposition above. Of manifest goods, however, we can rarely judge, since a just man can do almost nothing which a wicked man does not also do, as miracles, signs, and the like. He fasts, prays, and the rest of such things, which are signs of good. *Let every one abound in his own sense [n: (ibid.)]*, that is, let him be permitted in his own conscience, if it is not unto death, lest, called back from it, he sin more grievously.

[0509B]QUESTION CCCXVII. *We shall all stand before the judgment seat of Christ,* etc. It is asked how the Apostle says: We shall all stand, since elsewhere it is read written: *You shall sit upon twelve seats, judging the twelve tribes of Israel [n: (Matth. XIX)]*. If the saints will sit in the hour of judgment, how will they stand? or if they will stand, how will they sit? Solution. They are said to stand out of reverence for the supreme judge, they are said to sit as being themselves also about to judge.

QUESTION CCCXVIII. *Nothing is common but to him who esteemeth any thing to be common.* The Apostle seems to intimate that the amount of guilt is to be assigned according to the amount and quality of conscience, so that if anyone believe a venial sin to be mortal, committing it against conscience, he sins unto death: even [0509C] in lifting a straw from the ground, if he lift it while conscience dictates this to be a sin unto death. And thus from this it seems it can be held that every sin which is against conscience is unto death. For whoever acts against his conscience, even in committing a venial sin, seems to despise God; because he prefers himself to God. Solution. In a venial sin which is against conscience, some sin unto death, others venially: for if anyone commit a venial sin against conscience thus: that he believe himself to be eternally punished if he commit it, he sins unto death, and despises God. But if from some infirmity, his conscience dictating to him that he is not to be damned for it, although he go forth with it, he sins venially; nor does he [0509D] prefer himself to God, nor despise God. If anyone object that he sins against conscience, and thus the committed sin is pleasing, and thus he sins unto death; because he sins unto death, authority attesting: *Every one to whom sin is pleasing,* etc. Solution. "To please" is taken for "to bring into use": and whoever brings any venial sin into use is guilty of death, as Augustine says.

QUESTION CCCXIX. *It is good for a man not to eat flesh, and not to drink wine,* etc. Jerome against Jovinian uses this argument: It is good not to drink wine, therefore it is evil to drink; and if it is evil to drink wine, everyone sins who drinks wine, and the sentence of the Apostle seems false: *I know, and am confident in the Lord Jesus, that nothing is common through him [n: (Rom. XIV)]*. Solution. To drink wine according to [0510A] Jerome is called evil by occasion, because from the drinking of wine luxury easily is born: and the sense of the argumentation is: if by abstinence from wine evil is avoided, from the drinking of wine an occasion of evil is easily taken. The Apostle says therefore: It is good for a man not to eat, etc., that is, it is good, by abstinence from flesh, or wine, or any other food, to avoid that the neighbor be offended. Likewise the Apostle could have said the same of beans, and peas, and the like.

QUESTION CCCXX. *All that is not of faith is sin.* It is asked how faith is here taken, whether for the judgment of conscience, or for the catholic faith. Therefore the questions are to be considered according to each opinion. All then that is done against faith, that is, the judgment of conscience, is sin. [0510B] The judgment of conscience is that whereby we believe something is to be done for God's sake, against which it is not to be done, as if anyone believe even that a man is to be killed if he can: and if he do it not, he sins even unto death, as some say. But to others it seems not to be unto death, unless he himself, who believes this is to be done for God's sake, thinks that he sins mortally, and does it not.

QUESTION CCCXXI. It is asked, therefore, since this man with this intention kills a just man, since he ought to do this, whether in doing this he merits? For whoever does for God's sake what he thinks is to be done for God's sake, merits, but this man does for God's sake what he believes is to be done for God's sake: therefore from this he merits. But that he ought to do this is established from this; that if he leave it undone, he sins mortally. [0510C] Solution. Some plausibly say that here he has put himself in a strait, and so whether he go forward or draw back, he falls: as if someone see fire before him, and feel a precipice of water behind, he incurs peril on either side. But to others it seems that there are two things here, error and zeal: and that which is of error is evil; but that which is of zeal is good, and has merit. For there is a good will, but that in which it is exercised is evil; in this, then, he sins, in that he merits.

QUESTION CCCXXII. All the life of unbelievers is sin, as the expositor says. But do not even unbelievers do certain good things, as to sow fields, to build houses, to feed parents: which, although they are not worthy of eternal life, yet are laudable, and in no way [0510D] sins: how then is all the life of unbelievers sin? Solution. He calls unbelievers those acting against faith, not those not having faith.

QUESTION CCCXXIII. Now let us pursue the other part of the question according as faith is taken for the catholic faith. The Apostle therefore, saying: *All that is not of faith is sin [n: (Act. X)]*, seems to hold that no one not having faith does anything which profits him: which seems not to be true, since Cornelius, not yet having faith, praying, was heard, and elsewhere it is written: *They who faithfully administer the affair of Babylon will deserve at some time to be freed from Babylon.* Solution. The Apostle intends to say this, that nothing profits anyone not having faith toward attaining eternal life, unless he come to faith: [0511A] nay, according to some, all good things also are harmful to such a one; because good things mingled with evils are wont to harm, and the more one abuses virtues, the more he offends. Whence Augustine: The least goods are better than the greatest, when they are mingled with evils.

QUESTION CCCXXIV. It is asked here, since all that is not of faith is sin, whether a Jew sins in saying that Christ is God, when he says this under compulsion: for in saying this he seems to lie, since he speaks against conscience; whence he seems to sin unto death in saying it. Solution. It does not seem to sound well to the ears of the faithful, that a Jew in saying that Christ is God sins unto death; nor do we say this, though some seem to assert it; because to lie is to speak that which the judgment of conscience must dictate [0511B] ought not to be said. Or to lie is properly to speak what is false with the intention of deceiving: whence where there is no falsehood, neither is there a lie, yet someone can be guilty of a lie even in speaking the truth. No virtue is true, save that which is formed by the recognition of eternal truth; not even in the best morals, as appears in the philosophers, who flourished in the best morals, yet shone with a false virtue, because they lacked the knowledge of eternal truth.

QUESTION CCCXXV. *For Christ did not please himself. [n: (Rom. XV.)]* It is asked how this is to be understood. Is not the will of the Father and of the Son himself one? how then could he please the Father, and not himself? But that he pleased the Father in all things, there is no one who doubts. Solution. He did not please himself according to [0511C] the infirmity of the flesh, whose affliction he did not refuse, according to what he says elsewhere: *I came not to do my own will [n: (Joan. VI)]*, that is, he came not to fulfill that affection which he had from the nature of the flesh, but he subjected it to the command of reason, which reason in all things obeyed the divine will.

QUESTION CCCXXVI. *But the gentiles, for mercy,* etc. God in a certain manner bound himself by promise to the Jews; but to the gentiles he passed over by mercy alone: yet he did both of grace, both that he promised himself to those, and that he passed over to these. Yet it is said, on account of the things promised to the Jews, truth; but to the gentiles, mercy, because by grace alone and not by the promise of God he came to them, and took them up.



## I. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE ROMANS. (cont.)

[0511D]QUESTION CCCXXVII. But was not a promise made to the gentiles, since the Lord says in Hosea: *I will call my people that which is not my people [n: (Osee II)]*. Likewise Isaiah: *Rejoice, ye gentiles, with his people [n: (Rom. XV)]*. Likewise: *Rejoice, thou barren, that bearest not [n: (Gal. IV)]*, etc. Solution. A promise was not made to the gentiles, in such a way that the promise was directed to them; for no writings were given to them concerning the promise itself. Or a promise was not made to them, that Christ would come to them, and preach in his own person: for he came only to the Jews, and preached to them. Whence the Apostle says that he was a minister of the circumcision.

QUESTION CCCXXVIII. *There shall be a root of Jesse, and he that shall rise up to rule the gentiles,* etc. It is asked why he is called a root of Jesse. Solution. Because from him proceeded David, [0512A] who was constituted king, and by his power protected many, as a tree is wont to protect by its roots, or by its branches and leaves.

QUESTION CCCXXIX. It is asked also what sort of prophecy this was, since Isaiah began to be after the death of Jesse? For does prophecy use the future for the past, as conversely it often does? Solution. This prophecy is of the future, and the sense is: There shall be a root of Jesse, etc., that is, Jesse shall be called a root, and from that root there shall be one who shall rise up, namely Christ.

QUESTION CCCXXX. *I will set out through you into Spain,* etc. It is asked whether the Apostle was lying, when he said that through Rome he would go into Spain, and it is agreed that then, when he said this, he did not do it. For neither did he then come to Rome. Into Spain perhaps, [0512B] as Jerome thinks, he was carried over by ships: whence it is said, The sea shall be despoiled: which Jerome records was said of Paul alone: for he despoiled the devil of the Spaniards surrounded by the sea, by converting them to Christ. Solution. To lie is, as Gregory says, to speak what is false with the intention of deceiving: which, because Paul did not do, neither do we say that he lied. For he said this because he hoped that he could set out: yet the words which he spoke signified what was false; yet according to intention he did not lie: for he had so disposed to do, because he believed it would be so in fact.

QUESTION CCCXXXI. *According to the revelation of the mystery kept silent from eternal times,* etc. [n: (Rom. XVI.)] [0512C] It is asked how he calls them eternal times, saying, from eternal times. Solution. He calls eternal times all that which preceded the creation of the world from the creation of the angels, as some hold. In which times there was succession, yet not decession. Therefore in a wondrous manner there was there a certain immutability, and thus a certain eternity; and a certain mutability, and thus a certain temporality. Yet almost all say that the angels were not created before the creation of the world. Therefore perhaps he calls them eternal times, as if he said that the mystery was kept silent, and hidden from the origin of the world.

QUESTION CCCXXXII. *To the only wise God,* etc. It is asked whether by this name God is here understood the Trinity, or the person of the Father. Solution. Augustine says [0512D] that this is a name of the Trinity; according to some it is a name of the Father: but according to this it seems that the Father alone is wise, which is against faith; and so it must be known that "alone" is sometimes exclusive of a person, sometimes of nature only.

QUESTION CCCXXXIII. But when it is said, God the Trinity, wisdom, wise, it is asked by what wisdom, whether by the unbegotten, which is the Father; or by the begotten, which is the Son; or by the wisdom proceeding from the Father and the Son, which is the Holy Spirit; or by a wisdom which is neither unbegotten nor begotten, nor proceeding. Solution. This name Trinity signifies as much as the three persons, or the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit: and the Father indeed is wise by the unbegotten wisdom; the Son by the begotten [0513A] wisdom; the Holy Spirit by the wisdom proceeding from the Father, and the Son. And the unbegotten wisdom, and the begotten wisdom, [0514A] and the proceeding, are not three wisdoms, but one wisdom naturally.



## II. ON THE FIRST EPISTLE TO THE CORINTHIANS.

[0513]

[0513A]QUESTION I. *Paul, called to be an Apostle,* etc. [n: (I Cor. I.)] This Epistle, which is addressed to the Corinthians, was not written by the Apostle in the second place, as in the body of the epistles it is arranged in the second place. For both epistles sent to the Corinthians were written before [0513B] that which is to the Romans. Which is clear from this, that in the Epistle to the Romans he says that he will set out for Rome after he has assigned the collection made by the Achaeans for the poor who are at Jerusalem. Of which collection, to be made, he makes mention in these. But it is not without a rational cause that that to the Romans is set before the others in the body of the epistles, but on account of the dignity of the Romans. Or rather because in it first is taken away and destroyed that vice which is pride. But the Corinthians, converted to the faith by the Apostle, but afterward in manifold ways subverted and seduced by false apostles, were erring in the virtues of the sacraments, and especially in the virtue of baptism, thinking that, when conferred by an evil man, it had no virtue, a greater from a better, a lesser from a less good. [0513C] They also thought little of the Apostle, and held him in contempt: he who had not come to them in the words of human wisdom, but in the simplicity of faith for the instruction of the little ones. Concerning the resurrection also they did not think rightly, saying that it had already taken place: some also among them altogether denied it. Certain ones also wished to hold the law together with the Gospel as though necessary to salvation. In marriage also they sinned, as in many other things, and made schisms: in which the Apostle intends to correct them. For after pride is cut down in us, it remains that we cut down also the other vices. From these things, then, it appears what is the matter of this epistle, what the intention, what the mode and order of proceeding. But the special matter of this [0513D] epistle is the state of the Corinthians, in which they then were, when the Apostle wrote to them from Ephesus. But the intention is to recall them from contentions to the unity of faith. But the mode is such, that he instructs, rebukes, confirms, praises, according to the qualities of persons. The order is such: he sends beforehand a salutation, after the manner of those writing epistles, lest at the outset he seem to have chided them. Then he sets before them concerning good things, that others may be conformed to them, and thus he comes to reproving contention. He begins therefore from the salutation, saying, Paul, using a name of humility against pride; Apostle, which is a name of dignity and office.

QUESTION II. *Through the will of God,* etc. It is asked: what does he here call the will of God? For the will of God is sometimes called his counsel, sometimes [0514A] his precept, sometimes his disposition, sometimes his approbation, sometimes his permission. Solution. He calls the will of God his good pleasure.

QUESTION III. *Grace to you, and peace from God.* It is asked: what is the grace of God? Solution. The grace of God is said to be [0514B] God giving freely, and grace, that is, divine inspiration, namely the operation of God, from which the soul is moved to love God and neighbor.

QUESTION IV. *To them that wait for the revelation,* etc. It is asked whether to every just man it is revealed at once after his death concerning his salvation. For it seems not: for since that revelation is a full knowledge, which is eternal beatitude, it seems, if this be granted, that no one is in purgatorial punishments: for can anyone be at once in punishment and in glory? Solution. The perfect pass at once to the knowledge of the highest good, in which is eternal beatitude. But the less perfect, before they come to eternal beatitude, pass through purgatorial punishments, yet certain of their [0514C] rest at last. But that there are purgatorial punishments, Augustine makes manifest, saying: The mildest purgatorial punishment is heavier than any temporal punishment whatever that is among us: and so there cannot be at once punishment and glory in such a fire.

QUESTION V. It is asked how that is to be understood which is read: *The angels shall tremble in the day of judgment;* for if then there will be trembling in them, how are they blessed? Solution. That trembling notes veneration of a higher power, not that the punishment of fear can be there where there is eternal beatitude. It is to be noted that not every mortal sin is called a crime, but only that which is worthy of accusation and damnation.

QUESTION VI. *Unto the day of the coming of the Lord,* etc. It is asked whether the Lord will come on a day to judgment; [0514D] because often the time of his coming is called a day. Solution. The time of the coming of the Lord is called a day, not for the illumination of the air, but for the revelation of hidden things: for all things which now lie hidden will then be laid open. But it is not known: will he come by day, or by night? For not only by the name of day, as above, but also by the name of night is the time of judgment designated, as it is written in the Gospel: *At midnight there was a cry made: Behold, the bridegroom cometh [n: (Matth. XXV)]*; but it is called night on account of its exceeding hiddenness: the day of judgment is so called on account of the discerning of the good and the wicked.

QUESTION VII. *Now this I say, that every one of you saith: I am of Paul,* etc. It is asked whether the Apostle speaks truly, saying, Now this, etc., when [0515A] none of them said this. For they did not glory in the true apostles, but in the false. Whence in what follows he is about to say: *But these things, brethren, I have transferred in a figure to myself, and to Apollo [n: (I Cor. IV)]*. Solution. The Apostle speaks truly, and the sense of the words is: Now this I say, that every one, etc., that is, this which I say is like that which every one of you says. But he did not wish to designate those in whom they gloried by their proper names, lest he should seem to envy them; and that in greater matters he might show that in lesser ones there is no glorying.

QUESTION VIII. Augustine says that Christ retained to himself the power of baptizing, which, if he willed, he could have given to his servants. It is asked, therefore, what that power is, which Christ retained to himself, and could have given to his servants, [0515B] and yet did not give. Whether it is the power of outwardly dipping bodies; but this he gave to the apostles, on whom he conferred the ministry of baptizing; but if he calls the power of baptizing the inward washing of souls, this power God alone can have, whose alone it is to infuse himself into souls, and to wipe away the stains of sins: how then could he give this to his servants? Solution. In answering this question many labor, not knowing what Augustine signified by the name of power. But we say that he calls power a dignity: by which, through the invocation of the name of Christ, or of the whole Trinity, baptism is given: which dignity he could have given to his chief servants, so that baptism might be designated by their name, and be called the baptism of Paul, the baptism [0515C] of Peter, and baptism designated by their names might have as great a force as it has when designated by the name of Christ himself: yet he did not wish to give this to them, lest anyone place his hope in man.

QUESTION IX. *Or were you baptized in the name of Paul?* etc. What it is to be baptized in the name of Paul is wont to be asked. Solution. The faithful are not baptized in the name of Paul; because those who are baptized are not bidden to believe in Paul, but in Christ, nor is the name of Paul invoked over them or over the element, that the sacrament may be made, but the name of Christ, who alone baptizes in the Spirit.

QUESTION X. *Lest the cross of Christ should be made void by the wisdom of speech.* It is asked how by the wisdom of speech [0515D] the cross of Christ should be made void. Solution. The wisdom of the world, which is called the wisdom of speech, because it is commended by no virtue, but by the composition of words alone, supposes that natural genius suffices for knowing, and free will for living rightly: which if it were so, the cross of Christ would be made void, that is, Christ would have died in vain.

QUESTION XI. *I will destroy the wisdom,* etc. Is not all wisdom from God? how then is it said: I will destroy the wisdom *of the wise?* Does God destroy that which is from himself? Solution. Their wisdom in themselves is not the wisdom of God; because they ascribe it to themselves, and abuse it: whence while they think to comprehend the highest things, they become vain in their thoughts, and thus from their wisdom incur damnation to themselves. Therefore their false [0516A] opinion, by which they believed that God could not do anything against the accustomed course of nature, and by which they supposed that free will sufficed for salvation, is called by the prophet the wisdom of the wise, which God destroys by acting against it, according to the reasons above, working our salvation in the midst of the earth.

QUESTION XII. *The world by wisdom knew not God.* It is asked how this is true which he here says, since elsewhere it is written of the wise of the world: *When they had known God, they glorified him not as God [n: (Rom. I)]*: how are both true, the world knew not, and when they had known God? etc. Solution. That: When they had known God, etc., is understood of the possibility of knowing. But this, [0516B] the world knew not God, is taken of the act of knowing. Or rather that, when they had known God, is understood of a few of the wiser and more excellent. But this, the world knew not, is expounded of all, or the greater part. Whence he here says the world: by which name the whole, or the greater part, is wont to be understood. Or the world, that is, the wise of the world, even if they had known God according to the works of restoration, in power, not in piety; in majesty, not in humility. It is to be noted that there are theophanies in creatures, that is, divine apparitions, as in the world, whose greatness demonstrates the highest power of God; but the fullness or beauty of the world demonstrates the wisdom of God; but the usefulness of the world intimates the benignity of God: in power the Father, in wisdom the Son, in [0516C] benignity the Holy Spirit is understood.

QUESTION XIII. *He that glorieth, let him glory in the Lord,* etc. It is asked what it is to glory in oneself, or of oneself. Solution. He glories in himself, or of himself, who thinks that to be of himself which is of God, and therefore attributes praise to himself, who, so far as in him lies, takes from God what is his own: for God alone is the author of good things. Whence it follows that whoever says any good to be of himself, in a certain manner makes himself God. He also glories in himself who, although he know that what he has is from God, yet turns back to himself the end of it, which he ought to refer to God.

QUESTION XIV. It is asked also what it is to glory in the Lord. Solution. He glories in the Lord who by the grace [0516D] of God alone judges himself worthy of glory.

QUESTION XV. *And I came not to you in loftiness of speech, or of wisdom,* etc. [n: (I Cor. II.)] It is asked how the Apostle says that he came not to the Corinthians in wisdom, but preached to them Christ, and him crucified: as though it were less difficult to understand that God assumed in the flesh died, than that God is one in essence, and three in persons? Solution. In Christ there are two natures, human and divine: but those things which are according to the human nature are more akin to us than those which are according to the divine nature: wherefore by us they are more easily believed and understood. But those which are according to the divine nature, as concerning the three persons, and the same essence, and the like, are more remote, [0517A] and therefore more difficult to understand. Whence he well said that he came not to them in wisdom, when he discoursed to them of the death of Christ; because he preached those lesser things, and not those loftier ones.



## II. ON THE FIRST EPISTLE TO THE CORINTHIANS. (cont.)

QUESTION XVI. Is it not required, in order that anyone be perfect, that he believe God to be one and three? And thus the Apostle seems to have handed down to them an imperfect doctrine, not preaching those loftier things, and thus to have deceived them. Solution. Faith is spoken of in two ways: either when it is simply set forth what is to be believed; or when the distinction of the things which are believed is set out distinctly — what is to be believed, and how, and why. But thus the Apostle set before them, generally and without distinction of the several articles, what they ought to believe. For thus it behooves that in the first place [0517B] believers be initiated, and afterward that the advancing be perfected.

QUESTION XVII. *We speak wisdom among the perfect.* It is asked whom the Apostle here calls perfect. For some are perfect in faith and in knowledge; others perfect in knowledge, and less in faith; others perfect in faith, and less in knowledge. In faith and knowledge the apostles are perfect; perfect in knowledge, and less in faith, are certain clerics, who are less constant in faith than the country folk; in faith and not in knowledge are very many country folk, who have attained little of knowledge, and yet believe perfectly. Solution. It seems to us that the Apostle was speaking wisdom among those perfect in knowledge, because those who are perfect in faith alone seem incapable of the higher things: yet deservedly to such faithful ones many things are often revealed: [0517C] and therefore, according to some, he speaks among those perfect in faith.

QUESTION XVIII. *Neither of the princes of this world, which is brought to nought [who are brought to nought].* It is asked how the wisdom of the philosophers is brought to nought, since it seems to be itself a comprehension of the truth. Solution. It is brought to nought when it is shown to be false or nothing: for they thought, as has already been said, that God could do nothing against nature. And so, among the great and many things which they truly comprehended, they asserted many false ones, and that truth which they had from God they attributed not to God but to themselves, whence their wisdom was turned into darkness.

QUESTION XIX. *Which none of the princes of this world.* It is asked first whom he calls the princes of the world? Solution. Either the wise of the world, or the demons, or even [0517D] those learned in the law.

QUESTION XX. It is asked, therefore, secondly, how that is true, that none of the demons knew him, since it is written of those who said: *Jesus, thou Son of David, why art thou come to torment us before the time? [n: (Matth. VIII.)]* And again: *And there were many devils going out, and crying: Thou art the Son of God; and he suffered them not to speak, for they knew him to be the Son of God [n: (Marc I)]*. Solution. The demons are said to have known on account of their supposing: who rather supposed Christ to be God than truly knew it: for God always left the devil in doubt, so that always after some humble things he did lofty ones, and after the sublime returned to the humble: whence after that notable [0518A] miracle concerning Lazarus, when the devil altogether thought him to be God, he allowed himself to be seized, bound, scourged: whence again the devil judged him to be a mere man; but when he was now led to the governor, and the Jews themselves confirmed in evil, it was in some manner revealed to him that through him he would lose his own right, and he wished to persuade Pilate through his wife to release him.

QUESTION XXI. Likewise it is asked, thirdly, concerning the Jews, how it is true that they did not know him, since it is written of them in the Gospel parable: *Behold the heir; come, let us kill him [n: (Marc. XII)]*. And elsewhere: *That through envy they had delivered him up [n: (Matth. XXVII)]*: but if they knew him, and yet killed him, incredible cruelty was in them. Likewise, if they knew him to be God, they knew him to be immortal, and thus unable [0518B] to die: how then did they seek his death, if they knew that he could not die? Solution. Some say that the Jews knew Christ, which they could not deny. Or in truth some of them knew him to be that one who had been promised in the law and the prophets. But they did not believe him to be God; yet that they killed him whom they knew to be just, was envy. Whose nature is such, that against its own goods it seeks the harm of others: therefore they were utterly ignorant of the dispensation of the divine counsel.

QUESTION XXII. *That we may know the things that are given us.* It is asked what it is to know the things that are given us. Solution. To know from whom they are given, and not to be ungrateful to the giver: for he is said truly to have who knows whence he has; he does not have, who knows not whence he has, and is ungrateful [0518C] to him from whom he has: for no one is blessed by the gifts of God who is ungrateful to the giver.

QUESTION XXIII. *Comparing spiritual things with spiritual.* It is asked whom he calls spiritual: for some are perfect in life, and not in understanding; others in understanding, and not in life. Solution. Those whom above he named perfect, here he calls spiritual.

QUESTION XXIV. It is asked whom he calls the animal man. For a man is animal according to life, and according to understanding. Solution. He calls animal the man intent upon earthly wisdom, who, considering earthly things, reckons the things that are of God to be foolish.

QUESTION XXV. *But the spiritual man discerneth all things that are necessary to salvation, and he himself is judged of no man. [0518D] For the animal man cannot understand what he doeth, or why.* It is objected. Peter was rebuked by Paul, therefore a spiritual man is judged by a spiritual man. Solution. The spiritual man is judged of no man, that is, he is shown by no one to be damnable and worthy of damnation: for although he sin in something, yet this is not damnable to him.

QUESTION XXVI. *I could not speak to you as to spiritual,* etc. [n: (I Cor. II)]. Was not the Apostle spiritual, both in life and in knowledge, and thus able to speak spiritual things, which he also did? Whence above: *We speak wisdom among the perfect [n: (I Cor. II)]*; how then does he say, I could not speak to you as to spiritual? Solution. He does not say this as though he did not inwardly have the faculty of teaching greater things, but because they themselves were not fit hearers; yet often, when the perfect and imperfect [0519A] are mingled, the same things are said, even if they are not understood in the same way.

QUESTION XXVII. *I have planted, Apollo watered, but God now gave the increase.* It is asked how the Apostle divides between himself, and God, and Apollo, since Paul or Apollo does nothing except from God and through God, and thus whatever either Paul or Apollo works, God also works. Solution. The Apostle divides, that he may show what God works through Paul himself, and through Apollo, and through himself: for the Apostle, by preaching, was working unto the faith of Christ, which God also did through Paul. For in vain would the Apostle speak outwardly, unless the Holy Spirit were within, working, and moving the mind.

QUESTION XXVIII. *He that watereth, and he that planteth, are one. [0519B]* It is asked how one, since they are divided both in substance and in accident? Solution. They are one, that is, indifferent. For neither does Paul confer more from that which is Paul's than Apollo from that which is Apollo's. For in the conferring of gifts this one does no more than that one: for God alone confers the gifts; yet Paul labors more than Apollo, and a greater gift is conferred through the office of Paul than of Apollo, because through the faith which is from preaching one comes to baptism. Or, through the sacraments themselves no more is conferred from this one than from that one, or through this one than through that one.

QUESTION XXIX. *For we are God's helpers,* etc. Does God need anything? How then were the apostles God's helpers? Solution. They are called God's helpers because God works through them, although he could work that same thing [0519C] through himself.

QUESTION XXX. *As a wise architect, I have laid the foundation,* etc. It is asked what he calls the foundation. Solution. The faith of Christ, which works through love, which they obtained from the preaching of Paul himself: this foundation remaining, no one can perish, although some stains adhere, which are purged by fire. For although here they possessed some worldly things by desire, yet they did not prefer these to God, but God to all. But to prefer God to all is, if the option or choice were given of dying or of denying Christ, to choose to die rather than to deny Christ. Upon this foundation one builds this, another that.

QUESTION XXXI. It is asked, what is to be understood by *wood, hay, [0519D] and stubble?* Solution. Some say that by these three is understood venial sin: but how do these build upon this foundation, since rather any sin whatever pertains to destruction than to building? Likewise, how shall he who builds these be saved yet so as by fire, since faith without works is dead? Likewise, *if any man have all faith, so as to remove mountains, and have not charity, it profiteth nothing [n: (I Cor. XIII)]*: therefore, if there is no salvation without charity, how can anyone be saved building these three alone upon this foundation?

QUESTION XXXII. Others say otherwise, that by wood, hay, stubble are understood good works, but imperfect ones. According to which opinion it is asked how a good work is to burn, or how [0520A] it suffers loss? Solution. Not the good work, because it is good; but because it is imperfect it will be purged of its imperfection, and especially of certain carnal affections which the builders of such things have, which seem to be venial sins.

QUESTION XXXIII. *All things are yours; and you are Christ's; and Christ is God's,* etc. It is asked according to which nature we are Christ's, that is, whether we are his according to the human nature, or according to the divine. Solution. It can be said that we are his not only according to the divine nature, according to which he is our Creator, but also we are his according to the human nature, according to which he is our Redeemer; but he himself, according to both natures, is of God the Father, from whom he has whatever [0520B] he has; but in one way and in another: for according to that which he is God, he has by nature all things which are the Father's; and all the same things he has according to that which he is man, but by grace: this is the norm of right faith.

QUESTION XXXIV. *I am conscious to myself of nothing,* etc. [n: (I Cor. IV)]. It is asked how the Apostle says that he is conscious to himself of nothing, and yet is not hereby justified: and John in his Epistle says: *If our heart do not reprehend us, we have confidence toward God [n: (I Joan. III)]*. And Paul himself elsewhere: *For our glory is this, the testimony of our conscience [n: (II Cor. I)]*: wherefore we ought to think ourselves just, and thence to have glory, if our conscience does not reprehend us. For what is a greater perfection of justice than to have so pure [0520C] a conscience? Solution. Something can always lie hidden from us: and therefore, in order that we may have true justice, it does not suffice that our conscience does not gnaw at us: yet we can glory in this, that our conscience is clean and pure.



## II. ON THE FIRST EPISTLE TO THE CORINTHIANS. (cont.)

QUESTION XXXV. Likewise it is asked how the Apostle was not conscious to himself, since he knew that he was not without sin. Whence John: *If we say that we have no sin, we are liars, and the truth is not in us [n: (I Joan. I)]*. And the child, whose life is of one day upon the earth [n: (Job II)], is not without sin. Whence he himself says: *And I do that which I will not [n: (Rom. VII)]*. Solution. The Apostle was of such perfection that whatever his conscience dictated to him was to be done, [0520D] this he did. Whence, if at any time as a man he had sinned, either through ignorance, or by stealth, or by frailty, the force of divine love consumed the whole like stubble.

QUESTION XXXVI. *Judge not before the time,* etc. It is one thing to judge concerning ambiguous matters, which is forbidden, because damnable: it is another to suspect, which is of human infirmity, of which perhaps no one is free. But since the Apostle forbids judging, and the Lord himself forbids the same in the Gospel, what is it that elsewhere the Lord says: *Beware of false prophets, who come to you in the clothing of sheep [n: (Matth. VII)]*. And how they may also be discerned, he himself adds, saying: *By their fruits you shall know them [n: (ibid.)]*. Hence he seems to intimate that we ought to judge concerning such. Solution. [0521A] It is one thing to discern the evil, that they may be guarded against; another to judge them worthy of damnation: whom we do not definitely know whether they are good or evil.

QUESTION XXXVII. Likewise it is asked how false prophets can be discerned from their works, since they do all the same things that the good also do: for by fruits are understood works. Solution. By the virtue of perseverance the false are proved and discerned from the true.

QUESTION XXXVIII. It is asked whether that is true which the Apostle says: *You are now filled [n: (I Cor. IV)]*, since he speaks ironically: which does not seem to be, since the contrary is true. Solution. As often as such a kind of speech occurs, as to the surface of the word and the first signification of the utterance, what is said is false: yet he himself who speaks does not lie; [0521B] but rather speaks the truth, which happens from the nature of the trope.

QUESTION XXXIX. *Not to confound you.* Is it not good to bring it about by a word that a sinner be confounded, and blush for his fault, and thus be corrected: how then does the Apostle say, I say *not this,* to confound you? Solution. Confusion is twofold. There is a confusion which is unto death, from which some are made worse; and there is a confusion which is of correction, when one blushes from correction over his sins, that a man may abstain the more.

QUESTION XL. *Shall I come to you with a rod, or in charity?* It is asked how the Apostle divides, saying, whether with a rod, or in charity. Solution. The name "charity" is here, from the adjunct, restricted to the sweet and delightful things [0521C] which come from charity; but sometimes charity signifies all things, hope thou, and the sweet things, which pertain to salvation.

QUESTION XLI. The expositor says that in those who are punished by an inflicted penalty, sin is diminished: but it is asked how this is true, since they bear the inflicted penalty unwillingly, and in this are contrary to justice, and thus sin the more, and thus their sin is increased in this, that they are punished. Solution. It can be said of such, that at least they avoid, through the penalty which they suffer, the sins into which they would fall if they lived longer: for if they bore the penalty willingly, even the sin already committed would be forgiven.

[n: (I COR. V)]. *I have judged to deliver such a man [0521D] to Satan,* etc. From this place of the Apostle the Church takes the sentence of anathema, that is, of separation; and separation is made now of known and manifest persons, now of unknown; but whether of these or of those, they are first to be summoned before the Church. Some also are to be awaited longer, and more often admonished, according to the diversity and quality of persons and of sins, as if a king, through whose anger slaughter threatens the Church. But after they have been separated, if they are known, in no way is one to communicate with them: if unknown, if anyone communicate unknowingly, he does not sin; but if he suspect, if he can avoid it without scandal, let him avoid; if he cannot, let him communicate. But this vengeance ought to be done for the sake of the common utility, both of him and of others.

[0522A]QUESTION XLII. It is asked, then, whether to excommunicate someone is good, which seems so, because just. Solution. It is good, that is, expedient, yet not in itself and from its own quality is it good: but when such a thing is called just, it is understood thus, that is, requited according to desert, not that it is informed by the quality of justice.

QUESTION XLIII. Likewise it is asked whether to be excommunicated is good. Solution. According to the aforesaid reasoning it seems to be good, because excommunication seems to be expedient not only to the rest but even to him: for if he should resist the admonition of the Church, he would sin.

QUESTION XLIV. But it is asked, if it happen that he be excommunicated who did not deserve it, but someone is struck with the sentence of excommunication out of the hatred or [0522B] envy of a prelate, whether he ought to resist, or to yield. Solution. If without scandal he can resist, let him resist, and by good zeal repress the swelling of his prelate; if without scandal he cannot, let him yield, and for God's sake patiently endure: whence the sentence of a prelate is always to be feared, not always to be held. When someone is excommunicated, he is delivered into the power of Satan, that he may vex his flesh, so that at last he may thus come to his senses: yet Satan does not always vex. For the crafty enemy spares one, that he may gain many, or that he may keep him for himself.

QUESTION XLV. *Purge out the [sic: velus] leaven,* etc. It is asked what he calls the old leaven? Solution. Vain boasting, or the oldness of life, which is to be purged by [0522C] newness of life.

QUESTION XLVI. *Such cannot be gained to Christ,* etc. This is from the gloss. It is asked: Since the human mind is prone to evil rather than to good, and especially when it is urged to return to accustomed things, why did the Apostle grant to those newly converted to the faith to have table and conversation in common with the gentiles, since Christians would more easily come to idolatry by the persuasion of the gentiles than the gentiles come to the faith by the admonition of Christians? Solution. In the primitive Church those converted to the faith, fervent with charity, could not easily be torn from the foundation: which the Apostle knowing, granted to it only fellowship with the gentiles.

QUESTION XLVII. *If he who is named a brother among [0522D] you is a fornicator,* etc. Since there is now so great a multitude in the Church, it is asked whether we act against the precept of the Apostle when we take food with such, or whether all such are to be cast out of the Church and cut off with the sickle of anathema. Solution. So indeed it would be to be done, if it could be done without scandal: but yet, because so great and so many would rather make a schism, since they spurn correction and amendment, they are tolerated: and therefore nothing else remains but that Rachel weep for her children. It is to be noted that of fornications some are bodily, others spiritual: bodily is every use of those members apart from the lawful; spiritual is that which separates the soul from God, and takes it away, as a bride from the bridegroom.

QUESTION XLVIII. *Know you not that the saints shall judge this [0523A] world? [n: (I Cor. VI.)]* Here the Apostle shows that it is lawful for the saints to judge concerning secular matters: but if this is so, how does he afterward subjoin: *there is now plainly a fault among you, that you have lawsuits among yourselves [n: (Ibid.)]*: if it is lawful to judge, how is this a fault? Solution. It is lawful for the saints to have causes under discipline, but it is not lawful to contend among themselves.

QUESTION XLIX. It is asked how this is true, that it is lawful to demand back one's own, since the Lord says: *If anyone take away what is thine, ask it not again? [n: (Luc VI.)]* Solution. To demand back one's own, although it be not evil, yet is an occasion of evil: therefore the Lord counsels not to demand back one's own, for the sake of avoiding contentions. This is what the Apostle says: *All things are lawful to me, but all things are not expedient [n: (I Cor. X)]*.

[0523B]QUESTION L. It is asked in what manner the saints are to judge in the future? And it is said, not only by the comparison of a better deed, but also by authority and power. Whence not without reason it is asked what that power is, since it is written: *The Father judgeth no man, but hath given all judgment to the Son [n: (Joan. V)]*. Solution. As Daniel says: *The judgment sat, and the books were opened,* namely of the eternal reasons, according to which the judgment shall be made, and the books of consciences, concerning which the judgment shall be made [n: (Dan. VII)]. These books of the eternal reasons and of consciences will be open and manifest to those who are to judge with the Lord. And this revelation of hidden things, according to some, is their power and authority: according to others, the saints are therefore said to be about to judge with the Lord because by their comparison the evil [0523C] will appear damnable. Whence it is read that the saints shall judge the nations.

QUESTION LI. It is asked whether it is a precept, or a counsel, or a permission, this which he says concerning judgments? Now it is a counsel concerning the highest things, as that: *Concerning virgins I have no precept; but I give counsel [n: (I Cor. VII)]*. A precept concerning middle things, as: *Thou shalt not kill, thou shalt not commit adultery [n: (Exod. XX)]*, etc.; a permission concerning the lowest things, as concerning the taking of food. But it seems that it is a precept: for if I do not demand back my own, I foster in error him who has taken it away; wherefore for the sake of the common utility I ought to demand it back: wherefore I demand it back from precept; because every evil is to be fled from precept. Solution. It is a permission to demand back; but a counsel [0523D] not to demand back. The Apostle therefore shows what is lawful to the weak; but the Lord shows what befits the perfect, namely not to demand back: but to contend, and to carry on lawsuits before a judge, especially an unbeliever, is altogether a fault.

QUESTION LII. *He who is joined to a harlot is made one body.* It is asked concerning this unity, what it is, and according to what it is to be understood. Solution. He notes that such are one not only by bodily conjunction, but in the ardor of lust, and this is most foul, thus to be made one body with a harlot, who ought to be one spirit cleaving to God.

QUESTION LIII. *For they shall be,* he says, *two in one flesh.* It is asked how the Apostle brings forward this authority, which is concerning that conjugal union which is holy, [0524A] to show this foul union, which is between the fornicator and the harlot, since in no way can that union which is there be this. Solution. The sense is: If in that conjugal union one body is made of both, much more is this made one flesh on account of the greater delight of the vice.

QUESTION LIV. *Bear God,* etc. What is it to bear God? Solution. To live soberly, and justly, and piously. They therefore bear God who live well in him: for this is to put on his image and the form of justice, just as they trample God under foot who by living ill deny him; but they lay God down who grow lukewarm in charity and are made more lax and remiss.



## II. ON THE FIRST EPISTLE TO THE CORINTHIANS. (cont.)

QUESTION LV. The gloss says: It is graver to sin against God than against the neighbor; but do not those who sin against the neighbor [0524B] offend God? How then is this distinguished, both the one and the other? Solution. To sin against God is, through contempt in those things which pertain specially to him, as in the sacraments, and faith, and the like, to offend God. To sin against the neighbor is to injure him by some wrong, which is less grave than the first. Or, to sin against God is to violate the mind inwardly. To sin against the neighbor is outwardly to injure a man by word or example: not therefore is everyone who offends him said to sin against God.

QUESTION LVI. *Now concerning the things whereof you wrote to me,* etc. [n: (I Cor. VII.)] He treats of marriage in three ways: according to precept, according to permission, according to counsel, which several things let the diligent reader distinguish in their places. What marriage is must be seen, [0524C] what the efficient cause, what the cause on account of which it is contracted, who are the legitimate persons, and what are the goods of marriage. And so marriage or matrimony is the marital conjunction of male and female, retaining, between legitimate persons, an undivided manner of life. This definition befits only the marriage of the faithful. According to others: Marriage is the power of lawfully being joined, contracted from lawful consent. The efficient cause is the material consent expressed through words of the present. The consent, which is in the soul, ought to be shown before the Church, without which there is no marriage, whence it is read: Matrimony is made not by the union of bodies, but by the will of souls. The cause on account of which it is contracted is the procreation [0524D] of offspring, and the avoidance of fornication. The legitimate persons are those whom neither a vow of continence, nor blood, nor order, nor disparity of worship, nor condition, nor frigidity of nature impedes. Three are the goods of marriage: faith, offspring, sacrament — namely inseparability, which is twofold, of the sacrament and of matrimony itself: for this third good, namely the sacrament, is not marriage itself, although it is itself a sacrament, as is that also: but whether both are of the same thing can be asked, which here we defer to resolve for the sake of brevity, whose traces we follow: for in the [treatises on] sacraments and the sentences of our elders we pursue these things more diligently.

QUESTION LVII. *But this I say by way of indulgence.* It is asked what indulgence is, or is called? [0525A] Solution. Indulgence is the concession of a laxer life; so that what would be unlawful and a sin, where there was no concession, becomes lawful. For the carnal union of man and woman would be a sin, unless it had taken up the state of the married, and thus through concession the unlawful becomes lawful, which is wont to be done for the sake of avoiding a graver sin.

QUESTION LVIII. *But I would that all of you were as myself.* It is asked: how does the Apostle say that he wishes all chaste, since he knew that God does not? For if this were so, how would the propagation of the human race come about? Solution. There is an implicit condition, as if he said, I wish, and it is good, or it would please me, that all were such: perhaps, if all were good who now are, the number of the predestined would be filled from them.

[0525B]QUESTION LIX. *Let not the husband put away,* etc. It is asked whether a husband can put away his wife, if she be convicted of adultery? Solution. He can put her away. Likewise it is asked whether he, or she, can be joined to another while the other is living. Some say that he cannot.

QUESTION LX. It is asked whether marriage still remains, namely after divorce; if it remains, then the husband has power over the body of his wife, and conversely. Solution. It remains, and he has the power; but he cannot use this power, unless a reconciliation has been made.

QUESTION LXI. It is asked whether [sic: *au*] among unbelievers, or between a believer and an unbeliever, there is matrimony: which does not seem to be, since it is written that no marriage which was not made in God is ratified. Solution. [0525C] They say that there is marriage among unbelievers, as between Priam and Hecuba, and between Philip and Herodias. Whence John reproved Herod for adultery; for it is not lawful for thee to have thy brother's wife [n: (Matth. XIV)]: because, if there was adultery and marriage there, as Chrysostom says.

QUESTION LXII. *But if the unbeliever depart, let him depart.* It is asked, if between a believer and an unbeliever there is marriage, how the Apostle says: *If he will depart, let the unbeliever depart, and let the believer be joined to another [n: (I Cor. VII)]*, when above he says that she who is dismissed should remain unmarried: for if it is a true marriage, how can it be dissolved? If it is not a marriage, how does he persuade them to remain together, since every union apart from the lawful is fornication? Solution. The believer has power [0525D] over the body of the unbeliever, but not conversely; because, as is read, the injury done to the Creator dissolves the right of matrimony: the believer therefore can both remain and depart. Because also that debt which was owed was previously paid; but the power which she had, she did not lose. But the unbeliever departing, if he join himself to another, commits adultery.

QUESTION LXIII. Concerning the marriages of the ancients it is wont to be asked whether they were true marriages, seeing how one having several wives conducted himself toward each by marital law. How was there lawful consent there, retaining an undivided manner of life. Solution. Some say that they were not marriages, but held the place of marriage. But others say that there were true marriages among some, and that there are among the Jews and unbelievers: [0526A] for he who says that the marriage of such is not ratified does not deny it to be a marriage, but asserts it not to be ratified.

QUESTION LXIV. It is asked whether marriage is a greater good than virginity. And it seems that it is; because there are greater straits, and pains, and labors in marriage than in virginity, and to each one the reward will be rendered according to his labor. Solution. It does not follow, if the labor is greater here than there, that therefore the glory is greater. For virginity is a greater good in itself than marriage. Yet certain married persons are not of less merit than certain virgins.

QUESTION LXV. *Be not made the servants of men.* Above he said: *Wast thou called being a servant? care not for it* [0526B] [n: (I Cor. VII)]: how then does he here forbid that we be servants of men? Solution. He warns that men be not served for the sake of men: which is done when the hope of salvation is placed in them, as those who said: *I am of Paul, but I of Apollo [n: (I Cor. I)]*.

QUESTION LXVI. *But he that committeth fornication sinneth against his own body,* etc. From these words fornication seems graver than the other sins: for where there is greater delight and greater contempt, there is also greater offense. Solution. A certain delight, from the penalty inflicted for original sin, is present in our members: which although it be greater in delight than the other sins, yet does not seem to be greater than all other sins in guilt or fault. Likewise they wish to prove by the authority of Jerome that fornication is graver than the other sins. For he says: [0526C] As much delight as preceded in the deed, so much bitterness of mind ought to follow in the satisfaction, and thus it seems that where there is greater delight, there is greater fault. Solution. This, as I judge, is not said in comparison with all the others; but in any kind whatever the quantity of the crime is understood according to the quantity of the delight.

QUESTION LXVII. *Each one hath his first gift,* etc. It is asked how this is true, since there are many to whom nothing has been given whereby they can be saved? Solution. He speaks only of the faithful, of whom there are diverse grades, as continence, marriage, virginity: in which they can be saved, as if he said: He who would not contain, let him descend to the marriage [0526D] granted to him by God, that in it he may be saved.

QUESTION LXVIII. *For the figure passeth away.* It is asked how this is true, since it is written: *The earth standeth for ever [n: (Eccle. I)]*. Solution. He does not say the world passeth away; but the figure of the world, that is, the form and appearance which it now has, will change into a better form: yet if anywhere it be read that the world passeth away, this is to be understood according to form, not according to substance, which will always be.

QUESTION LXIX. *He that joineth not, doth better.* On account of a greater aptitude for meriting. But it is asked how there is a greater aptitude for meriting in virginity than in marriage: since the fight is greater here than there? For if there is greater difficulty of meriting in marriage, there seems to be a greater reward. Solution. In virginity, [0527A] with less labor, greater profit, and therefore a greater aptitude for meriting. In marriage, great labor, and little profit: in which grade it is a great thing at least to stand.

QUESTION LXX. *But I think that I also have the Spirit of God.* It is asked what spirit of God the Apostle says he had, that the Corinthians might acquiesce in his counsel? For if we say charity, it does not suffice. For he could have charity, and not knowledge, like very many; if knowledge, it does not suffice: for many have knowledge, but their life does not answer to their words. Whence the counsel of such can deservedly be reproved. Solution. By the Spirit of God he understands both knowledge and charity; because in both the Apostle excelled. Whence also in his counsel one ought to acquiesce. [0527B]

QUESTION LXXI. *Knowledge puffeth up,* etc. [n: (I Cor. VIII.)] Of itself, without charity, not from its own quality; but by occasion, as the law works wrath. But the same seems able to be said of charity. For many, remaining in the advance of charity and of the other virtues, sometimes grow proud, and thus fall. As therefore the occasion of transgressing is taken from knowledge, so it seems to be taken from charity, which is not to be granted. For from charity no one takes an occasion of evil. For *it dealeth not perversely; it is not puffed up [n: (I Cor. XIII)]*. Solution. Knowledge is such a thing that it puffs up: and yet it is from God, just as riches offer an occasion of evil, and yet are from God; just as also wood is apt for burning, yet is not burned unless fire be applied; [0527C] so also knowledge never puffs up unless the heart of man be kindled. But can charity ever be so kindled? For can anyone have charity, and be proud?

QUESTION LXXII. *An idol is nothing.* It is asked how this is true, since anything artificial is something. Augustine says: The matter is from God, but the folly of men gave the form. But again, since it is written: *Every substance, every form, every conjunction, and every joining is made by God:* how is the form of the idol not from God, since it is something? Origen says: *An idol is nothing,* that is, it has the likeness of no thing that exists. Or an idol is nothing, that which they think to be the idol, that is, a person made of the image and [0527D] of a presiding spirit: Or (which is better) an idol is nothing in the world, that is, it has no power in the things of the world, to change them for better or worse. Whence things sacrificed to idols, as far as concerns themselves, it is lawful to eat; but not in the presence of a weak person, lest he do this with veneration of the idol.



## II. ON THE FIRST EPISTLE TO THE CORINTHIANS. (cont.)

QUESTION LXXIII. *There is no God but one.* Is not the Father God, and the Son God, and the Holy Spirit God, and the Father is not the Son, or the Holy Spirit? How then is there but one God? Likewise the Father is unbegotten God, and the Son is begotten God, how then is there one God? Solution. There is one God in nature, not in person: but what is the sense of these words, God one in nature, not in person? A great question among the moderns. But to speak what I feel, many seem to me to savor of the Sabellian heresy, being ignorant of the manifold signification of this word, *unus,* or *unum,* or even *singulus,* or [0528A] *substantia.* For these three words are taken otherwise in theology, otherwise in the common usage of speaking, concerning which one must treat by itself.

QUESTION LXXIV. It is asked whether the Son, or the Holy Spirit, is to be called our Father? Solution. Not simply, lest the unbegotten person be understood: but it can be so with such a determination, namely according to the grace of regeneration: for the whole Trinity in common by its grace regenerates us into sons of adoption.

QUESTION LXXV. Likewise it is asked whether God the Trinity is that Father who is eternally Father, or another. [0528B] Solution. Not that one, nor another, but one and the same with the same.

QUESTION LXXVI. It is asked whether the angels are to be called gods, as holy men are called gods, as that: *I have said: You are gods, and all of you sons of the most High [n: (Psal. LXXXI)]*. Solution. The angels are not to be called gods, lest they seem to be worshipped with that service which is called latria. In three ways is one called God: substantively, as the Trinity; by adoption, as the saints; by appellation, as the gods of the nations.

QUESTION LXXVII. *For their conscience, being weak, is defiled.* It is asked how he says that their conscience is defiled: for if they believed themselves to do this for God, namely to eat things sacrificed to idols with veneration, would they not, if they left this off, [0528C] sin, since all that is not of faith is sin? Whence also Paul, if he did not persecute the Church, then when he believed that it was to be persecuted for God, would sin. Solution. Because they did this to a creature, as to the Creator, they had no excuse on account of ignorance: for so perverse and blinded was their mind, that they thought a wooden or stone image to be God, whence fittingly the Apostle says: *Their conscience, being weak, is defiled [n: (I Cor. VIII)]*, not that it is defiled on account of God. Note that it is lawful for a preacher to take necessary things from his subjects, yet let him do this without scandal.

QUESTION LXXVIII. *Doth God take care for oxen?* [0528D] [n: (I Cor. IX.)] Does not God take care of all things? and if of all, how does he not take care of oxen? Solution.
