One is the care of providence, which is had generally of all things; another is the care of precept, which God does not have concerning oxen. For he does not give precepts to men, that he may teach them how they ought to nourish oxen; for this care he has concerning men alone.

QUESTION LXXIX. *I became to the Jews as a Jew.* It is asked how, as a Jew? or truly a Jew? Solution. He truly used the rites of the Jews, as Augustine says; not by dispensation, as Jerome says. But it would be done by dispensation, if it were evil, and yet were done for a time for the sake of a greater good, namely, that through that feigning they might be converted to the true [0529A] faith of Christ. But those two pillars of the Church were diverse in this: yet we do not say that either of them lied, since to believe this or that is no peril of faith: concerning which controversy it shall be spoken in what follows.

QUESTION LXXX. *All ate the same [sic: spiritatem] food [n: (I Cor. X)]*. It is asked, how the same? Solution. The same in signifying, or the same in effecting. For that [sic: *sibus*] was of the same efficacy as this, as they hold: yet certain sacraments of the Old Testament have their virtue and efficacy from the sacraments of the New Testament: just as from the body of Christ, which is a sacrament, the manna taken by the faithful had its virtue.

QUESTION LXXXI. *Neither let us tempt Christ, as [0529B] some of them.* God tempts, that he may prove; the devil tempts, that he may cast down; man tempts, that he may explore and know. And so temptation is threefold. The first is of proving, which is good, as that of Job. The second is of deceiving, which is the trying of the mind unto deceiving. The third is of distrust and despair, as that of the Jews saying: *Can God prepare a table in the desert? [n: (Psal. LXXVII.)]* It is asked, therefore, how the Jews are said to have tempted Christ, since they seem to have worshipped only God the Father and God the Trinity. Solution. The Jews are therefore said to have tempted Christ, because all the preceding things figured Christ; hence it is that this temptation was rather said to be of Christ than of the Father, although it is not more of the Son than of the Father, although it is so said.

[0529C]QUESTION LXXXII. *And they perished by the destroyer,* that is, by the angel striking them outside the borders of the promised homeland. But if it be asked whether that angel was good or evil, we answer that the evil are sometimes punished by the good, sometimes by the evil; but the good are not punished except by the evil.

QUESTION LXXXIII. *Upon whom the ends of the ages are come.* But were the ages ended at the coming of Christ; but if they are not ended, how is it the end of the ages? Solution. They are ended in us, because, whereas there preceded diverse states of life, in all of which there was a variation and a certain expectation of the coming of Christ, but we expect no other state of life, because between this life and the future there is nothing [0529D] intermediate; but to those who still expect the Messiah, the end of the ages has not yet come, as to the wretched Jews.

QUESTION LXXXIV. *God is faithful, who will not suffer,* etc. It is asked how he says that God does not permit anyone to be tempted above that which he can bear, since many fall from temptation, and are separated from God, and thus are overcome by the devil, and so are tempted more than they can endure. Solution. As long as they are with God, he does not suffer them to be tempted above that which they can. But that some of them yield is because they are unwilling to resist temptation when they can; whence it is just that they be tempted to that degree, their fault demanding it. But God sometimes permits someone to be tempted for the sake of proving, and for the preservation of virtues: which temptation is of providence, [0530A] as with Paul, to whom *there was given a sting of the flesh [n: (II Cor. XII)]*, for the preservation of humility.

QUESTION LXXXV. *The chalice of benediction, which we bless, is it not the communion of the blood of Christ.* It is asked concerning the name of this sacrament, why it is called the Eucharist. Solution. The sacrament of the body and blood of Christ is called, on account of its excellent virtue, the Eucharist, that is, good grace, in which sacrament there is not only an increase of virtue and grace, but he himself is received who is the fountain and origin of all virtue and grace. In which sacrament there are three things: namely, the visible species of bread and wine, and the body and blood of Christ, and spiritual grace. The first is the sacrament of the second, the second is the reality of the first and the sacrament of the third, the third is the virtue of the first and the reality of the second. [0530B] And so the first is only a sacrament, the second is both sacrament and reality, but the third is only reality. The first sacrament therefore has two realities, one signified and contained, namely the true body and blood of Christ; the other signified and not contained, namely the unity of the Church.

QUESTION LXXXVI. It is asked likewise why, after the sacrament of the typical lamb, the Lord gave his disciples the sacrament of his body and blood? Solution. That he might show that the legal sacraments, among which the chief was the sacrament of the Paschal lamb, ought to cease, and that the sacrament of the new law be substituted: among which the Eucharist holds the first place: therefore also that he might imprint this sacrament more closely, [0530C] and commend it more tenaciously to the memory of the disciples.

QUESTION LXXXVII. Then it is asked why he gave this sacrament under another species, and not under its own? Solution. That faith might have merit, which is of invisible things, because faith has no merit when human reason offers proof; and lest the eye shudder at what the hand holds, and lest we be insulted by unbelievers.

QUESTION LXXXVIII. Likewise it is asked why under this species rather than under another. Solution. Because a thing of this species has an express likeness with both realities of this sacrament, because, as bread is from many grains, and wine from many grapes, so the material body of Christ is from many members, and the spiritual from many [0530D] faithful. And as in bread and wine there is a full and principal refreshment of bodies, so in this sacrament there is a full and principal refreshment of souls, because through the eating of the flesh one comes to the taste of the divinity.

QUESTION LXXXIX. Likewise it is asked: since the whole Christ is received under either species, why is it received not under one only, but under a twofold species? Solution. Therefore under two species, that it may be shown that he assumed the whole man, that he might heal the whole man; for he assumed body for the sake of body, soul for the sake of soul, and bread is changed into flesh, and wine into blood. Therefore indeed it is received under two species, that the taking-up of soul and body in [0531A] Christ, and the liberation of both in us, may be signified.

QUESTION XC. Likewise, since flesh and blood are under either species, it is asked whether the substance of either species is changed into the flesh and blood of Christ? Solution. Although either be under either species, yet the substance of bread alone is changed into flesh alone, and the substance of wine alone into the substance of blood alone. Nor ought they to be called two sacraments, but one. Nor is the sacrament therefore said to be repeated, because the benediction is not repeated over the same species. Nor ought other substances to be offered in the sacrifice of truth, because from others the body and blood of Christ cannot be consecrated, but from a grain of wheat reduced into bread, and from wine.

[0531B]QUESTION XCI. It is asked why water is put with wine in the chalice of the Lord. Solution. The water signifies the people: whence neither ought the wine, by which Christ is signified, to be offered without water, because Christ suffered only for the people, nor water without wine in any way; because the people is not redeemed except through Christ.

QUESTION XCII. But it is asked whether the sacrifice becomes void if the water be omitted. Solution. If anyone, not intending to introduce heresy, has omitted the water through forgetfulness or ignorance, it does not seem to be void: whence neither do the Churches of the Greeks add water. But water alone can in no way be offered in sacrifice, nor bread except of grain, that is, of wheat, nor the grain except reduced into bread.

[0531C]QUESTION XCXIII. But it is wont to be asked whether the water with the wine is changed into blood. Solution. Some say that it is changed; but to us it seems that it is not changed: which we received from master Achard.

QUESTION XCIV. It is asked whether Judas received the body of the Lord in the dipped morsel. Solution. Not then, but before with the others.

QUESTION XCV. It is asked concerning the accidents, which remain in species, taste, weight, and form, in what subject they are. Solution. Many doctors agree in this, that they are without a subject, just as the substances of flesh and blood are there without accidents of this kind.



## II. ON THE FIRST EPISTLE TO THE CORINTHIANS. (cont.)

QUESTION XCVI. It is wont also to be asked concerning the breaking [0531D] and partition which is there, in what thing it takes place; because there is no other substance than the substance of flesh and blood, which remains whole. Solution. The body of Christ remains whole in itself, and yet is broken and divided in the sacrament.

QUESTION XCVII. It is asked whether the evil man receives the true body of the Lord and the true blood. Solution. He truly receives both, but to his own harm; because unworthily — for he is unworthy who receives otherwise than Christ instituted, or who is in mortal sin.

QUESTION XCVIII. Likewise it is asked whether Christ is immolated daily. Solution. He who was once slain on the altar of the cross is immolated daily in memory of his passion in the sacrament, nor is it repeated from his infirmity, but from ours, who daily sin, and obtain the remission of venial sins, [0532A] and an increase of virtues if we participate worthily. It is objected concerning that which Augustine says, that the good man receives the sacrament and the reality of the sacrament; but the evil man only the sacrament, and not the reality of the sacrament, and it has already been said above that the evil man receives the true body and the true blood: therefore the evil man receives not only the sacrament, but also the reality of the sacrament, which is to be believed without doubt. Solution. In the aforesaid words Augustine calls the sacrament the true body and true blood of Christ; he calls the reality the spiritual grace, which the good man alone and not the evil receives.

QUESTION XCIX. It is asked what kind of body Christ gave to his disciples, mortal or immortal? If mortal, how could it without injury be ground [0532B] or broken by the teeth; if immortal, then he gave such as it was not yet. Solution. Soundly we say that he gave such as he willed, to whom nothing was impossible. Yet some assert that he gave a mortal one, which, as we do not assert, so we do not deny.

QUESTION C. It is asked what is the virtue of this sacrament? Solution. The remission of venial sins, the perfecting of virtues, and it is instituted for the increase of virtues, and as a medicine of daily infirmity.

QUESTION CI. It is asked whether one ought to communicate daily. Solution. Augustine says: to receive the Eucharist daily I neither praise nor blame: yet if anyone is in the disposition of sinning, he is more burdened by receiving than [0532C] purified; and if anyone is gnawed by mortal sin, let him make satisfaction with tears; and if for the rest he have the will of not sinning, let him approach securely.

QUESTION CII. It is wont to be asked whether depraved priests can confect this sacrifice? Solution. Although some be depraved in life, if they are within by name and by sacrament, it is believed that they truly consecrate: but those who are excommunicated, and manifestly marked with heresy, do not seem able to do this. In this sacrament three things must be observed, namely form, order, and intention: the form instituted by the Lord; the order, that he be a priest; the intention, that he intend to do this.

QUESTION CIII. It is asked whether the body of the Lord is touched or received by brute animals? Solution. In no way is it received either by a mouse, or by another little beast.

[0532D]QUESTION CIV. What then does the mouse receive, which eats it? Solution. God knows — perhaps nothing but the accidents which are there.

QUESTION CV. *Eat not for the sake of him who judged, and for conscience' sake,* namely of the weak, etc. It is asked how he says: Eat not for the sake of the unbeliever, or the weak believer, is it not better to eat for the sake of both than to abstain? For if one abstains, the unbeliever is offended. But the weak is rather to be left to himself, as was said above: *He that is weak, let him eat herbs [n: (I Cor. XIV)]*. For in this opinion, he who thinks that thou dost venerate the idol, if thou eatest things consecrated to the idol, he is to be left to himself: for although he judge that thou sinnest by eating, yet thou dost not eat with veneration of the idol, [0533A] nor is he himself, by thinking this, turned away from the faith, but rather by zeal for the faith believes thee to err in this that thou eatest, and therefore is indignant. Solution. It is better to instruct by abstaining him who ate, than by eating to foster him in error. Likewise, if thou shouldst eat in the presence of a weak brother, he himself would be scandalized, and from the scandal would perish. Therefore one ought rather to abstain than to eat.

QUESTION CVI. *Whether you eat, or drink,* etc. It is asked how it can be fulfilled, that we do all things to the glory of God, since we do many things naturally, which we do not do for this, that we may please God? Solution. Let all our works be done so circumspectly, that nothing be done against God.

QUESTION CVII. *As I also please all men in all things.* Elsewhere he says: *If I yet pleased men, I should not be the servant of Christ [0533B] [n: (Galat. I)]*. Was he, pleasing all men in all things, not the servant of Christ? Solution. That which he says: If I yet pleased men, is understood thus: If I pleased men so far as in me lies, so as to put the cause and end in man, I should not be the servant of Christ. But he who pleases for the sake of the truth, not he himself, but rather the truth itself pleases.

QUESTION CVIII. *If any man seem to be contentious, we have no such custom, neither the Church of God.* Here we have authority that the customs of holy Church are to be held, even if we are ignorant of the reason why they were thus established by her: for God does not permit his Church to err in those things which pertain to himself. Note: The Church is called the convocation of the faithful, and the house in which one assembles to receive the Eucharist, [0533C] not only for the commemoration of the death of Christ, but also that we may be conformed to Christ in morals and life.

QUESTION CIX. *The man is the image and glory of God, but the woman is the image and glory of the man,* etc. It is asked how the man is the image of God and not the woman, since in Genesis it is written of both, that they were made to the image of God? [n: (Gen. I.)] Solution. One is the image common to man and woman, of which it is treated in Genesis; another is that which befits the man alone and not the woman. The first consists in the natural power of knowing God; the second is understood in this, that, as from God are all things, so from one man are all men. Or it is to be taken morally: that by the man reason be understood; by [0533D] the woman, sensuality, and according to this the man and not the woman is the image of God.

QUESTION CX. *There must be heresies,* etc. Concerning the authority of the Church. It is asked who is to be called a heretic? Solution. A heretic properly is one who, for the sake of some temporal advantage, and especially of glory, or of his own principality, invents false and new and depraved sects, alien from the truth, or holds, follows, and defends sects invented by others.

QUESTION CXI. *But let a man prove himself,* etc. If anyone is in mortal sin, let him not approach, but say: *Lord, I am not worthy that thou shouldst enter under my roof [n: (Matth. VIII)]*. What then is it for anyone to prove himself, but to see whether his conscience gnaws at him concerning a mortal sin, and whether he is in the purpose of remaining still in [0534A] sin? But the priest ought to admonish him who is in mortal sin, though not manifest, not to approach; yet he cannot withdraw communion from him. But if public report or a crime accuse him, let him in no way approach, lest the priest give it to him. For if, either for favor of him, or convicted by money, he gives to such a one, so far as in him lies, he kills Christ: which is greatly to be feared.

QUESTION CXII. *Now there are diversities of graces, but the same Spirit,* etc. [n: (I Cor. XII.)] Since the works of the Trinity are undivided, it is asked why he attributes graces to the Holy Spirit, ministrations to the Son, operations to the Father? Solution. In grace especially appears benignity, which pertains to the property of the Holy Spirit: therefore he attributes graces to the Holy Spirit. In ministrations [0534B] indeed wisdom shines, which is wont to be referred to the Son. In operations, power, which is specially of the Father: therefore he refers operations to the authority of the Father.

QUESTION CXIII. *To one is given the word of wisdom,* etc. It is to be noted that wisdom and knowledge are taken here otherwise than there: *O the depth of the riches of the knowledge and wisdom of God [n: (Rom. XI)]*. For when he says: O the depth of the riches of the knowledge and wisdom of God, by wisdom and knowledge he calls the divine essence. But when he says, to one is given the word of wisdom; *to another the word of knowledge,* by wisdom he calls the knowledge of eternal things, but by knowledge, the knowledge of human things.

QUESTION CXIV. *All these things one and the same Spirit worketh.* It is asked why the Father is not called gift, [0534C] as the Son or the Holy Spirit, since he gives himself, as also do the other persons: for to no one is the Son or the Holy Spirit given without the Father. Solution. On account of the authority of the origin, lest he be understood to be from another, who is from none.

QUESTION CXV. *For as one body,* etc. It is asked whether the good alone participate in these gifts? Or also the evil? But that even the evil have these gifts of the Spirit is plain from that which is read in the Gospel, that they will say to the Lord in the day of judgment: *Lord, have we not prophesied in thy name, and cast out devils? [n: (Matth. VII)]* to whom the Lord will say: *Amen I say to you, I know you not,* etc. [n: (Matth. XXV.)] Likewise John the evangelist, when he saw a certain man, who did not follow the Lord, casting out devils in the name [0534D] of Christ, wished to forbid him; to whom the Lord said: *Forbid not; he that is not with me is against me [n: (Marc. IX)]*. From which it is plain that the evil also often use these gifts, and thus it seems that they are of the body of Christ, and are his members; but the same, since they are evil, are members of the devil. Solution. The Apostle does not say that all having the gifts of the Holy Spirit are established in the unity of the body; or the Church is taken broadly, namely the multitude of all participating in the sacraments of the Church. Among whom are certain rotten members, and many grains with chaff, which are said to be in the body, but not of the body. Whence John: *They went out from us, but they were not of us [n: (I Joan. II)]*. Note that the Apostle says all the members of the body, since they are many, are one body. This I say on account of [0535A] certain ones unwilling to concede that all the parts of some whole joined together receive the name of the whole, since it is also said in the creed of Athanasius, soul and flesh are one man.



## II. ON THE FIRST EPISTLE TO THE CORINTHIANS. (cont.)

QUESTION CXVI. *If I should have all faith, but charity,* etc. [n: (I Cor. XIII.)] Here the Apostle manifestly shows that faith, and the other gifts, cannot be had without charity. It is asked therefore, first of all, of what faith he here treats — whether of the catholic faith, or of another? But not of another, because by another mountains cannot be transferred from place to place, as by this of which he here treats: therefore he here treats of the catholic faith. Whence it is established that it can be had without charity, and thus can be had by the wicked, which many deny. But according to these, the catholic faith is a faith working [0535B] through love. Of whom it can be asked whether they call it one thing, or two — faith working through love — but this whole cannot be one? For he who has faith thus not only believes, but also loves. But these two cannot be in the wicked; yet so far as it is simply in faith, this whole can be even in the wicked, even in the devil. For what does this good man believe that this evil man does not believe? Does not this evil man, or even the devil, believe that Christ died, and was raised again from death, and the other things which are to be believed, which are necessary to faith? But it is objected, according to this, that the devil has the catholic faith, and thus becomes a catholic. Solution. "Catholic" is said in two ways: both he who lives in catholic wise, imitating the life of Christ; and he is called catholic who believes all [0535C] things that are to be believed, whether he have charity or not. Certain ones concede that even the devil, according to the other acceptation, can be called catholic — which sounds grievously to our ears, especially since that knowledge which he has of Christ is rather from subtlety of nature than from the inspiration of the Christian faith.

QUESTION CXVII. *Charity is the proper fountain of good things,* etc. It is asked whether charity can be had by those who are to be damned. Are they not strangers, who do not share in the proper fountain of good things? Solution. Charity is called the proper fountain of good things for this reason, that no one can at once have charity and be evil.

QUESTION CXVIII. *Charity never falleth away,* etc. [0535D] It is asked whether charity, once had, is never lost. For if it never falls away, and is never lost, then those who are to be damned can never at any time have charity. Solution. Charity is said never to fall away for this reason, that it is had both here and in the future; but had in the present it is lost, and once lost is again recovered.

QUESTION CXIX. *Knowledge shall be destroyed,* etc. It is asked how he says that knowledge is destroyed in the future. Shall we not have in the future the knowledge of those things of which we now have it? We shall indeed have it, and much greater than we have in the present: how then shall knowledge be destroyed? Solution. Certain ones say that knowledge shall be destroyed as to its partiality and imperfection, so that it be not partial and imperfect. But [0536A] this is for it to be increased and perfected, and not destroyed; because in the same way it could be said of charity, that it is to be destroyed. For as we know imperfectly, so we love imperfectly; and when that which is perfect is come, that which is in part shall be done away.

Solution. Certain ones say that not knowledge, but its enigmatic and shadowy mode is to be destroyed. But again, by the same reasoning, charity too seems to be destroyed: whose mode, namely of imperfection, is in the future to be done away. To this it is answered that it is true that the mode of imperfection shall be taken away from charity, but not every mode. For in the future the neighbor shall be loved for his own sake, and for God's sake, as he is loved in the present. Others also say that the act of knowledge shall be destroyed in the future. But charity, [0536B] which now is — neither its act, nor any mode of it — shall be destroyed in the future; but faith and hope shall be wholly done away; but knowledge in part shall be destroyed [sic: *destrueter*]: of which there shall be neither act nor mode.

QUESTION CXX. But there is another question, which presses us more: it is true, and it cannot be denied, that charity in the present is imperfect in comparison with the future; but the Apostle proves by such an argument that prophecies shall be done away, and that knowledge shall be destroyed: *We know in part, and we prophesy in part: but when that which is perfect is come, that which is in part shall be done away:* since therefore charity is in part, by a like argument it too shall be done away, when that which is perfect is come. Solution. But we say that [0536C] knowledge, or prophecy, is said to be in part by one reason, and charity imperfect by another reason. For from the fact that some know, and some do not know, prophecy or doctrine has place in this present, where one teaches and instructs another. But in the future, where all shall be taught of God, when all from the greatest to the least shall know God fully and perfectly, then the teaching of men shall have no place: when no one shall say to his brother: Know God. And therefore knowledge, that is, doctrine, shall be done away. But charity is not in this way said to be in part. For it does not have being for this reason, that some love and some love not: nay, it shall be much more truly when all shall love perfectly. Note: an enigma is an obscure likeness; and as in the present every creature is as it were [0536D] a kind of mirror in which God is seen, so in the future God himself shall be the mirror of every creature, in which all things shall be seen more truly than in themselves. Hence it is, according to some, why knowledge is to be destroyed: because this shadowy mode of knowing, which we now have, when full knowledge comes will be no more.

QUESTION CXXI. Faith and hope cannot be lacking to charity; but faith and hope can exist without charity. Yet certain ones say, as was said above, that faith cannot exist without charity: whose error is here destroyed. For when he says: *If I should have all faith, so that I could remove mountains, and have not charity, I am nothing:* he manifestly intimates that even perfect faith can be had without charity. [0537A] And the expositor, in the aforesaid words, manifestly refutes them, saying that faith and hope can exist without charity.

QUESTION CXXII. But concerning hope it can be asked how it can exist without charity. Is not hope a confidence of future goods, coming from preceding merits? But this cannot be without charity. And so it seems that hope cannot exist without charity. Likewise, if hope is, as it seems to others, the expectation of a future advantage, it will now be presumption rather than hope, if I am certain that he will reward, though there be no merits. To this it can be answered that hope is twofold: of reward, and of meriting: but the hope of meriting cannot exist without charity, while the hope of reward can (not) be had without [0537B] charity.

QUESTION CXXIII. *He that speaketh in a tongue, speaketh not to men, but to God [n: (I Cor. XIV)]*. It is asked what it is to speak in a tongue. Solution. Some say that to speak in a tongue is to speak in parables; others, in an unknown tongue. Augustine says that the utterance of signs, which the Spirit forms in the spirit of man, is said to be speaking in a tongue. Whence the Apostle says: *The Spirit speaketh mysteries [n: (Ibid.)]*. For to speak in a tongue and to speak in the spirit he says to be the same. The Holy Spirit, in the primitive Church, formed for the conversion of the faithful the signs of heavenly secrets in the minds of the faithful, and the utterance of such things the Apostle calls speaking in tongues. But it is to be noted that those who spoke in tongues sometimes understood, sometimes did not understand. But [0537C] someone will say: If he who speaks in a tongue understands what he says, then this is not to speak in a tongue, but to prophesy. Solution. Although he himself understand, unless he expound to others, he does not prophesy, but only speaks in a tongue. Note that certain things are done in the Church only for adornment, not so much for necessity: among which is contained speaking in tongues.

QUESTION CXXIV. *You shall be speaking into the air,* etc. From these words certain ones judge that to speak in tongues is to speak in diverse kinds of languages; but this is not true: nay, the Apostle brings this in to show that to speak in tongues without interpretation has little usefulness, just as to speak in all kinds of languages would make rather confusion than edification [0537D] for those who understood none of them.

QUESTION CXXV. *If I know not the power of the voice.* It is asked what he calls the power of the voice. Solution. He calls the signification of the voice the power of the voice.

QUESTION CXXVI. It is also asked why speech of this kind is said to be done by the tongue. Solution. This is so because what is in the tongue is in the voice and in the utterance; but what is in the heart is in the understanding; because in the heart is intelligence: whence, because this speech is only in the utterance, and not in the intelligence, it is therefore said to be done by the tongue; by the spirit also, and not by the mind. For the spirit is a power of the soul lower than the mind, in which the images of things are confusedly apprehended. For it is an imaginary and confused apprehension of visible things, without discernment of the properties of those things which [0538A] are apprehended: such as was that vision of Pharaoh concerning the cows and the ears of grain: for he saw only images. But Joseph had in his understanding the revelation of God concerning these things.

QUESTION CXXVII. It is asked what words thus uttered signify, when someone thus speaks in a tongue — to set down this example: *He that soweth went forth to sow his seed.* For if this is the signification of the voice, it was true: it is asked therefore whether that is said by it which is properly signified by it: if it be said to be so, it is inferred, therefore, that something false is signified by it. To this certain ones respond, saying: The signification of the voice is not to be sought in such things, but only that of the things, which pertains to allegory. Others say, to speak more freely, that it does indeed properly signify something false; [0538B] yet a falsehood is not spoken by it: because the statement is not there terminated; for the end of the speech is not that first signification of the voice, but the second, which is called allegorical. But it can be said that the speech itself signifies neither true nor false: for the things there signify only the truth, which is there mystically understood. Nor do I deny that there is there a signification of the voice, but only incomplexly, because this word, "man," signifies a man, and so of the rest; but they do not signify complexly; for they do not signify conjointly, so that the true or the false is signified, but so that they may signify those things by which the truth is signified. Or it can be said that the proposition itself, from the property of the things which are signified, is taken up [0538C] to signify that which is mystically understood, as this word, "seed," signifies the word of God, from the property of the thing which it signifies.



## II. ON THE FIRST EPISTLE TO THE CORINTHIANS. (cont.)

QUESTION CXXVIII. *Christ died for our sins,* etc. [n: (I Cor. XV.)] It is asked whether Christ, according to the flesh, had the necessity of dying? That he had the necessity of dying seems to be the intent of the authority upon that passage: As *it is appointed unto men once to die, and after this the judgment: so also Christ was offered once [n: (Hebr. IX)]*; so, that is, by the same necessity and law of nature by which the rest die. Likewise the authority says, *because it was his own will he was offered [n: (Isai. LIII)]*. Therefore by will alone: how then by necessity? Solution. It is established that in Christ there was no cause of dying, because no sin; yet, as certain ones will have it, among [0538D] the other penalties, he took up voluntarily also the necessity of dying; and thus this necessity does not impede the will, which, as he received it when he willed, so he laid it down when he willed: for if, they say, some greater benefit had not been conferred upon the assumed flesh, it would necessarily be subject to the law of nature — which necessity also some understand by mortality.

QUESTION CXXIX. *I am the least of the apostles,* etc. It is asked how the Apostle calls himself the least of the apostles, since he is of greater merit than the merits of the others: for he labored more than all. Solution. He says this according to his former state, not according to the present, in which he was not the least of the apostles, but the greatest. But it is objected: For he does not [0539A] say, I was the least, but *I am the least of the apostles:* therefore, since he is not among the least, but rather among the first, it seems that he lies, and thus sins unto death: because *the mouth that belieth killeth the soul [n: (Sap. I)]*. Solution. The sense is: I am the least of the apostles, that is, I do not prefer myself to others, but rather others to myself: for indeed all his sins, past or present, hidden or manifest, he had before his eyes; but the simplicity and innocence of others he considered, and thus, according to this consideration, he felt more humbly of himself: for as the proud man, if he has any goods, always attends to them, and to the infirmities of others, whence he judges himself alone great, despising others; so the humble man without ceasing sees his own infirmities, but weighs the goods [0539B] of others: hence it is that the saints, though they be greater, feel themselves the more humble, nor is it a deceiving opinion, because the goods which they have they judge to be not their own, but the gifts of God; but the evils to which they are subject they know to be their own.

QUESTION CXXX. *By the grace of God I am what I am,* etc. It is asked of what grace he speaks. Indeed, of operating and cooperating grace, or preventing grace and subsequent grace, it has already been said in what precedes: for preventing, or operating, grace is one and the same, which works in us without us, namely by preparing and healing free will, that it may will the good, which the Apostle notes, saying: By the grace of God I am what I am; but subsequent, or [0539C] cooperating, grace is one and the same, which works in us, not without us, by following after and helping, lest we will in vain, which the Apostle notes, saying: *And his grace in me hath not been void, because I have laboured more than all: yet not I, but the grace of God with me.* From this therefore, that he says: *I am what I am by the grace of God,* the error of the Pelagians is destroyed, who said that free will suffices for meriting salvation. Likewise, from the fact that he adds, *and his grace in me hath not been void,* he shows that man can do something from free will, which certain heretics deny, saying that man can merit nothing. But the Apostle demonstrates that man of himself can indeed do nothing, but only from grace supervening: for it is needful that man be prevented by grace; then free will, [0539D] already prevented by grace, ought to cooperate with grace itself: whose nature is such that it can shine back and cooperate, as the eye touched by the ray of the sun can see. What therefore man works, is from grace, with which he cooperates. For a certain grace, as has already been said, works without the help of man, because it pricks the mind and rouses it; but man without grace can neither consent to grace, nor effect anything else, but leans upon grace drawing and leading, and thus, helped by grace, merits. From which it appears that it is not grace only when man does anything of good, but also from free will, although the whole is the work of grace, either by itself, or working with man. And so there are certain things from grace alone, and certain things from grace and man.

[0540A]QUESTION CXXXI. *If there be no resurrection of the dead, then Christ is not risen again.* It is asked concerning the hypothetical, whether it be true, and how it is to be understood. Solution. It is true, and is thus understood: If it were impossible for the dead to rise, as certain heretics said, neither is it possible that Christ rose; or if the saints were not to rise, neither would it be true that Christ rose; because all that he did in the flesh — by dying, by suffering, by rising — he did for us.

QUESTION CXXXII. *If Christ be not risen again, our faith is vain.* It is asked how the Apostle says that our faith is vain, and *we are more miserable than all men [n: (I Cor. XV)]*, if there will be no resurrection of the dead, or of bodies. For since [0540B] the soul is immortal, and it alone is capable of the vision of God, and thus it alone can have beatitude, can we not be blessed without the resurrection of bodies? And to the same:

QUESTION CXXXIII. It is asked, to what end will be the resurrection of bodies, since there will be no need of them where Christ will be all in all, nor can they themselves receive the beatitude which will be of the soul alone? Solution. It is to be seen diligently what is said, and according to what. For that which he says: We are more miserable than all men, he says not according to the soul, but according to the body. For here the Apostle suffered greater misery than others. But the resurrection of bodies is, that we may glory also in the second robe: for that brightness [0540C] which will be in the body will be for the increase of beatitude itself, so that what before it had for misery, it may now have for glory and adornment. The Apostle proves the resurrection of the dead by the resurrection of Christ: which was done for this reason alone, that the resurrection of bodies might be believed, and might come to pass: *When he shall have delivered up the kingdom to God and the Father [n: (Ibid.)]*, that is, when he shall lead the Church — in which he now reigns through faith, through the knowledge which it had of the Son — to the knowledge and vision of the Father.

QUESTION CXXXIV. *Till he put all his enemies under his feet.* It is asked how he says that Christ must reign: *Till he put,* etc. [n: (Ibid.)] Will he not reign forever, and especially when his enemies are subdued? Solution. In like places, "till" is put [0540D] for "forever": for if he reigns then, when he has adversaries, it is established that he will reign when all are subject to him, and thus he will reign forever.

QUESTION CXXXV.-- *Then the Son also himself shall be subject unto him that put all things under him.* It is asked according to what nature he says the Son is subject to the Father. For if according to the divine, he will be less than him: which is false, because according to the divinity he is equal to the Father. Likewise, if this be said according to the human, then according to it all things are subject to him: and according to the human he is Lord of all: wherefore also Creator; and thus according to the same he seems to be equal to the Father, according to which he is less than him: whence it is read: Equal to the Father according to the divinity; less than the Father according to the humanity. Solution. This can [0541A] well be understood according to both natures, namely the divine and the human: according to the human all things are subject to him, according to which he was exalted to the equality of the Father, when the human nature was united in one person to the Word consubstantial with the Father, according to which he himself also received the fullness of gifts; of which *fullness we all have received [n: (Joan. I)]*, and thus are subject to him. Likewise, according to the divine nature, certain ones understand it thus, that the Son is subject to the Father, because from him he has being, from whom he has whatever he has. According to that: *My doctrine is not mine [n: (Joan. VII)]*. Likewise: *The Father is greater than I [n: (Joan. XIV)]*. Which some will have understood according to the divine nature. In a word of this kind a distinction is noted, because the Son is from the Father, not the Father from the Son [0541B]; whence the Father is called the principle of the Deity, because he is from none, and from him is both the Son and the Holy Spirit. For the Father is a principle not from a principle; the Son a principle from a principle; the Holy Spirit proceeding from both. But things of this kind are to be said only at a fitting place and time, lest the weak incur scandal.

QUESTION CXXXVI. *In unequal brightness there will be equal joy.* It is asked, if the brightness is unequal, how can the joy be equal? Will not the quantity of joy be according to the quantity of brightness! Will not the brightness itself be the joy itself? Likewise, if one is more blessed than another, the one will have greater joy than the other: how then will the joy be equal? Likewise, will not the joy of all [0541C] be that of each? How then, in unequal brightness, will there be equal joy? If the same coin be given to all, how is the brightness unequal? If the joy will be equal, then the joy of Peter will be the joy of Martin. Solution. One thing is the joy of experience, another that of the will; as the joy of Peter is of remuneration, and of experience, but for Martin it is not of experience, but of affection. For the good of Peter pleases him as much as it does Peter himself, yet he does not feel and experience in himself so great a beatitude as Peter feels. And so the beatitude is different according to quantity, although it be the same according to quality. Just as, therefore, at the health of someone recovering from infirmity I rejoice as much as he does, by affection, though not by experience, because I do not feel in myself the health which he experiences. [0541D] As two are covered by the same bed, yet one is warmer: so in the one vision of God, one will rejoice more intensely than another. But no inferior will envy any greater, nor will he desire for himself the greater joy of the superior, because each one will have as much as he wills, otherwise he would not be blessed. There is life without death, knowledge without error, love without offense. There will be seen the end of our desires, namely God without end, he will be loved without weariness, he will be praised without fatigue.

QUESTION CXXXVII. *It is sown a natural body,* etc. It is asked whether the body has animality, that is, sensuality, from the soul, or the soul from the body? Solution. Neither can the body have animality except from the soul; nor would the soul be animal, that is, having [0542A] sensuality, unless joined to a body. And so animality seems to be known from the conjunction of both; the soul alone, however, feels through the body, but the body receives it, and does not itself feel. For man was first so created that from creation he was passible, yet he would never have suffered, unless he had sinned. Whence he is also said to have been immortal before sin, because he was able not to die, for he was able not to sin, since, if he had not sinned, he would not die.



## II. ON THE FIRST EPISTLE TO THE CORINTHIANS. (cont.)

QUESTION CXXXVIII. It is likewise wont to be asked, since man before sin was said to be mortal, and in a certain manner immortal, whether he had both from nature, or neither, or one only. Solution. Saving the reverence due to hidden things, and without prejudice to a better opinion, we say that man before sin was naturally mortal and passible; [0542B] but by the benefit of the tree of life he would become immortal: whence the doctors do not say simply that he was then immortal, but they add "in a certain manner," and determine in what manner; but mortal they pronounce him simply to have been, according to this that is said: The first man was made into a living soul, that is, into a soul which should quicken and vivify the body, not so as to have no need of foods.

QUESTION CXXXIX. *Now this I say, brethren, that flesh and blood cannot possess the kingdom of God; neither shall corruption possess incorruption.* It is asked therefore what bodies we are going to have. Solution. The same bodies which we now have we shall have after the resurrection, but changed not according to substance, but [0542C] according to quality: these dissoluble, but those indissoluble; but those of the good impassible, but those of the wicked passible: whence they will be as if in perpetual death. But that they will be indissoluble, the Apostle teaches, saying: Flesh and blood shall not possess the kingdom of God [n: (Ibid.)]. But that they are going to have impassible bodies, the Apostle intimates, saying: *but we shall all be changed [n: (Ibid.)]*.

QUESTION CXL. *For the trumpet shall sound [sic: Canetenim],* etc. It is asked, what is signified by the name of the trumpet? Solution. The doctors say that the Apostle so calls some evident and manifest sign, by which the mystery of the future resurrection will be fulfilled: which trumpet is elsewhere called a cry, elsewhere the voice of an archangel or the voice of Christ.

[0542D]QUESTION CXLI. It is asked also concerning the voice of the trumpet, whether it will be material? Solution. It is manifest that there will be a material voice made by the ministry of an angel; because as by the trumpet the people of the Jews was called together to a feast or to war, so then they will be called to judgment; or some evident sign, by which the same is done that is wont to be done by the voice.

QUESTION CXLII. *Death is swallowed up in victory.* It is asked, what death, and in what victory it is thus swallowed up? For by the name of death is signified sometimes the devil, who is the author of death, sometimes sin, which separates from God, sometimes the dissolution of soul and body. Solution. It can well be understood both of the devil, and of sin, and of the dissolution of soul and body. It is established that in the victory of the Lord's resurrection the devil [0543A] is swallowed up: that he may not domineer, as before, when by the fear of death men were driven to any crime whatever; but now the saints despise death: but chiefly in the future general resurrection of all shall all death be swallowed up, when this mortal shall put on incorruption.

QUESTION CXLIII. *And the strength of sin is the law.* How this is to be understood may easily be weighed from those things which were said upon the Epistle to the Romans: for the law being given, both carnal concupiscence grew strong, and transgression came on. For the law by forbidding increases concupiscence, unless the Holy Spirit infuse charity.

QUESTION CXLIV. *Therefore, brethren, be ye steadfast and unmovable.* It is asked in what these two differ. Solution. [0543B] Steadfast in faith, lest of themselves they be moved by the foot of pride; unmovable in temptations, lest by the impelling hand of sins they forsake the faith. There are three kinds of temptations: one violent, another fraudulent, the third violent and fraudulent. The first is done through open persecutions; the second through false brethren and heretics; the third will be done through Antichrist. But every [0544A] kind of temptation the devil sends: whence he is also called a lion, by raging openly, and a dragon, by seducing secretly and stealthily: whence it is written: *Under his tongue is labour and sorrow [n: (Psal. X)]*.

QUESTION CXLV. *Concerning Apollo, I make known to you, that I greatly besought him to come unto you, but it was not his will to come now [n: (I Cor. XVI)]*. It is asked which of the two acted unreasonably — whether Paul in asking that he should go? or Apollo in not consenting, because it seems either that this one did not ask what was fitting, or that the other omitted what he ought to have done? Solution. It is true that both acted reasonably, because the Apostle asked that he should do this; whence Apollo, because he was thus asked, saw that the Apostle's petition should rather be set aside, wherefore [0544B] he did not consent.

QUESTION CXLVI. *Salute one another with a holy kiss.* It is asked why he added, "holy"? Solution. There is a kiss of wantonness; there is a kiss of betrayal, as of Judas; there is a kiss of holiness and concord, so that what is within may appear outwardly, namely the bond of charity, etc. Of this therefore he speaks, that he may exclude the rest.



## III. ON THE SECOND EPISTLE TO THE CORINTHIANS.

[0543]

[0543C] *[n: (II COR. I.)] Paul an apostle,* etc. This is the second epistle, which is addressed to the Corinthians. Now the Apostle sometimes writes two, sometimes one epistle only; but neither, when he writes one, does he say anything diminished and imperfect, nor, when he writes two, anything superfluous, as here can be seen. For this epistle, which follows, is the consummation and confirmation of the preceding. For in this second one he admonishes those to be corrected who had not yet been corrected through the preceding epistle. He marks them, that in almsgiving they were sparing. He commands the corrected fornicator to be received. Whence it appears what is the matter of this epistle, and also what the intention. The matter is the special state of the Corinthians, in which they then were. But the intention is to recall them to the unity and integrity of faith. But in this [0543D] special state he signifies and forms the general state of the Church, and invites all to the unity of faith. He sends beforehand, after his manner, and that of others writing epistles, salutations of those to whom he writes, courting benevolence: whence also he says, Paul an apostle, joining a name of humility and a name of dignity, that humility as companion may temper the excellence of dignity, without which every virtue is empty and vain. Likewise, of Jesus Christ: Jesus is a name of the person. Now many were called by this name, as Jesus son of Nave, Jesus the great priest, but all by way of designation. But Christ alone substantively, because he had both the name and the thing: who was truly the Savior of the world. For Jesus is interpreted *Savior.* But Christ is a name of the person, [0544C] which embraces both natures. For it is a name of office, like priest, soldier, and is interpreted *anointed.* In the Old Testament two persons were anointed, the royal and the priestly; but Christ was anointed with a royal anointing, who according to the divine nature was able to rule his own; but according to the human nature received the power of offering, who offered himself to God the Father: whence from the royal and priestly office he is called Christ. The will of God is taken in many ways, as was already said above, and to say it again will not be superfluous. For the will of God is called the disposition itself, and the good pleasure. Whence: *Whatsoever he pleased he hath done [n: (Psalm. CXXXIV)]*. The will of God is also called counsel, or precept: whence it is said: *God will have all men to be saved [n: (I Tim. II)]*, that is, [0544D] he counsels and commands them to do those things by which they may be saved, such as prohibition, or permission, and the like.

QUESTION I. *Whereas when I was thus minded, did I use lightness?* It is asked therefore whether the Apostle lied, in promising that he would come, when he did not come: for he himself said, I will come, and he did not come: therefore with him there was "it is" and "it is not," that is, affirmation and negation of the same thing, and thus a lie, and thus he is guilty of lying. Solution. A lie is a false signification of the voice with the intention of deceiving: whence he who says something false which he thinks true is not to be judged a liar, since he is rather deceived than deceives. But whoever, with the intention of deceiving, says what is true or false, is guilty [0545A] of lying: whence it is gathered that someone saying what is true is guilty of lying, or lies; and that someone says what is false, and yet does not lie or is not guilty of lying.

QUESTION II. But it is asked concerning those who lie piously, whether they are guilty of lying, as those Egyptian midwives? For from the intention of deceiving they pronounced something false. Solution. Those who thus lie sin, and while they provide for the lives of others, acting against their own conscience, they offend the truth, and incur the peril of their own soul.

QUESTION III. Likewise it is asked concerning those who speak falsehood in jest. Solution. Sometimes so to jest is evil, sometimes not; but if from habit, then it is a sin.

[0545B]QUESTION IV. *To some indeed the odour of death unto death,* etc. [n: (II Cor. II.)] It is asked whether the odour of death is good or evil, since the Apostle says that he is an odour, to some unto death, to some unto life: for if good, how unto death? Likewise, if evil, how good to God? Solution. The Apostle was nothing but a good odour, and yet by this good odour some were dying, that is, they took occasion through envy, as the law is good, and yet an occasion of evil, because of transgression.

QUESTION V. *Not that we are sufficient,* etc. [n: (II Cor. III)]. Here it is asked how the Apostle says that we are not sufficient to think anything of ourselves, since we can both think and do evil of ourselves. Likewise, since certain things we can do naturally, which are neither for reward nor for punishment [0545C] — and these are those which were given us from the first creation, as to raise a finger, to bend it, to lay it down, and the like. Solution. The Apostle here treats of those goods which have merit with God, which can in no way be done without grace supervening and helping. Whence *His mercy shall prevent me [n: (Psal. LVIII)]*, *and his mercy shall follow me [n: (Psal. XXII)]*. For grace prevents the will, that it may will, and follows after, lest it will in vain. Whence the Apostle here destroys the error of those who said that the beginning of good cannot naturally be without grace, but that the consummation of good is from ourselves: in this that he says, but our sufficiency is from God. For from free will we have the faculty of working well, yet we cannot use this faculty except [0545D] by the help of grace. For free will is through fault so depressed that it cannot use its own power, unless it be raised up by grace and helped, as, when I have the power of riding, yet I cannot have the exercise of this power without a horse.

QUESTION VI. *The letter killeth, but the spirit quickeneth,* etc. It is asked how the letter is said to kill: does it command such things as, when observed, kill? How then will that stand which the Apostle says elsewhere, that *the law is holy, and the commandment holy, good and just,* if the precepts kill? Likewise, if it be said that the letter without the spirit, that is, without grace, kills, the same can seem to be said of the Gospel. [0546A] Likewise, if it be said that the letter kills for this reason, that not all things can be observed there according to the letter: let him not perish who has observed them. In the same way it can be said of the Gospel. For if this, *except you eat the flesh of the Son of man, and drink his blood,* etc. [n: (Joan. VI)], be observed according to the letter, that is, as the letter sounds, so that we understand eating and drinking thus, this, as elsewhere, is foolish and damnable. Solution: The letter without the spirit kills, that is, without grace, because it is an occasion of death, as knowledge without charity puffs up. But the letter of the Gospel is not without the spirit. Or, as was already said elsewhere: *By the letter, or law, is understood the commandment without grace:* which always kills: because, increasing concupiscence, it adds on transgression. By the Gospel [0546B] is understood the commandment with the spirit, that is, grace: whence the Apostle calls the law *the ministration of death:* the Gospel, *the ministration of justice.*

QUESTION VII. *And not as Moses put a veil,* etc. It is asked concerning the veil, whether it blinds. Solution. One thing is the figured veil, which is the reading of Moses: which was figured by the veil that Moses, speaking to the children of Israel, put over his face. Another thing is the veil of blindness, which is placed over the heart of the Jews: both veils are taken away through Christ. In figure of which thing the veil of the temple was rent in the passion of Christ.

QUESTION VIII. *But we all, beholding the glory of God with open face, are transformed into the same image,* [0546C] etc. It is asked what he calls glory, and what image? Solution. The glory of God, which we behold, and the image, into which we are transformed, he calls the same, namely Christ, who is the glory, and the uncreated image of God, as man is the glory, and the created image of God.

QUESTION IX. *Who is the image of the invisible God [n: (II Cor. IV)]*. It is asked why the Son is called the image of the Father. Solution. That it may be shown that he is so from the Father, as to be shown through all things like and equal to him.

QUESTION X. But if it be asked why the Holy Spirit, since he is from the Father, and like and equal through all things, is not called the image of the Father as the Son is. It is answered that image, equality, and likeness pertain more to the property of the Son than to [0546D] the property of the Holy Spirit. For those things which are born, not those which proceed, are wont to be alike. It is to be noted that upon image and equality follows likeness: because wherever there is image, or equality, there is likeness, but it is not converted. Likewise, image neither infers equality, nor is inferred from it, because there can be image without equality, and equality without image.

QUESTION XI. Having the same spirit of faith. The expositor says upon this passage that the times are varied, not the faith, because whatever we believe, those ancients also believed, and conversely. Whence it is thus objected: Abraham believed that Christ was to be born, and we believe that he is born: but it is one thing to be [0547A] about to be born, another to be born: therefore he believed one thing, and we another? Solution. Whatever we believe, and the ancients, etc., that is, the same things are subject to our faith and to theirs: yet it does not follow that the same thing which is now past was past in their time; or that what was then future is now future. Likewise, if it is objected: Abraham believed that Christ was to be born, but now it is false that Christ is to be born: therefore Abraham believed something false. Solution. As it seems to us, the "what" is interpreted by the "of what kind." For when it is said: Abraham believed that Christ was to be born, the sense is, he had faith concerning the nativity of Christ, which was then future; but in the assumption, when it is said, now it is false that Christ is to be born, the sense is: the nativity of Christ is not future: whence [0547B] from those two premises it in no way follows that Abraham believed something false.

QUESTION XII. *But though our outward man.* It is asked whether the outward man and the inward man are two men, and whether the outward man and the old man, and the inward man and the new man, are the same. Solution. As the old man and the new man are not two men, but one, although according to one thing he be called old, according to another new, so the outward man and the inward man are not two men, but one and the same, so called according to diverse things. Nor is the old man the same as the outward man; nor is the new man the same as the inward man. For the old man consists in fault and punishment, which two are found not only in the outward man, but also in the inward man. But the new man [0547C] is understood according to justice and glory: which two also pertain to the inward man. But the outward man is called whatever we have in common with the beasts; the inward man, what is common to us with the angels.



## III. ON THE SECOND EPISTLE TO THE CORINTHIANS. (cont.)

QUESTION XIII. *Who hath given us the pledge of the Spirit,* etc. [n: (II Cor. V)]. It is asked how the Holy Spirit is called a pledge, and of what thing he is the earnest? Solution. The Holy Spirit is love, and from the love which we have toward God, we are certain of his promise; and because we have this certitude from the Holy Spirit, therefore the Holy Spirit is given to us by God as it were an earnest and a pledge. Now a pledge is the certitude of a thing believed, or promised, or to be believed. [0547D] *Knowing therefore the fear of the Lord, we persuade men.* Fear is divided into five species, as was already said in the Epistle to the Romans, nor is there need to go back over it.

QUESTION XIV. *For whether we be transported in mind,* etc. It is asked what are the excesses of mind. Solution. There are two excesses, either dread, or straining toward things above: so that in a certain manner lower things slip from the memory. In this excess of mind were all the saints: to whom the secret things of God, surpassing the world, were revealed.

QUESTION XV. *Christ died for all.* It is asked how Christ died for all: since his death does not profit all: for it does not profit those to be damned, but only the elect. Solution. [0548A] According to Jerome it is thus understood, for all who are to be saved. For universality is sometimes restricted, and this in many ways. For sometimes the universal sign gathers the individuals of the kinds, sometimes the kinds of the individuals, sometimes neither, but the greater or the more worthy part of the universality itself. Or according to Augustine, Christ died for all, because his death merited this, that through him all should be saved, unless it remained in themselves: for it was sufficient for the salvation of all.

QUESTION XVI. *God was in Christ, reconciling the world to himself.* It is asked how the Father is said to be in the Son, or the Son in the Father: or how that [0548B] is to be understood: *He that seeth me, seeth the Father also [n: (Joan. XIV)]*. Solution. The one is said to be or to be seen in the other for this reason, that their substance is naturally one. Which I add, that I may exclude the Sabellian heresy. There is unity where there is no diversity, but in every way indifference, and equality, and identity. Whence Hilary says: The Father is seen in the Son on account of the united likeness of nature: for so do we detest the pest of the Arians, that we no less execrate the madness of the Sabellians; so do we confess God to be three, that he is one, and so one, that he is three. For many in our days are Sabellians as to their understanding, who confess three persons: but when it is said that the three persons are one substance, they understand nothing else than what Sabellius understood: [0548C] which comes about because they do not attend to the manifold signification of this name, *substance.* For Hilary says that when it is said: The Father and the Son are one substance, such a manner of speaking has both the conscience of faith, and a fraud ready at hand. Then he opens up both, saying: If you signify the Father and the Son to be a singular God, the understanding is false; but if you say that the Father and the Son are one substance, or one simply, so as to understand one, equal and indifferent, through all things equal, in no part dissimilar, the understanding is true. By which words he most manifestly distinguishes between personal unity and natural unity: for the Father and the Son are one in nature, not in person.

[0548D]QUESTION XVII. *Behold, now is the day of salvation,* etc. [n: (III Cor. VI)]. It is asked why the time of grace is called the day of salvation, since also in the time of the natural and written law many were saved? Solution. This time of grace is called the day of salvation for this reason, that in this time the victim was offered, through which alone entrance lies open into the kingdom, through which also those who went before merited salvation. Whence also it is called the time of grace, on account of the greater powers given to us through the manifestation of faith and love: whence also the powers of the devil are diminished, and because now all things are done freely, not for the sake of any earthly advantage, and because those things which in other times were promised are in this time fulfilled.

[0549A]QUESTION XVIII. *In charity unfeigned,* etc. It is asked what charity is called feigned. Solution. That which does not persevere, or which is not sufficient for salvation, or that which is simulated, namely some sign of love shown outwardly, when within it is not in the heart, and this charity is not charity.

QUESTION XIX. *As dying, and behold we live.* It is asked, in view of these words, how the yoke of the Lord is sweet, and the burden light, since the saints suffer so many and so great hard and difficult things, and how those laboring and burdened, coming to him, find rest, since they seem to pass not from labor to rest, but rather from rest to labor. Solution. To the saints sustaining grievous and harsh things there is present the Holy Spirit, who in the corruption of the outward man renews the inward man, [0549B] from day to day, and, spiritual rest being tasted, by the hope of future beatitude relieves all harsh things, and thus in so many hard things the burden of Christ is lighter. For the love of God and of our Lord Jesus Christ makes all savage and monstrous things easy and well-nigh nothing.

QUESTION XX. *That there may be an equality, as it is written: He that had much had nothing over,* etc. [n: (II Cor. VIII.)] It is asked whether the lesser, who (as it were provincials) minister stipends to the soldiers of Christ, are equal to them in merits: which the Apostle seems to mean, saying, that there may be an equality. Solution. That equality is not of piety, but because both sustain, and are sustained by, one another. For the lesser sustain the greater in carnal things, and are sustained in spiritual things. And the greater sustain the lesser, that is, the spiritual the carnal, in [0549C] spiritual things, and are sustained in carnal things by the same.

QUESTION XXI. *For we provide good things not only before God,* etc. It is asked how the Apostle divides, namely before God, and before men, since it cannot be done before God, unless it be also done before men? Yet the outward work is not always required, namely when the faculty is lacking. But it is always required that the conscience be clean. Solution. That the Apostle may show that the conscience cannot be clean, unless good things also be provided before men, therefore he distinguishes between these two.

QUESTION XXII. It is asked: What is it to provide good things before God? Solution. So to dispose the mind, that [0549D] nothing be done against God, which cannot be done, unless the scandal of the brethren be avoided outwardly; or therefore he divides between the aforesaid, that he may show that there are certain things which, as far as pertains to God, are lawful to be done, because in themselves they are good, although it seem otherwise to men, and therefore they can be passed over. What therefore is expedient and fitting, let that be done which is expedient for us unto merit, which is fitting for an example to the rest. Therefore for the sake of conscience we ought to provide good things before God. For the sake of reputation we provide good things also before men: for he who, trusting in his conscience, neglects his reputation, is cruel, which he does who cares not whether what he does please or displease, and for the scandal of the brethren omits nothing.

QUESTION XXIII. *He who soweth sparingly, shall also reap sparingly.* [0550A] [n: (II Cor. IX.)] It is asked concerning the poor, who sow sparingly, or nothing, whether they too shall therefore reap sparingly. Solution. He does not sow sparingly who bestows little, if the mind be ready to give, if he had more. He therefore is to be said to sow sparingly who has little love, whether he give more or less: and this man shall reap sparingly, that is, he shall receive a small retribution unto life eternal.

QUESTION XXIV. *For we dare not insert ourselves,* etc. [n: (II Cor. II.)] That is, we do not usurp to ourselves power, but we use the power given to us by God. It is asked therefore what it is to usurp power. Solution. He usurps to himself power who, not elected, or not called, takes to himself honor, who thrusts himself in, and, power not being received, wishes to domineer.

[0550B]QUESTION XXV. *But we will not glory beyond measure.* It is asked: What is it to glory beyond measure? Solution. To glory more than one ought, and in that in which one ought not, which he does who extends himself into that to which he has no right. But to abuse power is to flatter, and to caress the vices of sinners. To use power is to reprove sinners, and to do the other things which pertain to edification.

QUESTION XXVI. *He that glorieth, let him glory in the Lord.* It is asked what it is to glory in the Lord, since elsewhere he says: *God forbid that I should glory, save in the cross of our Lord Jesus Christ [n: (Galat. VI)]*. And that: *Not only do we glory in the hope of the sons of God, but also in our tribulations [n: (Rom. V)]*. Is it the same to glory in the Lord, and in the cross of the Lord, and in tribulations? [0550C] What is it to glory? Solution. To glory is to rejoice in praise, and to judge oneself worthy of glory. To glory in the Lord is to attribute all confidence not to oneself, but to the Lord as author, and to exult in Christ with spiritual joy. To glory in the cross of the Lord can be understood in two ways. For he is rightly said to glory in the cross of the Lord who with joy, and with hope of the future life, imitates the passion of the Lord, and this is to glory in tribulations. Someone also is said to glory in the cross of the Lord who does not judge himself worthy of salvation, except through the merit of the Lord's passion, saying with the apostle Peter: *Because there is no other name under heaven whereby we must be saved [n: (Act. IV)]*: these things I say without prejudice to a better opinion.

[0550D]QUESTION XXVII. *For I am jealous of you with the jealousy of God,* etc. [n: (II Cor. XI.)] It is asked: What is jealousy? Solution. Jealousy is a motion of the mind toward good or toward evil on account of another's state. When it is toward good, then it is of love; when it is toward evil, then it is of envy.

QUESTION XXVIII. It is asked likewise: What is it to be jealous with the jealousy of God? Solution. To love unto the honor of God, or with that jealousy which God inspires.

QUESTION XXIX. *For I have espoused you to one husband, that I may present you a chaste virgin to Christ.* It is asked concerning this espousal of the Apostle, since not all who are in the Church are virgins, as the married, how then can he present those who are not virgins as a virgin, as it were one, and moreover chaste? Solution. Virginity is twofold: [0551A] of body and of mind: the virginity of the flesh is the body untouched; the virginity of the mind is the incorrupt integrity of the inward man. But this is required of every believer, without which that which is of the flesh does not profit. But all are called one virgin on account of the unity of entire faith, of solid hope, of sincere love. Chaste, not having the heat of an evil will.

QUESTION XXX. *Satan transformeth himself into an angel of light,* etc. It is asked whether it is perilous to believe Satan to be an angel of light, when he says or does those things which befit the good. Solution. If, at the time when he says or does those things which are fitting, there is no error, it is not perilous; but when he begins to lead in his deceits, that no one may go after him, there is need of vigilance: which is not done without God.



## III. ON THE SECOND EPISTLE TO THE CORINTHIANS. (cont.)

[0551B]QUESTION XXXI. [ *In hunger, and thirst,* etc. It is asked where is the promise of God who says: *First seek the kingdom of God, and all these things shall be added unto you [n: (Matth. VI)]*. For the promise seems to have wavered, since the Apostle says that he labored in hunger, and thirst, in cold and nakedness. Solution. That physician, to whom we have once committed ourselves wholly, and from whom we have the promise of the present life and of the future, knows when to apply these aids, or to withdraw them, as he judges to be expedient for us?

QUESTION XXXII. *Through a window in the gate by the brethren.* It is asked whether this deed was praiseworthy, or worthy of reproach: which seems [so] to some, because he was not freed by the help of God. Solution. The help of God is not needed before human aid has failed; nor ought anyone to await [0551C] the help of God, while he has something he can do, lest he seem to tempt God.

QUESTION XXXIII. *And thus I escaped his hands.* It is asked whether the Apostle acted, in fleeing, as a good shepherd, or as a hireling. Did he not, when the wolf came, desert the sheep, and flee? and thus it seems that he was not a good shepherd, but a hireling. Solution. When any shepherd is specially sought by persecutors, it is permitted him to give way, and to avoid the fury of the persecutors, and by fleeing to preserve himself for the benefit of the whole flock, and meanwhile let the others, who are not so sought after, furnish food to their fellow-servants. But when a common peril presses upon all, let those, who need others, not be deserted by those whom they need.

QUESTION XXXIV. *Whether in the body, or out of the body, [0551D] I know not, God knoweth,* etc. [n: (II Cor. XII.)] It is asked how the Apostle could have doubted whether he was caught up in the body, or out of the body, since no one existing in this life can see God as he is; whence he says to Moses: *Man shall not see me, and live [n: (Exod. XXXIII)]*. Likewise, if he was caught up out of the body, namely so that the soul was separated from the body, was his body meanwhile dead? Likewise, if he saw God with an intellectual vision, then he saw him truly, and had that knowledge in which is the highest beatitude, and thus he was in beatitude; but beatitude once had is never lost; or if God took from him what he had already received, it seems that God acted unjustly. Solution. Either could have happened: either that the soul separated from the body [0552A] saw God in itself, or that the soul, free from the senses of the body, contemplated God thus in the body itself; and since either of these could rightly have happened, the Apostle, in doubt, says whether in the body, or out of the body. Whence, whichever of these it was, that authority will be preserved: *Man shall not see me, and live,* because in either way he could have put off the man. But if he was out of the body, then the body was dead, and separated from the soul, and again quickened when the soul returned. And since he had full knowledge of God, and thus was in beatitude, if again the same glory was withdrawn from him, it is not to be wondered at, nor incredible, because thus God could show beforehand to his most beloved servant the beatitude and joy to come, which he was to receive, [0552B] that he might endure more firmly in his love and service, and desire more vehemently the very time of receiving it: just as, transfigured on the mountain, he showed the glory of his humanity to his three disciples.

QUESTION XXXV. But by the third heaven, and paradise, into which he was caught up, he understands the same thing, namely the full understanding, or knowledge, of the divinity. Note the fourfold opinion concerning the three heavens. According to the first opinion, the first heaven is the airy, whence [are] the birds of heaven. The second is the firmament, whence also he called the firmament heaven. The third is the empyrean, which as soon as it was made was filled with angels, where the angels, and the holy souls, enjoy the contemplation of God. According to the second opinion, the first heaven is corporeal vision, by which [0552C] heaven, and earth, and all things visible to the eyes are discerned. By the same vision certain things are sometimes seen by the gift of God, as Elisha [saw] the fiery chariots [n: (IV Reg. II)], and Balthasar the hand writing on the wall, *mane, thecel, phares [n: (Dan. V)]*. The second heaven is imaginary, or spiritual, vision, by which are seen not bodies, but the images of them: as is wont [to happen] in dreams, or in ecstasy, as Pharaoh [saw] the ears of grain [n: (Gen. XLI)], and Peter the vessel [n: (Act. X)]. The third is intellectual vision, by which neither bodies, nor images of bodies are seen, but incorporeal and immaterial things are beheld by the instinct of the mind, as substance, the Godhead, and every affection of the soul. The third opinion calls the three heavens the threefold hierarchy of angels according to Dionysius. The first, in the ascent, is that which contains the angels, and archangels, [0552D] and virtues. The second, powers, principalities, and dominions; the third, thrones, and cherubim, and seraphim. This third hierarchy, therefore, in the ascent, and the first in the descent, the Apostle calls the third heaven, or paradise: to which, when he says he was caught up, he shows that he saw God immediately, face to face. The fourth opinion is, that the first heaven is called the knowledge of heavenly bodies, the second [the knowledge] of heavenly spirits, the third the knowledge of the Godhead.

QUESTION XXXVI. *I know such a man,* etc. It is asked how he says a man was caught up whether in the body, or out of it, since man subsists in body and soul, how then does he say that a man can be caught up out of the body. Solution. The truth of man consists there, where is [0553A] the image, and likeness of God. Whence [comes] that saying: The mind of each is the man himself. By the name of man, therefore, he calls the inner man.

QUESTION XXXVII. *There was given to me a thorn of my flesh, an angel,* etc. It is asked by whom this thorn was given to him, whether by God, or by the devil. If by God, how is it called an angel of Satan, as though sent by him? Likewise, if sent by the devil, how is that true which follows: *Lest the greatness of the revelations exalt [me],* etc. Did Satan then buffet the Apostle through an angel sent by himself, lest he be exalted into pride? Solution. Both by God, and by the devil, was he sent who vexed the Apostle, but for one purpose by God, and for another by the devil. By God he was sent for this, lest the greatness of the revelations exalt him. By the devil, for this, that he might drag him to [0553B] failing. By God also he was sent for this, that virtue might be perfected in weakness.

QUESTION XXXVIII. *For which thing I besought the Lord thrice,* etc. The devil sought to tempt Job, and was heard [n: (Job I)].
