The Apostle asked that the angel of Satan might depart from him, and was not heard. Where then is divine justice? Was it just that the devil be heard, and not the Apostle? Solution. Those whom God has purposed to heal, he does not always hear according to their will, but according to their health. But some, in wrath, he sometimes hears according to their will, as the devil.

QUESTION XXXIX. Likewise, since the Apostle knew that this infirmity was given him for his profit, and for the preservation of humility, it is asked whether he reasonably prayed [0553C] that such a temptation might depart from him. Solution. Although he knew this, yet as a man he feared a fall from the affliction, [0554A] and so from fear he was humbled; but humility drove out the disease of proud elation, and by this dispensation of divine providence, that thorn was given him.

QUESTION XL. *And they did not do penance,* etc. It is asked whether penance is necessary for one amending his morals for the better; and in what ways penance is performed. Solution. It does not suffice to change one's morals for the better, and to withdraw from evils, unless by the sorrow of penance, and the groaning of humility, and the sacrifice of a contrite heart, satisfaction be made for the fault. Likewise it is to be noted that penance is performed in three ways: before baptism, and after baptism, for the graver [sins], and daily for the lighter and more frequent, according to that saying: You have avoided great things, see that you be not crushed by the sand.

QUESTION XLI. *We pray God, that you do no evil* [0554B] [n: (II Cor. XIII)]. Here the Apostle intimates that only by the grace of God is one turned aside from evil, saying: We pray, etc. For outward planting and watering avail nothing without the inward increase: which God gives by grace alone. It is asked, then, why in the sacred Scriptures we are often commanded both to turn aside from evil, and to do good: since free will is sufficient for neither of these, since it is the work of grace alone, proper to it, both this and that. Solution. The will does something, but does not do it alone. Therefore when it is commanded that this or that be done, we ought to recognize free will; but when prayer is made, the benefit of grace is sought. Concerning grace and free will it has already been said in what is above, and likewise what grace works of itself without the will, [0554C] and what it does not work without it; nor is there need that the same things be repeated again.



## IV. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE GALATIANS.

[0553]

[0553C] *[n: (Galat. I.)] Paul the apostle,* etc. This Epistle the Apostle sends to the Galatians: who, coming from Gaul, mingled themselves with the Greeks in a certain province of Greece. Whence that province was first called Gallograecia, then Galatia. Whence, since the Greeks are of sharp intellect, these [being] foolish, and slower to understand, are held to be like the unteachable Gauls. These were first instructed by the Apostle in the faith, [0553D] and in evangelical doctrine, [and] afterwards were subverted in many ways by false apostles, so that they believed the grace of Christ did not suffice for salvation without the law of Moses. Whence it is plain what the special matter is, namely the state of the Galatians in which they then were; but the general matter [is] the common state of the Church. But the intention of the Apostle in this Epistle is to recall the Galatians, deceived by the tricks of the false [apostles], to the truth of the catholic faith and of evangelical doctrine. The manner [is] such: he prefaces a greeting, where against the detractors, and those glorying in the works of the law, he briefly touches upon his own dignity, and the grace of Christ: commending his own person, when the false [apostles] disparaged [him]. After the salutation he reproves them for their fickleness: afterwards he commends his own person more fully. Then he disproves the law of Moses: [0554C] teaching that it is not to be kept after Christ, because not only does it not profit unto salvation and justice, but even hinders. Afterwards he commends the Gospel and the faith of Christ, which suffices for salvation.

QUESTION I. *Who gave himself for our sins, that he might deliver us from the present world,* etc. Since the world or age is the work of God, who is the author of good things [0554D] only, it is asked here how the whole world is set in evil, or how the age is called wicked. Solution. Not only places, but also times and instruments of evils contract the infamy of those [evils] which are done in them: whence [there are] evil days, and perilous times [so] called. Woodland passes too, full of robbers, are called evil; and the sword, by which blood is shed, and the cup, by which poison is administered; and thus the world, or age, obtains the name of wickedness on account of the things which are done in it.

QUESTION II. *But although we, or an angel, preach to you beside that,* etc. Were there not many things which he had not yet preached to them, which are to be believed and held by little ones, who are to be nourished with the milk of simple doctrine? Solution. He does not say more than you have received, but beside [0555A] that, he says, by which he understands the contrary. Whence he promised he would come to some, that he might fulfill the things that were lacking to them.

QUESTION III. *If I yet pleased men,* etc. The question, which is wont to be raised here, has been resolved in the first Epistle to the Corinthians. But someone will say: Whatever has been said elsewhere, I would like to hear how both are true, *if I yet pleased men, I should not be the servant of Christ;* and that: *Please all men in all things [n: (I Cor. X)]*. He himself does not wish to please men, that he may be the servant of Christ. But to us he commands that we please all men. Does he not wish us not to be servants of Christ, since we cannot [do] both, both please them, and be servants of Christ? Whence it is written: *He hath scattered the bones of them that please men* [0555B] [n: (Psal. XXV)]. The same question arises from the diverse words of the Lord. For in one place he says: *Let your light shine before men [n: (Matth. V)]*; and in another: *Do not your justice before men [n: (Matth. VI)]*. Solution. We are admonished nothing else, whether by the words of the Lord, or by the words of the Apostle, save that we place not the end of good works in the praise of men, and that we not desire the same as it were for the reward of good works.

QUESTION IV. *I persecuted the Church of God,* etc. It is asked whether the Apostle sinned by persecuting the Church of God, since he had zeal for the law, and in doing this believed it ought to be done for God's sake. For everyone is bound to do that which conscience dictates [0555C] ought to be done for God's sake, and so if he did not do it, he seems to offend God by contempt. Likewise, who would doubt that to persecute the Church is evil? Solution. Some say that, whether he did this or that, he would sin. But others say that the zeal, which the Apostle had, was good, but that work was evil, and of error, namely the persecution of the Church.

QUESTION V. *But I saw none other of the apostles, save James the brother of the Lord.* Why James the less, son of Alphaeus, is called the brother of the Lord is wont to be asked. Solution. Some say that he was called the brother of the Lord because he was the son of Joseph by another wife, who was thought to be the father of the Lord: but this is not established, since Joseph was believed to be a virgin; another solution therefore is to be sought. [0555D] It is to be known, then, that Mary the mother of the Lord was the daughter of Joachim and Anna, who married Joseph, and so Joseph was the putative father of the Lord. But when Joachim was dead, Cleophas the brother of Joseph took the same Anna to wife, and begot of her a daughter, whom he called Mary, who married Alphaeus, who begot sons, namely James, Joseph, Simon, and Jude. But when Cleophas was dead, a certain Salomas married the same Anna, and begot of her a daughter by name Mary, who married Zebedee: and had of him sons, James, who was called the Greater, and John the evangelist. Three husbands, therefore, Anna had, and three daughters. Now it is to be seen why James [son] of Alphaeus was also called the less, [and] the brother of the Lord. He was called the less in comparison with the other, who first clung to the Lord, [0556A] and therefore was called the greater, not according to the time of birth, but of conversion. But he was called the brother of the Lord, according to some, because he was the son of his maternal aunt, or on account of the likeness of his sanctity, or rather because he was the nephew of the paternal uncle of Christ, that is, of Cleophas. For the Hebrews call those joined by natural consanguinity on the fathers' side brothers. It is to be noted that in the Scriptures men are called brothers in four ways. By nature, as Esau and Jacob; by nation, as all the Jews are called brothers among themselves; by kinship, as all those who are of the same family; when from one root a multitude spreads out, as Scripture calls Abraham, and Lot, and Jacob, and Laban brothers. By affection, all Christians [are brothers], partakers of the same grace, having the same heavenly Father.

[0556B]QUESTION VI. *Thou compellest the Gentiles to judaize [n: (Galat. II)]*, etc. Concerning this rebuke it is wont to be asked: was it true, or dispensatory, and did Peter sin, and was he truly reprehensible? Solution. In the answering of this question those two illustrious lights, Jerome and Augustine, seem to disagree. Jerome says that that rebuke was dispensatory, and not true, and that Peter did not sin, nor was reprehensible. But Augustine asserts that the rebuke was true, and not feigned, and that Peter was truly reprehensible; nor did he walk according to the truth of the Gospel; not in this, that he became weak to the weak, but because by his example he compelled the Gentiles to judaize: otherwise the consequence will be that Paul wrote what was false, which is by no means to be believed. [0556C] Likewise concerning the abolition of the legal [observances] after Christ, the same two masters nonetheless do not think the same. For Jerome says that after Christ those legal observances are deadly. Augustine says that it was permitted the Jews then, in the primitive Church, to observe them, only let them not put hope in them. For before the coming of Christ they seem to have been necessary; on the very border of law and grace [they were] indifferent, if hope be not put in them; but now they are deadly. Note that a dispensation is the concession of an inferior state for the sake of avoiding scandal, in which a lesser evil is done, that a greater may be avoided. Saving the reverence of [these] secret [matters], we prefer the opinion of blessed Augustine to the opinion of blessed Jerome upon the aforesaid rebuke and abolition of the law. [0556D] Whence to the objections of blessed Jerome we answer thus. The first is: Christ is the end of the law, that is, the consummation and fullness fulfilling the law, and consummating [it] in himself, and in his own. Yet not so that through no interval of time could the law, after the coming of Christ, lawfully be kept by anyone. Likewise, *the law and the prophets until John.* Venerable old man Jerome, answer me with pure understanding, how is this to be understood, the law and the prophets until John? Is it thus, that after John it was not lawful to keep the law? Which seems [to follow] according to your disputation. But did not Christ even after John keep the law, celebrating the old pasch? Did Christ do what was not fitting? It is therefore to be understood thus: *the law and the prophets until John,* that is, from John the grace of the New Testament [0557A] began both to be preached and to be shown forth: and from then the Old Testament began to cease. Likewise at that time it was not heresy to observe the legal ceremonies, though it would be now, especially if anyone believed that grace did not suffice for salvation without the law. Likewise if they are to be observed, they bring salvation. Do we not observe certain things, which do not confer salvation, but which we can, at our own discretion, use, and not use?

QUESTION VII. *Man shall not be justified by the works of the law, but by the faith of Christ.* It is asked how faith justifies and not works, since God renders to each according to his works. Is there not, from the fact that there is merit and reward, [also] crown and justice? If then the crown is from works, it seems that justice is from the same. Solution. In the Epistle to the Romans it has been disputed [0557B] according to our measure concerning this question: here let it now suffice to say: faith is said to justify for this reason, because from the certitude of things invisible the eternal goods are loved; and love justifies. He says therefore that we are justified from faith, because it is first, from which the rest are obtained. Nor, when he says that we are justified from faith, are good works made void, but he says this for the reason that the works themselves are from the grace of faith.

QUESTION VIII. *If I build again the things which I destroyed, I make myself a prevaricator.* But someone will say: Did he not destroy the faith which he assailed, and again build it up by preaching, and thus seem to be a prevaricator? Solution. He who destroys a false thing, which can be destroyed, if he build it again, is a prevaricator. But faith cannot be destroyed, though [0557C] it can be assailed. Although, therefore, Paul first tried to destroy our faith, and again to build it up, yet he was not a prevaricator.

QUESTION IX. Likewise it is objected concerning the same thing thus: *If I build again the things which I destroyed.* Behold he manifestly says that he destroyed the legal [observances]; but elsewhere he says: *Do we then destroy the law? God forbid! but we establish the law [n: (Rom. III)]*. How then is each true? Solution. In two ways is anyone said to destroy the law. He destroys the law who says it useless in its state before the incarnation of the Word, and asserts it was not given by God, and in this way Paul did not destroy the law. He also is said to destroy the law, who shows that after the coming of Christ it is not to be kept according to the carnal [sense], [0557D] and in this way Paul did destroy the law, saying: *If you be circumcised, Christ shall profit you nothing.*

QUESTION X. Is Christ then the minister of sin, since the law is good, and the commandment good, just, and holy? how [is] Christ [the minister of sin], if he administers the law, and [that] sin? Did God, when he gave the law, administer sin? Solution. The law indeed [is] good, yet the occasion of sin: according to which reasoning the minister of the law is called the minister of sin.



## IV. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE GALATIANS. (cont.)

QUESTION XI. *For through the law I am dead to the law,* etc., that is, through the authority of the law I have dismissed it: but by what authority, it is asked. Solution. Moses says: *I will raise up for you a prophet from your brethren, whom you shall hear as me [n: (Deut. XVIII)]*. And Jeremiah: *I will complete a new testament with the house of Israel.* And David: [0558A] *And holocaust for sin thou didst not require: then said I: Behold I come [n: (Psal. XXXIX)]*. From this too, that it is written in the law: *Thou shalt hate thine enemy [n: (Matth. V)]*, since no one can be saved with hatred of an enemy, it is established that the law justifies no one. And therefore from it one must flee to the grace which justifies. And because the law restrained only the hand, and not the mind, in the doing of outward things, and set all its worshippers under a curse, it is clear that through it one must die to it, that one may live in God.

QUESTION XII. *Who loved me, and delivered himself for me,* etc. Recall to memory that question, how the Father, the Son, and Judas concur in the delivering up of the Son, treated upon the Epistle to the Romans according to our ability. For if [0558B] justice [is] through the law, then Christ died in vain: but Christ did not die in vain, that is, without cause, or benefit: therefore justice is not from the law. Reader, recall the authority to memory as often as it is needful for you to prove that justice is not from the law: this I say, because there are authorities by which it seems it could be shown that justice is from the law: as was shown above.

QUESTION XIII. *Who hath bewitched you that you should not believe the truth,* etc. It is asked what bewitching is. Solution. Magical mockery, by which things are shown to the eyes of men otherwise than they are: [this] is called a bewitching, or bewitchment: or commonly [it is called] fascination, which harms infants. For the eyes of certain [persons] are said to burn by their gaze, and this act is reckoned a bewitching. Thus envy harms not only [0558C] the envious man, pining away at another's happiness, but also those in whom some good things begin to be. Whence it is written: *The bewitching of trifling obscureth good things [n: (Sap. IV)]*.

QUESTION XIV. *They who are of faith shall be blessed: they who are of the works of the law are under a curse.* It is asked what it is to be of faith, [and] what it is to be of the works of the law. Solution. They are of faith whose being depends on faith, that is, who through faith tend to true being, and who through the grace of faith seek to be justified. And these alone shall obtain from God the blessing of eternal life. But they are said to be of the works of the law, who seek to be justified from them; and therefore they are under a curse, as transgressors of the law.

QUESTION XV. *Cursed is everyone who abideth not [0558D] in all things which are written in the book of the law, to do them.* It is asked whether God commanded anything in the law which cannot be fulfilled? If it be said nothing, how are all, who are of the works of the law, under a curse? Likewise, if God commanded man something which he himself would not [be willing to] do, God seems unjust and cruel. Solution. Some say that nothing was commanded in the law which man cannot fulfill. Against whom the expositor says, that God commanded many things, all of which no one could fulfill. Whence the apostle Peter: *Why tempt you God, to put upon us a yoke, which neither we, nor our fathers were able to bear [n: (Act. XV)]*.

QUESTION XVI. *Cursed is everyone that hangeth on a tree.* It is asked whether Christ is contained under this universality of the curse [0559A]? But he is the one who is over all things God blessed forever; through whom every curse is taken away, and who blessed us with every blessing in the heavenly [places]. For he himself [is] the promised seed of Abraham, in whom is [made] the blessing of all nations. How then can he be cursed? Solution. There is one curse of fault, which is far from Christ: there is another of punishment, which Christ voluntarily took upon himself, made for us a curse, or sin, that is, a victim for sin: that he might take away from the midst the curse both of fault and of punishment. It can also be said that Christ was cursed with the curse of fault, not truly, but according to the opinion of men: whence he was reputed as a sinner with the wicked.

[0559B]QUESTION XVII. *For if the inheritance [be] from the law, [it is] not from the promise.* Concerning this consequence it is asked whether it is true. For its truth it requires that to be from the law and [to be] from the promise be opposites: otherwise it would not follow, if from the law, [then] not from the promise. What is it, then, [for] the inheritance to be from the law: what is it to be from the promise? what also does he call the inheritance? Solution. By inheritance he calls eternal life: to be from the law, is to be from the works of the law, for the fulfilling of which man seems to himself to suffice, nor to need the grace of God, which is [for] the inheritance to be from merits. But for the inheritance to be from the promise, this is, to be from grace. See then, that to be from the law, and [to be] from the promise are opposites. Whence elsewhere the Apostle says: *If from works, [then] now not from grace [n: (Rom. XI)]*, that is, if from debt, [0559C] [then] not from grace.

QUESTION XVIII. *What then [is] the law?* Since the former saints, who were before the law, were just through the grace of faith, and obtained the promise, it is asked why the law was given? and what usefulness it conferred? The solution of this question the Apostle sets down, saying: *The law was set because of transgression,* which can be understood in two ways, namely for the restraining of transgression, that at least through fear men might cease to transgress, so that at some time they might do the same voluntarily; or the law was set because of transgression, that is, that it might make men transgress, and thus they might be humbled, and seek a physician, and implore the help of grace. It was given therefore that it might humble the proud, and betray [their] weakness, [0559D] and [be] a scourge to the hard and a sign of things to come.

QUESTION XIX. It is asked why the law was not given immediately after the fall of man? or why the Son himself did not come at once? or why he permitted so many men to perish? Solution. By great counsel this was done, that after the fall of man the law was not immediately given, or the Son sent. For unless the pride of man first made trial of the powers of his own choice, it would arrogate sufficiency to [its] liberty, and would judge that the law was given superfluously, and that the Son came in vain. Nor did all, who then were, perish: just as neither are all, who now are, made saved. And so this was done, that man might recognize the disease of his own weakness, and implore the grace necessary for him. For in the natural law he was left to himself, [0560A] that thus he might make trial of the powers of nature, and was convicted of ignorance, and confessed [it], because the light of his eyes failed: yet still he believed that he had a virtue, by which he could fulfill whatever was necessary for salvation. Whence also he said: *There is not lacking one who fulfills, but there is lacking one who commands.* As if he said: I need knowledge, not virtue. And so the law was given, which might illuminate ignorance, but not aid weakness: which uncovered sin, but did not consume it, by the giving of which the disease grew strong, and concupiscence was increased, not by the fault of the law, but of nature, and by the instigation of the devil, that thus, the insufficiency of both laws being recognized, and his own weakness, [man] might cry to the physician, and seek the help of grace: and thus, with weaknesses multiplied, they hastened to the physician, who coming in the form [0560B] of a servant healed the wounds of the sick. For this is that Samaritan, who drew near to the wounded man, who fell among robbers, and bound up his wounds: whom the priest, and the levite, that is the law and the priesthood, passed by [n: (Luc. X)]: because *the law brought no one to perfection [n: (Hebr. VII)]*. This is Elisha, who after sending the staff not effective of health, came to raise up the dead son of the Shunammite [n: (IV Reg. IV)]. This is the Angel of great counsel, who descended into the pool, and when the water was moved one was healed [n: (Joan. V)]. This is the omnipotent Word, who came from the royal seats, while a middle silence held all things [n: (Sap. XVIII)]. Note that there are three silences. The first silence is the ignorance of languor, which was under the natural law. The second silence is the despair of salvation, [0560C] which was under the written law, and this silence is the middle one. The third silence is the attainment of health: which will be in the glory of eternal beatitude. While therefore all things held a middle silence, that is, despaired of salvation: the Son of the most high King descended from the light of heaven to the darkness of the world or of hell. And coming he spoke peace, gave grace, set forth mercy, promised pardon. And thus, the middle silence being broken, the sick began with pure faith, and true confession, as with great cries to demand a remedy, and to hasten to the physician, by whom the sick might be healed, and [their] wounds cured.

QUESTION XX. *The law was set in the hand of a Mediator.* It is asked why Christ is called a Mediator? Solution. [0560D] Because he mediates us, that is, reconciles [us] to God.

QUESTION XXI. But since not only the Son reconciles us to himself, but also God the Father, as the Apostle says: *God was in Christ, reconciling the world to himself [n: (II Cor. V)]*, it is asked why the Son alone is called the Mediator of God and men. Solution. The whole Trinity by the exercise of [its] power reconciles us to itself: but the Son alone by the fulfilling of obedience, and by the undertaking of the sacraments, justifies and reconciles us. Whence not undeservedly he alone is called Mediator.

QUESTION XXII. Since Christ [is] God, and man, the mediator of God and men, it is asked according to which nature, whether according to the divine, or according to the human, or according to both, he is Mediator. Solution. The authors say that he is not mediator according to [0561A] that which is God, but only according to that which is man, drawing near to us by mortality, [and] to God by justice.

QUESTION XXIII. *But scripture hath concluded all under sin.* It is asked what he calls scripture. Solution. The law, which elsewhere he calls the letter, here the Apostle names scripture: because it only commands, [and] does not help: it convicts of disease the sick man, who seemed to himself whole, and thus by showing sins, and not taking [them] away, concludes all under sin. The law was given therefore, that grace might be sought: and grace was conferred, that through it the law might be fulfilled: to this accords what he says elsewhere: *God hath concluded all in unbelief, that he might have mercy on all [n: (Rom. XI)]*.

QUESTION XXIV. *As many of you as have been baptized [0561B] in Christ, have put on Christ.* It is asked concerning him, who feignedly approaches baptism, whether he is baptized in Christ, and has put on Christ. For if it be said so, it follows that he is conformed to Christ: but if he is not baptized, [and] if he repent of his feigning, he will be able to be baptized, but this is not granted him: it is established therefore that if baptized. Solution. To be baptized in Christ, and to put on Christ is understood in two ways, either by the reception of the sacrament, which is common to good and bad; or by inward sanctification, and conformity of life, which belongs to the good alone.



## IV. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE GALATIANS. (cont.)

QUESTION XXV. It is asked how Christ is the garment of the saints, and whether the saints are the garment of Christ? Solution. Christ is called the garment of the saints, and the saints also the garment [0561C] of Christ. But in one way and another. For Christ is called the garment of the saints through the overshadowing of the Holy Spirit, protecting them from the heat of vices: but the saints [are] the garment of Christ, as it were having him within themselves, and surrounding [him]. Also they honor him by their holy manner of life: as the wicked, by living wickedly, blaspheme [him].

QUESTION XXVI. *If you [be] Christ's, then [you are] Abraham's,* etc. Christ is called the seed of Abraham, and the faithful are called the seed of Abraham. Whence it is asked whether according to the same signification both Christ and the faithful are called the seed of Abraham? Solution. Christ is the seed of Abraham corporally, because born of his stock: the faithful are the seed of Abraham spiritually, that is, just through [0561D] faith, as he was.

QUESTION XXVII. *We were serving under the elements of the world [n: (Galat. IV)]*. If then the Jews also served under the elements, in what did they differ from the pagans? Solution. The Jews under the elements served God, not the elements themselves: but the pagans exhibited divine worship not to God, but to the elements themselves.

QUESTION XXVIII. *When the fullness came,* etc. It is asked why the coming of the Savior is called the fullness of time? Solution. For this reason, because at this time are fulfilled the things which in preceding times had been foretold, and it would seem more properly said the time of fullness, than the fullness of time, and the end of the age is called the same.

QUESTION XXIX. *He sent his Son,* etc. It is asked [0562A] whence and whither the Son was sent. Hear: *I came forth from the Father, and came into the world [n: (Joan. XVI)]*.

QUESTION XXX. But someone will say: Was he not *in the world, and the world was made by him [n: (Joan. I)]*. Was he then sent thither, where he was before? Solution. He was before in the world by power, and by essence, but he began to be otherwise in the world, made visible through the undertaking of the servile form. As therefore when he came forth from the Father, he did not desert the Father; so he came into the world, in which he was before. But why the mission of the Son, or of the Holy Spirit, since it is the work of the Trinity, is attributed to the Father, has already been said in what precedes.

QUESTION XXXI. *Made of a woman.* Since the Lord was born of a virgin, it is asked why the Apostle asserts [him] made of a woman? Solution. He used the Hebrew [0562B] manner of speech, which puts woman for female, as Eve in Genesis, not yet having suffered intercourse, is called a woman. Note that the expositor says that the Creator, who always was, was made, that he might be a creature; because he was made man, that he might become what he was not; not that what he was might perish; this I say on account of certain [persons], who deny that he was made anything.

QUESTION XXXII. *Made under the law, that he might redeem them, who were under the law,* etc. Did he not by his death redeem both those who were under the law, and those who were without the law? How then does the Apostle say Christ [was] made under the law, that he might redeem those who were under the law, as though he redeemed them through the observance of the law? Solution. He was made under the law, that he might fulfill it, and having fulfilled [it] make it cease; and thus also [0562C] redeem the Jews from the prevarication of the law. For unless he kept the law, in which the promise was made, who would believe that he was the seed of Abraham promised to Abraham, in whom not only the Jews, but also all the nations should be blessed?

QUESTION XXXIII. *That we might receive the adoption of sons.* What does he call adoption; whether the gratuitous goods pertaining to present justice, or to future glory, through which we are made sons? Solution. Some think a distinction is to be made between the sons of adoption and the sons of grace, but I judge them to be the same.

QUESTION XXXIV. *God sent the spirit of his Son,* etc. Note that the Trinity is here manifestly signified. There are some, who say, that as the eternal generation of the Word [0562D] is from the Father alone, so also is his temporal mission; because according to these, for the Father to send the Son, is [for the Son] to be from the Father, and to appear to us in the flesh assumed. Similarly they assert concerning the Holy Spirit, that as his eternal procession is from the Father, and the Son, and not from himself, so also [is] the temporal: but Augustine manifestly says that the temporal mission both of the Son and of the Holy Spirit is the work of the Trinity; whence the Son himself says that he was sent by the Holy Spirit.

QUESTION XXXV. *Crying: Abba, Father.* It is asked why the Apostle set down two words signifying the same thing; for one seems to be set down superfluously. Solution. He does this for this reason, that he might intimate two peoples joined by one faith. For the Hebrew name signifies the Jews, the Greek word the gentile people; [0563A] the same signification of both words figures the unity of faith and of the Spirit.

QUESTION XXXVI. *To those, who by nature are not gods.* Here he intimates that the nature of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit is one. For if the Son is not by nature God, then [he is] neither to be worshipped, nor adored. But it is objected to us thus: Is not the humanity of Christ worshipped and adored? and yet it is not by nature God. Solution. That which is assumed is adored not on its own account, but on account of the one assuming; therefore we adore the humanity of the Savior not alone and bare, but united to the Godhead.

QUESTION XXXVII. *But rather, being known by God.* Does not God know all things from eternity? How then is God said then as if first to know us, when we begin to believe in him? Solution. By a tropical [0563B] manner of speech what we do by God's authorship, is attributed to him; whence it is said: He asketh for the saints, because he maketh them ask, so [he is said] to know us, because he bestoweth on us the knowledge of himself, and to rest, because he maketh us rest in himself.

QUESTION XXXVIII. *How are you turned again to the weak and needy things?* The Galatians did not before keep the law, how then does he say: *you are turned again to the needy things,* etc. Solution. That he might show that legal observance after the coming of Christ differs little, or nothing, from idolatry. Or he says this for this reason, because they wished not only to keep the law, but were also turned to their former errors; thus by a double error they had been deceived by the false [apostles].

QUESTION XXXIX. *And the seventh of the seventh decade, [0563C] which is called jubilee.* What is this that the expositor says? was not the fiftieth year called jubilee in the law? how then is the seventh year of the seventh decade called jubilee? for the seventh of the seventh decade is the sixty-seventh. Solution. The children of Israel, sometimes oppressed by captivity, could not keep the jubilee year in its order. But in the sixty-sixth year license of returning was given by Cyrus, and Darius, and in part they returned, and kept the sixty-seventh year for the jubilee. But someone will say that before the seventy years were fulfilled, they did not return from captivity. Solution. Not indeed generally and wholly did they return before the seventieth year; yet in the [0563D] sixty-sixth year, as we said before, license of returning was granted to some, which is signified by the Alleluia, which is sung on the Saturday after Good Friday, which is the sixty-sixth day of Septuagesima. But since some were still detained in captivity, there follows the tract, which is a sign of labor, as the Alleluia is a sign of joy. But on the following Saturday there is sung also a second [Alleluia] together with the first Alleluia, prefiguring the general return of the Jews which is [at] the completed seventy years.

QUESTION XL. *If it could have been done, you would have plucked out,* etc. Was not that able to be done which the Apostle says? Solution. Holy Scripture says that thing cannot be done, which is not justly done; whence Job says: *Would that I could kill myself [n: (Job X)]*. And the Lord to Lot: *I cannot [0564A] do anything, until thou enter thither [n: (Gen. XIX)]*. He said that he could not [do] what without doubt he could through power, but not through justice.

QUESTION XLI. *Abraham had two sons.* Did he not have more sons of Cetura after the death of Sara? How then does the Apostle say he had two, as though not more than two? Solution. If more than two, then two; for he does not say only two, but yet rather speaks of these than of the others, because Scripture follows out the particulars concerning these, intimating that something excellent is prefigured.

QUESTION XLII. *But these are the two testaments.* Concerning this and like expressions it is asked how they are true. Is not the figure one thing, the truth another; the signifier one thing, the signified another? how [0564B] then is the name of truth predicated of the name of figure, when it is said: These are the two Testaments, and *the rock was Christ? [n: (I Cor. X.)]* Solution. This word, to be, in places of this kind, is set for to signify.

QUESTION XLIII. Likewise why holy Scripture in such a place uses the substantive verb, can be asked. Would it not be plainer, if it were said: But these signify the two testaments, and the rock figured Christ? Solution. It belongs to words to signify, to things to be, properly. But in theology not only do words have signification, but also things are set for the signification of other things, that is, certain things are signified by other things. As often, therefore, as holy Scripture wishes to show, what thing has to signify what thing, it does not say: this signifies that; but this is that, as the rock [0564C] was Christ; for if it were said the rock signifies Christ, it would seem that the signification of this name rock, and not of this thing the rock, was pointed out.

QUESTION XLIV. Note that above he says, that Ishmael was born according to the flesh; Isaac not according to the flesh, but through promise, but was not Isaac, like Ishmael, born by the intercourse of both sexes? how then was he not born according to nature or according to the flesh? Solution. Ishmael was born by the accustomed law of nature, namely from the concurrence of natural causes; but the generation of Isaac was the operation not of nature, but of divine power, and grace. For to such intercourse, as could be at such an age between old Abraham, and Sara aged and [0564D] barren, nature does not grant children; but what nature denied, grace conferred.

QUESTION XLV. But how then did he, who was born according to the flesh, persecute him who was born according to the spirit, or promise; so also now. It is asked: Where this is found, that Ishmael persecuted Isaac. For in Genesis it is read, that the elder played with the younger [n: (Gen. XXI)]. What evil then did he do? what did he sin? how by merely playing did he persecute him? Solution. The play of the elder with the younger Sara understood to be a mockery. Whence also, indignant, she said: *Cast out the handmaid, and her son. [n: (Ibid.)]* And the Apostle calls such mockery persecution. So also mockers persecute us more than open persecutors.

QUESTION XLVI. *If you be circumcised, Christ [0565A] shall profit you nothing. [n: (Gal. V.)]* Did not the Apostle circumcise Timothy? Then he deceived him, and made [it so] that Christ profited nothing? Solution. He says this of those who sought to be justified from circumcision; and therefore circumcised themselves, and thus fell from grace, which they believed to be insufficient. But to those, who received it out of a certain reverence, yet not putting hope in it, it was not pernicious according to Augustine, but according to Jerome it harmed all who received [it], unless a dispensation were made. Concerning which controversy it has been more fully said above.



## IV. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE GALATIANS. (cont.)

QUESTION XLVII. *I testify to every man circumcising himself, that he is a debtor to do the whole law.* Will he, if he fulfill the whole law, even so be able to obtain justice? Which seems [to follow] from these words, but [0565B] elsewhere it is manifestly held that justice is not from the law. Likewise, is not every man bound to fulfill the law, at least according to the spirit? What is it then that every man circumcising himself owes this, [and] not another? Likewise: Is not everyone bound to love his neighbor as himself? but he who loves his neighbor fulfills the whole law. Does it not seem from these things, that everyone is bound to fulfill the whole law? What is it then that he says: I testify to every man circumcising himself, etc. Solution. He who seeks to be justified from circumcision or from the law, is bound to this, that, in order to have justice, or be worthy of life, he fulfill whatever is commanded in the law, namely that he covet nothing; and love God with his whole heart, in which two consists perfect justice; which, if anyone had, he would not need the grace [0565C] of faith, as neither [do] the angels of God, [a justice] which is granted to no one in the present [life], save only to the Mediator of God and men: whence it is necessary, that if anyone wishes to be justified or beatified, he flee to grace alone; for if he seeks to be justified in the law, that is required of him which cannot be discharged by man.

QUESTION XLVIII. *In Christ Jesus neither circumcision availeth, nor uncircumcision.* The expositor says that for those who are in Christ Jesus vices are to be fled, virtues to be sought; but the middle things [are] neither to be fled, nor to be sought, among which he places circumcision. Whence it seems that it neither profits, nor hurts; but above it was said that circumcision not only does not profit, but even hurts. And the Apostle says: *If you be circumcised, Christ shall profit you nothing.* If then [0565D] it takes Christ from us, it hurts much. Solution. Circumcision simply received, in that the cause of salvation is not placed in it, seems to be indifferent, and so neither profits, nor hinders; but if [received] with this intention, and this mind, that from it salvation be sought, [it] hurts [when] received.

QUESTION XLIX. *The whole law is fulfilled in one word: Thou shalt love thy neighbor as thyself.* Since there are two precepts of love, namely of God and of neighbor, it is asked how in the love of neighbor the whole law is fulfilled? Solution. In the love of neighbor is contained the love of God; for who can love his neighbor for God's sake, unless he love God? Likewise no one can love his neighbor as himself, unless he love [0566A] himself; but no one loves himself, unless he love God: for what is it to love oneself, but to love one's own good? but the true and highest good is Christ. Otherwise he who loves iniquity, hates his own soul.

QUESTION L. It is asked who is [one's] neighbor? Solution. Every man. We are bound therefore by precept to love every man, and to exhibit the offices of piety to everyone in need.

QUESTION LI. *As thyself,* etc. It is asked whether we ought to love [our] neighbor as much as ourselves? Which seems [so], since it is said, as thyself. Solution. "As" is not of quantity, but of quality: this indeed we are bound to do to others, which we wish according to reason to be exhibited to us by others. Note: Since each precept of love is contained in [0566B] each [other], often one is set down rather than two, for if both were set down together, it would seem that one could be had without the other, or could be had.

QUESTION LII. *The flesh lusteth against the spirit.* It is asked how the flesh is said to lust, since the soul alone lusts. Solution. What the soul does from the flesh, this is attributed to the flesh, as the ear is said to hear, the eye to see, since the soul does these things through instruments of this kind.

QUESTION LIII. Since therefore the soul and the spirit are the same according to substance, by what does the soul lust against the spirit; is it against itself? Solution. He calls the lusting flesh the carnal delight, which the soul has from the flesh. But by the name of Spirit he signifies the spiritual delight. [0566C] And on account of these two delights the inner man is divided, and obtains diverse names. For according to the lower delight, he is called now flesh, now man, now soul, or animal, or carnal; but according to the higher he is called spirit, or spiritual, or new man.

QUESTION LIV. *If you be led by the spirit, you are not under the law.* It is asked what it is to be under the law? Solution. To abstain from every evil work by the fear of punishment, not by the love of justice, this is to be under the law. As he is said to be under the law, who seeks to be justified from it, who is a debtor to fulfill the whole law.

QUESTION LV. *But manifest are the works of the flesh, which are fornication,* etc. It is asked how the Apostle [0566D] enumerates among the works of the flesh certain things which are not vices of the flesh, but rather of the soul, as anger, envy, etc. Solution. By the name of flesh he signifies the whole man, who by living according to himself has fallen into these things. For to believe that all vices are from the flesh, is an error, lest the devil, who has no flesh, seem immune from these.

QUESTION LVI. *But the fruit of the spirit is charity.* The expositor says that the Apostle calls the works of the spirit fruits, because they are to be sought for their own sake. But Augustine says that the virtues are to be sought for the sake of beatitude alone, but for their own sake nothing is to be loved, save the highest good, whose fruition makes us blessed. What then is to be said? what is to be held? Solution. The virtues are to be sought for their own sake, because they delight their possessors [0567A] with a sincere delight, but not only for their own sake, but also for the sake of beatitude, which is the supreme end.

QUESTION LVII. *If a man be overtaken in any fault [n: (Gal. VI)]*, etc. It is asked what it is to be overtaken, and what he calls a fault. Solution. He is overtaken, who falls into some fault, or sin, either because he is ignorant of what is to be done or to be omitted, or when he knows [it], but yet weakness impels [him] to sinning. A fault is the desertion of good, as sin is the perpetration of evil, or a fault is what is done ignorantly; sin what [is done] by one knowing, yet indifferently the one is set for the other.

QUESTION LVIII. *Every one shall bear his own burden,* etc. It is asked how both are true. [0567B] *Bear ye one another's burdens,* etc., and this, which he says here: Every one shall bear his own burden? Solution. There are some burdens of the weakness to be shared, of which he treated above; there are others of the account to be rendered to God concerning our acts, of which he treats here. It is true therefore that in the present we ought to help one another; and [that] those who are stronger [ought] to sustain the weaknesses of others; and yet in the future everyone shall bear his own burden, that is, for his own sin and not for the sin of another.

QUESTION LIX. *Let us work good to all,* etc. It is asked whether we are bound by precept to bestow the offices of piety even on enemies? Which seems [so], since the Apostle says: Let us work good to all; and the Lord [0568A] in the Gospel: *Love your enemies; do good to them that hate you [n: (Luc. VI)]*. But Augustine says: To love enemies is not of so great a multitude, as I believe to be heard in the Lord's Prayer, where it is said: *Forgive us our debts, as we also forgive our debtors [n: (Matth. VI)]*. And Gregory says: It suffices not to hate enemies, that is, to will their salvation; even if works of mercy be not bestowed on them. Solution. By precept we are bound to will the salvation of all; but to bestow also the offices of piety on enemies is, as regards the weak and less perfect, counsel, [but] as regards the perfect, precept. Diligently therefore is it to be noted what is said according to counsel, [and] what according to precept.

QUESTION LX. Likewise since it is established, that it is good [0568B] to do good to the wicked, and to sinners, and to the impious, what is it that Scripture says: *Give to the merciful, and receive not the sinner, and to the impious, and to sinners render vengeance, and do good to the humble, and give not to the impious [n: (Eccles. XII)]*. Solution. The sense of the aforesaid authorities is that you do good to no sinner for this reason, because he is a sinner, but because [he is] a man, that is, we ought to foster no one's fault, but [his] nature. In each let us love what God loves, and hate what he hates; but if we cannot help all, both good and bad, we ought to bestow the good things which we can on the servants of God; whence the Apostle: *But most of all to those of the household of the faith.*



## V. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE EPHESIANS.

[0567]

[0567C] *[n: (Ephes. I.)] Paul, an apostle,* etc. This Epistle he writes to the Ephesians, whom Paul did not found in the faith, but, having been founded by John the apostle, he confirmed — men who firmly persisted in faith and good works, whom he exhorts to advance in good things, writing to them from Rome, from prison. And so the matter of the apostle in this epistle is the state of the Ephesians, in which they then were. But the intention is to confirm them in the good things they possess, to spur on [sic: *adulteriora*], and also to form them to humility and the giving of thanks. The mode of proceeding is such: after his manner he sends a salutation beforehand; then he gives thanks, setting forth the manifold benefits of God, conferred by grace alone — now upon the human race, now upon the apostles themselves, now upon the Ephesians themselves. Then he shows the dignity and preeminence of Christ. Afterward he invites them to patience [0567D] and charity, commending the unity of faith and of the Church, and enumerating the gifts of grace; at last, exhorting them to the contest, he describes the armor of the Christian soldiery against the princes of darkness.

QUESTION I. *Blessed be God, who hath blessed us,* etc. Since "to be blessed" is said of God and of man, it is asked whether it is said in the same manner, and according to the same signification. Solution. Otherwise and otherwise of each. [0568C] For God is blessed by man when he is extolled with worthy praises; but man is blessed by God when God imparts to him the gifts of his grace.

QUESTION II. The expositor says that the Apostle here sets down two preordinations: one concerning present justice, the other concerning the future crown. But since the predestination of God is one, which is God himself, it is asked how they are said to be two? Solution. They are not said to be two as though it were not one; but because it has two effects, namely through present justice and through future glory. To which solution it is thus objected: If the number of predestinations is to be assigned according to the number of effects, then there will be infinite predestinations, since the effects of predestination are infinite. Solution. All instances of justice, because they differ in number and not in species, [0568D] are said to be one effect; so also all crowns, because they are distinguished not in species but in number alone, are said to be one effect of predestination. But since present justice and future glory differ not only in number but also in species, they are not incongruously said to be two effects of predestination. It is to be noted that there are many things of this kind which, though they be many in number and one in species, are said to be simply not many, but [0569A] one, according to the usage of speaking; as my faith and his faith, though they be two in number, are nevertheless said to be not two simply, but one, because they are one in species; so of grass, of voice, of understanding, of sight, and of many other things — letter, figure, element, vowel — the like distinction is to be made.

QUESTION III. *As he chose us, that we should be holy,* etc. The Pelagian says that those whom God chose from eternity, he chose them for this reason, because he foreknew that they would be good, and through the choice of free will, not because he was going to sanctify them. But the Apostle destroys this heresy of his when he says: He chose us, that we should be holy *and unspotted;* he does not say because we were going to be holy, but that we should be. For if it were as the Pelagian says, our justification would be the cause [0569B] of the divine election, not the divine election the cause of our justification; and thus that which is temporal would be the cause of that which is eternal, and not conversely: which cannot be.

QUESTION IV. *In whom we have redemption,* etc. It is asked whether he calls redemption and the remission of sins the same thing? for if they seem the same, then one of them is superfluously set down. Solution. By redemption he calls that price by which we were redeemed, through which the power of returning is given to us; but the remission of sins, which is conferred upon us in baptism, is the effect of that redemption itself.

QUESTION V. *To restore [sic: omnio], which are in the heavens,* etc. Since Christ did not die for the angels, it is asked how the things which are in the heavens were restored through Christ? [0569C] Solution. This is said for this reason, because those who are saved by grace fill up the diminished number of the angels; or by the things which are in the heavens he understands the souls which are already in heaven; by the things which are on the earth, the saints still dwelling in this life.

QUESTION VI. *You are sealed with the Holy Spirit, who is the pledge of the inheritance.* It is asked how the Holy Spirit is called the pledge of our inheritance; is not a pledge that which is given for a time for some price, and again, when the price is paid, is taken away? Is the Spirit then taken away from us when the inheritance itself is given to us? Solution. Here "pledge" is set down for "earnest," which is part of the price itself, and is not taken away when the price is paid; whence certain manuscripts have "earnest," not "pledge."

[0569D]QUESTION VII. *Setting him at his right hand,* etc. God is spirit, and is not bounded or enclosed by the form of a body: how then is the Son said to sit at the right hand of the Father, since the Father has no right or left side, because he has no body? Solution. By the right hand of God in Holy Scripture is sometimes signified eternal beatitude, sometimes the equality of the divine nature, sometimes judiciary power. For Christ is at the right hand of the Father, because in the majesty of the Father he remains equal, and because *the Father judgeth no man, but hath given all judgment to the Son [n: (Joan. V)]*. By the right hand of God therefore, or by hand, or arm, or finger, or eye, or ear, and the like, nothing corporeal is to be understood, but the whole spiritually.

[0570A]QUESTION VIII. *Above every name, which is named not only in this world,* etc. Are there not nine orders of angels? Angels, archangels, virtues, powers, principalities, dominations, thrones, cherubim, seraphim — and there are no more than these, which are all named in the present. How then does he say: Set above everything that is named not only in this world, *but also in that which is to come,* as though there were some to be named in the future who are not named in the present. Solution. The denominations of certain angels are given to them according to the offices which they exhibit toward us in the present, as angels, archangels, virtues, powers, principalities, dominations; therefore they are said to be names in the present, because in this manner their denomination looks to the present. [0570B] Whence in the future they will be made void, when Christ shall deliver up the kingdom to God and the Father, when God *shall be all in all.* But the denominations of certain angels look to the future state, as thrones, cherubim, and seraphim; whence they will not be made void even in the future, but above all these orders of the supercelestial spirits the humanity of the Savior has been exalted.

QUESTION IX. *And him he gave to be head over all the Church.* It is asked according to which nature, namely the divine or the human, Christ is the head of the Church. Solution. It can be said that it is according to the divine, according to which he is the head, the beginning, and the author of all the faithful. To which solution it is thus objected: By the same reasoning it can be said that he is the head of a stone, and of every creature, of which he is the author; and nothing would be said [0570C] of the Church. Therefore we say that Christ is properly the head of the Church according to his humanity.

QUESTION X. Why Christ is called the head of the Church. Why he is said to be the head may not unfittingly be asked. Solution. Christ is called the head of the Church for this reason: because as in the head of a man are fully all the carnal senses, namely sight, hearing, smell or scent, taste, and touch; so in Christ is the fullness of all the spiritual senses, namely the fullness of grace, *of whose fullness we all have received [n: (Joan. I)]*; whence we are also called members, as not all having some one sense, just as the other members of the body have but a single sense; none has all except the head.

QUESTION XI. *We were by nature the children of wrath (Ephes. [0570D] II),* etc. God is the author of nature; if therefore we are by nature children of wrath, it seems that we are this with God as author. Solution. Nature is taken in three ways in Holy Scripture, namely for that whole and interrupted good in which man was created, according to which acceptation it is said that every creature of God is good. Nature is also called the corruption of sin, in which every man is conceived, and with which he is born, and thus it is taken here when it is said: We were by nature children of wrath. For the vice has grown ingrained in place of nature: because therefore, on account of the guilt of original sin, with which we are born, we are worthy of Gehenna, therefore we are called by nature children of wrath. Nature is also taken for the remnants of that natural good which remained in us after sin, and thus [0571A] it is taken where it is read that *the Gentiles do by nature the things that are of the law [n: (Rom. II)]*.

QUESTION XII. *To me the least of all the saints is given this grace [n: (Ephes. III)]*. Since the Apostle was not the least in the number of all the saints, but rather among the apostles was one of the first, how does he speak truly in naming himself the least of all. Seek the solution of this question upon that passage: *I am the least of the apostles,* in the First Epistle to the Corinthians.

QUESTION XIII. *The unsearchable riches of Christ,* etc. It is asked how the Apostle understood or evangelized, if the riches are unsearchable. For those things are called unsearchable which cannot be comprehended. Solution. Those things which are by their nature unsearchable were made searchable, through the grace [0571B] and revelation of the Holy Spirit, not only to the Apostle, but also to the rest of the faithful, whom the anointing itself teaches concerning all things.

QUESTION XIV. *Of the hidden mystery,* etc. It is asked, what things are called hidden. Solution. In the world the causes are hidden of all things that come to be naturally. But in God alone are hidden the causes of all things that come to be through grace. Concerning these two kinds of causes something has already been said above.

QUESTION XV. *That it may be made known to the principalities and powers in the heavenly places through the Church,* etc. It is asked whether the mystery of the incarnation was revealed to the supercelestial essences before its fulfillment. In the solution of this question Jerome [0571C] and Augustine seem contrary. For the blessed Jerome says that the angelic dignities did not understand purely the aforesaid mystery; whence they thus ask: *Who is this that cometh from Edom? [n: (Isa. LXIII.)]* And elsewhere: *Who is this king of glory? [n: (Psal. XXIII.)]* But the blessed Augustine says that the mystery of the kingdom of heaven was not hidden from the angels; whence the text is expounded in diverse ways, That it may be made known, etc. But this contrariety, which seems to be between the aforesaid doctors, can be resolved thus: to those who are of greater dignity the aforesaid mystery was revealed; but to others not purely, as the blessed Jerome says. But that the angels grow daily in knowledge, from those things which come to be in the world, many doctors assert with concordant voice. [0571D] Whence we hold this as if for certain, since canonical Scripture seems manifestly to intimate it.



## V. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE EPHESIANS. (cont.)

QUESTION XVI. *Of whom all paternity in heaven and in earth is named.* From this passage we have that not only is God our Father, but also the angels and men are called our fathers. But elsewhere the Lord says: *One is your Father, who is in heaven [n: (Matth. XXIII)]*. If therefore there is one, how are there many? or if many, how one? Solution. As God, who alone truly is, and who alone is truly good, imparts the name of his essence and of his goodness to the rest, so that they too are said both to be and to be good; so also he, who alone is truly Father — of all by creation, and of the faithful by regeneration — has granted the name of paternity to the rest. It is to be known thus, that God the Trinity is the Father of all [0572A] by creation and of the faithful by regeneration. But God unbegotten alone is the Father of the Only-begotten by nature. But the angels are called our fathers by the authority of example, by reason of benefit, by care, and by providence. But men are fathers both by nature, and by the authority of example, and by reason of benefit, and by care and providence.

QUESTION XVII. *What is the breadth, and length, and height.* What is the breadth of love, what the length of eternity, what the height of power, what the depth of knowledge — according to this, which is expounded of God, such can be the understanding.

QUESTION XVIII. *To know also the charity of Christ, which surpasseth knowledge.* If the charity of Christ surpasses knowledge, how can it be known? if it cannot be known, how does the Apostle pray that the disciples may know it? [0572B] Solution. That which human knowledge cannot comprehend is known in part through grace, which the Apostle prays may come to pass.

QUESTION XIX. *That you may be filled unto all the fullness of God,* etc. Shall we not have less than God himself? how then can we be filled unto all the fullness of God? how can anyone have the fullness of God, and not be full God and equal to God? Solution. The sense is, that you may be full of God, not full God. This is to be filled unto all fullness: to have in the present the fullness of virtues, and in the future the fullness of glory; for Paul does not wish that the fullness of the divine nature be conferred upon anyone, but that we be full of God in the present and in the future.

[0572C]QUESTION XX. *One Lord, one faith, one baptism [n: (Ephes. IV)]*. How are they to be understood? Solution. One Lord: the Father, and the Son, and the Spirit in nature, not in person. One faith not in number, but in kind, because it is alike in all, just as of all who will the same thing there is said to be the same will. Concerning faith it has already been spoken. It is called one baptism, because it is equal, and of the same essence by whomsoever it is given, and therefore also because it cannot be reiterated.

QUESTION XXI. *He gave gifts to men.* But does David say: *Thou hast received gifts in men [n: (Psal. LXVII)]*? if he gave, how did he receive? or if he received, how did he give? Solution. As God he gave; he received not only in himself according to that he is man, but also in his members, in which he is, of which receiving [0572D] the Prophet treats: whence he says: Thou hast received gifts in men.

QUESTION XXII. *What gifts he gave in ascending.* It is asked what gifts he gave in ascending. Solution. The Holy Spirit, and also the gifts which are not the Holy Spirit, which when he gives, in them also he gives the Holy Spirit; he does not give separately the Holy Spirit and his gifts which are not himself, but in giving the Holy Spirit he gives the gifts; and in giving the gifts of the Holy Spirit he gives him. This I say on account of those who will not grant that the Holy Spirit is given, since he is immutable, but grant nevertheless that his gifts are given. For the Holy Spirit is then said to be given to us by the Father, and the Son, and even by himself, when he infuses into our hearts through charity this power, [0573A] to the end that we may love God and neighbor.

QUESTION XXIII. Since Christ is God and man, it is asked according to which nature he gave the gifts? Solution. He gave according to that, as the expositor says, according to which he ascended and descended, and thus he gave according to that he is man; because the divinity, which is everywhere, neither descends nor ascends. But this seems to be contrary to the aforesaid, where I asserted that he gave as God, not as man. When therefore it is read that he gave as man or according to his humanity, it ought to be understood thus: he who is man, not because he is man, but because he is God.

QUESTION XXIV. Whether the Deity descended, and how. Likewise another exposition says that Christ descended according to [0573B] his Deity, and ascended according to his humanity, which seems contrary to the foregoing. For it was said that the divinity which is everywhere neither descends nor ascends; but here it is said that the Deity descended: what then is to be held? Solution. When it is said that God descended, this is understood of the descent of the incarnation, by which God was made man. When it is said that man descended, this ought to be understood of the descent to the lower regions, which was only according to the soul, not according to the body, which lay in the sepulcher, nor according to the Deity, which was everywhere.

QUESTION XXV. *Unto a perfect man,* etc. It is asked, by what perfection. Solution. Perfection can be understood neither of the whole Christ, that is, of body and head, nor [0573C] of the individual members. For Christ, consummated in himself, grows and advances in others, but in the future at last he will be perfected, so that nothing be added either in powers or in number. Each one also will then be a perfect man, as much by the consummation of virtues as by the stature of the body. Whence there follows: *Unto the measure of the age of the fullness of Christ.* For each one will rise again in that perfection in which he was, or to which he was going to come, when he was thirty years of age; for not all will be of the same magnitude, nor of that of which Christ was when departing from this life.

QUESTION XXVI. *Be angry, and sin not,* etc. The Apostle permits anger, which cannot be avoided, but the Lord forbids anger, saying: *Whosoever [0573D] is angry with his brother shall be in danger of the judgment [n: (Matth. V)]*. Whence it seems that what the Lord forbids, the Apostle permits. Likewise it was said above: that nothing is forbidden in the New Testament which cannot be avoided, just as nothing is commanded which cannot be fulfilled; which does not seem to be true, if the Lord forbids anger, and no one can avoid being angry. Solution. The first motion of anger, which is not in our power, is permitted by the Apostle: but the Lord does not forbid the first motion, which is called pro-passion, but the will and the purpose of harming another. And so one thing is permitted by the Apostle, and another is forbidden by the Lord, which can be avoided with grace helping.

QUESTION XXVII. What are the gates of death and of life. Note that concupiscence and the fear of the world are the two [0574A] gates of death, through which the devil enters; as the fear and love of God are the gates of life, through which Christ enters. Each stands at the door; and both Christ knocks and the devil: but the enemy is driven out, Christ is brought in.

QUESTION XXVIII. *Grieve not the Holy Spirit,* etc. It is asked how anyone can grieve the Holy Spirit, since he is impassible and cannot receive into himself the passion of sadness. Solution. He has used a certain trope, sufficiently customary in theology, by which those things which come to be in us with God as author are attributed to him; because therefore those whom he fills with charity, as they rejoice over the progress of others, so are grieved over the lapses of those who fall, the Apostle says: Grieve not the Holy Spirit, that is, those in whom [0574B] the Holy Spirit dwells through charity.

QUESTION XXIX. Forgive *one another,* etc. Scripture says: Unless we forgive our fellow servants, God requires again what was remitted; but it is asked how God requires it again? Will God punish again for sins for which satisfaction has already been made through the contrition of the heart, and the confession of the mouth, and through worthy fruits of penance? Will he punish twice for the same thing? Solution. To the end that anyone may bring forth worthy fruits of penance, it is required that henceforth he sin not mortally; whence whoever sins after penance renders his former satisfaction useless to himself.

QUESTION XXX. What is it to punish twice for the same thing? Now to punish twice for the same thing is to punish again in another life for a sin destroyed by penance and not repeated, [0574C] which God does not do. But if any penitent is afflicted for some sin and again repeats the same; if in this life or in another he is punished for it again, this is not to punish twice for the same thing.

QUESTION XXXI. *For an odor of sweetness [n: (Ephes. V)]*, etc. If the death of Christ was a sweet odor to God, then he willingly accepted his death; then the Jews did not sin, who crucified him; because they did that which was good, and which pleased God. Solution. The action of the Jews was evil, nor did that which was done by them please God; but the passion of Christ was good, and the cause of our salvation.

QUESTION XXXII. *The days are evil,* etc. Do not the days have their being from God as author, as it is written: *Thine [0574D] is the day, and thine is the night? [n: (Psal. LXXIII.)]* How then are they called evil? Solution. On account of the malice and misery of men the days are called evil; otherwise, as to the spaces of the hours, they are ordered, and good.

QUESTION XXXIII. *No man ever hated his own flesh,* etc. Do not holy men hate the flesh, persecute, crucify, and mortify it, not nourish nor cherish it? how then is it true, No man ever hated his own flesh? Solution. The saints hate and persecute not the flesh, but the vice of the flesh.

QUESTION XXXIV. *For this cause shall a man leave his father.* It is asked how this pertains to the Son of God, who never forsook the Father, but always remained inseparably with him? Solution. Because the Son did not [0575A] appear to us in the form in which he is equal to the Father, he is said to have forsaken or left the Father; yet he never departed from him: he came into the world, and did not forsake the Father.

QUESTION XXXV. *He that loveth his wife.* It is asked of what love he here treats; for if of the love by which we love one another for the sake of God, the sense is: He that loveth his wife *loveth himself,* that is, does what is useful to himself: according to this he could say the same of any neighbor whatever. Solution. The Apostle says this for this reason, because it is more incumbent upon a man to provide for his wife than for another: whence if for the sake of God he acquires for her the necessaries, and governs her, he also loves himself, that is, does what is useful to himself; because thereby he merits with God.

QUESTION XXXVI. *Servants, obey your masters [n: (Ephes. VI)]*. Here it is asked whether it is lawful for a Christian to have a servant, [0575B] since the Apostle permits this. The Catholic Church too, and especially the Church of the Gauls, receives this. Solution. It would be better not to exact servitude of this kind; nor does the Church receive it as a good, but tolerates it as an evil.

QUESTION XXXVII. *Children, obey your parents; and you, fathers, provoke not your children to anger.* It is asked why the Apostle commands parents to [0576A] love their children, and children to honor their parents, since nature compels them to love one another mutually. Solution. Natural love has no merit unless it be done for the sake of God: therefore he commands that they love one another for the sake of God, and so have merit.



## V. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE EPHESIANS. (cont.)

QUESTION XXXVIII. *Our wrestling is not against flesh.* Do not the saints fight against flesh and blood, against evil men, or against the vices which arise from flesh and blood? how then does he say: *Our wrestling is not,* etc.? Solution. Our wrestling is not only against flesh and blood, but also against demons.

QUESTION XXXIX. *Against the rulers of the world.* Does not God, who founded the world, govern and rule the world, so that the same is founder and ruler? how then does [0576B] the Apostle call the demons rulers of the world? Solution. Here by the name of the world he signifies the lovers of the world. This is the world which is wholly set in wickedness: this is the world in which is found nothing but the concupiscence of the flesh, the concupiscence of the eyes, and the pride of life: this world the Apostle calls darkness, adding *of this darkness,* and elsewhere saying: *You were heretofore darkness; but now light in the Lord [n: (Ephes. V)]*.



## VI. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE PHILIPPIANS.

[0575]

[0575C] *[n: (Philip. I.)] Paul and Timothy,* etc. This epistle the Apostle writes to the Philippians, who are Macedonians. For Philippi is a metropolis city in Macedonia, which is a certain province in Greece, which Philip the father of Alexander built, calling it by his own name. But these, having received the word of preaching from the Apostle, were firm in the faith, and did not receive the false apostles. These therefore he fortifies against a twofold war, namely of tribulations and of false preachers. And so the intention of the Apostle in this epistle is to exhort the Philippians to patience against tribulations, and to constancy against the false apostles. The mode is such: after the customary manner he sends a salutation beforehand. Then he gives thanks for them, imploring for them greater goods, that growing in virtues they may come to perfection. Then [0575D] he admonishes them to the patience of tribulations by his own example and Christ's. Afterward, that they beware of the cunning of the false apostles. At last he interposes an admonition of mortality, and near the end he says that he rejoices concerning the grace which the Romans had sent him through Epaphroditus.

QUESTION I. *For what? so that by every means, whether by occasion or by truth, Christ be announced.* It is asked how the Apostle permits the wicked to preach the evangelical doctrine, since elsewhere it is written: *To the sinner God hath said: Why dost thou declare my justices [n: (Psal. XLIX)]*? etc. Likewise: *Praise is not comely in the mouth of a sinner [n: (Eccli. XV)]*. Likewise: He whose life is despised, it remains that his preaching be neglected. Solution. In such a case the preacher is rebuked on his own account, not on account of others. For what he preaches profits not himself, but others: [0576C] whence the praise of God in the mouth of sinners is not comely. The hirelings therefore are to be tolerated. Whence the Lord: *Upon the chair of Moses have sat the Scribes and Pharisees: what they say, do ye [n: (Matth. XXV)]*, etc. And so the preachers who preach for the truth are to be loved; the hirelings, who from occasion and temporal advantage announce the truth, are to be permitted; thieves and robbers, or wolves, are to be guarded against, nay, to be fled from.

QUESTION II. *What I shall choose, I know not,* etc. It is asked how the Apostle is in doubt which he should choose, whether to remain in the flesh, or to be dissolved and to be with Christ [n: (Philipp. I)], since he knows that the stronger the fight, the greater the crown. Did he not know that it is of greater merit to fight than to rest? Solution. Affection drew him to both: therefore he says that he knows not which [0576D] he should choose. The Apostle therefore doubts from affection, not from ignorance. But then it is asked: How does he say that it is much better to be dissolved and to be with Christ. Solution. "Better" is here set down for "sweeter" and "safer."

QUESTION III. *To you it is given not only that you believe in him, but also that you suffer for him,* etc. It is asked whether to suffer itself is a good, and whether it is a gift of God? for if it is, it is to be desired. Solution. From him who is the faith of believers is the endurance of the suffering. But the punishment itself in itself is not good, nor to be desired for its own sake, nor ought it to be numbered among the gifts of God. Yet to suffer for Christ is good and to be desired; because it has great merit. Nor does the Apostle say simply: To you it is given to suffer; [0577A] but thus: To you it is given to suffer for Christ.

QUESTION IV. *Who being in the form of God [n: (Philipp. II)]*. It is asked what "form" here denotes. Solution. Form is sometimes set down for representation, sometimes for the truth of the thing, as here: Who being in the form of God, that is, in the truth of the divine essence and in the equality of substance; for as a man begets a man, a dog a dog, so God begot God, equal in all things, not unequal: whence the Son also is said to be this which the Father is; and since this can be understood according to person and nature, Augustine shows the understanding of this saying, saying: The Son is therefore said to be that which the Father is, because as the Father is God, so also the Son, and as the Father is omnipotent, so also the Son, and as the Father is immutable, so also the [0577B] Son. For therefore the Father and the Son are one, and supremely one, of whom there is no diversity of nature or of will. Scripture therefore says both: that the Son is equal to the Father, and that the Father is greater than the Son, but this according to the form of a servant, and that according to the form of God.

QUESTION V. *And in habit found as a man,* etc. In how many ways is something had? Solution. In four ways something accedes to another so as to be had: either so as to change and not be changed, as wisdom; or so as to change and be changed, as food; or so as neither to change nor be changed, as a ring; or so as not to change but to be changed, not from its own nature, but from some prior form, as a garment when it is put on; it does not change the man, but is changed; not from its own nature, but it receives an appearance and form which [0577C] cast aside it did not have; according to which comparison and likeness the incarnation is understood: for the form of a servant so acceded to the form of God that it did not change or convert it, but the form of a servant itself was changed, not from its own nature, but into a more excellent and worthier state than it had been before. All this therefore which Augustine says of habit he adduces for this reason, to show that when the Word was made flesh it was not changed or converted into man; just as neither is a man changed into a garment when he puts it on.
