But this, which certain ones add of their own, that as a man when he puts on his garment does not become something, so neither did God, when he took the form of a servant, become something: this, I say, they do not have from the words of Augustine, [0577D] nor of any other saint, since it is false: for if they wish to hold the likeness adduced from the garment in all respects, they must concede that, as a man does not become a garment when he puts it on, so neither did God become man when he clothed himself with the form of a servant. For if Christ is nothing but that which he was from eternity, he had no being common with his mother: therefore not consubstantial with his mother; because nothing substantial is said of both. For when it is said: Christ is man, and the Virgin mother of the Lord is man, the same is not signified by this name, man, according to these new heretics. Of these things it has been spoken more fully upon the Epistle to the Romans.

[0578A]QUESTION VI. *Who humbled himself, becoming obedient,* etc. It is asked whether Christ merited anything. Solution. He merited for us, not for himself. For we by his merit were made worthy of eternal life; but he by his death acquired for himself nothing of which he was not worthy before. Yet it can be said: Truly by the humility of his passion he merited the brightness of the resurrection, namely impassibility and immortality. I do not say that Christ, unless he died, would not become immortal and impassible; but I say that such a merit was worthy of such a reward.

QUESTION VII. It is asked how at the same time in the same soul there could be the highest beatitude and sadness: for before the passion these two were in Christ. For that there was beatitude according to the mind [0578B] from the time he was man, is to be believed: otherwise, how he would be true God, I do not see. Likewise that there was sadness is established, since he himself says: *My soul is sorrowful even unto death [n: (Matth. XXVI)]*. Solution. As in Paul there was a lower power and a higher power, so in Christ there was a lower power, namely a motion of the soul, or a certain affection, which shrank in horror from death: yet the higher reason, free in its beatitude, flourished. For the affection of the flesh in him, as in the rest, did not impede the reason in anything, so as to contemplate the divinity less.

QUESTION VIII. Likewise it is asked how he did not have greater [sic: *beatudinem*] after the resurrection, when he put on immortality and impassibility? Solution. He did not then have more as to the mind, but what [0578C] he had before, he then possessed more tranquilly, just as a king possesses his kingdom more tranquilly and securely when the enemies cease to harass it.

QUESTION IX. *For which cause he gave,* etc. Did he not have before the name which is above every name? How then does he say, For which cause he gave? for not only according to that he is God was this name given to him from eternity according to nature, but also according to that he is man it was given from his conception to the man assumed according to grace. Solution. A thing is then said to be made, when it becomes known: for what he had before, then first after the resurrection became known to men and demons.

QUESTION X. Likewise it is asked to whom this name was given, [0578D] whether to God, or to man? Solution. Augustine says that to man and not to God was given the name *which is above every name [n: (Philipp. II)]*, namely that he be God and the Son of God by the grace not of participation, but of union, and that the man assumed have by grace all that God begotten has by nature. By grace therefore the man has it that he is a son, not a son of grace, but a son of nature. Ambrose says otherwise, that to God and not to man was given the name which is above every name.

QUESTION XI. But do Augustine and Ambrose think contrary things upon this? Solution. One is the gratuitous giving, of which Augustine speaks; another the natural, [0579A] of which the blessed Ambrose speaks. It was given to the man that he be God by personal union immediately in his conception: for when God was made man by condescension, the man too was made God by grace, not an adoptive God, but a natural God, an eternal God, an omnipotent God. But to God begotten it was given from eternity to be God by nature. And so there is no contrariety in understanding between the doctors of truth.

QUESTION XII. *We who serve God in spirit,* etc. [n: (Philip. III.)] It is asked with what service. Note that the service which is owed to God alone is called *latria*; whence also one is called an idolater who exhibits to idols the worship of God, and the abomination is called idolatry, and the house an idol-temple, and the sacrifice an idol-offering. But the service by which through charity [0579B] we are bidden to serve one another is in Greek called *dulia*: yet both are named by the Latin word "service."

QUESTION XIII. *A Hebrew of Hebrews, according to the law a Pharisee.* It is asked whence the Hebrews are so called. Solution. The Hebrews are called from Heber, not from Abraham, as it once seemed to the blessed Augustine, which he afterward retracted: which is clear from this, that Abraham himself is in a certain place called a Hebrew: which would not be, if this name were derived from him. Likewise Abraham is interpreted *father of many nations;* the Hebrews, *those passing over.* It is established therefore to be true that the Hebrews are called not from Abraham, but from Heber: who alone in the division of tongues retained the Hebrew [0579C] language.

QUESTION XIV. *According to the justice which is in the law.* It is asked how he says that he conversed according to the justice which is in the law, without complaint, since elsewhere he says that he walked with others in the desires of the flesh while he was under the law, whence he also names himself a child of wrath? Solution. Both are true, both that he walked according to the desires of the flesh, and yet that he conversed without complaint according to the justice which was in the law — which makes one serve through the fear of punishment, not through love; which restrains the hand, not the mind: such was the justice in the law.



## VI. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE PHILIPPIANS. (cont.)

QUESTION XV. *Not having my own justice,* etc. Did not God give the law, and command obedience to it? [0579D] how then does he call the justice which is from the law his own? The solution of this question is contained in the foregoing. For while he was under the law, seeking to be justified from the works of the law: in this, that he believed he could fulfill the law itself by his own powers, he made the justice of the law his own.

QUESTION XVI. *In faith unto knowing,* etc. This is understood of the faith which has not the time of working; but is not to love a work of faith? But faith without love, after one is grown, is never of avail. How then does the expositor say that this ought to be understood of the faith which has not the time of working, that it avails for knowing God, and for other things? Solution. This is said for this reason, lest anyone think it [0580A] suffices for salvation, namely to believe and to have faith without works, when he has the faculty of working well.

QUESTION XVII. *Whose names are written,* etc. It is asked what the book of life is. Solution. The book of life is the predestination of God, in which all who are to be saved are written, whether at least through predestination alone, or also through justice and predestination.

QUESTION XVIII. *Rejoice in the Lord always [n: (Philipp. IV)]*, etc. The expositor says that joy in the world and joy in God are contrary, and cannot be at the same time in the same person. But is there not someone who is divided, and rejoices partly in the world and partly in the Lord, just as he loves partly the world and partly God? Solution. Anyone is said [0580B] to rejoice with that joy which is known to preponderate in him; and that part of the inner man which the joy of the world occupies, the joy which is in the Lord does not hold, because they cannot be in the same person concerning the same thing.

QUESTION XIX. *Let your petitions be made known with God,* etc. Does not God know all things fully and perfectly before they come to pass? how then does the Apostle say, That the petitions be made known with God? Solution. The sense is: Let your petitions be so vehement and not lukewarm that they be worthy to be heard and fulfilled. For when they are fulfilled, it becomes known to us that they reach to God.

QUESTION XX. The expositor says that the angels offer [0580C] our prayers to God: therefore it is asked how this offering is to be understood. Solution. Saving the reverence due to hidden things, we say that for our prayers to be offered to God by the angels is nothing other than for them to be made worthy through them, that they may be heard by God. Likewise the angels are intermediaries between us and God: for as they announce the divine secrets to us, so the things which are done here among us they are said to announce to God.

QUESTION XXI. But since God's own perfection suffices for knowing all things, it is asked to what end the angels announce anything to him? Solution. Not that they may teach him, but that they may consult his eternal and unchangeable truth, and that they may know what is to be done by them, what, and to whom, and when it is to be announced.

[0580D]QUESTION XXII. *I seek not the gift, but the fruit,* etc. It is asked what difference there is between gift and fruit. Solution. He calls the gift that which is given, as food, coin, garment. But the fruit is the intention of the giver: which is therefore called fruit, because according to it the work is judged useful and fruitful. Or he calls the fruit the reward itself. Whence a good work is called a flower, from which is born the fruit of eternal life. Note that by the raven which fed Elijah is understood the gift, where the good work was without a good intention. By the widow who fed the same prophet is understood the fruit; where there was a good work with a good intention.



## VII. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE COLOSSIANS.

[0581]

[0581A] *[n: (Coloss. I)] Paul an apostle,* etc. The Apostle writes this epistle to the Colossians, who are Asians: to whom the Apostle himself did not preach, but his disciples, namely Archippus and Epaphras. But Archippus had received a ministry among them; Epaphras indeed was sprung from them, and, instructed by the Apostle, confirmed the teaching of Archippus. But afterward, when false apostles came in and preached carnal observances, it had come into doubt among them whom they ought to believe. Whence the Apostle, whose authority was renowned, judges as it were a mediator which side ought rather to be held, writing to them from Ephesus. The intention therefore of the Apostle in this epistle is to confirm the Colossians in that faith and doctrine which they received from his disciples, and he teaches that hope is to be placed in nothing besides [0581B] Christ. The manner of treatment is such: after his wonted fashion he sends greeting beforehand; then he gives thanks for their good things, commending their faith and love. He prays that they may be perfected in Christ, whose benefits, and primacy according to both natures, he commends, and afterward recounts the dignity of his own ministry, and warns them not, seduced by philosophy or by the ceremonies of the law, to depart from Christ. Finally he instructs all together, and separately — namely the sexes, and ages, and conditions — morally. At the end he warns Archippus to be solicitous of his ministry.

QUESTION I. *Giving thanks,* etc. The expositor says that here the Apostle begins to show that the law [0581C] does not profit, but harms. Is not the law good, and given by God, and did it not only not harm, but profit in its own state? how then is it true that it does not profit, but harms? Solution. It is true that the law before grace profited; but after grace it was useless. To which solution it is thus objected: Does not the law even now bear witness to the truth, and through it are we not illuminated in the knowledge of the Deity? therefore even now in the time of grace the law profits us. How then is it true: The law does not profit, but harms? Solution. The law read, and spiritually understood, profits, and does not harm: but observed according to the letter it does not profit, but harms.

QUESTION II. *Who hath made us worthy to be partakers of the lot of the saints.* It is asked what he calls the lot, of which [0581D] we have been made partakers. Solution. The future inheritance, which is given by lot — that is, by divine grace and will — without merits, the Apostle here calls the lot.

QUESTION III. Likewise it is asked how the future inheritance is given to the saints without merits, since through faith, and love, and good works they merit it. Solution. [0582A] It is to be understood without merits preceding faith, not without those following: which are of grace, and therefore are not excluded, when it is said that by grace alone future glory is given. Of these we have already spoken above.

QUESTION IV. *He hath translated us into the kingdom of the Son.* It is asked what he means by *the kingdom of the Son of God.* Solution. The kingdom of heaven, or of the Son, is taken in several ways, namely for future glory, as there: *Thy kingdom come [n: (Matth. VI)]*; or for the present Church, as there: *When he shall have delivered up the kingdom to God and the Father [n: (I Cor. XV)]*; or for the faith of Christ, as there: *The kingdom of God is within you [n: (Matth. XII)]*; or for Holy Scripture, as there: *The kingdom of heaven shall be taken away from you, and shall be given to a nation yielding fruit [n: (Matth. XXI)]*.

[0582B]QUESTION V. *Of the Son of his love.* Is not the Holy Spirit the love by which the Father loves the Son, and the Son the Father, and is Christ the son of love? It seems therefore that he is the son of the Holy Spirit: which faith does not receive. Solution. Love is commonly taken for the divine nature, or substance, or essence, when he is proclaimed to be the son of love. For the Son is consubstantial and coessential with the Father in nature, substance, and essence. But what love signifies, when it is said: The Holy Spirit is the love by which the Father loves the Son, and conversely, has been expounded elsewhere. Concerning redemption likewise, and the image, and that Christ is called the head of the Church, it has been already said: nor is it needful that we do things already done.

[0582C]QUESTION VI. It is asked, however, whether Christ according to his humanity was the head of the saints who preceded the incarnation. It seems that he was: but he who was not yet [head] according to his humanity, how could he be their head? Or if [he was], how could they have [a head] that was not yet head? Solution. Christ even according to [sic: *humanitatom*] can be called the head of those who were from Abel, because through faith all who are to be saved, as many as are saved, [are saved]. Note that Christ is proclaimed to be of the same nature with the Father, where he is shown to be the son of love. But where [he is called] the image, not only the identity of the same substance, but also personal property is intimated. Where he is said to be the firstborn of every creature, there he is asserted to be coeternal with the Father. Where [0582D] it is read that all things were founded through him, his omnipotence is declared. Where it is said that *all things consist in him [n: (Coloss. I)]*, his immensity is made manifest, whose eternity is intimated in John, where it is written: *In the beginning was the Word [n: (Joan. I)]*; and personal property when it is subjoined: *And the Word was with God [n: (ibid.)]*; and identity of substance, [0583A] when it is said: *And the Word was God [n: (Joan. I)]*. And so the Son is coeternal, consubstantial, and co-omnipotent with the Father; another in person, the same with the Father the beginning of all creatures.

QUESTION VII. *And he,* namely Christ, *is the head of the Church.* It is said that the Church began first from Abel, who was the first just man, but was not Adam just? For it is believed that after the fall he was justified through penance and faith: why then is the Church not said to have begun from Adam? Solution. From Adam the Church contracted the stain of original sin, from Abel none, and therefore it is better said to have begun from Abel than from Adam; because Scripture makes mention of the sin of Adam, and not of his justice, and of the justice of Abel, and not of his sin. Christ [0583B] therefore is always the head of all who have been from the beginning, as of those who followed him: through whom alone we have access to the Father.

QUESTION VIII. *See that no one deceive you through philosophy and vain deceit [n: (Coloss. II)]*, etc. Was not the knowledge of natural things, which the philosophers handed down, useful for knowing the invisible things of God? For through the visible forms of visible things, the teaching of which is taught in mathematics, one comes to the invisible causes of visible things, which physics teaches: through which one comes to know invisible substances, and the invisible natures of invisible substances. How then does the Apostle call such knowledge vain deceit? Solution. The Apostle does not reprehend the philosophers for [0583C] this, that they inquired into the natures of things; but for this, that they endeavored to confine the power of God, though it be infinite, under natural causes, saying that God does nothing against nature. Whence they said that for God to become a creature, for a virgin to bear, for a dead man to live again, was impossible. This error of theirs therefore the Apostle rightly reprehends: not the true knowledge which they had concerning natures.

QUESTION IX. *In whom dwelleth all the fulness of the Godhead.* Since the fulness of the Godhead is one and simple, how does he say all the fulness of the Godhead, since 'all' is wont to gather a multitude? Solution. It is said 'all the fulness of the Godhead,' that may be noted [sic: *I otetur*]: that it is there in every mode of indwelling, even personally. Or otherwise: All the fulness is said, that it may be intimated [0583D] that in him is the fulness not only of knowledge, but also of power and goodness. Whereby is destroyed the error of those who say that the man assumed has all the knowledge through grace which the Word has through nature, but not all the power.

QUESTION X. *The fulness of the Godhead bodily.* Since the Godhead is incorporeal, it is asked how it can dwell bodily in Christ. Solution. He says 'bodily,' that is, completively, solidly, and truly, with regard to the legal figures, which were the shadow of things to come, whose body — that is, fulfillment and truth — is Christ. Or otherwise: 'Bodily' signifies not the nature of the existing thing, but rather [0584A] the mode of existing. For just as a body so has being naturally in one place that it cannot at the same time be in another: so the fulness of the Godhead has being in the man assumed: in which mode it is in no other creature. In every creature it is by essence, in the just by grace, in Christ alone according to personal property. This then is for the Godhead to dwell bodily in Christ, that is, personally. Just as a body is in several places at once, not bodily but spiritually. In saying this I do not deny the truth and the essence of the Lord's body to be at once in several altars, but by this I intimate that it does not have that mode of existing common with other bodies, or from the nature of a body, but rather common with a spirit — not created, but uncreated. Both are [0584B] wonderful, and true: that a created spirit is somewhere bodily, and that the sacramental body of Christ is in several places spiritually.

QUESTION XI. *Blotting out the handwriting of the decree, which was contrary to you,* etc. It is asked what he calls the decree, what the handwriting. Solution. By 'decree' he names either the law of Moses itself, or the precept of God which he gave to the first man in paradise, saying: *Of the tree of the knowledge of good and evil thou shalt not eat [n: (Gen. II)]*; by 'handwriting,' the record of each violated decree: and the handwritings of all faults have been blotted out by the blood shed without fault.

QUESTION XII. *Which are a shadow of things to come, but the body is Christ's.* It is asked how shadow, body, and spirit are distinguished. Solution. One thing is shadow, [0584C] another body, another spirit: which three are called by other names figure, thing, truth, so that the same is shadow and figure; the same body and thing; the same spirit and truth. The legal ceremonies were called the shadow and figure of things to come. The sacraments of grace are the body, or thing, of those shadows or figures: the spiritual grace, namely, which the sacraments of the New Testament confer and signify, is called the spirit or truth: but the sacraments of the Old Testament only signify spiritual grace, and do not confer it: and this is the difference between these sacraments and those. Likewise it is to be known that those things which in the time of the law were precepts, now in the time of grace are not precepts; but only testimonies of truth, whence then it was a sin not to [0584D] observe even one. But now it is not a sin not to keep them; nay, he who keeps them, offends.

QUESTION XIII. *Mortify your members [n: (Coloss. III)]*, etc. It is asked what, or what things, he calls members, which are to be mortified. Are the eye, the hand, the foot to be mortified? Solution. Here the Apostle calls members the concupiscence of the members, as he himself subjoins, and expounds *fornication,* etc.

QUESTION XIV. *Covetousness, which is the service of idols,* etc. Is not covetousness one thing, idolatry another? how then does he call covetousness the service of idols? Solution. He therefore compares covetousness to idolatry, because the malice is not unlike. For just as the idolater worships a block, so the covetous man his coin. And just as the idolater strives to take away from God his honor, so the covetous man usurps to himself alone the things [0585A] which God created in common for all.

QUESTION XV. *Putting off the old man,* etc. It is asked what he here calls the old man. Solution. The old man is sometimes called Adam the first man, who by sinning clothed himself and all his posterity with the tunic of oldness. The old man is sometimes called any man who bears the image of the earthly; but sometimes the old man is called oldness itself, which consists in fault and in punishment; sometimes the old man is named the habit of vices, as here, according to the expositor.



## VII. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE COLOSSIANS. (cont.)

QUESTION XVI. *And put on the new man.* It is asked likewise what he here calls the new man? For the new man is sometimes called Christ, from whom [0585B] is all newness; sometimes the new man is called anyone renewed in Christ; sometimes also the habit of virtues; sometimes specially the mind of reason. But above, that which is called the inner man was said to be the mind; here, however, it is said that the inner mind is called the new man: which seems to be contrary, since the new man is one thing, the inner another, as has already been shown. Solution. The expositor says that he calls the rational mind the new man, nor is it contrary to what was said above, that the inner mind is called man: for the same thing, on account of diverse properties, obtains diverse names.

QUESTION XVII. *Who is renewed unto the knowledge of God according to his image,* etc. Likewise it is asked how [0585C] the mind is said to be renewed according to the image of God, since it is itself the image of God — can it be renewed according to itself? Solution. It is the same image which is renewed, and that according to which it is renewed, just as we say that someone is dead according to the body, that is, in body, not according to the spirit. And so the mind is renewed according to itself, that is, in itself. In the Epistle to the Ephesians, however, it is said that this very renewal is made according to God: therefore according to God, lest it be said to be according to a creature. For through such a renewal it is made like and conformed to God: therefore it is said to be according to him. Note that this image, which is renewed in the knowledge of God, is found equally in the man and in the woman: which is other [0586A] than that of which it was said above: *The man is the image of God, and the woman of the man.*

QUESTION XVIII. *Forgiving one another, if any have a complaint against another.* This same thing the Lord commanded, saying: *Forgive your debtors [n: (Matth. VI)]*, but what is it that can be forgiven by us? what is the debt that can be exacted by us from him who has done us injury? Solution. This is to forgive the injury inflicted, to demand for ourselves in no way satisfaction on account of the injury: certain other things upon this have been said by us elsewhere.

QUESTION XIX. The expositor says that he who abstains from unlawful things has praise; but he who tempers himself from lawful things, praise and reward — as if he said: The one avoids punishment, the other also merits a crown. But does not also he [0586B] who abstains from unlawful things have not only praise, but also reward? Solution. He who abstains from unlawful things always avoids punishment, but does not always merit a crown, except only then, when he is tempted and assailed.

QUESTION XX. *Do all things in the name of the Lord.* By 'all things' he understands nothing but good things, which are to be done by us. Whence it is asked whether he prescribes that all things to be done be done for God's sake. Am I by the precepts constrained to give my cloak to a poor man whom I see to be in need, and which I know to please God? For if I withhold it, I seem to despise what I know he wishes to be done. Solution. It is to be understood thus: Do all things in the name of the Lord, that is, do nothing against God, and in good things seek not your own glory, but God's. Not [0586C] however in all things that we do do we merit; because certain things are indifferent, as to sit, to stand, to draw breath, to close the hand, to open the eyes, and the like. But certain things are such as have merit if they be done; and are not a sin if they be not done, as to give a cloak to a poor man, and to sell all things, and give to the poor: which is of the perfect, who follow Christ naked.

QUESTION XXI. *Say to Archippus: Take heed to the ministry which thou hast received in the Lord, that thou fulfil it [n: (Coloss. IV)]*. It is asked whether subjects may admonish prelates. Solution. Behold authority, that subjects may admonish prelates, lest they be slothful in foreseeing those things which pertain to salvation.



## VIII. ON THE FIRST EPISTLE TO THE THESSALONIANS.

[0585]

[0585D] *[n: (I Thess. I.)] Paul and Silvanus,* etc. The Apostle writes this epistle to the Thessalonians. Thessalonica is the metropolis of Macedonia, which is a province of the Greeks. The Thessalonians therefore are Macedonians, or Greeks, who, converted by the Apostle, could be moved from the truth of the faith neither by tribulations nor by false preachers. These the Apostle praises together, [0586D] because they had drunk in the spirit of so great a faith that, even in the hope of things to come, with devoted mind for the name of Christ he sustained perils from his fellow-citizens who did not believe. Yet there were some among them idle and curious; some also thinking less rightly about the resurrection, and therefore grieving too tenderly over the death of their friends. These therefore the Apostle corrects in this epistle, and warns the perfect [0587A] not to yield to adversities and false apostles, and to correct others. He wrote, moreover, from Athens. And the intention of the Apostle in this epistle is to correct the depraved and the uncorrected, and to exhort the good to perseverance and to the correction of others. The manner is such: after his own fashion he sends greeting beforehand: which sent beforehand, he gives thanks for their good things, recounting not their faith and works only, but also the manner of their conversion and their endurance of evils, that he may provoke them to persevere; and he inserts also somewhat concerning his own labors, and the truth of the Gospel, and his prudent conduct among them, and with how great affection he desires to see them. Then he beseeches the depraved to restrain themselves from luxury, and idleness, and curiosity, and he confirms that the dead shall rise again. Toward the end is moral instruction. And so he says, Paul and Sylvanus, etc. [0587B] Timothy, etc. Note that it was the custom of the Apostle to join to himself in the salutation those who either had been with him among them, or were to be.

QUESTION I. *To the living and true God,* etc. It is asked why he says, true. Solution. There is one God naturally; many by participation: to distinguish from whom he is called the true God, inasmuch as he is God of himself, not from elsewhere. For the others are gods not from the fact that they are, but from the fact that they have received.

QUESTION II. *For the wrath of God is come upon them. [n: (I Thess. II)]*. It is asked how, on account of their sins, the wrath of God and punishment came upon them, that is, before death. Is eternal punishment lightened by temporal punishment? for if it is not lightened, God seems merciless, who punishes here, and there. Likewise, if the eternal [0587C] is lightened by the temporal, it seems that such punishment proceeds not from the wrath of God, but from mercy; and therefore it is to be called not wrath, but mercy. Solution. Great indeed is the mercy of God to call to penance through temporal punishment; but they, unwilling to repent, turn the mercy of God for themselves into wrath, and the punishment inflicted, which could have been a means of salvation, becomes for them the beginning of eternal punishment.

QUESTION III. *We hastened to see your face with much desire,* etc. The expositor says wherein evils would come upon them; but it is asked how from the presence of the Teacher evils would come upon the disciples: since rather good things than evils seem to come from it: whence he himself desired to see them for the profit of his own presence, [0587D] namely that he might confirm them, and add over what was lacking. Solution. Greater is the crown, and more praiseworthy the victory, if they should stand immovable, the master being absent: yet the Apostle humanly feared lest they fall, and therefore desired to come to them.

QUESTION IV. *At his coming,* namely of the Lord. It is asked, at what coming. Solution. Threefold is the coming of the Lord, one in the flesh, one in the spirit, one in the flesh. He came in the flesh made man. He comes in the spirit when he pours his Spirit into our spirit, healing and justifying it. He shall come in the flesh at the judgment. He comes also secretly to each one at death, either to purge them, or to open the gate of the kingdom: to the evil, to punish them.

QUESTION V. *That you be not sorrowful, even as the rest [0588A] who have no hope. [n: (I Thess. IV)]*. It is asked whether to be sorrowful and to weep for the death of friends is a sin, since the Apostle says: That you be not sorrowful? Solution. It is necessary that, when death seizes one beloved, it sadden the affection of love; it is therefore not a fault, if we be saddened by the necessity of losing, if we be consoled by the hope of receiving again: whence the Apostle does not say simply, that you be not sorrowful, but says, that you be not sorrowful as the rest who have no hope, as if he said: It is lawful to be sorrowful, but with hope.

QUESTION VI. It is asked how the Lord wept for Lazarus, dead but presently, at his own bidding, about to live? Solution. Not the dead man, but death, which he had not deserved by sinning, did he bewail: to which, and to the miseries of the world, Lazarus was again to be recalled: and thence was the Lord [0588B] moved.

QUESTION VII. It is asked whether prayers, alms, the sacrifices of the altar, profit all for whom they are done. Solution. The aforesaid profit after death only those who so lived before death that they are worthy that these be profitable to them after death: for they profit not those who went forth from this life without faith working through love; because, living here, they lacked the pledge of salvation, namely unto salvation.

QUESTION VIII. Since Christ can do all things, it is asked why he did not straightway grant immortality to his faithful, that they should never at all experience death. Solution. If this were done, a certain felicity indeed would be given to the flesh, but the fortitude of faith would be diminished. No one then would hasten to the grace of Christ for the sake of the future [0588C] life; but only for the sake of fleeing the trouble of death, and so in a certain manner delicately would men believe in Christ. Where then would be the glorious triumph of the martyrs?

QUESTION IX. *And those who have slept.* It is asked, since he calls Christ, who now lives, dead, why he calls the faithful dead not dead, but sleeping? Solution. He therefore says Christ died, and rose again, that, while we hear this, we may hope the same, namely that we shall rise again through the power of the Godhead. But the elect he therefore calls sleeping; because no one can be so easily wakened from [sic: *sommo*], as easily as God will waken them all from the [sic: *sommo*] of death.

QUESTION X. *At the voice of the archangel,* etc. Concerning this voice [0588D] it is asked, whether it will be material. Solution. Some say that it will be material, formed by the ministry of angels, just as that was material which was heard when the Lord was baptized, which is therefore called God's; because God will give it efficacy, that through it the dead may rise. Others say that it will be some evident sign. Concerning the uncertain, an ambiguous solution. For all things future at the last day are uncertain to us.

QUESTION XI. *Then we who live.* It is asked how the Apostle says that those *first shall rise* who were *dead* long since, and then those who shall then be found alive, since he himself elsewhere says that the resurrection shall be *in the twinkling of an eye and in a moment [n: (I Cor. XV)]*; wherein there is nothing prior, nor posterior, but all things at once? Solution. It can be said that the resurrection of the dead is therefore said [0589A] to be made at once, because there will be a little delay.

QUESTION XII. It is wont, however, to be asked whether those whom Christ shall find alive are to die, or to pass without death to immortality? Solution. Concerning this we have nothing certain; yet it seems to some that in the very rapture they die, and revive, since elsewhere the Apostle says: *In Christ all shall be made alive [n: (I Cor. XV)]*, and elsewhere: *that which thou sowest is not quickened, except it first die [n: (ibid.)]*. Nor ought this to seem incredible, since in a moment, and in the twinkling of an eye, the common and general resurrection is believed to be about to be.

QUESTION XIII. *We shall be caught up to meet Christ in the air.* It is asked whether this is said of all? Solution. The good, as it were light, shall run to meet Christ in the air; but the evil, [0589B] as it were weighty, shall cleave not to meet Christ in the air, but to the earth, which they always loved. Note that there are four kinds of those who shall come to the judgment; for some shall come to judge only, as the very good, not to be judged, of whose salvation it is settled. Others are the less perfect, of whose salvation it is not yet settled; these shall come to the judgment, to be judged. So there are two kinds of the evil; some shall come to the judgment, to be judged, as the less evil, of whose damnation it is not settled. But others, not to be judged; [0590A] because they are already judged, since concerning their damnation it is already certain. Why then shall they come? shall it be to judge? God forbid! Why then? that they may hear with the devil: *Go, ye cursed, into everlasting fire [n: (Matth. XXV)]*, etc. Concerning the manner of the judgment it has already been said in the first epistle to the Corinthians; namely how the saints shall judge, whether by comparison alone, or also by power.

QUESTION XIV. *The day of the Lord shall so come as a thief in the night [n: (I Thess. V)]*, etc. Since no one knows whether by day or by night the Lord shall come to the judgment, it is asked how the Apostle calls the time of the Lord's coming day. Solution. Day in such a place is not put for the illumination of the air as elsewhere; but for revelation, that is, manifestation, because then all things shall be manifested. For each one shall know why the Lord saves or damns him [0590B] or another.

QUESTION XV. *To pray without ceasing.* It is asked how this precept of the Apostle can be fulfilled. For who can always pray? Is it not needful sometimes to sleep? Solution. It is thus understood: Without ceasing at the fixed hours. Or by prayer is understood holy desire, and pious affection, and so the just man never ceases to pray, unless he cease to be just; for he who always does well, always prays well.



## IX. ON THE SECOND EPISTLE TO THE THESSALONIANS.

[0589]

[0589C] *Paul, and Silvanus [n: (II Thess. I)]*, etc. The Apostle writes this epistle to the Thessalonians. For, a graver tribulation having arisen among them, he warns them to patience, showing the just judgment of God, that the good may attain glory, the evil punishment. And because in the first epistle he says certain things concerning the Coming of the Lord and concerning the resurrection of the dead, whence the day of the Lord was thought to be at hand; now he writes another epistle, in which he signifies, though obscurely (for he cannot do so openly), concerning the abolition of the Roman kingdom, concerning the appearing of Antichrist, and his damnation, and concerning the disquiet of certain brethren. He writes also that the day of the Lord is not at hand, as by occasion of the former epistle it seemed to some. And so the intention of the Apostle in this epistle is to move the good and the quiet [0589D] to patience, and to correct the unquiet; and to open somewhat here what he had said obscurely in the former epistle. The manner is such: first he salutes, then he gives thanks for their good things; afterward he warns to patience, and to constancy; thence he asserts that Antichrist shall forestall the coming of Christ, and announces some signs of the coming of Antichrist, though obscurely, treating of the abolition of the Roman kingdom and of the slaying of Antichrist. But toward the end he beseeches them to rebuke the curious and the idle.

QUESTION I. *In a flame of fire, giving vengeance to them who have not known God.* It is asked, whether the Lord [0590C] shall come to the judgment surrounded in fire? Solution. He is said to be about to come in a flame of fire, because he shall appear terrible to the ungodly, to burn and to torment them. Fire indeed shall precede his coming, by which the elements shall be dissolved, that they may be renewed.

QUESTION II. It is asked how those shall be kept alive whom the Lord shall find alive. Solution. Just as the fire did not hurt the three children in the furnace of the oven; so that fire preceding the coming of the judge shall not hurt the good.

QUESTION III. It is asked what punishments the souls of the ungodly now suffer among those below, whether material, or only tortures of conscience? Solution. It is believed that they suffer material punishments, as fire and cold. Whence it is said: They shall pass *from the waters of snows to [0590D] excessive heat [n: (Job. XXIV)]*.

QUESTION IV. Likewise it is asked how they are tormented by these punishments, since spiritual things cannot be touched by bodily. Solution. It can come about that they be punished by those things by which they are not touched, just as one contracts a great horror from something seen or imagined, although he be not touched by it.

QUESTION V. Likewise, their bodies being resumed, since these shall thereafter be immortal, how shall they be punished in them, since they cannot be dissolved? Solution. The bodies of the evil shall indeed be immortal, but passible, [0591A] that is, such in which the evil may suffer, yet without harm to the natural quantity, or essence, of the bodies themselves.

QUESTION VI. *Except there come a departure first [n: (II Thess. II)]*, etc. It is asked: how shall that which he says concerning the departure come to pass? Solution. This can be understood in four ways, either of the earthly Roman empire, or of the spiritual empire of the Roman Church, or of the faith, or of Antichrist. For another translation says the Runaway. Note that it is read that Antichrist shall be born in Babylon of the tribe of Dan. According to which Jacob says: *Let Dan be a serpent in the way, and a horned snake in the path [n: (Gen. XLIX)]*. Who, when he shall first manifest himself, shall come to Jerusalem, and shall circumcise himself, saying: I am Christ promised to the Jews.

[0591B]QUESTION VII. *Who is lifted up above all that is called God,* as the gods of the nations or the saints; *or that is worshipped,* as God the Trinity. But it is asked how he shall be able to lift himself up above God the Trinity? Will not this be his greatest pride and self-exaltation, that he shall say himself to be Christ, and thus equal to God — for he will not say himself greater; how then is he lifted up above all that is worshipped as God? Solution. As it seems to me, this self-exaltation is understood in this, that that wicked one, *the man of sin, the son of perdition,* shall cause the veneration and worship due to God the Trinity alone to be exhibited to himself and not to God. It is read that just as in Christ dwelt the fulness of the Godhead, so in Antichrist there shall be the fulness of malice, and of all iniquity, and, so to say, the fulness of devilishness.

[0591C]QUESTION VIII. Whence it can be asked whether he will be a man simply, or whether the devil will assume him personally, as God man. Solution. The devil cannot so assume a man that he be personally united to himself; he will therefore be a pure man, whom nevertheless the devil will so possess that he shall fill him with all the power of his wickedness, and with all iniquity, so that, utterly given over and devoted to him, he shall wish nothing, be able to do nothing, save what the devil wishes and is able to do.

QUESTION IX. *The mystery of iniquity already worketh.* The expositor says that in Nero and in other evil men the devil already works secretly, who in Antichrist shall openly rage. Whence it is asked how he works secretly in Nero. Did not Nero deny Christ, and [0591D] openly persecute those who preached Christ? Solution. He is therefore said to work secretly in Nero; because Nero did this by the fraudulent suggestion of certain men. Or, which is better, the devil is therefore said to work secretly in Nero, not because that persecution was not manifest, but because it is a shadow, and figure, and a certain image of that which shall be done through Antichrist, which shall be much graver than all which have gone before. For he shall reign three years and a half; and, sitting in a pavilion on the Mount of Olives, he shall be slain by the power of the Holy Spirit through Michael, as the doctors say, or the Lord himself, or some other angel.

QUESTION X. *Whom the Lord shall destroy with the brightness of his coming,* etc. Shall not Antichrist first be slain [0592A] before the Lord come to the judgment? For from the book of Daniel it is understood that forty-two days shall be granted to the elect for penance after the death of Antichrist. But how long after the Lord shall come is utterly unknown not only to men, but even to the angels. How then does the Apostle say: With the brightness of his coming (since before the coming he is to be slain) the Lord shall destroy him? Solution. Antichrist shall indeed be slain, as is believed, before the coming of the Lord, by the dissolution of body and soul; yet the Lord shall destroy him, now raised again with his whole body, with the brightness of his coming, damning him, and sending him into everlasting fire, saying to him and to the other reprobate: *Go, ye cursed, into everlasting fire [n: (Matth. XXV)]*.

[0592B]QUESTION XI. *According to the working of Satan,* etc. The expositor says, yet not without sense, as the frenzied, who have no fault for the evils which they do. Whence it can be asked, if Antichrist shall do the whole with the devil instigating and cooperating, and in a manner compelling, why it is imputed to him rather than to the frenzied, or if the frenzied do this with the devil instigating, why it is not imputed to them. And how are they without fault for the evils which they do, rather than that wicked one? Solution. The frenzied, who are without sense, are rather to be said to suffer something than to do something; and evils are done rather through them than by them; since they themselves are not the authors, seeing they do not apply the will to this. But Antichrist shall so do all things, with the devil instigating, that he shall apply his whole will and [0592C] sense to the same; and therefore all things shall be imputed to him unto eternal punishment.

QUESTION XII. *With signs, and lying wonders,* etc. It is asked concerning those signs which Antichrist shall do through the devil, whether they are therefore called lying, because they are not true as they seem, but phantastic; that is, because he is about to deceive mortal senses through phantasms, or whether they are therefore called lying, because by God's permission they shall draw men to a lie? Solution. It seems to us to be probable, that all those signs which the devil shall be able to do, Antichrist shall also do truly. But those which the devil has not in his power, neither shall that wicked one do truly, but by magic art alone he shall delude the eyes of mortals, that he may seem to do what [0592D] he shall not truly do. Whence the Apostle says: Whose coming shall be in all power, as regards those things which he shall do truly, and in all seduction, as regards those things which he shall not do truly. Yet all avail unto the seduction of the ungodly. But what those things are which the devil shall be able [to do], and what things lie not subject to his power, it is not for our littleness to unfold.

QUESTION XIII. *God shall send them the operation of error, to believe a lie, that they may be judged,* etc. It is asked, however, concerning those who, moved by the signs and wonders of that wicked one, shall believe him to be good, and the Son of God: whether they have any excuse? For if they do not exhibit divine worship to him, since their conscience dictates to them that he is to be adored as God, they shall sin unto death, and thus they seem to be inexcusable [0593A]. Solution. The Apostle says that because they received not the brightness of the truth, that they were saved — that is, Christ formerly manifestly preached — God shall send them the operation of error, that they may believe a lie and be judged: wherein he sufficiently teaches that, on account of their preceding sins, they shall have no excuse, even if they judge themselves to render obedience to Christ, that is, to the true God, by believing in Antichrist.

QUESTION XIV. Augustine says that to be able to have faith, or charity, is of the nature of the faithful: to have faith, or charity, is of the grace of God. Whence it can be asked how this ought to be understood. Is not that which is natural in our own faculty? but since we cannot have faith, or charity, without grace, how is to be able to have faith of the nature [0593B] of the faithful? Solution. To be able to have faith, that is, the potency of having, is natural; but to have the use of this potency is of grace alone, and not of nature.

QUESTION XV. *He that will not work, let him not eat* [0594A] [n: (II Thess. III)]. It is asked of what work he here treats. For if of the outward, how does he prescribe that he not eat who will not work? For many are worthy to eat, though they work not. Solution. Augustine says that the Apostle wills the servants of God to work bodily, that they be not compelled by want to seek necessaries; yet they do not act ill who postpone outward works for spiritual ones when they have whereof to live, because *Mary hath chosen the best part [n: (Luc. X)]*. But those who, given over to curiosity, will not work, are reprehended; not those who forsake lesser goods for greater.

QUESTION XVI. *If any obey not our word by an epistle, note that man, and do not keep company with him, that he may be confounded.* The Apostle says thus, that he [0594B] who resists the admonition of the Church is to be excommunicated. Whence it can be asked in what manner this is to be done. And it is certain that it is to be done with great discretion, lest the Church incur detriment, nor is such punishment to be exercised for a light cause.



## X. ON THE FIRST EPISTLE TO TIMOTHY.

[0593]

[0593C] *Paul an apostle [n: (I Tim. I)]*, etc. The Apostle writes this epistle to Timothy, who was the son of a certain faithful woman, begotten of a gentile father: and since he was not circumcised, and was himself a gentile, and the brethren who were in Lystra and Iconium gave good testimony of him, Paul wished this man to set out with him: and therefore that he be circumcised on account of the Jews who were in those places. And he was learned as well in the divine Scriptures as in the liberal arts. This man the Apostle created bishop: therefore, leaving him in Asia, he instructs him in this epistle concerning the episcopal office, namely how he should resist false apostles and how he should instruct the Church, what sort of priests or deacons he should ordain; what sort of widows he should honor, and how he should conduct himself in the Church, or how he should rule it. And the intention of the [0593D] Apostle in this epistle is to instruct Timothy concerning the office of the episcopal dignity. The manner is such: first he salutes him; then he warns him to resist the false ones; afterward he instructs him concerning the episcopal office, teaching what sort of priests and deacons he ought to ordain. Then what sort of widows he ought to receive; afterward he instructs him concerning the manner of correption; but at the end he warns him to avoid profane novelties.

QUESTION I. *Of God the Father our Saviour,* etc. Since the Son is frequently called in Scripture Saviour, who alone died for our salvation, not the Father, not the Holy Spirit, it is asked why the Apostle refers our salvation to God the Father, saying here: Of God the Father our Saviour. Solution. God the Father, in that he deigned to send us his Son, and to bestow the Holy Spirit in the sacraments of the New Testament, [0594C] is the author of our salvation and therefore is called Saviour.

QUESTION II. *And of Jesus Christ our hope.* Likewise it is asked why he refers hope specially to the Son, saying, and of Jesus Christ our hope, since we hope and believe in the whole Trinity. Solution. We therefore refer our hope to Christ, because he rose from the dead, and ascended into the heavens; because through his resurrection we hope to attain to the glory of the future resurrection.

QUESTION III. *Grace, mercy and peace,* etc. It is asked why, beyond his wont, the Apostle in this salutation puts three, saying, grace, mercy, and peace. Solution. The nine preceding epistles he wrote in common to the Churches. But this one specially to Timothy [0594D] his fellow-bishop; therefore he puts three. By mercy understanding the same thing which in the others he understood by grace, namely the remission of sins; by peace, the tranquillity and foretaste of the future life; but by grace he understands the donation of the Holy Spirit, wherewith the ministers of Christ are armed.

QUESTION IV. *That they should not attend to fables,* etc. It is asked what the Apostle here understands by fables? does he perhaps so call the words of the law, and the divine oracles in this place? and elsewhere he says: *Avoid profane and old wives' fables [n: (I Tim. IV)]*. Solution. By fables he here means the doctrine of those who preach that the law with grace is necessary. Or by fables he here means the traditions which the Jews hold unwritten, and one transfuses into another by speaking, which they call deuterosis: wherein they say, and believe, that God first created two wives, from whom [0595A] they weave infinite genealogies of men, bringing forth unfruitful questions. Concerning which traditions, foreign to the sacred Scriptures, the Lord says in the Gospel: *Why have you made void the commandment of God for your traditions? [n: (Matth. XV.)]*.

QUESTION V. *Now the end of the commandment is charity, from a pure heart, and a good conscience, and faith unfeigned.* Here the Apostle defines charity, as the expositor says. Whence it can be asked what is the definition here given, whether this: the end of the commandment? or this: from a pure heart, and a good conscience, and faith unfeigned? Solution. The definition of charity is, to be from a pure heart, and a good conscience, and faith unfeigned.

QUESTION VI. Likewise it can be asked whether this definition fits all charity. Which if it be granted, no one [0595B] is having charity who is not perfect, because consummating and perfecting the law; because charity is the end of the commandment, that is, perfection and consummation. For if 'end' is here put for consummation. But elsewhere it is put for consumption, as when it is said, the bread is finished. Elsewhere for a boundary, as when it is said: Here the field ends. Likewise, who can have a pure heart and a good conscience without charity? does not cleanness of heart, and a good conscience, proceed from charity? how then does charity proceed from a pure heart? Solution. It seems to me that here the definition of perfect and consummated charity is assigned, and thus this definition does not fit imperfect charity. First is given love, which cleanses the heart, and from which good [0595C] works are done, and from which is born a good conscience; at length, the heart being cleansed, and a good conscience procured, charity is perfected and consummated, which is the end of the commandment. These things I say without prejudice to a better opinion.

QUESTION VII. *The law is good, if a man use it lawfully.* It is asked what it is to use lawfully? Solution. As the expositor says, he uses the law lawfully who, understanding it spiritually, through it recognizes the disease, and seeks the physician, and who knows it to have been given for a time, and forsakes it for Christ. It is to be noted that the unjust man uses the law lawfully, when he understands why it was given, and by fear of it flees to the grace of Christ, that he may become just. But the just man uses the law lawfully, when by terrifying he imposes it upon the unjust.

[0595D]QUESTION VIII. *The law is not made for the just man.* Were not David and other just men, who were under the law, bound to keep the law, how then is the law not made for the just? Solution. The law is not imposed upon the just man, so as to have dominion over him, and to coerce him by fear, and the just man is not under the law, but rather with it, as a friend of the law.

QUESTION IX. *Because I did it ignorantly,* etc. The expositor says that this 'because' can note the cause or the consequence. But [sic: *Ssd*] it is asked how ignorance, which is a fault, can be the cause of divine mercy. Solution. There is a certain ignorance from infirmity: from which if anyone act against God out of good zeal, out of the affection which he has, he merits to be freed from that ignorance.

[0596A]QUESTION X. *He came into this world to save sinners,* etc. It is asked concerning little ones, who are born of baptized parents, whether they pertain to the sinners for whose saving Jesus came into the world. For these have no actual sin, and how can they contract original sin from parents, which the parents themselves have not; because it is forgiven them? Solution. It is to be known that original sin is so forgiven in baptism, not that it be not at all, but that it be not a fault. But the vice of corruption remains even in the baptized, who beget, not from that whereby they are reborn, but from the oldness of corruption, through which original fault passes into the little ones: whence they too need a remedy. Whence also the physician himself says: *Suffer the little ones to come unto me [n: (Marc. X)]*.

[0596B]QUESTION XI. *Of whom I am the first.* Were not Cain and Saul before Paul? how then is Paul the first among sinners? Solution. Paul was first not in order of time, but in greatness of iniquity. To which solution it is thus objected: Did not Paul sin through ignorance, and many others knowingly? but it is a greater sin to sin knowingly than through ignorance: therefore Paul was not the first among sinners in greatness of iniquity, since others, especially those who sinned against the Holy Spirit, are much worse than Paul even according to his first state, in which he was a blasphemer, a persecutor, and contumelious. Solution. Paul, considering the greatness of his own offenses, judged himself worse than all sinners, [0596C] although in the eyes of God it was otherwise.

QUESTION XII. *Now to the king of ages, immortal, invisible, the only God, honor and glory for ever and ever. Amen.* The expositor says that the man assumed by the Word is king of ages from that time in which he was assumed by the Word; but if the man assumed is not man, nor God, nor person, as certain men have dared to profess, how is the man assumed, from the time that he is assumed, and not before, king of ages? But we say that the man assumed by the Word is man and God: the Word itself, by whom he was assumed, both began in time to be king of ages, namely when he began to be assumed by the Word. For when God began to be man, man also began to be God, as many authorities attest.

[0596D]QUESTION XIII. *Who will have all men to be saved [n: (I Tim. II)]*. Since God is omnipotent, and no one can resist the divine will, how are not all saved, since God wills all to be saved? Or is it perhaps his will that the reprobate be saved? Ambrose thus resolves it: God wills all to be saved, if they themselves will. But do not many will to be saved, who nevertheless are not saved? how then is it true, God wills all to be saved? And therefore others thus expound: God wills, etc., it would please him, if all were saved, or all, that is, of every kind of men some. Or thus: To all he offers grace, through which, if they will, they can be saved. But how is this solution true? Are there not many who are and were, who did not even hear the word of preaching? Or he wills all to be saved, [0597A] that is, he makes the holy to will that all be saved. Or thus: He wills all to be saved, that is, no one is made saved, unless he himself will it. In like manner is understood that: *Who enlighteneth every man [n: (Joan. I)]*, that is, no one is enlightened except by him, and all are made alive in Christ, according to the same manner it behooves to be understood. Thus concerning the ambiguous diverse opinions are given.

QUESTION XIV. *One mediator of God and men, the man Christ Jesus.* Concerning the mediator it has already in part been said above. But here let it suffice to consider this which the expositor says, that in so far as he is man, he is mediator, not in so far as he is Word. But is he not mediator between the angels and God the Father, and also between the Holy Spirit, in so far as he is Word, though otherwise between the angels and God the Father, and otherwise between the Holy [0597B] Spirit and the Father? Solution. It can well be granted that, in so far as he is Word, he is mediator between the rational creatures and God the Father; but he is not mediator between God the Trinity and men in so far as he is Word, but in so far as he is man like to God in justice, to men in mortality. For if, in so far as he is Word, he were mediator between men and God the Trinity, then there would already be something mediate between himself and another, because the Word between himself and men; which is not to be granted. Someone will say: Christ is now not mortal, how then is he mediator between mortal men and the immortal God, since he was therefore called mediator; because he is like to men through mortality, and to God through justice? Solution. Even now Christ reconciles us to God the Father as the best [0597C] mediator, through that which he did for us in the days of his flesh, that is, of his mortality.



## X. ON THE FIRST EPISTLE TO TIMOTHY. (cont.)

QUESTION XV. *Adam was not seduced, but the woman.* It is asked which of the two sinned more, whether Adam or Eve? Solution. The doctors say that the woman not only sinned, but also made the man sin. Against which solution it is thus objected: Adam was seduced, because he did not believe to be true what the enemy urged; but the woman was seduced, believing to be true what the serpent was saying; and thus it follows that he sinned knowingly, and she through ignorance; but it is graver to sin knowingly than through ignorance. Whence it follows that the man sinned more than the woman. Solution. [0597D] That ignorance did not have excuse, because it proceeded from fault; for doubting she answered, saying: *Lest perhaps we die [n: (Gen. III)]*. But the man thought of the mercy of God, and of penitence; and therefore he sinned less.

QUESTION XVI. It is asked how it is true that he was not seduced? Did he not believe that to be committed as venial, which was mortal? Therefore he too was in some way deceived. Solution. He was not seduced first, and in that in which the woman was, as the authority says. But is he not seduced, if he was not seduced first, and in that in which the woman was? For if someone is not wounded in the head, is he therefore not wounded? Does a simple negation follow upon a determinate negation? Solution. The doctors of the New Testament sometimes say [0598A] that something was not, which the Scripture of the Old Testament does not say was. Whence, because the scripture of Genesis, speaking of the seduction of the woman, says nothing of any seduction of the man, therefore the Apostle says: Adam was not seduced, but the woman. According to the same manner of speaking, elsewhere the same Apostle asserts that Melchisedech had no beginning, nor end, no father, no offspring, although in truth he had a beginning, and an end, and a father, and perhaps offspring; but because the scripture so introduces him that it said nothing either of his genealogy, or birth, or death, therefore the Apostle asserts that he had none of the aforesaid things. And Ambrose says that the man is blessed with the first woman, and not with the second, as if he were to say: In Holy Scripture we read the blessing of the man with the [0598B] first woman, but we do not read where God blesses the man with the second.

QUESTION XVII. *But the woman shall be saved through the generation of children.* If the generation of children saves, what will the continent woman or the virgin do? Will she not be saved, because she has no children? What is it that elsewhere the Apostle says: *But she will be more blessed, whether widow or virgin, if she so remain?* Solution. He does not place the cause of salvation in the generation of children, but rather in faith and love, whence he adds: *If she remain in faith and love.* And the sense is: even if the woman was the cause of sin, nevertheless she shall be saved — not only the continent woman, or the virgin, but even the married woman, though, never ceasing from the generation of children, but going forward through the generation of children, she should depart from this world, provided that they remain [0598C] in faith and love. Or it will avail her for an increase of salvation, if her children, through her teaching and diligence, remain in faith and love. Or rather it is understood mystically. The woman is a type of the flesh, which elsewhere is signified by the turtledove. Adam is a figure of reason, which is figured by the sparrow. The children are good works, which are understood by the young of the turtledove, which live only in the nest of the Catholic faith; outside they do not thrive, nay they are trodden down, because good works do not profit pagans, Jews, heretics, schismatics. Whence he says here: If she remain in faith and love, namely the children of the woman, the young of the turtledove, the good works.

QUESTION XVIII. *If any man desire the office of a bishop, he desireth a good work [n: (I Tim. III)]*, etc. But this ambitious man [0598D] desires the episcopate; can it be inferred, therefore he desires a good work? Solution. The episcopate is the attention over one's own and others' life, and thus it is the name of a work, which the good man desires, who, Rachel of beautiful countenance being set aside for a time, but not abandoned, with Jacob enters in to Leah bleary-eyed, on account of the fruitfulness of children, and descends from the mountain of contemplation with Moses to the fields of action. For no one ought so to be at leisure that in that same leisure he does not think of the profit of his neighbor, nor so busy that he does not seek the contemplation of God. The episcopate is also the name of a dignity, which the ambitious man desires, who wishes rather to preside than to profit.

QUESTION XIX. *The husband of one wife,* etc. It is asked whether he ought to be called a monogamist, and can be ordained, who [0599A] before baptism had a wife; and, she being dismissed, was reborn in Christ and afterward married a believing woman. Solution. Jerome says that such a man is not a bigamist, and that he can be ordained; to whom, now made new, neither debaucheries nor other things are imputed. But Augustine says that he is not a monogamist, nor ought to be ordained, although what he had done before does not stand against him; on account of the mystery which ought to be preserved in those to be ordained; which in the aforesaid man cannot be preserved.

QUESTION XX. But do Jerome and Augustine hold contrary opinions in the solution of the aforesaid question? Solution. Jerome says that such a man is a monogamist as regards merit, and it is not a sin if he be ordained. But Augustine says that he is not a monogamist as regards the force of the sacrament, nor can he be [0599B] ordained, where the sacrament of monogamy is to be preserved; nor is it required that it be preserved in every one to be ordained, otherwise a virgin could not be ordained, since he is not the husband of one wife. The Church is the convocation of many to the worship of the one God.

QUESTION XXI. *Great is the sacrament of piety,* etc. It is asked what is here called a sacrament. For sometimes a sacrament is called a sign of a sacred thing, as the sacrament of baptism and of the altar; sometimes it is called something hidden and secret, or a mystery. Solution. Christ, hidden according to his deity, is called a sacrament, who is the cause of all religion, and of true worship. For who was ever pious and devout in faith, except the knowledge of this sacrament having first been received?

QUESTION XXII. *Which was manifested in the flesh,* etc. [0599C] It is asked of what manifestation this treats. Solution. Of that which was made by preaching, by powers, and by signs shown in the assumed flesh.

QUESTION XXIII. *Justified in the spirit.* Did not the discourse precede concerning the Word of God? but this was not justified, but rather born just from eternity. Solution. When discourse is made about Christ, it must be diligently observed what is said, and in respect of what; this therefore, justified in the spirit, is understood of Christ according to the man assumed, according to whom he is free from all sin, having justice in every mode.

QUESTION XXIV. *He appeared to the angels.* Here we have authority that the angels advance in knowledge through [0599D] those things which are done in the Church, which we have already diligently pursued above upon the passage: that the manifold wisdom may become known, etc.; and the expositor testifies the same in this place.

QUESTION XXV. *Nothing is to be rejected that is received with thanksgiving [n: (I Tim. IV)]*. It is asked whether this is contrary to the legal doctrine, which distinguishes certain foods, calling some clean, others unclean? Solution. In the law certain animals were called unclean not by nature, but by signification: so that, if concerning the pig and the lamb it be inquired, each is clean in nature, because every creature is good; yet in signification the lamb is clean, the pig unclean.

QUESTION XXVI. *Piety is profitable to all things, having the promise [0600A] of the life that now is,* etc. It is asked what he calls the promise of the present life; for concerning the promise of the future life it is established. Solution. The sufficiency of temporal things and the abundance of spiritual things. For God promises both to his worshippers. The sufficiency of temporal things is promised, where it is said: *Seek first the kingdom of God, and all these things shall be added unto you [n: (Matth. VI)]*. The abundance of spiritual things, where it is written: *Whosoever shall leave these or those things for my sake, shall receive a hundredfold [n: (Matth. XIX)]*, that is, in the present the delightfulness of spiritual goods exceeds the affluence of temporal goods by as much as a hundred surpasses unity.

QUESTION XXVII. *Which was given to thee by prophecy,* etc. It is asked, what does he call prophecy? Solution. [0600B] He calls prophecy the choice of the holy ones who chose him for bishop. Or rather the inspiration of the Holy Spirit, through which the Apostle knew him to be worthy of the episcopate.

QUESTION XXVIII. *With the imposition of hands.* What is it? Solution. He calls the imposition of hands the mystical words, by which the one chosen is confirmed for this work, receiving authority, his conscience being witness, that he may dare to offer sacrifices in the Lord's stead.

QUESTION XXIX. *The laborer is worthy of his hire [n: (I Tim. V)]*, etc. It is asked whether that which is given by the people to the preacher is a due and worthy wage; and whether he sins, if he preaches for it; and whether he who does this sells the Gospel. Solution. Who would dare to say that is not a wage, which Truth itself calls a wage? [0600C] Yet he ought not to preach for such a wage, but that he may receive an eternal wage from the Lord. It is therefore of necessity to receive that whereby one lives; it is of charity to bestow, nay a debt, that those who receive spiritual things should minister carnal things. The preacher ought to receive, that he may preach; not to preach, that he may receive.

QUESTION XXX. *But them that sin reprove before all.* To this seems to be contrary what the Lord says in the Gospel: *If thy brother shall offend against thee, rebuke him between thee and him alone [n: (Matth. XVIII)]*. Does so good a disciple disagree with so good a Master? Solution. Both are true, and sometimes that which the Lord says with his own mouth, namely when thou art alone, is to be done; for a hidden fault [0600D] ought to be reproved in secret, as the just Joseph did concerning Mary, alone suspecting adultery; otherwise, if thou do otherwise, thou wilt be a betrayer, not a corrector. Sometimes also that is to be done which Truth said by the mouth of Paul, namely when one sins openly. For a public offense needs a public satisfaction.

QUESTION XXXI. *Use a little wine.* It is asked how he counseled this. Solution. He gives a special counsel, that he govern himself by wholesome instruction; for God wishes to be served prudently, not that by excessive abstinence they become weak, and afterward require the help of physicians for themselves.

QUESTION XXXII. *Languishing about questions, and strifes of words [n: (I Tim. VI)]*, etc. The question was, whether anyone was a slave, since all have arisen [0601A] from the same fathers, and Christ has redeemed all. Likewise a strife of words, he may have freed [them], because the Lord says: *If the Son hath freed you, you are free indeed [n: (Joan. VIII)]*, which and similar things seem to dissent from the apostolic doctrine, which exhorts servants to be subject to their masters, and likewise from the words of the Lord saying: *Render therefore the things that are Caesar's to Caesar,* etc. [n: (Luc. XX.)] But such a controversy of words is to be resolved thus: this — if the Son shall have freed you, you are free indeed — is to be understood of spiritual liberty, not of carnal. But Paul speaks of carnal liberty and servitude, which the spiritual does not take away.



## X. ON THE FIRST EPISTLE TO TIMOTHY. (cont.)

QUESTION XXXIII. *The root of all evils is cupidity.* Elsewhere Scripture says: *The beginning of every sin is pride [n: (Eccle. X)]*. But here, that it is avarice or [0601B] cupidity. For if avarice is held as a species for a genus, how is each true? for if pride is the beginning of every sin, therefore also of cupidity; and if cupidity is the root of all evils, therefore also of pride; and thus the same thing is the cause and the effect of the same. Solution. When it is said that the root of all evils is cupidity, or that pride is the beginning of every sin, if the individual genera are understood, it is false; but if the genera of individuals, it is true. For there is no genus of sin which does not sometimes arise from pride, sometimes from cupidity. For there are some who covet riches, that through them they may be able to reach the summit of honor; there are others who seek dignity for this reason, that they may become richer.

QUESTION XXXIV. *Alone powerful.* Are not the angels powerful [0601C], whence they are also called Virtues and Powers? Solution. God alone is powerful by nature, from that which he is; but the angels are powerful by grace, from that which they have received.

QUESTION XXXV. *Who alone hath immortality,* etc. Are not souls and angels also by nature immortal? Solution. God alone hath immortality, that is, immutability, because he neither could, nor can, nor will be able to sin. This immortality neither the angels nor the souls of the saints have; for although they are immortal by grace, yet they are truly believed to be mutable by nature.

QUESTION XXXVI. *Who inhabiteth light inaccessible.* The prophet says: *Come ye to him, and be enlightened [n: (Psal. XXXIII)]*: is not God himself the very light which he is said to inhabit? How are we admonished to come [0602A] to this light, if it is itself inaccessible? Solution. No one comes to it of himself, but he to whom it is given by his gift.

QUESTION XXXVII. *Whom no man hath seen, nor can see.* Did not Abraham see God, and Moses, and the other fathers? How then is it true that no one has ever seen God? Likewise, do not we too hope for the vision of him? But how, if no one can see him? Solution. God in his own nature is invisible to the eye of flesh; but those visions were exhibited to the holy fathers in a subject creature. Whence Moses, after those figures in which God was seen, said: *Lord, show me thy face [n: (Exod. XXXIII)]*. But in the future God shall be seen by the clean of heart, and now he is seen by the holy angels.

[0602B]QUESTION XXXVIII. *Avoiding the profane novelties of words,* etc. It is asked whether all novelties of words are to be avoided. Solution. No, because not all are profane, as this very name "Christian," "homoousion," "the new commandment," and "the new testament," and "the new song" — they call these novelties not profane, but sacred, and befitting religion. But "hypostasis," in the time of the heretics, denoted a profane novelty, which name the heretics used, now in the signification of person, now in the signification of substance, for the deception of the simple, so that, if they granted the Trinity to be one hypostasis, they would infer: Therefore they are one person; but if they said the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit to be three hypostases, they would conclude: Therefore they are three substances. But now [0602C] this word does not denote a profane novelty, because it has been reduced to the signification of person. Whence we now simply grant the Trinity to be three hypostases, and not one; which formerly was not to be granted without a qualification, when it still retained a manifold signification.

QUESTION XXXIX. *And the oppositions of knowledge falsely so called,* etc. It is asked what the Apostle calls knowledge of a false name. Is it logic? But if it is this, how does Augustine call dialectic the science of sciences, which not only makes one knowing, but even demonstrates the knower? Solution. As I judge, he calls knowledge of a false name the sophistical art, whose oppositions are to be avoided by the pious and meek, because they avail for nothing but the subversion of the simple.



## XI. ON THE SECOND EPISTLE TO TIMOTHY.

[0601]

[0601D] *Paul an apostle [n: (II Tim. I)]*, etc. Now about to pass from the world, he writes this second epistle from Rome, from prison, to Timothy, wearied by infirmities and adversities, that he may labor constantly in the grace of God entrusted to him, exhorting him to martyrdom in many ways, [0602D] and that he may persevere in the office of right preaching, and in holy working: and foretelling what shall be in the last times, and concerning his own death. And the intention of the Apostle in this epistle is to exhort Timothy to the diligent execution of his office, and to the palm [0603A] of martyrdom, and he adds still some things about the episcopal office. The manner is such: first he greets, then he gives thanks for the good which he has, where he shows his desire of seeing him: afterward he admonishes to preaching, and to the endurance of martyrdom, by his own example and in other ways: thence he says what men will be like in the last days: finally, concerning the time of his own dissolution which is at hand.

QUESTION I. *God hath not given us the spirit of fear.* It is asked of what fear this is said. Solution. Of the fear which the love of God expels.

QUESTION II. *Which was given us before the secular ages,* etc. When could anything be given to us, we not yet existing? Solution. It was given, that is, foreseen to be given: whence he does not say simply "it was given," but added "in Christ Jesus."

[0603B]QUESTION III. Likewise it is asked why he does not say [sic: *sempliciter*] *before the ages,* but adds, *secular.* Are not all times secular? To the difference of what, then, does he say "secular"? Solution. Some say that the angels were created before the creation of the world, and those spaces which were from the creation of the angels up to the creation of the world the Apostle elsewhere calls eternal times, to the difference of which he here says "secular times."
