But someone will say: Does not time denote mutability, and eternity immutability? How then can any times be eternal? how be at once mutable and immutable? Solution. The aforesaid doctors understand this thus: In those spaces which are called eternal times there was succession, and no passing-away: and thus a certain immutability, [0603C] and according to this a certain eternity. To this opinion Jerome seems to accord. But nearly all the doctors say that the angels were created not before, but with the world; but what, according to them, ought to be understood by "eternal times," let him see and inquire who follows their opinion. But to me the opinion of the former seems the stronger, saving the reverence due to hidden things: this I say, asserting nothing rashly.

QUESTION IV. *No man being a soldier to God entangleth himself with secular businesses [n: (II Tim. II)]*, etc. Do not active men war for God, and yet are entangled in secular businesses? how then does the Apostle say: No man being a soldier to God, etc.? Solution. He calls businesses "secular" when the mind is occupied with the care of gathering money: [0603D] which no one can do at the same time, and war for God. But active men do not devote themselves to outward things for this, that they may gather money, but only for this, that they may provide necessary things for themselves and for others.

QUESTION V. *Of the seed of David according to my Gospel, wherein I labor even unto bonds.* It is asked of what tribe Christ and Mary were. But from this passage note that Christ was born of David: as in the beginning he says: *Of the seed of David according to the flesh.* And the archangel Gabriel of the same: *The Lord God shall give unto him the throne of David his father [n: (Luc. I)]*. These things I say on account of those who will not have the mother of the Lord to be of the seed of David, who was of the tribe of Judah, but say that she was only of the tribe of Levi, and on account of Joseph the husband of Mary, who was of the family of David, they say Christ is called of [0604A] the seed of David: and Origen (as it is said) thought thus. Others say — which also seems so to us — that Mary too proceeded from the stock of David, and so from the stock of Judah, not only from the stock of Levi. For the two tribes, the royal and the priestly, were intermingled, and therefore it is no wonder that the Virgin drew her origin from both, as one about to bring forth a true King and Priest. *Contend not in words.* Among the servants of God there ought to be no contention, but conference, and a modest inquiry into truth.

QUESTION VI. *Saying that the resurrection is already made.* It is asked of what resurrection the Apostle speaks: whether of that which is of minds, or of that which is of bodies? For if this be said of that which is of minds, which is made in baptism, how did those who said this [0604B] to be already made subvert the faith of some, since this is true and to be believed? Likewise, if they said this of the resurrection of bodies, how did they by this subvert the faith of some, since it is established to all that this is false? Solution. This can be understood of both resurrections: for by this, that they said the resurrection of minds to be already made, and no other of bodies to be future, they deceived, namely because they slyly denied the resurrection of bodies. Or also in this they subverted faith, that they said the resurrection of bodies to have been made: taking thence the occasion of their error, that it is written in the Gospel: *Many bodies of the saints arose, and appeared in the holy city [n: (Matth. XXVII)]*, [0604C] denying that the general resurrection, which we await, is to come.

QUESTION VII. *In a great house there are not only vessels of gold and of silver,* etc. It is asked whom he understands by the vessels of gold and silver, and whom by the vessels of wood and of earthenware? Solution. By the vessels of gold and silver he understands the predestined; by the vessels of wood and earthenware, the reprobate. Or by the golden vessels are understood the good, whether they be predestined or not; by the wooden and earthen vessels, the evil, whether they be foreknown unto life, or reprobate. And thus according to this opinion, of both there are some unto honor, some unto contumely. But according to the former, unto honor only are the golden and silver vessels; unto contumely, the wooden and earthen. *Avoid foolish questions, and without discipline,* [0604D] etc. Here he hints that not all questions are to be avoided, but only those in which there is no fruit. *Having an appearance of piety, but denying the power thereof.* Such are many in this time, as the Apostle here describes, who love themselves, not God: having the name and habit of sanctity, while within they are full of all uncleannesses. He calls the power of piety charity, of which it is written: *Love is strong as death [n: (Cant. VIII)]*: this contempt of the world necessarily precedes.

QUESTION VIII. *All who wish to live piously in Christ shall suffer persecution [n: (II Tim. III)]*. Does not the Church have a time of peace in which many live piously, and yet do not suffer persecution? Solution. The saints suffer in many ways, as in heart and body: [0605A] now from the devil, now from evil men, now from their own concupiscences; to whom also the manner of life of the weak is a persecution. For these say with the Apostle: *Who is weak, and I am not weak? who is scandalized, and I am not on fire? [n: (II Cor. XI)]*. Therefore those who live piously in Christ are not without persecution, even in time of peace.

QUESTION IX. *I have finished my course [n: (II Tim. IV)]*, [0606A] etc. Did there not still remain, for the consummation of his course, a sharper and crueler enemy, namely the sword of Nero? How then was it true that Paul had finished his course before the triumph of his passion! Solution. He says this not with the thing complete, but with sure hope. For he who made him victor in the contest had already, through revelation, rendered him certain and secure concerning the consummation.



## XII. ON THE EPISTLE TO TITUS.

[0605]

[0605B] *Paul the servant of God [n: (Tit. I)]*, etc. This epistle he writes to Titus, left as bishop of Crete, too forbearing out of humility and simplicity, from Nicopolis, concerning the episcopal office to be handled commandingly and with authority, having prescribed to him by his own useful authority. For a bishop ought to have maternal tenderness and paternal severity: that he be strong toward the proud, and gentle toward the modest: having neither a corner of fear, nor the eyebrow of haughtiness. *Let him burn and shine:* whence in the vestment of the legal high priest there was scarlet twice-dyed, which has the appearance of fire. But fire does two things: it burns and it shines: so also the bishop, with the sword of preaching, namely with fiery speech, ought to burn with biting rebuke and with fearful threatening: and to shine with soothing words, by cherishing and by promising delightful things. And therefore it is said of the manna that it hardened [0605C] at the fire, and melted at the sun. And the pontifical staff pricks from the lower part, and at the top is extended toward the front, returning upon itself: because the ecclesiastical doctor ought with the sword of the word to prick, that is, to reprove sharply those who sin, which is from the lower nature, and to direct those corrected toward the things before, yet so that by consideration of himself he return to his own conscience, if perchance he have in himself that which he announces by reproaching others. The form of the staff therefore figures this, that the bishop ought to prick the rebellious, and to draw the meek to himself, whence a certain one says: *The curved part draws the meek, the sharp point pricks the rebellious.*
The intention of the Apostle in this epistle, then, is to instruct Titus about the episcopal office, and to admonish him to handle it commandingly, and to shun heretics. The manner is such: [0605D] First he greets, then he instructs him about the episcopal office, teaching him what he ought to do, and what kind of bishops to constitute throughout the cities: then how he ought to instruct those differing whether in sex, or in age, or in condition: afterward he admonishes him about avoiding heretics.

QUESTION I. *Which he promised before the secular ages,* etc. It is asked how God promised eternal life before all times, since there were not yet men to whom he might promise? Solution. He promised, that is, in himself the eternal one he immutably purposed, that in time he would give eternal life to those whom he had already predestined.

[0606B]QUESTION II. *Without crime,* etc. He does not say "without sin": because, as the authority says, no one, however piously he live, even if he be worthy of the name of just, is without sin. But someone will say: Is there not in baptism, and through true penitence, a plenary remission of all sins? And if this is so — nay, because it is so — it is established that this baptized man, and the truly penitent, is without sin. How then is it true, No one is without sin? Solution. When it is said, No one is without sin, it is thus understood: No one, however good he be, can pass through this life without sin; yet he can live without crime, that is, without a graver sin, and without a ground of complaint, that is, without such a sin as is worthy of accusation and of damnation, as adultery, homicide, theft, and the like.

[0606C]QUESTION III. *The Cretans are always liars,* etc. It is asked why the Apostle interwove into his own teaching, in which there is divine authority, the words of a Gentile and unbelieving author, and, speaking to the Athenians, says: *In him we live, and move, and are [n: (Act. XVII)]*; and elsewhere he says: *I found an altar on which it was written, To the Unknown God, which is certain to have been taken from the writings of the heathen [n: (ibid.)]* Solution. It is permitted to the divine authority to take a testimony of truth from whence it will, which it judged to be necessary. For it does not on that account approve all the other things which are there, and judge them to be true. In figure of which thing the Hebrews despoiled the Egyptians of gold, and silver, and other things which were necessary for the divine worship. And in the law it was commanded to the Jews that, if they bought a Gentile slave, his hairs should be shaved, and the growths of his nails [0606D] cut off, and then he should be taken up for domestic uses. So, the vain and superfluous superstitions of the Gentiles being shaved off and cut away, whatever is found pure is to be taken up for the ministry of the house of God. It is to be noted that the divine authors put testimonies of this kind in a signification other than that in which they were put by their own author, as that: *It is expedient that one man die for the people, [and not] that the whole nation perish [n: (Joan. XI)]*; Caiaphas understood it one way, the evangelist another. For he by whose power and might the she-ass, as by an instrument, spoke with the voice of a man, used Caiaphas in the utterance of the aforesaid words.

[0607A]QUESTION IV. *They confess that they know God, but in their works they deny him.* Note, that some confess God with words only, others also with works; some deny God with works only, some both with words and with works; those who confess God with words and works are good. But those who deny both with words and with works are unbelievers; but those who confess with words, and deny with works, are they to be called simply confessors, or deniers? Solution. Hear. *By their fruits you shall know them [n: (Matth. VII)]*, he does not say "by their words." All the evil, therefore, deny God by their works; all are antichrists who deny Christ by their life. Antichrist has not yet come, and yet already there are many antichrists in the world.

QUESTION V. *A heretical man, after the first and second correction, avoid [n: (Tit. II)]*, etc. He calls a heretic [0608A] one who by the law assails the law. But why is such a one to be avoided? Would it not be better to confer with him often, and to recall him from error to truth? Solution. Since he is incorrigible, it is better to avoid him, because if he were more often corrected, it would be an exercise unto evil.

QUESTION VI. *Since he is condemned by his own judgment.* It is asked how such a one is condemned by his own judgment, for he believes what he says to be true, and thus does not judge himself damned. Solution. Of him who knowingly sins, who recognizes the error and knows the truth, and yet praises the error and blames the truth — of this man the Apostle seems here to speak, and such a one, his conscience being witness, is damned, as is clear to all.

## XIII. ON THE EPISTLE TO PHILEMON.

[0607]

[0607B] *Paul, a prisoner of Christ Jesus,* etc. This epistle he writes to Philemon of Colossae, who was endowed with no dignity of ecclesiastical ministry, but was a praiseworthy man among the people, to whom he sends a familiar letter on behalf of Onesimus his slave, who had fled to his loss, but, having heard the Gospel from the Apostle, was baptized, for whom the Apostle both prays pardon, and deprecates his fault, writing to him from Rome, from prison. And the intention of the Apostle is to implore pardon for Onesimus with Philemon. The manner is such: first he greets him with his wife and son; then he gives thanks to God for their good things, commending their faith and charity, afterward he beseeches Philemon, though he could command him, to spare Onesimus, and to give thanks to God, because he received him such, that he should esteem him not a slave, but a most beloved brother. Then he says that he should prepare him a lodging, as he hopes to come to him.

QUESTION I. *To Philemon, and to Appia, and to Archippus,* etc. [0608B] It is asked why in this salutation he does not preserve the dignity of persons, namely why he does not put first Archippus, who was bishop of Colossae, but puts him after, and puts Philemon first, who was endowed with no ecclesiastical dignity — is it perhaps because the one was father, and the other son? Solution. He puts the layman before the ordained man for this reason, that a domestic matter is being treated.

QUESTION II. *So may I enjoy thee, brother, in the Lord,* etc. What is it to enjoy? Is it not to cleave to something for its own sake through love? but thus God alone is to be enjoyed, because God alone is to be loved for his own sake; how then does the Apostle say to Philemon: So may I enjoy thee, brother, in the Lord? Solution. He does not say simply, "I shall enjoy thee, brother"; but he adds "in the Lord," by this hinting that he has placed the end of his love in the Lord. Or "to enjoy" is said to be "to use with a certain delight," and thus it can be taken here.



## XIV. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE HEBREWS.

[0607]

[0607C] *In many ways [n: (Hebr. I)]*, etc. Paul, the excellent doctor, the Apostle of the Gentiles, wishing to honor his ministry. According to which he says in the Epistle to the Romans: *As long as I am the Apostle of the Gentiles, I will honor my ministry, trying if by any means I may provoke to emulation my own flesh [n: (Rom. XI)]*. To the churches of the Hebrews he writes this epistle, treating of the eminence of Christ according to both natures, and of the uselessness of the Mosaic law; building up in many ways that the faith of Jesus Christ, without the legal observances, suffices for justice and salvation. But that the legal observances, after Christ's passion, [0608C] not only do not profit, but even hinder, excluding the estimation of certain Hebrews, who, confessing Christ, thought that the legal observances were to be kept; and by their authority had led into this error even some who had come to Christ from heathenism; therefore the Apostle, providing for the Gentiles, lest thenceforth they be drawn into this error by the authority of the Hebrews, provoking the Jews also to emulation, commends the grace of God made to the faithful in this time through Christ the true high priest, showing the law to be reprobated. The intention, therefore, of the Apostle [0609A] in this epistle is to show the eminence of Christ, and the sufficiency of faith, as well as the insufficiency and uselessness of the law.

The manner of treating is such. First he proposes that the words of Christ are to be heard as those of the prophets, and further, by comparing him with the prophets, and preferring him; because in him God spoke as in the prophets, and he is greater than they. Then he commends him alternately according to both natures, namely the human and the divine, afterward he compares him with the angels, and prefers him, interweaving many things about his excellence according to both natures. Then he compares him with Moses and prefers him. Then by many reasons and authorities he declares that the grace of faith is to be carried through for the shadow of the law; and the priesthood of Christ to the Levitical [0609B] priesthood, and the New Testament to the Old; and he shows that its one sacrifice is to be set before the many sacrifices of that; because there is the shadow, here the truth. Finally he sets down a description of faith, commending it by many testimonies. But toward the end he adds a moral instruction.

QUESTION I. First it is asked, why to this epistle, as to the others, he did not prefix his own name, which is Paul; why also he was silent about the name of dignity, which is Apostle? Solution. Because he was odious to the Hebrews, to whom he seemed a destroyer of the law, he kept silent his name, odious to them, lest the ill-will toward his name, prefixed, should exclude the usefulness of the reading that follows; knowing also their pride, and showing his own humility, he was unwilling to put forward the dignity of his order by naming [0609C] himself Apostle, but, keeping silent the merit of his office, himself humble toward the proud, he did not name himself apostle, lest the proud be indignant. But someone will say? Does not the Apostle write to the faithful who were at Jerusalem, to whom the name of Paul was not odious, nor did they envy his dignity as though he were proud; how then is it true that he kept silent his proper name or the name of dignity because it was odious to the Hebrews, since to those to whom he wrote he was not odious, but beloved? Solution. Among those to whom, as an excellent preacher of grace, he was much pleasing, there were certain zealots of the law, who thought and preached that the law was to be kept together with grace, and to these the name of Paul was odious. For they found no one so contrary to their false opinion as Paul, whence they persecuted [0609D] him as much as they could. It is to be noted that there were some saying that this epistle was by no means the apostle Paul's, because his name is not prefixed to it as in all the others, and also because it shines with a more splendid and more eloquent style than the others; but that it was either Luke's, or Barnabas's, or Clement's. To whom Jerome thus responds: If it is therefore not to be called Paul's, because it is not inscribed with his name, then neither is it any of theirs, nay of no one at all, since it has no one's name in the title, which was done for the aforesaid cause already given. But that it shines with greater eloquence than the others is not to be wondered at, since it is natural for each one to be more capable in his own language than in a foreign one; since therefore he wrote this one [0610A] alone in the Hebrew language, but the others in Greek, what wonder if it shines with greater eloquence?

QUESTION II. *In these days.* It is asked what days he calls them. Solution. The Apostle calls the time of grace "days" on account of the eminent doctrine of faith and the knowledge of salvation, whence elsewhere: *Behold, now is the day of salvation [n: (II Cor. VI)]*.

QUESTION III. *Whom he hath appointed heir of all things,* etc. It is asked, according to which nature Christ is here called heir of all things. Solution. It can well be said that according to the divine nature he is here called heir, that is, possessor, and Lord of all things, that is, of all nature. Or according to his humanity he is called heir of all things, namely of those to be saved, or of Jews and Gentiles. For this is the heir of the world, that seed in which all nations are blessed, [0610B] to whom the Father speaks, saying: *Ask of me, and I will give thee the nations for thine inheritance [n: (Psal. II)]*.

QUESTION IV. *By whom also he made the ages.* Did not God the Father make all things through the Son, visible and invisible, mutable and immutable? Why then does the Apostle not say: By whom he made all things, but "by whom he made also the ages"! Solution. These visible and mutable things are more known to us; and therefore in these we more admire the power of God than in those which are unknown; and this is the reason why he rather named the ages specially.

QUESTION V. *Who, being the brightness of glory,* etc. It is asked why he uses this likeness, and elsewhere another? Solution. The Apostle, wishing to show that, although the Son is from the Father, yet he is coeternal with him, [0610C] uses a proportional likeness of temporal things, saying: who, being the brightness of glory, as if he were to say: as the brightness of fire, although it be from the fire, yet is coeval with the fire, and would be coeternal with it, if it were eternal; for the fire was never without brightness; so the Son, although he be from the Father, yet is coeternal with him, because the Father was never without the Son. Likewise, wishing to show the identity of nature which the Son has with the Father, he uses a proportional likeness of other things, calling the Son the figure of the substance, because he could not demonstrate both in the same things. For in creatures nothing is found which has being from another, and is of the same nature or substance, and which is not preceded by it. But the Son so has being from the Father, that he is coeternal with him; as the brightness [0610D] is coeval with the fire, and is of the same essence or nature with the Father. As the man begetting, and the man begotten of him — for as a man cannot beget except that which he himself is, that is, because a man begets a man, so God begets nothing other than what he himself is, that is, God begets God. Reader, diligently observe these words of the Apostle, and the exposition of the holy ones upon them; and thou wilt notice how both the Sabellian heresy and the Arian is here manifestly destroyed.

QUESTION VI. *And upholding all things by the word of his power.* It is asked how God is said to uphold all things by the word, since to uphold does not pertain to the property of the Word. Likewise, whether he is beneath all things, and not rather over and above them, that he should be said to uphold them. Solution. [0611A] He is said to uphold all things by the word rather than by power for this reason, that in upholding — that is, in containing, and governing, and preserving — he may be understood to sustain no labor or difficulty, he who is more inward than every thing, because all things are in him; and higher than every thing, because he is above all; and more ancient than every thing, because he is before all; and newer than every thing, because he is after all, that is, after the beginnings of all things.

QUESTION VII. *He sitteth at the right hand,* etc. The expositor says that the man assumed was exalted even to equality with the Father, which is understood by the "right hand": therefore it is asked whether it is to be granted that the man assumed is equal to the Father; but since the Son says: *The Father is greater than I [n: (Joan. XIV)]*: which is to be understood [0611B] according to that which is assumed, how is the same equal to the Father, since the Son according to it is less than the Father? Solution. That man, inasmuch as he is man, is less than the Father, and not equal: but inasmuch as the man assumed is God, the Son of God, he is not less than the Father, but equal. It is therefore to be seen what is said of what, and in respect of what, as often as discourse occurs about Christ; yet there are many who do not grant that the man assumed is God, as has already been said above.

QUESTION VIII. *Being made so much better than the angels.* It is asked according to which nature he here speaks of Christ: that according to divinity he is better than the angels, there is no question; but yet he was not made better than the angels, but rather born. By this therefore, [0611C] that he says "made," we are compelled here to understand of Christ according to his humanity, namely that he was made better than the angels. But to this seems contrary what is found in the Psalm: *Thou hast lessened him below the angels [n: (Psalm. VIII)]*. How, according to the same nature, can he be better, and less? Solution. He was made less than the angels by the mortality and passion of the flesh; and he is greater and better than the same by the fullness of grace, from which the angels themselves also receive.

QUESTION IX. *I have this day begotten thee,* etc. It is asked of what day he speaks. Solution. Augustine says that this can be understood of that day on which Christ was born according to the flesh. Yet it can be understood more divinely of his eternal generation: whence he says "I begot," [0611D] lest it be understood as new; for "today" is said of a past begetting; and thus it is intimated to be eternal, in which nothing is past, as though it had ceased to be, nor future, as though it were not yet.

QUESTION X. But again it can be asked how this can be expounded of the temporal generation of Christ, since God the Father did not beget Christ according to human nature? For as Christ has no mother according to his Deity, so neither a father according to his humanity. Since therefore he is not his father according to this nature, how can it be said that he begot him according to it? Solution. To beget does not always denote a natural generation, but sometimes a gratuitous one, as to thee: *Of his own will he begot us by the word of truth [n: (Jac. I)]*. Because, therefore, the temporal generation of Christ [0612A] was the work of the whole Trinity, it can not unfittingly be said that the Father begot him even according to his humanity. But someone will say: Therefore Christ is a son of grace, and so adoptive? Solution. It is not true: by grace indeed that man was made a son, not of grace, but of nature.



## XIV. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE HEBREWS. (cont.)

QUESTION XI. And again he says: *Therefore to him for a father, and he himself shall be to me for a son,* etc. It is asked concerning this authority how it pertains to Christ, since neither the things preceding nor the things following that place, whence this authority is taken, seem to admit this. For in the book of Kings God the Father is brought in speaking to David: *Thou shalt not build me a house, because thou art a man of blood; but thy son, who shall reign after thee: [0612B] who if he shall have done wickedly, I will correct him with the rod of men, and in the blows* [stripes] *of the sons of men, and I will establish his kingdom for ever and ever, and I will be to him for a father, and he himself shall be to me for a son [n: (II Reg. VII)]*, etc. All these things can be fitted neither to Solomon nor to Christ. Solution. In divine Scripture often in the same series certain things are set down which refer to the history alone; certain things to the mystical sense alone; certain things also which can be taken both ways, as in the aforesaid; certain things refer to Solomon, certain to Christ.

QUESTION XII. Likewise it happens in many others. For if anyone, following the history, considering the things preceding and following, where it is written: *Behold a virgin shall conceive, and shall bear a son [n: (Isa. VII)]*: it will seem to him [0612C] rather that this authority refers to that young woman of Isaiah than to the Virgin, since there are there certain things which can in no way agree with the mother of our Emmanuel, which pertain to the history alone, although a certain mode of speech and property of relation sufficiently indicate that all things refer to the same person: Thy son, he says, who shall reign after thee, etc. Solution. It is to be known that there are three kinds of relations: for one is personal, as Saul, who is also Paul; another general and simple, as the woman who condemned, saved; another vocal, as my hands, which made you, are fastened with nails.

QUESTION XIII. Likewise it is asked according to which [0612D] nature the Father says: I will be to him for a father. For according to the human he is not father; according to the divine he was father from eternity; how then does he say: I will be father to him? Solution. Then a thing is said to be made, when it begins to be known; because therefore through the resurrection it was made plain to all the faithful, and in the future will be made plain even to the unbelieving, that he himself is Father, and that one Son, therefore he says: I will be to him for a father. Or thus: I will be to him, that is to the man assumed, for a father, and that man himself shall be to me for a son, yet not according to humanity, but according to divinity.

QUESTION XIV. *And when again he bringeth in the first-begotten,* etc. It is asked how the Apostle calls "entrance" that coming which the Lord himself calls "going forth," saying: *I came forth from the Father, and am come into the world [n: (Joan. XVI)]*, calling it entrance [0613A] when he says, *And again when he bringeth in [n: (Hebr. I)]*. Solution. As regards the Father, who was within, the coming of the Lord is called a going forth; but as regards us, who were without, it is called an entrance, or conversely.

QUESTION XV. *And let all the angels of God adore him,* etc. Concerning the man assumed it is wont to be asked whether he is to be adored with that adoration which is called latria: for latria is exhibited to God alone, and not to a creature; but the man assumed is a creature, and thus it seems that latria is not to be exhibited to him. Solution. Latria is exhibited to that man not because he is man, but because he is God; concerning this it has already been said above; but that adoration which is exhibited to men or to angels is called dulia.

[0613B]QUESTION XVI. *Who maketh his angels spirits,* etc. It is asked how he does this? Solution. "Spirit" is the name of a nature, as "man"; "angel" is the name of an office, as "soldier": therefore of spirits are made angels, as of men are made soldiers; not of angels are made spirits, just as neither of soldiers are made men.

QUESTION XVII. *And his ministers a flame of fire,* etc. It is asked how he makes a flame of fire, that is, the seraphim, his ministers, since the seraphim always stand by: how then is that order said to minister, if it always stands by? For they are not said to minister except from the fact that they minister. Solution. It can be said that the seraph receives immediately from God that which he reveals to an inferior, who is sent to us: whence also the inferior [0613C] is reckoned by the name of the superior, whose office he bears, or rather from whom he receives the office. Whence Isaiah says: *One of the seraphim flew to me, and touched my lips [n: (Isa. VI)]*. Or it can be said that, when some great things are to be done or announced, then those superiors are sent, whose office nevertheless is not to minister, but rather to stand by. Note that nothing is done which is not done either by God commanding or by God permitting: by which words it is intimated that not everything which is done is a work of God: for what he only permits, he does not do. This I say on account of those who say that whatever is, in that which it is, is from God. If this be so, the aforesaid distinction is null, that some things are done [sic: *finnt*] by God commanding, others are done by God permitting.

[0613D]QUESTION XVIII. *Therefore he anointed thee,* etc. The expositor says that Christ was anointed for this, that he might love justice. But did he not, from the moment he existed, love justice, nor was he anointed before he was full of love? how then is he said to be anointed for this, that he might love justice? Solution. By the oil of anointing is understood grace itself, and virtue: by "to love" he insinuates the very act proceeding from virtue. Causally therefore the anointing precedes the act of loving, and not in time. Or pursue another exposition.

QUESTION XIX. *Therefore he anointed,* that is, therefore because thou hast loved justice, he anointed thee. But did he not from his very cognition have the fullness of anointing? what anointing then did he receive as it were in reward, because he loved justice? Solution. By the oil of gladness, [0614A] as it were anointed by the merit of love, according to certain men is understood the stole of the mature resurrection.

QUESTION XX. *God, thy God, hath anointed thee,* etc.— the other, that is the former, is the vocative case, as if he should say: O Son, God, thy God hath anointed thee. But what God has a God? what God is anointed? Solution. Christ is God, and has a God, not inasmuch as he is God, but inasmuch as he is man, and according to that he is anointed.

QUESTION XXI. *They shall perish,* etc. It is asked concerning which heavens he says that they are to perish. Solution. Concerning the airy heavens, as the expositor says, which perished through the flood, and are to perish by fire. Whence it is asked, if they already perished through the flood, how are they again to perish through [0614B] fire? If they have already perished, have they not ceased to be? and if they have already ceased, how shall they again perish by fire? Solution. They perished through the flood, that is, they were changed for the worse; and those same are to perish by fire, that is, they are to be changed into a better state. But concerning the earth and the higher heavens there is no question but that they are to be changed for the better: whence Peter says: *We look for new heavens and a new earth [n: (II Pet. III)]*. Concerning water and air it is doubted whether they are to be changed into a better state, like earth and heaven, because in the Apocalypse it is written: *And the sea was now no more [n: (Apoc. XXI)]*. And here concerning the airy heavens it is written: They shall perish. Whence it seems to some that water and air will lose those properties from which these names belong to them, [0614C] and with them will lose also the names, yet they will not be wholly annihilated; but neither water nor air will they any longer be called, these things being taken away and annihilated whence before they were so called.

QUESTION XXII. *They are all ministering spirits,* etc. It was said above that it is not the office of the superiors to minister, how then are they all ministering? Solution. It can be said that all spirits minister to us either immediately, as the inferiors; or through the mediation of others, as the superiors and the middle ones; or by "all" he gathers only those who are of the lowest order, who are properly called angels, whose office it especially is to minister, and therefore they alone are properly ministering.

QUESTION XXIII. *What is man, that thou art mindful [0614D] of him [n: (Hebr. II)]*, etc. It is asked how this is to be expounded. The solution is expounded in three ways. First thus: That by "man" is understood the old man, by "the son of man" the new man is understood. Secondly thus: That by "man" is understood any good man, by "the son of man" Christ. Thirdly thus: That by "man" and "the son of man" the same is understood, namely Christ.

QUESTION XXIV. *Thou hast made him a little less than the angels,* etc. Above he said that he was made better than the angels: here he says that he was made less than they: and we have said in what way both are true. But here it is asked whether it is to be granted absolutely that Christ is less than the angels. Which certain men wish thus to prove: Christ is all that he was made, but [0615A] he was made less: therefore he is less than the angels. Solution. It is not to be said absolutely that he is less than the angels; but that "a little less" he was made less, is thus understood, that is, according to something he was made less, namely according to the infirmity of the flesh and the passion of death, as the authority says: nor is it consequent that, if he is less according to something, he is therefore absolutely less.

QUESTION XXV. *Thou hast subjected all things under his feet [n: (Psal. VIII)]*. "All" sometimes gathers the whole, as all the angels of heaven are just. Sometimes by determination it is reduced to a part, as *all mine are thine [n: (Joan. XVII)]*, for this God the Father says to the elder son, that is to the Jewish people: therefore by "all" here it behooves to understand, not those things which belong to God according to [0615B] nature, but only those which are necessary for salvation. Sometimes also "all" has the force of a negation, as *all things which I have heard from my Father, I have made known to you [n: (Joan. XV)]*, that is, none except those which I have heard. Whence it can be asked how this is to be taken when it is said, thou hast subjected all things. Solution. Some say that it is reduced to a part, and by "all" they understand only angels and men. But if the more excellent things are subjected to Christ, how not the lesser? Since Ambrose says that just as nothing is excepted from the work of God, so neither from the power of Christ. And Augustine says: No creature will be not subject, to whom the chief angels are subjected. And the Apostle himself shows that nothing is excepted, when he subjoins: *For in that he subjected all things to him, he left nothing not subject to him.*



## XIV. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE HEBREWS. (cont.)

[0615C]QUESTION XXVI. *But now we see not yet all things subject to him,* etc. The prophet says, thou hast subjected all things to him: which the Apostle, expounding, subjoined, he left nothing not subject to him; how then does he say here: We see not yet all things subject to him? Solution. David, after his manner, uses the past for the future out of certitude, saying: Thou hast subjected all things to him. The Apostle, understanding this, said above that the world was to be subjected to him: and here he says: We see not yet all things subject to him; as if he should say: already in part the prophecy is fulfilled, in part still to be fulfilled. But someone will say: do not all things serve the divine dispensation, whose will nothing can resist? and thus already, willing or unwilling, all things are subjected to him. How then does the Apostle say, We see not yet all things subject to him? Solution. Subjection [0615D] is one kind general, another special. Likewise one hidden, another necessary, another voluntary. According to the general, and the hidden, and the necessary, all things are already subject to God; according to the voluntary, not yet are all things subject which shall be subjected. Not yet does every tongue of things heavenly, earthly and infernal confess that the Lord Jesus is in the glory of the Father [n: (Philipp. II)]. Not yet are all enemies made the footstool of his feet [n: (Psal. CIX)]. All which indeed shall wholly come to pass in the future; therefore the Apostle says: We see not yet all things, namely voluntarily, or openly, subject to him. Note that only God is greater than the human mind, not any of the angels. Angels indeed can be greater divine ones than men as regards the body and the mind [0616A] weighed down by the corruption of the body [n: (Sap. IX)]: but not as regards the very purity of the mind, in which it was assumed by the Word.

QUESTION XXVII. *That by the grace of God he might taste death for all,* etc. The death of Christ did not profit all; how then did he taste death for all? Solution. The whole is here reduced to a part: namely, for all the predestined he tasted death. Or it is therefore said that he tasted death for all, because, as far as in him lies, the death of Christ suffices for all.

QUESTION XXVIII. *It became him for whom are all things, and by whom are all things,* etc. It is asked how it became God the Father to perfect the author of the salvation of the faithful through the passion of death. Wherein consists this [0616B] fittingness, since it rather seems unfitting that the Lord of glory should die? Solution. If Christ did not die, man would perish: and if this were so, God would not be glorified by the whole; and this would be unfitting, since all things were made for God to be glorified. Likewise, if Christ did not die, man would not be saved, and thus the divine predestination would not be fulfilled, and this would be unfitting, that it should be made void. Nor was it unfitting that the author of salvation should die for us, since he is our neighbor, and brother, and it befits a neighbor to provide for the advantage of his neighbors as much as he can; whence it follows:

QUESTION XXIX. *For which cause he is not ashamed to call them brethren,* etc. It is asked whether Christ is therefore called our brother, because he was made a partaker of our nature, [0616C] taking the form of a servant [n: (Philipp. II)]. But since the nature assumed by God is common to good and bad, it seems that Christ is not only the brother of the faithful but also of the unfaithful. Solution. Christ is not called our brother only because he shares in our nature, but also because we have received of the fullness of his grace. Therefore, for anyone to be called a brother of Christ, it behooves that he be joined to him by nature and by grace.

QUESTION XXX. *And he himself,* namely Christ, *in like manner partook of the same,* namely of the children, *or of flesh and blood.* But it is asked how Christ partook of the children? Solution. That is, he was made a child consisting of soul and flesh, as the expositor says. Or he partook of flesh and blood, that is, he was made [0616D] man, and this "in like manner," that is, passible and mortal.

QUESTION XXXI. *That he might destroy him who had the empire of death.* Since death is a punishment justly inflicted by God, and thus is not unfittingly said to be from God, it is asked how the devil is said to be the author of death. For the same thing does not seem able to be from God and from the devil. Solution. Because death happened from sin, which the devil persuaded, therefore he has the empire of death, or is said to have it; because he was the cause of death, therefore he was called its author: whence it is said that God does not make death, that is, its cause has not its being from God. Note that this is the justice by which we were redeemed. For because the devil shed the blood of one who was not a debtor, [0617A] he was commanded to give back the debtors; and because he shed the blood of the innocent, in whom he found nothing, he was commanded to withdraw from the guilty, whom by a certain right he seemed to possess.

QUESTION XXXII. The expositor says that unless it were a man who conquered the devil, not justly, but by violence would man be taken from him. But had not the devil done injury to God, who first fraudulently deceived the servant, and afterward violently possessed him? What injustice then would God do, if by the sole word of his power he should snatch man from the hand of the most unjust invader? Solution. All the works of God are just, but in some there is also manifest power, and justice lies hidden; in some also hidden power lies, and justice is manifest. If therefore Christ in our liberation had used [0617B] manifest power and hidden justice, it might seem to some less discerning that man was taken from the devil not justly, but by violence: but that all iniquity might stop its mouth [n: (Psal. CVI)], our Lord used in our redemption manifest justice and hidden power.

QUESTION XXXIII. *That he might deliver them, who through the fear of death,* etc. It is asked to whose servitude they were subject, whom the Lord delivered? Solution. The devil, before the coming of Christ, obtained the effect of his wickedness through death: by the fear of which he cast down those whom in no other way he could cast down. For they gave all things for their life. Whence in Job it is read: *Skin for skin; all that a man hath he will give for his life [n: (Job I)]*. And so, conquered by the fear of death, they yielded, and succumbed to temptation, [0617C] until Christ came, who by dying and rising took away the fear of death from their midst. Whence the saints deride death, and desire to be dissolved, and to be with Christ [n: (Philipp. I)]. Or it can be understood of the fear of punishment, whereby the Jews served under the law, whom Christ, the law being emptied, through grace liberated from the burden of the law and from servile fear.

QUESTION XXXIV. *For nowhere doth he take hold of the angels,* etc. It is asked whether God could have assumed the angelic nature into the unity of the person as he did the human? Solution. It seems to us that he could, but would not; wherein the dignity of our race is understood: for to man, who had received less than the angels in his creation, more was conferred in his glorification through [0617D] grace. For human nature does not adore the angelic nature above it, but rather conversely.

QUESTION XXXV. *Wherefore he ought in all things to be made like unto his brethren.* Was he not by grace alone made like unto his brethren in this, that he was born man, that he was reared, suffered, died? how then does the Apostle say that this "ought" to be, since this is not a debt, but a gratuitous gift of God? Solution. He does not say simply "ought," but added, *that he might be merciful [n: (Hebr. II)]*: for by dying he could not have mercy on us, unless he first became passible and mortal. Or he therefore says "ought," that is, it was fitting for him to be made like unto his brethren. For by this, that he bore our infirmities and death for us, he provoked us to humility, and kindled charity in us.

[0618A]QUESTION XXXVI. *For in that he himself hath suffered and been tempted, he is able,* etc. Was he not before his passion, nay before his incarnation, able to help the tempted? for his power was not augmented through the passion; what then is it, "in that he himself hath suffered"? "he is able to help those who are tempted"? Solution. After the passion he well knows even by experience what that temptation is, and the tribulation of those who suffer: whence with much alacrity he stretches out his hand to compassionate; therefore he is said to be able to do these things, and perfectly to know them. For he knows who they are who suffer for him, he knows when and how and to whom he ought to stretch out his hand. Whence the Prophet: *The Lord is become a refuge for the poor in his opportunities, in tribulation [n: (Psal. IX)]*.

[0618B]QUESTION XXXVII. *By so much hath he greater honor than the house who framed it [n: (Hebr. III)]*, etc. Does not often he who dispenses in the house have greater glory in it than he who framed it? how then does the Apostle say: By so much greater, etc. Solution. This is to be understood of the spiritual house, as if he should say: Christ as Lord and Creator is greater than Moses the servant and minister.

QUESTION XXXVIII. *According to the day of temptation,* etc. It is asked by whom temptation is made. Solution. Man tempts, that he may know what he is ignorant of, according to which acceptation temptation is a certain soothing foretaste of the mind, running before, for the recognizing of something which before was unknown. God tempts, that he may prove through affliction and tribulation, that he may crown the proved. [0618C] The devil tempts, that he may cast someone down into sin: whence it is said: *And lead us not into temptation [n: (Matth. VI)]*, that is, suffer us not to be cast down into sin. The flesh tempts, when we feel its inordinate motions. The world tempts, when it provokes us through vanity to the love of itself.

QUESTION XXXIX. *Wherefore I was offended,* etc. Another translation has "I was near." How then, if offended, was he near; and if near, how offended? and if both readings cannot stand, which rather is to be held? Solution. He was near by applying the scourges of correction; because it is the part of a neighbor to correct his neighbor. He was offended and angry, because even through the scourges they would not repent. Or toward the good for forty [0618D] years he can be understood angry in this; because for so long a time he did not bring them into the land of promise: this opinion nevertheless the following text does not seem to approve. But toward the wicked he can be understood near for forty years in this, that for so long a time he bore with them, nor wholly destroyed them.

QUESTION XL. *If they shall enter into my rest.* It is asked by what figure this is said. Solution. It is the figure which is called aposiopesis, and the sense is: If this shall be, anything impossible will be, or let me no longer be believed in anything. Note that there are four Sabbaths, or rests. The first rest is that of God, of which it is written in Genesis: *And God rested on the seventh day [n: (Gen. II)]*; the second rest is the land of promise; the third, the quiet of the mind; the fourth is the eternal. The first and [0619A] fourth are of God; the second and third of man. The first is a figure of the fourth, the second of the third.



## XIV. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE HEBREWS. (cont.)

QUESTION XLI. *As I swore in my wrath,* etc. It is asked what here is called the wrath of God: for it is agreed that such a passion does not fall upon God. Solution. He calls the wrath of God the immovable sentence of eternal justice, by the figure which is called anthropopathos: which occurs whenever those things which, said of men, signify passion, are figuratively attributed to God. God therefore swears in wrath, when he firmly determines to punish for the obstinacy of sin. Note that there are four kinds of symbols, that is of signs, that is of those things which are figuratively said of God. For some symbols are like, and are taken from corporeal things, as fire, light, sun and others of this kind. Others are like and taken from incorporeal things, [0619B] as reason, intellect, spirit and the like. Others are unlike and taken from corporeal things, as lion, bear, worm and such like. Others are unlike, and taken from incorporeal things, as wrath, fury, grief, repentance and the like.

QUESTION XLII. *That none of you be hardened by the fallacy of sin,* etc. It is asked what he calls the fallacy of sin. Solution. The fallacy of sin is when one presumes upon the grace of God: and secure in this confidence lies in sins. Or when one promises that in the coming year he will amend, that in the present he may sin more securely, which is done at the instigation of the devil. There can also be a threefold fallacy noted here, so that the first is when we consent to a depraved motion; the second, when we break forth into an evil work; the third, when in the habit of sin [0619C] we take delight.

QUESTION XLIII. *But with whom was he offended for forty years,* etc. It is asked how this is to be understood. Solution. The number forty indicates the entirety of years: therefore he is said to be offended with them for those forty years, because he is angry with sinners even to the end of their life.

QUESTION XLIV. *And indeed the works from the foundation of the world [n: (Hebr. IV)]*, etc. It is asked where mention is made of that eternal rest, which is signified by the land of promise: for the rest of the Sabbath is there treated of: And God rested on the seventh day *from all the work which he had done.* But of the rest of the land of Palestine mention seems to be made there, where it is said: If they shall enter into my rest [0619D]. Solution. Where the rest of the Sabbath, or the rest of the land of Palestine according to the letter, is treated of, there also the true rest is treated of anagogically, which is signified by those two.

QUESTION XLV. *God rested on the seventh day,* etc. Scripture says that in six days God made all his works, so that he made nothing new afterward; but does he not daily create new souls? how then does he make nothing new? Solution. God makes nothing except from matter made in the first condition, as any corporeal thing: or after the likeness of things then already made, as incorporeal spirits, namely human souls.

QUESTION XLVI. *Again he determineth a certain day, To-day,* etc. It is asked how, by that which the Apostle here [0620A] brings in, he compels the Jews to understand a rest other than the other, which was that of the land of promise, saying: *To-day if you shall hear his voice,* etc. Solution. Those to whom he wrote had faith, and believed David to be a prophet: according to which it was necessary to understand another day, and another rest than that rest of the land of promise, from the words of the prophet there set down: and thus the proof of the Apostle proceeds:

QUESTION XLVII. *For the word is living,* etc. The expositor says that the Son of God sees how reason and sensuality agree in their differences: but do they not differ in their differences? how then do they agree in their differences? can any things agree and differ in the same? Solution. He does not say that in the differences by which reason differs from sensuality, [0620B] or conversely, these two agree, but that reason has differences by which it is distinguished from itself, while it gazes upon God, thinking of the divine *usia*, or lower, considering the heavenly things, it contemplates the natures of invisible spirits, or on earth it deals with the things of the world rightly to be handled. Likewise sensuality too has differences by which it is divided from itself, while, more given to the lowest things, it is lower, or, called back from them, it is worthier. So then the Son of God sees how the superior difference of sensuality, by consenting, agrees with the differences of reason; or the inferior difference of reason, pressed and captive, sometimes consents to the inferior difference of sensuality. Note that "soul" is put either for sensuality, or for carnal sins, or for carnal [0620C] thoughts: so also "spirit" for reason, or for spiritual sins, or for good thoughts. When it is said that the word of God reaches even to the division of the soul and the spirit [n: (Hebr. IV)], whence thou wilt find a threefold exposition in the gloss on account of the threefold acceptation of soul and spirit.

QUESTION XLVIII. *Tempted in all things for the likeness, without sin.* In that he was tempted, he knows how to compassionate. In that he is without sin, he is able to deliver. The expositor says that it is impossible for a man to know afflictions, unless he has experienced them. But do not many from sight alone know the afflictions of others, which they have nowhere suffered: so much even that they compassionate them? how then is it true, *it is impossible for a man,* etc.? Solution. He who has not experienced does not know so perfectly [0620D] as he who knows by experiment: which manner of knowing the Apostle notes, saying: *We have not a high priest who [cannot compassionate,* that is, has known.

QUESTION XLIX. *Neither doth any man take the honor to himself [n: (Hebr. V)]*, etc. Do not many very great men in this time thrust themselves forward: and, not called, sometimes even by violence take the honor to themselves? how then is it true: *Neither doth any man take the honor to himself?* Solution. No one acting piously and religiously thrusts himself forward. *Neither did Christ glorify himself,* etc., since this glorification of the Father was made concerning the Son according to the human nature.

QUESTION L. It is asked how this authority: [0621A] *This day have I begotten thee,* which according to Augustine is expounded of the eternal generation, is in this place brought in by the Apostle: how does the Father, saying, *This day have I begotten thee,* testify that Christ, according as he is man, is his Son, and the redeemer of the world? Solution. Jerome says that this authority is thus understood of the human generation of Christ: *This day,* that is in the time of grace and light, *have I begotten thee,* that is, I have incarnated thee: or "I have begotten," that is, I have shown thee to be begotten: according to which this authority is well brought in.

QUESTION LI. *According to the order of Melchisedech,* etc. The expositor says that Melchisedech was not temporal: but was he not only a man, and thus temporal, because every man is temporal? Solution. It is therefore said that he is not temporal: because Scripture [0621B] was silent as to the beginning and end of his life, in figure of Christ, who lacks beginning and end. Or he can be said a priest not temporal on account of his priesthood, which remains in Christ for ever.

QUESTION LII. *He was heard for his reverence,* etc. It is asked what it is that Christ was heard for his reverence. Was not Stephen likewise heard for his reverence, and the other saints? What then is great concerning Christ if he was heard for his reverence? Solution. It is no wonder if certain things are said of Christ common to the martyrs, since certain things also common to the legal priests are here set down. Yet it can be said that Stephen was heard for the reverence of Christ rather than for his own: for Christ entered by himself, others through him: [0621C] whence also *of his fullness we all have received [n: (Joan. I)]*. And thus something special is said of Christ when it is read that he was heard for his reverence: which is noted in this, that without sin and by charity alone he died and suffered.

QUESTION LIII. *He learned obedience by the things which he suffered,* etc. We learn those things which we do not know: since therefore Christ knew all things as God, how did he learn what he was not ignorant of? Solution. This question has already been sufficiently discussed above. For it was said that cognition is twofold, one of comprehension, the other of experience: that which is of comprehension he had from eternity, but the other he learned in time.

QUESTION LIV. *Which are the elements of the beginning of the words of God,* etc. It is asked what is the difference between these [0621D] three: elements, beginning, word of God? Solution. He calls the word of God the evangelical doctrine; the beginning, the Creed and the Lord's Prayer: the elements are the matter which the Creed contains, as the nativity, the passion, and the other articles of faith.

QUESTION LV. *Every one that is a partaker of milk,* etc. The expositor says: He who does not grasp, *The Word was made flesh [n: (Joan. I)]*: how will he grasp, *In the beginning was the Word [n: (ibid.)]*? It is asked therefore how it is lighter to understand, The Word was made flesh, than that, In the beginning was the Word, since that concerning the incarnation of the Word is against human reason: but the former are higher, and concerning the Trinity of persons, and the unity of essence, investigated by reason, as from the prophets [0622A] it appears? Solution: those things concerning the incarnation are therefore said lighter, because they were visibly exhibited: but these more difficult, because comprehended by the intellect alone of few.

QUESTION LVI. Likewise it is asked how those to whom the Apostle here speaks needed to be taught what are the elements of the beginning of the words of God. Were they not faithful? how were they ignorant of the nativity, the passion, and the other articles of faith: since without these no one is faithful? Solution. They knew these things, but not fully.

QUESTION LVII. *Wherefore, leaving the word [n: (Hebr. VI)],* etc. Since the word of beginning seems necessary to them, how does he say, leaving the word of beginning? Solution. The sense is: [0622B] you ought not always to tarry in those things which pertain to the beginning, but to tend to perfection.

QUESTION LVIII. *Not again laying the foundation,* etc. This seems contrary to the aforesaid. For if they still needed the doctrine of the elements, the foundation was necessary to them. Solution. The glosses suffice for this question, expounding how "not again" is to be understood.

QUESTION LIX. *From dead works,* etc. He calls dead works either mortal sins, or good works which have been mortified by an ensuing evil: it is asked therefore how it is true, that no good is unrewarded, as no evil unpunished. Solution. Such goods are therefore called dead, because they [0622C] do not profit unto eternal life: and yet in this they shall be rewarded, because they who did them shall be less punished in the future. Or perhaps a temporal advantage was given for them. Likewise such goods are called dead, because through penance they can revive according to certain men: not that they be any more rewarded for them: but because from goods before done they will more easily attain grace. But it seems to us that, the sin being blotted out by penance, through which they were mortified, they are, as before, before they were mortified, worthy of eternal life.



## XIV. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE HEBREWS. (cont.)

QUESTION LX. *Of baptisms,* etc. It is asked how he here says in the plural "of baptisms," since elsewhere he says: *One Lord, one faith, one baptism [n: (Ephes. IV)]*, etc. If one, how many? Solution. [0622D] As faith is said to be one not in number, but in kind, as the authority teaches, so baptism can be said to be one not in number, but in kind, because it is one form: nor can it be repeated: and because there is there one operation of the three persons, many are spoken of as regards the several washings. Or he therefore says "baptisms" in the plural, because there is baptism in power, in blood: and not for this reason, that the sacrament of baptism is celebrated except in water: but because the shedding of blood, faith, and penance supply the place of baptism: there only where the moment of necessity, not contempt of religion, excludes the sacrament of baptism. To which solution it is thus objected: *Unless a man be born again of water and the Holy Spirit, he shall not enter into the kingdom of heaven [n: (Joan. III)]*. [0623A] According to this authority it seems that no one is worthy of salvation without the reception of this sacrament. Solution. The doctors thus expound the aforesaid words of the Lord, *Unless a man be born again of water,* etc., that is, unless a man be born again by that spirit and regeneration by which are reborn those who are reborn of water, and by the Holy Spirit he can be saved. But by this spirit men are reborn in diverse ways. Some are reborn through penance, others through the shedding of blood, others through baptism. Note that the foundation of the Christian religion is divided into six, which are here set down by the apostles, namely penance, faith, baptism, imposition of hands, resurrection, future judgment: which pertain to the instruction of neophytes.

[0623B]QUESTION LXI. *It is impossible for those who were once illuminated,* etc. Here the Apostle seems to deny penance to those gravely fallen, as in the following, where he says: *To those who sin voluntarily there is now left no sacrifice for sin.* Solution. It seems to us that in both places he describes the sin against the Holy Spirit, into whose pit whoever once falls, it is impossible that he attain penance or salvation. But the doctors say that this "impossible," etc., does not deny all renovation, but only the repetition of baptism. Or it can be understood of the future state: to which opinion certain glosses are of service.

QUESTION LXII. The expositor says that baptism avails even against subsequent sins. But it is asked how sins which are not yet done are already [0623C] forgiven. Solution. Some say that baptism is therefore said to avail against subsequent sins, because through the grace conferred in baptism future sins are avoided. But according to this solution what is this, which in the same gloss follows, "if penance be done concerning them"? It seems to us to be thus understood, that baptism avails against subsequent sins: because by the virtue of baptism they can be blotted out with less satisfaction: so that if it happen that a faithful man and an unfaithful commit the same sin, the penance which suffices for the reborn unto salvation, the same does not suffice for the unfaithful or not yet reborn. This I say without prejudice to a better opinion.

QUESTION LXIII. *The Lord entered in by himself,* etc. Since the Lord says in the Gospel: *Let your speech [0623D] be: Yea, yea; No, no: and that which* over and above *is, is of evil [n: (Matth. V)]*, it is asked how the Lord then swore to Abraham. Would not Abraham believe, or would he believe more slowly, God, if he promised without an oath? Solution. Not on account of Abraham did the Lord swear, since without an oath he firmly believed him: but on account of those who were to come after him, who would either not at all believe, or would more slowly believe, the promise made to Abraham, unless it were confirmed by an oath, and thus that oath was not from the evil of Abraham, but of those who were to come.

QUESTION LXIV. If it be asked: Why did the Lord swear by himself? Solution. Because men are wont to swear by one greater than themselves, but God had not [0624A] a greater: whence it is agreed that they sin who swear by something inferior. For that by which they swear they in a manner constitute above themselves.

QUESTION LXV. *And an oath for confirmation is the end of all their controversy.* What then shall we say of the judgment of fire, or of water, or of others of this kind, which the Church seems to receive? Is not in those also the end of controversy? Solution. These are not judgments of the Church: whence also the guilty are many times thereby absolved, and the not-guilty are sometimes condemned: which would by no means happen, if mother Church held these. Whence in certain places she rather tolerates these than commends them. But someone will say: The same judgment ought to be made concerning an oath. This we grant. For the Church did not institute that, just as neither the aforesaid.

[0624B]QUESTION LXVI. *According to the order of Melchisedech [n: (Hebr. VII)]*, etc. It is asked how? Solution. Christ is said to be a high priest according to the order of Melchisedech in many ways. Both because that man was king and priest alone, so is Christ; both because he was instituted not with visible oil, as Moses, but was anointed with the oil of gladness and the purity of faith; nor did he immolate animals, but in bread and wine offered dedicated the sacrifice of Christ; so also Christ was anointed by the Father with the Holy Spirit; who offered himself on the altar of the cross to God the Father; and because he gave his true body and blood to his disciples at the supper, and because his priesthood remains for ever according to rite and dignity.

QUESTION LXVII. It is asked what that oblation of Melchisedech in bread and wine profited those who received it? Solution. [0624C] Some say that it profited those who received with faith as much as now the body of Christ profits. Which does not seem to us to be true: for the sacraments of the natural law, or of the written law, did not profit, nor could profit, as much as the sacraments of grace.

QUESTION LXVIII. *That which is less is blessed without any contradiction by the better.* Is not often a holy man blessed by one who is of less sanctity: as when a monk is blessed by some secular priest, how then is it true, that which is less is blessed by the greater? Solution. Someone can be less than another in excellence of merits, and greater than the same in dignity: but here it is treated of the majority and minority which consists according to dignity. That therefore which [0624D] is less in dignity is blessed by that which is greater in excellence of dignity. Nor is it here treated of any blessing whatever, but only of that which belongs to the consecrated.

QUESTION LXIX. *Through Abraham, even Levi, who received tithes, was tithed,* etc. Augustine says that just as, Adam sinning, those who were in his loins sinned; so, Abraham giving tithes, those who were in his loins were tithed. But did Christ either sin, Adam sinning; or was he tithed, Abraham giving tithes, since he was in the loins of both, according to the flesh? Since therefore Levi and Christ were alike in the loins of Abraham, how was Levi tithed, and not Christ? Or [0625A] if Christ was tithed like Levi, how does the Apostle prove the priesthood of Christ to be greater than the levitical priesthood: by this, that the levitical order in Levi was tithed in Abraham? Solution. There were some, and perhaps still are, saying that the flesh of Christ from Adam even to the Virgin was kept entire and incorrupt, who are not to be heard. Levi therefore was tithed, and not Christ, although both were there, because Levi contracted thence that whereby he lay subject to tithing, that is, fault. But Christ contracted nothing thence whereby he might be subject to tithing, whose flesh contracted thence not a wound, but the medicament of a wound. To which solution it is thus objected: All the flesh which was in Isaac descended from Abraham according to the common law, namely through concupiscence; [0625B] but the flesh of Christ was in Isaac: therefore the flesh of Christ descended through concupiscence from Abraham. Solution. It is not to be granted absolutely that the flesh of Christ so descended thence through concupiscence: for this would be, that the flesh of Christ was made through concupiscence; yet it can be granted by division: a certain flesh, which afterward was Christ's, descended thence according to the common law, which from the first man even to Mary was under original sin.

QUESTION LXX. *The priesthood being translated, it is necessary that a translation also be made of the law.* It can be asked how, upon the translation of the priesthood, the translation of the law necessarily follows. Solution. Because both were given at once by the same one under [0625C] the same promise: what is asserted of the one is necessarily understood also of the other. Or therefore, the priesthood being translated, the law is necessarily translated; because the translation of the priesthood is made for this, that by its ministry no one was justified, and thus on account of its insufficiency and infirmity it was translated; but the law is equally insufficient and infirm. Whence the Apostle: *The law brought nothing to perfection* [n: (Hebr. )]. By the same necessity therefore is it translated. Because neither therefore could consummate, of both is made a translation: which was figured in the priest and levite, the languishing man, who fell among robbers, passing by and not conferring the mercy of healing [n: (Luc. X)].

QUESTION LXXI. *For it is manifest that our Lord sprang [0625D] out of Juda.* It is asked therefore whether his mother also? Solution. Call back to memory that question which we set down above upon the Epistle to Timothy, which the words of Origen make, saying that Mary was only of the tribe of Levi, and on account of Joseph only was the Lord said to be of the tribe of Juda. But, as I said, two tribes were intermingled, the royal and the sacerdotal, and the Virgin was on the father's side, of her father, of the tribe of Juda, and on the mother's side of the tribe of Levi.

QUESTION LXXII. The law brought nothing to perfection, etc. Were not certain men perfect in the time of the law, as David, and many others; and had they not that justice, since they were just, from obedience to the divine law? how then were they not just from the law? Solution. The just of that time had justice not from the law, but from faith in him who was to come: [0626A] for the law showed sins, it did not take them away.

QUESTION LXXIII. *To make intercession for us,* etc. It is asked how Christ makes intercession for us? Solution. The expositor says that it is by the representation of himself, which is thus to be understood, that the merit of his passion, which he exhibited in his humanity, reconciles us believers to the Father.

QUESTION LXXIV. *Who hath not daily necessity [n: (Hebr. VIII)].* It is asked concerning the sacrifices of the law what utility they conferred: perhaps the remission of sins? Solution. For certain sins the sinners were forbidden entrance, and through the sacrifices they were so reconciled that it was permitted them to enter into the temple: but not through such remission were they made [0626B] worthy of eternal life.

QUESTION LXXV. *If then he were upon earth, he would not be a priest,* etc. It is asked how these things are to be understood. Solution. The text of this chapter is less coherent, and cut off: and therefore brings in obscurity. Whence it is expounded in five ways, as is had in the glosses. Note that in every sacrifice four things are considered: namely, to whom it is offered, and by whom it is offered, what is offered, and for whom it is offered. The same one mediator of both, reconciling us to God through the sacrifice of peace, would remain one with him to whom he offered, would make one in himself those for whom he offered; he himself would be one who offered and what he offered.



## XIV. ON THE EPISTLE TO THE HEBREWS. (cont.)

QUESTION LXXVI. *Who serve the exemplar and shadow,* [0626C] etc. It is asked what is here called "exemplar." Solution. The truth shown on the mount to Moses is called the exemplar of the figures made according to it. Likewise the figures themselves are called the exemplar of the truth, which was afterward fulfilled: and thus the figures themselves, in a different respect, can be called both example and exemplar. For "exemplar" [sic: *onim*] is properly said that to whose likeness something is made; "example," that which is drawn from it.

QUESTION LXXVII. *For if that former had been without fault,* etc. Is not the law good, holy and given by God? how then is it not without fault? Solution. For that reason the Old Testament is said not to be without fault, for which the law is said to work wrath, namely, because it does not justify, and because it commands what cannot be done without grace.

[0626D]QUESTION LXXVIII. It is asked why we do not worship God with that rite with which the Hebrew fathers worshiped him? Solution. Because God commanded us another thing through the fathers of the New Testament: nor are these things which we observe against the Old Testament, but foretold and pre-announced in it.

QUESTION LXXIX. Likewise, why do we hold the authority of that testament? Solution. Lest we extinguish the prophets, and take away the testimony of truth from our midst. When therefore something is read which is not observed by us, we must only ask what it signifies, not reprehend it; because by the very fact that it is now not observed, it is proved not condemned, but fulfilled.

QUESTION LXXX. Likewise it is asked whence that is called the Old Testament, this the New, since the law is fulfilled [0627A] through the New Testament? Solution. The Old Testament is called "old" for the old harm, which is not healed through the letter commanding and threatening. But this is called "new" on account of the newness of spirit, which heals a man from the vice of oldness. Note diligently what is the difference between the two testaments: that old, this new. There the letter, which alone kills, here the spirit giving life. That is written on tables of stone, this on minds and hearts. That promises earthly things, this heavenly. That has sacraments signifying salvation, not conferring it; this has sacraments conferring salvation. Likewise note that "testament" is said both of the promise itself, temporal or eternal, and of the writing containing the promise itself. The Old Testament therefore contains the promise [0627B] pertaining to the old man. But the New Testament, which contains the promise of the eternal inheritance which belongs to the new man.

QUESTION LXXXI. *For if the ashes [sic: vitulaeaspersus] sanctify the defiled [n: (Hebr. IX)]*, etc. It is asked what he calls the defilement, from which the sprinkled ashes of a heifer sanctify? Solution. He so calls the touch of the dead; or leprosy, from which those ashes cleanse, that is, from the punishment which according to the law was due to such uncleanness: by which touch is signified the consent of sin.

QUESTION LXXXII. *And as it is appointed unto men once to die.* The expositor says that Christ died by the same necessity and right of nature by which necessity, and in fact, other men die. [0627C] Whence it is asked whether Christ died by necessity, and whether he had the necessity of dying? Which seems manifest according to the aforesaid authority. But if by necessity, how did he die by will alone, as it is written: *He was offered because he willed it? [n: (Isa. LIII.)]* Solution. Some say that among the other penalties which the Lord took up with our nature without fault, he took up also this penalty, namely the necessity of dying, voluntarily: which does not exclude the will, nor is excluded by it. Nor yet is it to be understood but that Christ could by the power of divinity lay aside this penalty without the dissolution of soul and body, and clothe over the nature assumed with the stole of immortality; but if he conferred no more [0627D] than was conferred on it before death, he would necessarily die.

QUESTION LXXXIII. *To them that expect him unto salvation,* etc. Upon this place the expositor says that Christ died not by necessity, but by will for the sin of those expecting him unto salvation: but above it was said that he died by necessity: which seems to be contrary. Solution. What he says here, understand thus. Christ died not by necessity, that is, in him there was no sin for which it was necessary that he die: whence, as it were expounding what he had said, he subjoins: but he died for their sin, namely of those who expected him unto salvation.

QUESTION LXXXIV. *Otherwise they would have ceased to be offered [n: (Hebr. X)]*, etc. The legal victims, as the Apostle says, if [0628A] they made perfect would cease to be offered: whence it is asked why the salutary victim of the New Testament, since it perfects, and consummates the sanctified, is more often offered, and does not cease to be offered? Solution. Once indeed it was offered through the passion of death on the altar of the cross in human form; nor is it again so offered through death, but yet in the sacrament it is more often offered, not by reason of its own infirmity, but rather of ours, who daily sin: and especially for the remembrance of the death of Christ, that his love may be more deeply fixed in our hearts through this, that we are mindful of so great a benefit.

QUESTION LXXXV. *But a body thou hast fitted to me,* etc. It is asked in what he fitted the body of Christ? Solution.
